Git Mastery

What Git actually is underneath — four kinds of immutable object, a set of pointers into them, and three views of your project — and why every command, every error message and every recovery technique falls out of that. Plus the parts nobody teaches: the wire protocol you can read with curl, the packfile layer that turns three megabytes into seventy kilobytes, and the one flag that stops a force push destroying a colleague's work.

How to use this course

  • The mechanism comes first. Sections 3 and 4 build Git's object database and reference layer by hand, using only low-level commands, and by the end of §3 you will have made a repository with real history without once running git add or git commit. Everything after that is explained rather than memorised. It is an hour, and it is the difference between using Git and guessing at it.
  • Everything here was run. Every transcript came out of a real terminal against Git 2.53, and the surprising results were kept rather than tidied — including the measurement showing that a widely recommended flag is defeated by a background git fetch, and the one showing that a cache everyone recommends makes the command they actually run no faster at all.
  • It assumes no Git. It assumes you are comfortable in a terminal and can read a little Python. Section 1 starts at what version control is for.
  • The authority is Git itself. The syllabus was derived from the full table of contents of Pro Git, 2nd edition including chapter 10, Git Internals, with the material that book predates — worktrees, sparse-checkout, partial clone, git maintenance, reftable, SHA-256 — taken from the Git 2.53 reference manual. Where a claim is version-dependent it is named, and several pieces of very common advice turn out to describe versions of Git that no longer exist.
  • Each section is Concept → Mechanism → Practice → Exercises with solutions.
  • You need a terminal and Git installed. Nothing else: fifteen of the sixteen sections work entirely on your own machine with no server and no account.
git --version                # 2.34+ for almost everything; 2.45 for §4.9, 2.49 for §15.11
git config --global user.name 'Your Name'
git config --global user.email 'you@example.com'
git config --global init.defaultBranch main

Two of those settings are worth a word. user.name is recorded on every commit you make and is never verified by anything, so setting user.name wrong is a mistake you will find months later in git log. And init.defaultBranch decides what a new repository's first branch is called — set init.defaultBranch once and every git init after it agrees with your team.

Versions every transcript in this course was produced against: Git 2.53.0 · git-filter-repo · Git LFS 3.7.1 · GnuPG 2.4.8 · OpenSSH 10.2p1 · Ubuntu 26.04. Your object ids will differ where a commit's timestamp is part of the hash; the blob and tree ids will match exactly, because they depend only on content.

One note that will save confusion later: this course prefers git switch and git restore over git checkout. All three still work, and you will meet checkout everywhere; §7.10 explains why the split happened and which of checkout's two jobs is the dangerous one.

What you are actually learning

Not a list of commands. Four mechanisms, each of which turns a family of Git behaviour from something to memorise into something you can predict:

  1. Content-addressed objects. Four object types, each named by the hash of its own bytes, and a commit that holds a whole tree rather than a diff. That is why a blob carries no filename, why Git has no concept of a rename, and why identical content has the identical id on every machine (§3).
  2. Pointers into them. A branch is a small file holding a commit id, HEAD usually points at a branch, and a detached HEAD is the absence of a name rather than a fault. Every "destructive" operation writes new objects and moves a pointer, which is why the reflog can almost always bring work back (§4, §7–§8).
  3. Three trees. The working tree, the index and HEAD. add, commit, reset, restore and stash each move content between two of them, and the three reset modes are three stopping points along one operation (§1, §7).
  4. A graph you can query and a protocol you can read. Revision ranges, -S against -G and bisect run interrogate history (§9); a fetch is a negotiation over a set difference that you can watch on the wire (§10–§11).

By the end you should be able to predict what a command will do to each of the three trees before running it, recover work that looks lost, rewrite a branch without silently dropping a commit, and tell the difference between a thing that is gone, a thing that is unreachable, and a thing that was never recorded.

SECTION 1

Version Control, the Repository and the Three Trees

Git has a reputation for being hard, and the reputation is deserved but misattributed. The commands are not hard. What is hard is that most people learn Git as a list of incantations without ever being told what the thing underneath is — and a mental model that is nearly right generates confident wrong predictions forever.

So this section builds the model first. It is short, it is genuinely simple, and it is the thing that makes the rest of the course possible: Git is a content-addressed object store with a few pointers into it. Every command in the next fifteen sections is a manipulation of that store or of those pointers, and every behaviour that looks arbitrary is a consequence of it.

If you already use Git daily, do not skip §1.4 and §1.5. The three-trees model in particular is the thing most working developers are missing, and it is the direct explanation for add, commit, reset, restore, stash, and the entire structure of a merge conflict.

1.1 What Version Control Is For

The obvious answer — a backup, an undo button — is true and is the least of it. The properties that matter are the ones a directory of project-final-v2-REAL.zip files cannot give you:

A record of why
Each change carries a message. Six months later, "why is this timeout 5 seconds?" is answerable in one command (§9.11) rather than by asking whoever is still on the team.
Parallel work
Several people, several unfinished things, one codebase, without anyone waiting. This is what branching and merging are for (§5).
A search space
"Which change broke this?" becomes a binary search over history that takes ten steps on a thousand commits (§9.10).
A named state
"Version 2.4.1" is an exact set of bytes anyone can reproduce, which is where every release and deployment process starts.

Git delivers all of these, and it does so with one unusual property that shapes everything else: it is a distributed version control system. A centralized version control system — CVS, Subversion, Perforce — keeps the history on a server and gives each developer a working copy of one revision; a distributed version control system gives every participant the whole thing. Every clone is a complete repository with the entire history, not a checkout of one. There is no privileged copy — the one on the server is special only because everyone agreed it is.

That is not just a resilience story. It is why committing is instant and works offline, why branching is cheap, why history is rewritable before you publish it, and why "the server" in §11.8 turns out to be a directory with no software in it.

The word for what a centralized version control system gives you is a working copy; the word for what Git gives you is a clone. That difference is why a centralized version control system needs the server to be reachable before you can see yesterday's version of a file, and Git does not.

Two of those deserve a name. "Which change broke this" is answered by a binary search over the commit graph — git bisect (§9.10) — and a binary search over a thousand commits is ten tests rather than a thousand. And a repository is a .git directory plus the files beside it; copying that .git directory copies the entire project history, which is §1.4's subject.

1.2 Snapshots, Not Differences

Most version control systems store a file plus a list of changes to it. Git does something else, and this is the first place intuition goes wrong.

Every commit records a complete snapshot of the whole project. Not a diff against the previous commit — a full description of every file's content at that instant.

The obvious objection is disk space, and it is answered twice. First, content is stored once per unique content: a commit that changes one file in a thousand reuses the other 999 by reference, because they have not changed and Git identifies content by what it is rather than by where it is (§3.9). Second, underneath the object model there is a storage layer that does use differences — packfiles compress similar objects against each other, and in §15.3 you will watch 2,984 KiB of loose objects become 71 KiB.

The consequence worth carrying forward: the diffs you see are computed, not stored. git show produces a patch by comparing two snapshots on the spot. That is why checking out a ten-year-old commit is as fast as checking out yesterday's, and why Git has no concept of a rename (§3.9) — it sees two snapshots and infers one.

1.3 Everything Is Named by Its Content

The second unusual property: every object in Git is named by the cryptographic hash of its own bytes.

A file's content, a directory listing, a commit — each gets an identifier like c02e471f8618617bd24a14d5ab57abc2f6f2b17e, computed from the content itself. Not assigned, not incremented: computed. Change one byte and the identifier is completely different.

Five things follow, and each is load-bearing later:

Deduplication is free
Identical content is the same object. Two files, a hundred commits — one copy.
Integrity is free
Corrupt one byte and the object no longer matches its own name. git fsck finds it with certainty (§15.8).
Nothing can be edited
Objects are immutable. "Editing history" (§8) means writing new objects and moving a pointer — the old ones are still there, which is why §7.9's recovery works.
A commit id covers everything
A commit names its tree and its parents, so its id is a checksum of the entire history behind it. That is what makes a signature meaningful (§11.11).
Distribution is easy
Two repositories can compare what they have by exchanging identifiers, with no coordination and no central authority (§11.6).

Linus Torvalds has described Git as a content-addressable filesystem with a version control interface on top, and that is a literal description of the architecture rather than a joke. §3 builds that filesystem from the bottom.

The technical term for this is content-addressable storage, and it is worth saying that Git is not the only system built this way — a content-addressable store underlies every modern container registry and every deduplicating backup tool. What is unusual about Git is that the addressing is exposed all the way to the user interface, so the identifiers you paste into a chat message are the same identifiers the storage layer uses.

1.4 The Repository

git init -q myproject
cd myproject
find .git -maxdepth 1 | sort

.git
.git/config
.git/description
.git/HEAD
.git/hooks
.git/info
.git/objects
.git/refs

That is a complete repository. Seven entries, and three of them matter now:

objects/
The object database. Every version of every file, every directory listing, every commit — all named by content (§3).
refs/
The pointers. Branches and tags: human-readable names for object ids (§4).
HEAD
Which branch you are on. One line of text.

config holds this repository's settings (§13.1), hooks/ holds scripts Git runs at certain moments (§13.7) — initially just disabled samples — and info/ and description are minor.

The repository is a directory. Copy .git and you have copied everything: all history, all branches, all objects. Delete it and you have a directory of files with no history. There is no database server, no daemon, no registry, and nothing outside this directory that knows the project exists.

The files you actually edit sit next to .git, and that is the working tree — an ordinary directory that Git writes into when you check something out and reads from when you commit.

1.5 The Three Trees

Here is the model. Git maintains three views of your project at all times, and every command in this course is a statement about moving content between them.

Working tree
The files on disk. Your sandbox — edit freely, Git is not watching.
Index (staging area)
The proposed next commit. A real file, .git/index, listing every path with the id of its content.
HEAD
The last committed state — the snapshot the current commit describes.

The everyday commands move between adjacent pairs:

git add
Working tree → index. "Include this in the next commit."
git commit
Index → a new commit; HEAD's branch moves to it.
git restore --staged
Index ← HEAD. Unstage (§7.2).
git restore
Working tree ← index. Discard an edit (§7.2).
git reset --hard
Move HEAD's branch, then force both others to match (§7.3).
git status
Report the differences between all three.

The middle one is what makes Git unusual. Most systems commit whatever is in your working directory; Git inserts a step where you compose the commit. That is not ceremony — it is what lets an afternoon of tangled work become three clean, reviewable commits (§2.6), and it is where a merge keeps the three versions of a conflicted file (§6.4).

Once you hold this model, a large set of otherwise-arbitrary facts collapses into one:

Editing after git add
leaves the old version staged, because add copied content at that moment.
git diff showing nothing
means nothing is unstaged — it compares the working tree to the index, not to the commit (§2.7).
Three reset modes
are three stopping points along the same operation (§7.3).
A conflicted merge
puts three versions of the file in the index at once (§6.4).
A stash
is a commit that records the working tree and the index separately (§7.7).

One more file appears the moment you stage anything: .git/index. Everything above is on disk before you have run a single command that changes a file, and .git/index is the one piece of the three trees that does not exist in a fresh repository — it is created by the first git add.

1.6 First-Time Setup

Git needs to know who you are, because every commit records an author and a committer (§3.6).

git config --global user.name 'Ada Lovelace'
git config --global user.email 'ada@example.com'
git config --global init.defaultBranch main
git config --global core.editor vim

--global writes ~/.gitconfig and applies everywhere; omit it inside a repository to set it for that repository only. git config --list --show-origin shows every setting and which file it came from, which is the answer to every "why is Git doing that" configuration question (§13.2).

The address is not verified by anything. It is a string in the commit, which is why signing exists (§11.11) and why §13.3 shows how to keep work and personal identities from being mixed up by accident.

A handful of other settings are worth changing on a new machine, each explained where it is introduced:

git config --global pull.ff only
git config --global merge.conflictstyle zdiff3
git config --global fetch.prune true
git config --global rebase.autoStash true
git config --global push.useForceIfIncludes true

They are collected with their justifications in §13.5. Setting them now costs nothing and removes several classes of confusion before you meet them.

git config --list prints everything Git believes, which after five minutes of setup is a surprisingly long list — most of it defaults you never set. Adding --show-origin says which file each value came from (§13.2). The core.editor setting is worth getting right early: Git opens it for commit messages, for interactive rebase todo lists (§8.3), and for conflict resolution, and being dropped into an editor you cannot exit is a genuinely common first-day experience.

1.7 The Command Surface

git version
git --list-cmds=list-mainporcelain | wc -l
git --list-cmds=main | wc -l

git version 2.53.0
45
169

git version and git --version are the same command; git version --build-options additionally reports the platform and compile-time settings, which is what a bug report wants.

A hundred and sixty-nine commands, and this is where Git's reputation comes from. But the number is misleading, because they fall into two clearly separated groups:

Porcelain
The user-facing commands — add, commit, merge, log, push. Forty-five of them, and you will use perhaps fifteen daily. They are allowed to change their output between versions, because humans read it.
Plumbing
The low-level commands the porcelain is built from — hash-object, cat-file, rev-parse, update-ref. Forty-four of them. Their output is stable, which is what makes them the right tools for scripts and for finding out what is really going on.

That split is why this course starts with §3. git commit is a composition of four plumbing commands, and the fastest way to understand it is to run those four by hand — which takes about ten minutes and permanently changes how the rest of Git looks.

Two universal options worth knowing immediately. git -C <dir> runs a command as if from that directory, which keeps scripts honest without cd. And git -c <key>=<value> sets a configuration value for one invocation, which is how you try a setting without committing to it.

The distinction is not merely documentary. A plumbing command promises stable output and a stable exit status, which is what makes it safe to build on; the porcelain promises neither, and its output has changed many times. Every script in this course that parses Git's output uses a plumbing command for exactly that reason.

1.8 Getting Help

Git's documentation is unusually good and is installed on your machine.

git help <command>
The full manual page. git <command> -h gives the short summary instead.
git help -g
The concept guides, which most people never discover.
git help revisions
The complete revision-naming language (§9). The single most useful page Git ships.
git help glossary
Precise definitions of every term Git uses.
git help everyday
A minimum working set of commands by role.
git help workflows
The collaboration models of §12, from the source.
git help attributes
Everything .gitattributes can do (§13.9).

One thing worth internalising early: git status is help. It names the state you are in — mid-merge, mid-rebase, detached HEAD, branch diverged — and lists the commands that apply. When something has gone wrong, it is the first thing to read and it usually contains the answer.

A useful way to hear the distinction: porcelain is the fixtures you see in a bathroom and plumbing is what is behind the wall. git commit is a porcelain command; the four commands §3.7 uses to reimplement it are plumbing.

A porcelain command is one you type; a plumbing command is one a porcelain command calls. Most of the time the distinction is invisible, and the two occasions it matters are writing a script — where a porcelain command's output may change under you — and understanding what a command really did, which is §3.7.

1.9 A First Repository, End to End

git init -q myproject
cd myproject
printf 'def greet(name):\n    return "Hello, " + name\n' > greet.py
git status -s

?? greet.py

Untracked: Git can see the file and knows nothing about it.

git add greet.py
git status -s

A  greet.py

Staged. The content is now in the object database and listed in the index; the commit does not exist yet.

git commit -m 'add a greeting function'

[main (root-commit) 2683185] add a greeting function
 1 file changed, 2 insertions(+)
 create mode 100644 greet.py

(root-commit) because it has no parent. 2683185 is the first seven characters of the commit's id — computed from its content, not assigned.

git log --oneline
git status

2683185 add a greeting function
On branch main
nothing to commit, working tree clean

"Working tree clean" means all three trees agree. That is a complete, permanent, checksummed record of the project, created in four commands with no server involved.

1.10 How the Rest of This Course Is Arranged

The order is deliberate and is worth stating, because it is not the order most Git material uses.

§2
The everyday loop: status, add, commit, diff, log. Enough to work.
§3–§4
The mechanism. The object database and the reference layer, built by hand. Everything after this is explained rather than memorised.
§5–§6
Branching and merging, ending with conflicts as a data structure rather than an accident.
§7–§8
Undoing and rewriting — where the object model pays for itself.
§9
The revision language and the investigative commands: log's filters, grep, bisect, blame.
§10–§12
Other people: remotes and refspecs, the wire protocol and signing, then the collaboration conventions built on top.
§13–§14
Configuration, attributes and hooks; then submodules, subtrees and worktrees.
§15
Scale: packfiles, garbage collection, partial and shallow clones, sparse checkouts.
§16
A capstone that uses the lot.

Sections 3 and 4 are the investment. They are the reason this course claims you will be able to reason about Git rather than recall commands, and skipping them is the one thing that will make the rest harder than it needs to be.

Exercises

1. Explain, without using the word "diff", what a Git commit contains.

Solution

A commit contains a complete snapshot of the project at one instant, plus metadata: who wrote it, who recorded it, when, a message, and the identifier of the commit or commits it came after (§1.2).

It does not contain a change, a patch, or a list of what was modified. When git show displays a patch, Git computes it at that moment by comparing this commit's snapshot with its parent's.

The two objections this raises are both answered by §1.3. Space: content is stored once per unique content, so an unchanged file is shared by reference across every commit that contains it, and the packfile layer (§15.3) then delta-compresses similar objects underneath — measurably turning 2,984 KiB into 71 KiB in one worked example. Speed: checking out any commit is a direct read of that snapshot, with no chain of patches to replay, which is why an old commit is as fast as a recent one.

The most practically important consequence: since only snapshots are recorded, a rename is not recorded either. Git detects renames afterwards by noticing similar content under a new path (§3.9).

2. Someone deletes the .git directory of a project and asks whether the history is recoverable from the files on disk. Answer.

Solution

No. The history was entirely inside .git (§1.4): the object database in .git/objects, the branches and tags in .git/refs, and HEAD. The files next to it are the working tree — one checked-out snapshot with no history attached.

What survives is exactly what you would have had without version control: the current state of the files.

Recovery therefore depends entirely on another copy of the repository existing. Because Git is distributed (§1.1), a git clone on a colleague's machine or on a server is a complete repository, not a partial one — every commit, every branch, all the history. Cloning it back restores everything except purely local state: uncommitted changes, stashes (§7.7), the reflog (§4.10), and local configuration.

The general lesson is worth stating plainly: a Git repository is not a backup until a copy of it exists somewhere else. Committing protects you from your own edits; pushing protects you from your disk.

3. You run git add report.py, then keep editing report.py, then git commit. Which version is in the commit, and why?

Solution

The version as it was at the moment you ran git add.

git add copies the file's content into the index (§1.5) — writing it into the object database and recording its identifier. It is a snapshot, not a subscription: later edits to the file do not update the index entry. git commit builds the commit from the index, so it records what was staged.

git status -s shows this as MM report.py: the first column says the index differs from the last commit, the second says the working tree differs from the index (§2.2). Three versions exist at once.

This is the staging area behaving as designed rather than a trap — it is what makes git add -p able to compose a commit from part of a messy working tree (§2.6). But it does mean git diff --staged before committing is the habit that catches the surprise, because plain git diff shows the other comparison and can be empty while a commit is pending.

git commit -a stages tracked modifications first and sidesteps it, at the cost of committing everything (§2.5).

4. Give two consequences of Git identifying objects by the hash of their content that would not hold if commits had sequential numbers.

Solution

Any two of these (§1.3):

Integrity checking is inherent. An object's name is a checksum of its bytes, so corruption is detectable with certainty and no separate manifest — git fsck re-hashes and compares (§15.8). With sequential numbers, a flipped bit in a stored file would be undetectable.

Two repositories can compare state by exchanging names. A fetch works by sending identifiers and asking what the other side lacks (§11.6). Sequential numbers would need a central authority to allocate them, which is precisely what a distributed system cannot have — two people committing offline would both produce "commit 47".

A commit id certifies its whole history. A commit names its tree and its parents by hash, and those name theirs, so the id at the tip is a checksum of everything behind it. Changing anything anywhere in history changes every descendant's id. This is what makes signing a single commit meaningful (§11.11), and it is the mechanical reason rewriting history produces new commits rather than modifying old ones (§8.1).

Identical content is stored once, automatically, with no comparison step (§3.9).

5. git diff prints nothing but you know you changed a file. Give two possible explanations from the three-trees model.

Solution

git diff with no arguments compares the working tree against the index (§1.5), not against the last commit. So:

You staged the change. After git add, the working tree and the index match, and git diff is correctly empty even though the file differs from the commit. git diff --staged shows it; git diff HEAD shows everything either way.

The file is untracked. Git only diffs tracked content, so a brand-new file appears in no diff at all — it shows as ?? in git status. git add -N <path> records its existence so diffs can see it (§2.3).

In both cases git status would have said so immediately, which is the reason to run it first and often. This is also the clearest single demonstration of why the three-trees model is worth holding: without it, "the diff is empty but the file changed" looks like a bug, and with it there are exactly two possibilities and both are obvious.

6. Why does git help revisions exist as a separate page rather than being part of git help log?

Solution

Because the revision-naming language is not a feature of git log. It is a shared language that every command taking a commit accepts — show, diff, merge, rebase, reset, checkout, bisect, cherry-pick and the plumbing commands all parse the same expressions (§9).

HEAD~3, main@{yesterday}, v1.0^{commit}, HEAD:src/app.py, :/fix the parser, A..B, A...B — these mean the same thing everywhere. Documenting them once, separately, is correct factoring; repeating them in forty manual pages would guarantee they drift.

This is also a good general clue about Git's design. The concept guides (git help -g) describe the shared vocabulary — revisions, glossary, attributes, workflows — while the command pages describe individual tools. When something seems to work in several commands, there is usually a concept page for it, and reading that page once is worth more than reading five command pages.

git help revisions in particular repays a careful read about once a year; it is dense, short, and contains most of §9.

SECTION 2

Recording History

This section is the everyday loop: change files, decide what belongs in a commit, make the commit, look at what you did. It is a small set of commands, and most people learn them as a ritual — git add ., git commit -m, repeat — without ever finding out what the middle step is for.

That is the thing worth getting right here. Git's staging area is unusual: most version control systems commit whatever is in your working directory, and Git deliberately inserts a step where you compose the commit. It is not bureaucracy. It is the mechanism that lets a messy afternoon of work become three clean, reviewable, revertible commits, and §2.6 shows exactly how.

Everything here is the porcelain over §3. git add writes blobs and updates the index; git commit writes a tree and a commit object and moves a ref. When something behaves surprisingly, that is the layer to think in.

2.1 The Three Places a Change Can Be

Working tree
The files on disk. What your editor sees.
Index (staging area)
What the next commit will contain. A real file, .git/index (§3.5).
HEAD
The commit you are on. The last committed state (§4.3).

Two commands move state one step:

git add
Working tree → index.
git commit
Index → a new commit; HEAD's branch moves to it.

And two move it back, which is §7's subject: git restore --staged (index ← HEAD) and git restore (working tree ← index).

A file is tracked if it has an index entry, and untracked otherwise. Git only knows about tracked files — an untracked file is invisible to git diff, is not in any commit, and is not backed up by anything.

2.2 git status

git status reports the differences between all three places, and it is the single most useful command in Git. Run it constantly.

git init -q
git status

On branch main

No commits yet

nothing to commit (create/copy files and use "git add" to track)

printf 'def greet(name):\n    return "Hello, " + name\n' > greet.py
git status

On branch main

No commits yet

Untracked files:
  (use "git add <file>..." to include in what will be committed)
	greet.py

nothing added to commit but untracked files present (use "git add" to track)

The long form is verbose on purpose: it names the state you are in and tells you the commands available. During a conflicted merge or a paused rebase it is the fastest way to find out what is going on and what to type next — read it rather than guessing (§6.3, §8.12).

The short form is what you want once you know the vocabulary:

git status -s
git status -sb

?? greet.py
## No commits yet on main
?? greet.py

Two columns per file, and this is the key to reading it:

Column 1
The index against HEAD — what is staged.
Column 2
The working tree against the index — what is not.
A  extra.py       staged as a new file, nothing further on disk
 M greet.py       modified on disk, nothing staged
MM app.py         modified, staged, then modified again
?? untracked.py   Git has never heard of it

Note the space in M: that is column 1 being empty. M and M are different states, and MM means three different versions of the file exist right now (§7.2). The letters are A added, M modified, D deleted, R renamed, C copied, U unmerged (§6.4), ? untracked and ! ignored.

--porcelain gives the same thing with a stability guarantee for scripts; -s is for humans and its format may change.

2.3 Staging: add, and Why It Exists

git add greet.py
git status -s

A  greet.py

git add copies the file's current content into the index — writing a blob object into the object database in the process (§3.3). Two consequences that catch people out, and both follow from "copies the current content":

Staging is a snapshot, not a subscription. Edit the file after git add and the new edit is not staged; you get MM. The commit will contain what you staged.

git add on a directory expands at that moment. git add src/ stages the files under src/ as they are now. A file created afterwards is not included.

Forms of the command:

git add <path>
One file or directory.
git add .
Everything under the current directory — including untracked files.
git add -A
Everything in the whole repository, wherever you are standing. Adds, modifications and deletions.
git add -u
Only files Git already tracks. Picks up modifications and deletions, ignores new files.
git add -p
Interactive staging, one diff hunk at a time (§2.6).
git add -N <path>
Record the file's existence without its content, so git diff and git add -p can see it. The fix for "my new file does not show up in git diff".

git add . is the habitual one and is worth being slightly wary of: it stages whatever happens to be lying around, which is how credentials, editor swap files and 200 MB of build output end up in history. A well-maintained .gitignore (§2.8) is what makes it safe.

Why a staging area at all? Three reasons that justify the extra step:

Compose the commit
Work rarely arrives one logical change at a time. The index lets you commit the bug fix now and the refactor separately, from the same dirty working tree (§2.6).
Review before recording
git diff --staged shows exactly what you are about to commit. Committing straight from the working tree removes that checkpoint.
Resolve conflicts
During a merge, the index holds three versions of each conflicted file, and git add is the act of declaring it resolved (§6.4). Without an index there would be nowhere to put them.

There is a fourth, quieter one: the index also caches each file's size and modification time, which is how git status decides a file is unchanged with one lstat() instead of hashing it (§3.5). That cache is why git status is fast on a large repository.

One more term for the vocabulary. Git calls the content the index holds a staged change and everything else in the working tree an unstaged change, and git status's two columns are exactly those two categories. Interactive staging with git add -p is the tool for converting part of an unstaged change into a staged change, one diff hunk at a time, and a diff hunk is simply a contiguous run of changed lines plus its surrounding context.

The object written by git add is a blob object (§3.3), and it is written whether or not you ever commit — which is why staging something is a cheap way to make it recoverable. A blob object created by a git add you later abandoned is unreachable rather than absent, and survives until garbage collection (§15.5).

2.4 Removing and Moving

git rm <path>

Deletes the file and stages the deletion. Deleting with rm and then git add -A does the same thing in two steps.

git rm --cached <p>
Untrack it, keep it on disk. This is the fix for "I committed a file that should have been ignored" (§13.6) — and note it only stops future commits; the file remains in history and needs §8.10 to remove.
git rm -r <dir>
Recursive.
git rm -f
Force, when the file has staged or unstaged changes Git would rather not lose.
git mv <old> <new>
Rename and stage it.

git mv is worth understanding rather than trusting. It is exactly mv old new && git rm old && git add new — Git does not record renames (§3.9). What you get in the tree is a deletion and an addition of the same blob id; git status reporting R old -> new is Git detecting the rename after the fact by noticing that a deleted path and an added path have similar content.

So renaming with your editor or the shell is equally fine, as long as you git add -A afterwards. And a rename that also rewrote most of the file may not be detected at all, which is why git log --follow (§9.8) is a heuristic rather than a lookup.

git rm --cached deserves its own note because it is the answer to a very common question. It removes the index entry and leaves the file on disk, which is what "stop tracking this without deleting it" means; the same command with -r handles a whole directory. What git rm --cached does not do is remove the file from history, which needs §8.10.

2.5 Committing

git commit -m 'add a greeting function'

[main (root-commit) 2683185] add a greeting function
 1 file changed, 2 insertions(+)
 create mode 100644 greet.py

The branch, (root-commit) because it has no parent, the new commit's abbreviated id, the subject, and a summary. Underneath, exactly §3.7's sequence: write a tree from the index, write a commit object pointing at it with HEAD's commit as parent, move the branch ref.

git commit
Opens core.editor with a template and the status as comments.
git commit -m '…'
Subject on the command line. Repeat -m for the body: each becomes a paragraph.
git commit -a
Stage all tracked modifications and deletions first. Does not include new files, which is the trap.
git commit -v
Put the full staged diff in the editor. Worth setting commit.verbose = true — reviewing your own change while writing its message catches a surprising amount.
git commit --amend
Replace the previous commit (§8.2).
git commit --allow-empty
A commit with no changes. Occasionally useful to trigger CI or mark a point.
git commit -s
Add a Signed-off-by: trailer (§12.3).

Two ways a commit is refused. Without user.email and user.name, Git stops with Please tell me who you are — set them per repository or globally, and see §13.3 for keeping work and personal identities apart. And a commit-msg or pre-commit hook can reject it (§13.7).

Message content is §12.3's subject and it matters more than anything else here: the diff records what changed, and only the message can record why.

2.6 git add -p

This is the command that makes the staging area worth having. Two unrelated changes in one file:

git diff

@@ -1,4 +1,4 @@
-LIMIT = 10
+LIMIT = 25
@@ -11,4 +11,4 @@ def summarise(items):
-    return items[:LIMIT]
+    return sorted(items)[:LIMIT]

Raising a limit and adding a sort are separate changes that belong in separate commits. git add -p walks the hunks and asks:

git add -p app.py

(1/2) Stage this hunk [y,n,q,a,d,s,e,p,P,?]?

Answer y to the first and n to the second:

git diff --staged
git diff
git status -s

-LIMIT = 10
+LIMIT = 25
-    return items[:LIMIT]
+    return sorted(items)[:LIMIT]
MM app.py

One change staged, the other still in the working tree. Commit the first, then stage and commit the second — two clean commits from one messy edit.

y / n
Stage this hunk / do not.
s
Split into smaller hunks. Git groups nearby changes, so this is often the first thing to press.
e
Edit the hunk by hand — the escape hatch when two changes are on the same line and s cannot separate them.
q
Quit, keeping what you have staged so far.
a / d
All remaining hunks in this file / none of them.
?
Help.

The same -p works on git restore (discard part of a change), git reset (unstage part), git stash push (set part aside) and git checkout. It is one interface across the whole undo family.

This is not a tidiness exercise. Small, single-purpose commits are what make git revert surgical, git bisect (§9.10) able to identify a cause rather than a range, and review possible at all. Composing them at commit time is far cheaper than splitting them afterwards with §8.5's recipe.

2.7 Three Diffs

git diff compares two of the three places, and which two depends on the flags. This is the most frequently misunderstood thing in this section.

git diff

diff --git a/greet.py b/greet.py
index b8b0e32..0f59730 100644
--- a/greet.py
+++ b/greet.py
@@ -3,4 +3,4 @@ def greet(name):


 def farewell(name):
-    return f"Bye, {name}"
+    return f"Goodbye, {name}"

git diff --staged

@@ -1,2 +1,6 @@
 def greet(name):
     return f"Hello, {name}!"
+
+
+def farewell(name):
+    return f"Bye, {name}"

git diff HEAD

 def greet(name):
     return f"Hello, {name}!"
+
+
+def farewell(name):
+    return f"Goodbye, {name}"
git diff
Working tree vs index. "What have I not staged yet?"
git diff --staged
Index vs HEAD. "What am I about to commit?" (--cached is the same thing.)
git diff HEAD
Working tree vs HEAD. "What have I changed altogether?"

Look at what the three outputs show. Plain git diff shows only the Bye → Goodbye change, because that is the only thing not yet staged. --staged shows the whole farewell function being added, with Bye — because that is what was staged. Neither is wrong; they answer different questions, and reading the wrong one is how a commit ends up containing something you did not intend.

git diff showing nothing does not mean nothing changed. It means nothing is unstaged. This is the single most common confusion in this section, and git diff --staged is the answer.

Two more forms, both from §9: git diff <commit> <commit> compares two commits, and git diff <a>...<b> compares b against the merge base — the one to use when reviewing a branch (§12.5).

Useful flags: --stat for a summary, -w to ignore whitespace, git diff --word-diff for prose, --name-only for scripts, and git diff --check for whitespace errors and leftover conflict markers (§13.8).

Two flags deserve to be habits rather than lookups. git diff --check reports trailing whitespace, space-before-tab and leftover conflict markers in whatever you are about to commit, which is three classes of embarrassing mistake caught by one flag — and it is the whole body of a good pre-commit hook (§13.8). And git diff --word-diff marks changes within a line instead of replacing whole lines, which is the difference between a readable and an unreadable diff of prose or of a long single-line configuration value.

2.8 Ignoring Files

Untracked files clutter git status and eventually get committed by accident. .gitignore lists patterns Git should not offer:

__pycache__/
*.pyc
.venv/
node_modules/
dist/
*.log
.env

.gitignore is a tracked file, so the whole team gets the same rules. Three principles:

Project artefacts go here
Build output, dependency directories, local environment files.
Your editor does not
.idea/, *.swp, .DS_Store belong in your global core.excludesFile (§13.6). Your tooling is not the project's business.
It only affects untracked files
A file already committed stays tracked no matter what you add to .gitignore. git rm --cached is the fix.

Each line is a glob pattern: *.pyc matches at any depth, a trailing slash means directory-only, and a leading ! makes a negated pattern that re-includes something an earlier line excluded. git check-ignore -v <path> names the file and line of the rule that matched, which answers "why is this ignored" definitively. The full matching rules — including why a negated pattern inside an ignored directory does nothing — are in §13.6.

A glob pattern here is not quite the shell's: * does not cross a / unless the pattern contains no slash at all, and ** matches across directories. Your own editor and OS droppings belong in core.excludesFile rather than here — git config --global core.excludesFile ~/.gitignore sets it, and the file it names uses exactly the same glob pattern syntax.

2.9 Reading History

git log

Full entries: id, author, date, message. Then the flags, which combine freely:

--oneline
One line each.
--graph
Draw the branch structure (§5.10).
--stat
Files changed and how much.
-p
The full diff of each commit.
-n <count>
Limit.
git log --since
Also --until: git log --since='2 weeks ago'.
git log --author
By author, matched as a pattern against the name and email.
git log --grep
By message. Combine several with --all-match.
git log --no-merges
Skip merge commits, which usually have no content of their own.
git log --decorate
Show which refs point at each commit. On by default in recent Git.
-- <path>
Only commits touching that path. The -- matters when a path and a ref could share a name (§9.3).
git log --oneline -5 -- src/parser.py

is the everyday form: the recent history of one file, compactly. §9 covers the rest of the query language — ranges, content search with -S, --first-parent, blame and bisect — which is where git log becomes an investigative tool rather than a listing.

Four of these are worth committing to memory because they answer questions you will ask weekly. git log --grep searches messages, which is how you find the commit whose message mentions a ticket. git log --author filters by who wrote it. git log --no-merges removes merge commits, which have no content of their own and clutter any survey of what changed. And git log --decorate — on by default now — is what puts the branch and tag names beside the commits, without which --graph is much harder to read.

2.10 git show

git show <commit> prints one commit: its metadata, message, and diff. With no argument it shows HEAD, which makes it the natural "what did I just commit" check.

It also shows any other object (§3.3), and the <rev>:<path> syntax (§9.2) makes it a time machine for single files:

git show HEAD~3:src/config.py

prints that file as it was three commits ago, without touching your working tree, your index, or your branch. That is often what people reach for git checkout to do, at considerable risk.

One flag on git commit is worth calling out because it looks like a shortcut and is a trap. git commit -a stages tracked modifications and deletions before committing, so git commit -a never includes a new file — the commit looks complete, builds on your machine, and fails in CI with an import error.

2.11 The Working Loop

Put together, the loop is:

1
git status — where am I, what is dirty.
2
Edit.
3
git diff — read your own change before deciding what it is.
4
git add -p — stage one logical change, not everything.
5
git diff --staged — confirm that is exactly what you meant.
6
git commit — with a message that says why.
7
Repeat from 4 while anything is left unstaged.

Steps 3 and 5 are the ones that get skipped and the ones that pay. Reading your own diff before committing catches the debugging statement, the commented-out block, the file you did not mean to touch, and the merge marker — all of which are much cheaper to catch now than in review, and vastly cheaper than in production.

The habit to build is commit early and often. A commit is local, costs nothing, is trivially amended (§8.2) and reworked (§8.4), and is the only thing that makes work recoverable (§7.9). Unstaged, uncommitted edits exist in no object and are the one thing Git cannot get back for you.

Exercises

1. git status -s shows MM app.py. Say precisely how many versions of app.py exist and where each is.

Solution

Three. The two columns compare different pairs (§2.2): column 1 is the index against HEAD, column 2 is the working tree against the index. MM means both comparisons found a difference, so the three places disagree pairwise:

  1. HEAD — the version in the last commit.
  2. The index — a version staged with git add, different from HEAD.
  3. The working tree — the file on disk, changed again since staging.
git show HEAD:app.py    # 1
git show :app.py        # 2
cat app.py              # 3

Committing now records version 2, not version 3 — which is the point of the staging area and also the classic surprise. git diff shows 2→3 and git diff --staged shows 1→2 (§2.7).

The state arises naturally: git add a change, then keep editing.

2. You fixed a bug and did a refactor in the same file. Commit them separately without stashing or copying anything.

Solution 
git add -p app.py

Answer y for the hunks belonging to the bug fix and n for the refactor's; press s first if Git grouped them into one hunk, and e if they are on the same line.

git diff --staged
git commit -m 'fix: guard against a null input'
git add -p app.py
git commit -m 'refactor: extract the parsing helper'

git diff --staged between the two is not optional — it is the only confirmation that you selected what you meant to (§2.7).

This is what the staging area is for (§2.3). Without it you would have to stash half the change, or copy the file aside and edit it back, both of which are error-prone.

If the two changes are genuinely interleaved line by line, an easier route is to commit everything as one and then split it during an interactive rebase with §8.5's git reset HEAD^ recipe — the same git add -p, with a working tree that already has both changes.

3. A colleague says git diff shows nothing so their change must be gone. It is not. What happened?

Solution

They staged it. git diff with no arguments compares the working tree against the index (§2.7), and after git add those are identical — so it prints nothing even though the file differs from the last commit.

git diff --staged      # index vs HEAD: their change is here
git diff HEAD          # working tree vs HEAD: everything, staged or not
git status -s          # 'M ' means staged, ' M' means not

The second possibility, if --staged is also empty: the file is untracked. git diff only knows about tracked content, so a brand-new file shows as ?? in git status and appears in no diff at all. git add -N <path> records its existence so diffs can see it, or just git add it.

The general rule worth teaching: git status first, always. It reports all three places at once and would have answered this immediately.

4. Explain why git commit -a is not a shortcut for "commit everything".

Solution

-a stages modifications and deletions of files Git already tracks, and then commits. Untracked files are not included (§2.5).

So a new file — the module you just created, the test you just wrote — is silently left out. The commit looks complete, the build passes locally because the file is on disk, and CI fails with an import error. This is a routine and confusing mistake.

git status before committing shows it: the new file appears under Untracked files and as ?? in the short form.

git add -A && git commit includes everything, which is safe only with a well-maintained .gitignore (§2.8) — otherwise it is how build output and .env files reach history. git add -p (§2.6) is better still, because it forces you to look at what you are committing.

5. You committed a .env file two commits ago. Stop tracking it without deleting your local copy, and say what this does and does not fix.

Solution 
echo '.env' >> .gitignore
git rm --cached .env
git add .gitignore
git commit -m 'stop tracking .env'

--cached removes the index entry, staging a deletion, while leaving the file on disk (§2.4). Adding it to .gitignore stops it being re-added, which matters because .gitignore has no effect on already-tracked files (§2.8).

What this does not fix: the file is still in the two earlier commits, and therefore in every clone, in the forge's storage, and in anyone's fork. git show HEAD~2:.env prints its contents in full (§2.10).

So if it contained real credentials:

  1. Rotate them. First and non-negotiable — everything below is cleanup, not containment.
  2. Rewrite history with git filter-repo --path .env --invert-paths (§8.10), force-push, and have everyone re-clone.

Preventing it is cheaper: .gitignore from the start, plus a pre-commit hook that greps staged content for credential patterns (§13.8).

6. Rename src/parser.py to src/tokenizer.py two ways, and explain why Git does not care which you used.

Solution 
git mv src/parser.py src/tokenizer.py

or

mv src/parser.py src/tokenizer.py
git add -A

Identical results, because Git does not record renames (§3.9). A tree entry is a name pointing at a blob id (§3.4); renaming produces a new tree in which the old name is absent and the new name points at the same blob. git mv is literally the shell version plus the staging, as a convenience.

git status showing R src/parser.py -> src/tokenizer.py is Git detecting the rename afterwards, by noticing a deleted path and an added path with identical or similar content. That is why:

  • Rename detection has a similarity threshold (-M50% by default) and can be tuned with -X rename-threshold= during merges (§6.9).
  • A rename that also rewrote most of the file is reported as an unrelated delete and add.
  • git log --follow (§9.8) is a heuristic that can lose the trail, and -S on a distinctive string is the fallback.

The practical advice: make the rename its own commit, with no content changes. Detection then works perfectly, and both git log --follow and the eventual merge behave.

SECTION 3

The Object Database

Everything Git does is a consequence of one design decision made in the first week of its existence: Git is a content-addressable filesystem with a version-control interface bolted on top. Linus Torvalds put it almost exactly that way, and it is not a joke about the interface. The storage layer knows nothing about branches, merges, history or files-that-changed. It knows about four kinds of immutable record, each named by the hash of its own bytes.

This section builds that layer from the bottom, using only the low-level commands. By the end of it you will have created a repository with real history — two commits, a subdirectory, an annotated tag — without once running git add or git commit, and git log will read it back happily, because there is nothing else for git log to read. Everything in sections 5 through 15 is a convenience over what is built here, and every confusing thing Git does later is explicable from it.

The reason to do this early rather than as a curiosity at the end is economic. A merge conflict, a detached HEAD, a lost commit after a bad rebase, a submodule that will not update, a 4 GB clone that should be 40 MB — each of those is a puzzle if you have memorised commands, and each is obvious if you know what is on disk. You are spending an hour here to stop guessing for the rest of your career.

3.1 Four Object Types and Nothing Else

Git's object database stores exactly four types of object. There is no fifth, and there has never been a fifth.

blob object
The bytes of a file. Nothing else — no name, no permissions, no timestamp, no history. A blob does not know it is a file.
tree
A directory listing: a sorted table of (mode, name, object id) rows, where each row points at a blob or at another tree.
commit
One tree — the whole project at one instant — plus zero or more parent commits, an author, a committer, and a message.
tag
A named, optionally signed pointer at any other object, with its own author and message. This is the object created by git tag -a, and it is the reason there are two kinds of tag.

Four properties are shared by all of them, and each one earns its keep later:

  1. They are named by their own content. The object id is a cryptographic hash of the object's bytes. Two objects with the same content are the same object; there is no way to have two.
  2. They are immutable. Nothing in Git edits an object. Amending a commit, rebasing, changing a file — all of these write new objects and move a pointer. The old objects sit in the database until garbage collection removes them, which is why §7.9's recovery techniques work at all.
  3. They are compressed and stored individually at first, then batched into packfiles later (§15).
  4. They reference each other only by object id, which makes the whole database a directed acyclic graph that can be verified end to end. Corrupt one byte anywhere and git fsck finds it (§15.8).

Everything else you will ever type at Git — branches, tags-that-are-not-tag-objects, remotes, the index, the reflog, stashes, notes — is either a pointer into this graph or a cache derived from it.

One term from that list will recur constantly: a commit's parent commit is the commit it came after, recorded inside it by object id. A commit can have zero parents (the first commit in a repository), one parent (the ordinary case), or two or more (a merge). Nothing records the reverse direction, so a parent commit does not know its children.

3.2 The Hash Is of the Content Plus a Header

Start with an empty repository, and look at what git init actually creates.

git init -q objdemo
cd objdemo
find .git -maxdepth 1 | sort

.git
.git/config
.git/description
.git/HEAD
.git/hooks
.git/info
.git/objects
.git/refs

Seven entries, and two of them matter right now: .git/objects is the object database and .git/refs is where pointers live (§4). There is no .git/index yet, because nothing has been staged. Note also what is not there: modern Git no longer creates the .git/branches directory that older versions did, and the .git/hooks directory contains only disabled samples (§13.7).

Now write a piece of content into the database. git hash-object is the command that computes an object id, and with -w it also stores the object.

echo 'what is up, doc?' | git hash-object --stdin
echo 'what is up, doc?' | git hash-object -w --stdin
find .git/objects -type f

7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293
7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293
.git/objects/71/08f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293

Three things happened there, and all three are worth naming.

The id is identical with and without -w. The name is a pure function of the content, so computing it does not require storing it. That is what makes git hash-object safe to run on anything, and it is how Git decides that a file has not changed without reading history.

The file path is the id, split after two characters. 71/08f7ec… rather than 7108f7ec…. That split exists for a filesystem reason: a large repository has hundreds of thousands of loose objects, and directories with hundreds of thousands of entries were pathological on the filesystems of 2005. The first byte of the hash gives 256 buckets, which is enough.

The stored bytes are not the content. This is the part people guess wrong, and it is checkable:

import hashlib
content = b'what is up, doc?\n'
store = b'blob ' + str(len(content)).encode() + b'\x00' + content
print('content sha1 alone :', hashlib.sha1(content).hexdigest())
print('with git header    :', hashlib.sha1(store).hexdigest())
print('header bytes       :', repr(store[:12]))

content sha1 alone : e8dcc48ee51fa69c976f09084b020f9293b44589
with git header    : 7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293
header bytes       : b'blob 17\x00what'

The object id is sha1(type + " " + length + "\0" + content), and that prefix is the object header. It is why a Git blob id never equals the sha1sum of the same file, which surprises people who try to look up a file by its checksum. It is also a small but real defence: because the type is inside the hash, a blob and a commit with the same bytes cannot collide, and an object cannot be reinterpreted as a different type.

The bytes on disk are that same buffer, zlib-deflated:

import zlib
raw = open('.git/objects/71/08f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293', 'rb').read()
print('compressed', len(raw), 'bytes; first 4:', raw[:4].hex())
print('inflated  ', repr(zlib.decompress(raw)))

compressed 33 bytes; first 4: 78014bca
inflated   b'blob 17\x00what is up, doc?\n'

78 01 is the zlib header for "deflate, 32 KB window, fastest compression" — Git uses level 1 for loose objects because they are short-lived, and saves the expensive compression for packfiles (§15.3). Seventeen bytes of content became thirty-three on disk; loose object storage is not a space optimisation and was never meant to be one.

The object header is worth one more sentence, because it is the piece people leave out when reimplementing this. Its format is exactly <type> <length-in-ascii-decimal> followed by a NUL byte, and the type is one of the four names from §3.1. Since the object header is inside the hash, an object's type is bound to its identity and cannot be reinterpreted.

3.3 Blobs: hash-object and cat-file

git cat-file is the inverse of hash-object, and its three most useful flags answer three different questions.

git cat-file -t 7108f7ec
git cat-file -s 7108f7ec
git cat-file -p 7108f7ec

blob
17
what is up, doc?

-t gives the type, -s the size in bytes, -p pretty-prints according to the type. Note the abbreviated id: eight characters were enough here, and Git resolves any unambiguous prefix of four or more (§9.1).

To see the whole database at once, --batch-all-objects walks every object and --batch-check prints one line of metadata per object instead of the contents:

git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check

7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293 blob 17

One object. Keep that command in mind — it is the single most useful diagnostic in this section, because it answers "what is actually in this repository" without going through any of Git's higher-level abstractions. We will run it again after every step.

Note carefully what the blob does not contain. There is no filename. The content what is up, doc? is in the database, but nothing anywhere says it is greeting.txt, or that it is a text file, or that anyone ever committed it. A blob is content, full stop. Filenames live one level up.

git cat-file -s is the one of the three flags that gets forgotten and is the most useful in a script: it prints an object's size in bytes without printing the object, so checking whether a blob is enormous costs nothing. Combined with --batch-check it is how §15.2 finds what is making a repository large.

3.4 Trees: the Only Place Filenames Live

A tree is a directory listing. To build one by hand you first put an entry into the index — Git's staging area, which is also the scratch space every plumbing command that builds a tree reads from — and then ask Git to write the index out as a tree object.

git update-index --add --cacheinfo 100644,7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293,greeting.txt
git ls-files -s

100644 7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293 0	greeting.txt

--cacheinfo means "take this object that is already in the database and give it this name and mode", as opposed to reading a file from the working tree. That distinction is what makes the demonstration honest: there is no file called greeting.txt on disk. The index now claims one exists. Git is perfectly comfortable with that, and §3.7 shows what happens next.

git ls-files -s shows the index in its stored form: mode, object id, stage number (the 0, which only becomes interesting during a merge — see §6.4), and path.

Now write the index out:

git write-tree

107385e5a58d405be7e9aa9e2eb07e01a5254bf4

git cat-file -t 107385e5
git cat-file -p 107385e5

tree
100644 blob 7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293	greeting.txt

cat-file -p renders a tree in a readable table, but the real bytes are binary — the object ids are stored raw, twenty bytes each, not as hex:

import zlib, glob
for f in glob.glob('.git/objects/*/*'):
    d = zlib.decompress(open(f, 'rb').read())
    if d.startswith(b'tree'):
        print(repr(d))
        break

b'tree 40\x00100644 greeting.txt\x00q\x08\xf7\xec\xb3E\xee\x9d\x00\x84\x19?\x14|\xda\xd4\xd2\x99\x82\x93'

Read that literally. The format of one tree entry is <mode-in-ascii-octal> <space> <name> <NUL> <20 raw bytes of object id>, repeated, with no separator between entries and no trailing terminator. The forty-byte payload is one entry: six bytes of mode, a space, twelve bytes of name, a NUL, twenty bytes of id.

Three consequences fall straight out of that format:

  • A filename cannot contain a NUL byte, which is why Git's -z output modes exist: a path is otherwise arbitrary bytes, including newlines.
  • Entries are stored sorted, so the same set of files always produces the same tree object regardless of the order they were added. Content-addressing requires that; otherwise two identical directories would get different ids.
  • The mode is stored, but only partially — see §3.10.

Add a subdirectory and the nesting becomes visible.

mkdir -p lib
echo 'def parse(s): return s.split()' > lib/parse.py
git add lib/parse.py
git write-tree

1cb5f18871751f4ca2bbb4427cb3b785f4f2289f

git ls-tree 1cb5f188

100644 blob 7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293	greeting.txt
040000 tree ac91203d42ac87ca34e99fd987c4f358aec22cae	lib

The top-level tree has two rows: one blob and one tree. git write-tree created the lib tree object as a side effect, because a tree cannot reference a directory it has not written.

There is also git mktree, which reads that same table on standard input and writes the tree object directly, skipping the index entirely — the tool for building a tree that never corresponds to anything staged.

git ls-tree and git cat-file -p show the same thing for a tree, but ls-tree takes options that make it far more useful in practice:

git ls-tree -r 1cb5f188

100644 blob 7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293	greeting.txt
100644 blob d97274c15413cfbdecfdd4114a663413062abb55	lib/parse.py

git ls-tree -r -t 1cb5f188

100644 blob 7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293	greeting.txt
040000 tree ac91203d42ac87ca34e99fd987c4f358aec22cae	lib
100644 blob d97274c15413cfbdecfdd4114a663413062abb55	lib/parse.py

-r recurses and flattens, collapsing the nesting into full paths; -t puts the intermediate tree rows back in. git ls-tree -r --name-only HEAD is the canonical way to ask "which files does this commit contain", and unlike find it does not have to touch the working tree at all.

git mktree is worth trying once for the same reason git hash-object is: it takes the exact table ls-tree prints on standard input and writes the corresponding tree object, so git ls-tree HEAD | git mktree round-trips and prints back the same id. That is a small proof that the table really is the object.

3.5 The Index Is Flat; Trees Are Nested

Look at the index and the tree side by side after adding the subdirectory:

git ls-files -s

100644 7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293 0	greeting.txt
100644 d97274c15413cfbdecfdd4114a663413062abb55 0	lib/parse.py

The index has two rows and no directories. The tree it produced has two rows, one of which is a directory, plus a second tree object one level down. This is not a cosmetic difference, and three real behaviours come out of it.

Git cannot track a directory
There is no index row for lib. A tree exists only because a blob underneath it does. This is the entire explanation for why an empty directory disappears from a clone, and why the .gitkeep convention exists — it is an ordinary file with a conventional name, and Git has never heard of it.
Staging is per file, not per directory
git add lib/ expands to the files under it at the moment you run it. It is not a subscription.
The index is a cache, and a big one
Each row also holds the file's size, inode number, and modification time, so git status can decide a file is unchanged with one lstat() instead of a hash. That cache is why git status is fast on a large repository and why it gets slow when the cache is invalidated — see core.fsmonitor in §15.11.

You can see the extra cached fields with git ls-files --debug, which prints the stat data Git recorded for each row. That is the mechanism behind a class of confusing behaviour: touch a file without changing its contents and git status may briefly need to re-hash it, because the mtime moved even though the content did not.

3.6 Commits: a Tree, Some Parents, Two Identities

A commit object wraps exactly one tree. git commit-tree takes a tree id and a message and produces one.

echo 'first commit' | git commit-tree 1cb5f188

8c157e7c6ab469779a5bcc451aea839d9f684609

git cat-file -p 8c157e7c

tree 1cb5f18871751f4ca2bbb4427cb3b785f4f2289f
author Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000
committer Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000

first commit

That is the whole format: a header block of key value lines, a blank line, then the message. It is plain text — you can read a commit object with cat if you inflate it first.

A commit contains a whole tree, not a change. There is no diff in a commit object and there never has been. When Git shows you a patch it computes it, on the spot, by diffing this commit's tree against its parent's tree. That is why git show on the very first commit of a repository shows every file as added, and it is why Git can check out any commit at the same speed regardless of how old it is: there is no chain of patches to replay. §3.9 addresses the objection this immediately raises about disk space.

There are two identities, and they are different on purpose. The author is who wrote the change; the committer is who put it in this history. They differ whenever a commit is rebased, cherry-picked, amended by someone else, or applied from an emailed patch — the author is preserved, the committer becomes whoever ran the command. Two consequences: git log shows the author by default but git log --pretty=fuller shows both, and a rebase changes the committer date on every commit it moves, which is why rebased branches look "all committed at once" in tools that sort by committer date.

The commit timestamp is a Unix epoch second plus a stored offset. 1787824800 +0000. The offset is not a timezone name and Git does not convert anything; it records the offset that was in effect and reprints it. This is why a commit made in Tokyo keeps showing +0900 when you read it in London.

Now make a second commit with the first as its parent.

echo 'more' >> greeting.txt
git add greeting.txt
git write-tree

18e230f99f7193515e52132c0ca71f33b0797702

echo 'second commit' | git commit-tree 18e230f9 -p 8c157e7c

c02e471f8618617bd24a14d5ab57abc2f6f2b17e

git cat-file -p c02e471f

tree 18e230f99f7193515e52132c0ca71f33b0797702
parent 8c157e7c6ab469779a5bcc451aea839d9f684609
author Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787825100 +0000
committer Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787825100 +0000

second commit

One parent line. The parent is stored in the child, which fixes the direction of every graph operation in Git: history is walked backwards. A commit knows its parents and cannot know its children. That single fact explains why git log is cheap and "what branches contain this commit" (git branch --contains) is expensive, why git log A..B is the natural query shape (§9.5), and why a commit's id cannot change without changing every descendant's id — the parent id is part of the child's content, so rewriting any commit rewrites everything after it. That is the mechanical reason rebasing produces new commits rather than moving old ones (§8.1).

A merge commit is exactly this with two parent lines, in order: first parent is the branch you were on, second is the branch you merged in. Nothing else distinguishes it. §6.13 uses that ordering, and git revert -m 1 (§7.5) depends on it entirely.

The commit timestamp appears twice — once on the author line and once on the committer line — and git log --pretty=fuller is what shows you both. More generally git log --pretty=format:'%ad %cd %s' lets you print either or both, which is how you diagnose a history whose commits appear in an order that makes no sense (§9.6).

3.7 Building a Repository With Nothing but Plumbing

The two commits exist. Ask Git for the log:

git log --oneline

fatal: your current branch 'main' does not have any commits yet

Nothing is broken. The commits are in the database; no reference points at them, and git log with no argument means "start from HEAD", and HEAD names a branch that does not exist yet:

cat .git/HEAD
ls .git/refs/heads | wc -l

ref: refs/heads/main
0

Create the branch by writing the commit id into a ref — which is all a branch is (§4.2):

git update-ref refs/heads/main c02e471f8618617bd24a14d5ab57abc2f6f2b17e
git log --oneline

c02e471 second commit
8c157e7 first commit

That is a complete repository with real history, built out of hash-object, update-index, write-tree, commit-tree and update-ref. Every porcelain command in the rest of this course is a composition of those five plus their inverses. git commit is write-tree then commit-tree then update-ref, with a great deal of care about the cases where something goes wrong.

The full object graph at this point:

git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check | sort -k2

7108f7ecb345ee9d0084193f147cdad4d2998293 blob 17
d97274c15413cfbdecfdd4114a663413062abb55 blob 31
ef49dd86a6957875edcd0bff210337d6b6dd063c blob 5
8c157e7c6ab469779a5bcc451aea839d9f684609 commit 173
c02e471f8618617bd24a14d5ab57abc2f6f2b17e commit 222
ac91203d42ac87ca34e99fd987c4f358aec22cae tree 36
107385e5a58d405be7e9aa9e2eb07e01a5254bf4 tree 40
18e230f99f7193515e52132c0ca71f33b0797702 tree 70
1cb5f18871751f4ca2bbb4427cb3b785f4f2289f tree 70

Nine objects: three blobs, two commits, four trees. Note 107385e5 — the tree from §3.4, holding only greeting.txt. Nothing references it any more; the second write-tree superseded it. It is now an unreachable object, and git gc will eventually delete it (§15.5). It is doing no harm in the meantime, and the fact that Git tolerates unreachable objects for weeks is precisely what makes recovery from a mistaken reset possible.

And the three trees are genuinely independent of each other and of the disk. This is the clearest possible demonstration:

git status -s
git ls-tree -r HEAD --name-only
git ls-files
ls

A  copy1.txt
A  copy2.txt
greeting.txt
lib/parse.py
copy1.txt
copy2.txt
greeting.txt
lib/parse.py
copy1.txt
copy2.txt
greeting.txt
lib

Four different answers to "what files are in this project", from four different places: what git status thinks changed, what the commit says, what the index says, and what is on disk. Section 7 is about moving state between exactly these, and git status exists to summarise the differences.

3.8 Tag Objects, and Why There Are Two Kinds of Tag

Tags are the one place where Git's user-facing vocabulary genuinely hides two different mechanisms behind one word. Make both in a fresh repository:

git tag -a v1.0 -m 'first release'
git tag v1.0-lw
git cat-file -t v1.0
git cat-file -t v1.0-lw

tag
commit

git tag -a created a tag object, the fourth object type. git tag with no -a created only a reference — a file under refs/tags/ containing a commit id, with nothing else stored anywhere.

git cat-file -p v1.0

object 80da5a42e84a552289d69092c5358d06b7b06127
type commit
tag v1.0
tagger Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000

first release

The same header-block-then-message format as a commit, with the tag message after the blank line and a tagger line recording who made it. Reading the two refs directly makes the difference unambiguous:

cat .git/refs/tags/v1.0 .git/refs/tags/v1.0-lw
git rev-parse v1.0 'v1.0^{commit}' HEAD

0cafe487d0766631144f18d4c919fac8ade72488
80da5a42e84a552289d69092c5358d06b7b06127
0cafe487d0766631144f18d4c919fac8ade72488
80da5a42e84a552289d69092c5358d06b7b06127
80da5a42e84a552289d69092c5358d06b7b06127

The annotated tag's ref points at 0cafe487, a tag object, which points at the commit 80da5a42. The lightweight tag's ref points straight at 80da5a42. v1.0^{commit} is the peel syntax (§9.2): follow the chain until you reach an object of that type. It is quoted here because { and } are shell metacharacters — unquoted it happens to work in bash, and fails in others, so quoting revision expressions containing braces is the habit worth having. Most commands peel automatically, which is why the distinction between the two kinds of tag is invisible until it is not.

Annotated tag
Has a tagger, a date, a message, and can be GPG- or SSH-signed (§11.10). git describe uses it by default. Use this for releases — the metadata is the point.
Lightweight tag
A ref and nothing more. No record of who made it or when. Use it for private, temporary bookmarks.

git tag -l lists tags, optionally filtered by a glob (git tag -l 'v1.*'), and git tag -d deletes one — deleting the ref, and leaving any tag object unreferenced until garbage collection (§15.5).

A tag object can point at any object, not just a commit — you can tag a blob, which is occasionally used to ship a signed public key inside a repository. git cat-file -p on such a tag shows type blob.

Two more git tag forms complete the picture. git tag -d <name> deletes a tag, which removes only the ref — an annotated tag's tag object survives as an unreachable object until garbage collection (§15.5). And the tag message is what distinguishes an annotated tag from a lightweight one in practice: writing a tag message forces you to say what the release is, and it is the text git show <tag> prints above the commit.

3.9 Deduplication, and Why Git Stores Snapshots Rather Than Diffs

The obvious objection to "every commit stores a whole tree" is disk space. Copy a file twice and watch what happens:

cp greeting.txt copy1.txt
cp greeting.txt copy2.txt
git add copy1.txt copy2.txt
git ls-files -s

100644 ef49dd86a6957875edcd0bff210337d6b6dd063c 0	copy1.txt
100644 ef49dd86a6957875edcd0bff210337d6b6dd063c 0	copy2.txt
100644 ef49dd86a6957875edcd0bff210337d6b6dd063c 0	greeting.txt
100644 d97274c15413cfbdecfdd4114a663413062abb55 0	lib/parse.py

Three names, one object id. Adding two identical files added zero new objects to the database. That is content-addressing paying for itself: identical content is stored once no matter how many names or how many commits mention it.

Now apply that to history. A commit that changes one file in a thousand-file project writes:

1 blob
the new file content
1 tree per changed directory
the file's directory, and each ancestor up to the root — typically 2 to 5
1 commit
≈200 bytes

Every other tree and every other blob is reused by id from the previous commit. The nine hundred and ninety-nine unchanged files cost nothing. So the snapshot model is not extravagant: it is a snapshot in the sense that the data structure describes a complete state, while the storage is shared structurally, the same way a persistent immutable data structure shares subtrees.

This has a direct practical consequence that catches people out. Git has no concept of a rename. Renaming a file writes a new tree with a different name pointing at the same blob id. When git log --follow or git status reports a rename, Git detected it after the fact by noticing that a deleted path and an added path have identical or similar content. That is why rename detection has a similarity threshold (-M50% by default) and why it can miss a rename that also changed most of the file's contents. The information was never recorded; it is being inferred every time.

The remaining objection — that a large file edited a hundred times stores a hundred near-identical blobs — is real, and it is answered not here but in the packfile layer, which delta-compresses similar objects against each other after the fact (§15.3). The split matters: the object model is snapshots, the storage format uses deltas, and keeping them separate is what lets Git change its storage format without changing any object id.

3.10 File Modes: the Six Values Git Uses

Git stores a mode in every tree entry, but it deliberately stores almost nothing. Build a directory with three kinds of entry:

echo 'plain' > plain.txt
printf '#!/bin/sh\necho hi\n' > run.sh
chmod +x run.sh
ln -s plain.txt link.txt
git add -A
git ls-files -s

120000 dab8c79946b1756dcd7db770a986ad40d00c07f4 0	link.txt
100644 b9bca019c83a65e6d717d0b6da86215f45dde1b3 0	plain.txt
100755 4163036efa65bd4a469e752267498f01ea36a55c 0	run.sh
file mode 100644
Regular file, not executable. This is the mode you will see on almost every entry.
100755
Regular file, executable.
120000
Symbolic link. The blob's content is the link target as a string.
040000
A subtree. Appears in tree objects, never in the index.
160000
A gitlink: a commit id belonging to another repository. This is a submodule (§14.1) and it is the only entry type whose target is not in this object database.
100664
A regular file recorded with group-write. Git normalises this to 100644 on write — it exists only in trees written by very old versions and by some import tools.

That is the complete list. There is no mode for "read-only", no owner, no group, no setuid bit, no ACL, and no timestamp. Git records exactly one bit of permission information — executable or not — because that one bit is the only one that is a property of the content rather than of the machine. Everything else is deployment configuration, and reproducing it is the job of your packaging or provisioning system, not of version control.
