The symlink case is the neat one. Its blob is not a link; it is a short text file:

git cat-file -p "$(git rev-parse :link.txt)"

plain.txt

Nine bytes with no trailing newline — the link target as a plain string. The 120000 mode is what tells git checkout to call symlink() instead of open(). On a filesystem that cannot make symlinks, Git checks the blob out as an ordinary file containing that text, which is exactly what happens on Windows without developer mode enabled and is the source of a long-running class of confusing bug reports.

Note also the syntax git rev-parse :link.txt. A leading colon means "the index entry for this path" (§9.2), which is how you get at an object id without knowing it.

In practice you will meet only two of these six. File mode 100644 is almost every entry you will ever see; 100755 is the next commonest; and the rest appear once each in a repository that uses symlinks, subdirectories or submodules. Note that file mode 100644 is written in octal and is not a full Unix mode — the leading 100 is the object type (regular file), not permission bits.

3.11 SHA-1, SHA-256, and the Transition

Git was built on SHA-1. SHA-1 has been considered cryptographically weak since 2005 and was demonstrated broken in practice by the SHAttered collision in 2017, so the natural question is whether Git is broken. The answer has three parts, and all three are worth knowing because the topic attracts confident nonsense.

First, Git ships a hardened SHA-1. Since 2017 it uses sha1collisiondetection, an implementation that detects the specific disturbance vectors a collision attack must use and refuses the input rather than returning a hash. The two SHAttered PDFs cannot be committed to a modern Git repository — it errors out. That closed the practical attack while leaving object ids unchanged.

Second, a collision would not silently rewrite history anyway. Git refuses to overwrite an object that already exists, so an attacker who produced a colliding blob would find their object not stored — the existing one wins. The realistic attack is subtler than "swap a file" and the hardened hash blocks its known construction.

Third, the real fix exists and is usable today. Git supports SHA-256 repositories:

git init -q --object-format=sha256 sha256demo
cd sha256demo
echo 'what is up, doc?' | git hash-object -w --stdin
git rev-parse --show-object-format

735020bef5a1bfeaef19078f61e7520eb8f004aefdf86ab95d00fd3f14b3c562
sha256

Sixty-four hex characters instead of forty. The repository records the choice in its config, and it also bumps the repository format version so that an older Git refuses to touch it rather than corrupting it:

cat .git/config

[extensions]
	objectformat = sha256
[core]
	repositoryformatversion = 1
	filemode = true
	bare = false
	logallrefupdates = true

repositoryformatversion = 1 is the mechanism that makes every extensions.* key safe: version 0 means "any Git can read this", version 1 means "refuse unless you understand every extension listed". The same mechanism gates the reftable backend in §4.9 and worktree config in §14.9.

The reason SHA-256 is not yet the default is interoperability, not readiness. A SHA-256 repository cannot push to or fetch from a SHA-1 repository; the interop layer that would translate ids between the two object formats is the remaining piece of work. Every major hosting provider still speaks SHA-1, so in practice you will use SHA-1 repositories for years yet — and the important thing is to know that the object ids are a parameter of the format rather than a constant, so that code you write against Git does not hard-code [0-9a-f]{40}.

The name for the choice between the two is the repository's object format, and git rev-parse --show-object-format reports it. Note that the object format is fixed when the repository is created: there is no in-place conversion, so moving a project to SHA-256 today means creating a new repository and importing the history.

3.12 Reading the Object Database Directly

Six commands cover essentially every question you can ask of the object layer. They are worth committing to memory because they are the tools you reach for when a porcelain command is telling you something that seems impossible.

git cat-file -t / -s / -p
Type, size, contents of one object.
git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check
Every object in the database, with type and size. The inventory command.
git ls-tree -r -t
What a tree contains, recursively, without touching the working tree.
git ls-files -s
What the index contains, with modes and stage numbers.
git rev-parse
Turn any revision expression into an object id (§9.2). --verify makes it fail loudly on nonsense instead of guessing.
git count-objects -v
How many loose objects, how many packs, how much disk. The first thing to run on a repository that is unexpectedly large (§15.2).

Two habits make these much more useful.

Use --batch-check with a format string when you want a specific field. git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check='%(objecttype) %(objectsize:disk) %(objectname)' gives on-disk size rather than logical size, which is the number that matters when you are hunting for what made a clone slow.

Use git rev-parse --verify in scripts. Plain git rev-parse foo will happily echo foo back at you if it cannot resolve it, on the theory that it might be a filename — which turns a typo into a confusing error three commands later instead of an immediate one.

Exercises

1. Without running any Git command, predict the object id of a file containing exactly the five bytes hello with no trailing newline. Then check your prediction.

Solution

The object is blob 5\0hello:

import hashlib
print(hashlib.sha1(b'blob 5\x00hello').hexdigest())

b6fc4c620b67d95f953a5c1c1230aaab5db5a1b0

Check it with printf 'hello' | git hash-object --stdin. Note printf rather than echo, which would append a newline and give a six-byte object with a completely different id — a good illustration of why a stray trailing newline shows up as a change in git diff.

2. A colleague says "Git stores the differences between versions, that's why it's efficient." Give two pieces of evidence from the object database that contradict this, and one sense in which they are nonetheless partly right.

Solution

Evidence against:

  1. git cat-file -p on any commit shows a tree line and no diff. The object contains a complete snapshot reference; the patch you see in git show is computed at display time by diffing against the parent.
  2. git cat-file -p on that tree lists every path in the project, not just changed ones — including entries whose blob ids are identical to the parent's.

The sense in which they are right: the packfile layer (§15.3) stores many objects as deltas against a similar object, so on disk a long-lived repository really is mostly deltas. The distinction is that this is a storage encoding beneath the object model, invisible to object ids, and Git can and does repack the same history into different delta chains without any id changing.

3. Build a commit whose tree contains a file called README.md with the content # Demo, using only hash-object, update-index, write-tree, commit-tree and update-ref. Do not use git add or git commit. Verify with git log --stat.

Solution 
git init -q handmade
cd handmade
BLOB=$(printf '# Demo\n' | git hash-object -w --stdin)
git update-index --add --cacheinfo "100644,$BLOB,README.md"
TREE=$(git write-tree)
COMMIT=$(echo 'handmade commit' | git commit-tree "$TREE")
git update-ref refs/heads/main "$COMMIT"
git log --stat --oneline

95f38fe handmade commit
 README.md | 1 +
 1 file changed, 1 insertion(+)

Your ids will differ, because commit-tree stamps the current time into the commit and the timestamp is part of the hashed content. The blob and tree ids, however, will match anyone else's exactly — they depend only on content.

Note that git status at this point reports README.md as deleted, because the working tree never got a copy. git checkout . or git restore . fixes that by copying the index out to disk (§7.2).

4. Two files in a repository have identical content but different names and different permissions — one is executable. How many blobs does the database hold for them? Explain from the tree format.

Solution

One blob. The mode and the name both live in the tree entry, not in the blob, so two entries with the same object id and different mode/name fields reference the same object:

100644 blob 4163036efa65bd4a469e752267498f01ea36a55c	script.sh
100755 blob 4163036efa65bd4a469e752267498f01ea36a55c	run.sh

This is also why chmod +x on a tracked file shows up in git diff as a change with no content lines — the tree entry changed and the blob did not:

old mode 100644
new mode 100755

5. You have a commit id and you want to know, without checking anything out, whether the file src/config.py existed in that commit and what its contents were. Give the two commands.

Solution 
git ls-tree -r <commit> -- src/config.py
git cat-file -p <commit>:src/config.py

The first lists the tree entry if the path exists and prints nothing if it does not. The second uses the <rev>:<path> syntax (§9.2), which resolves the path through the commit's tree and prints the blob. Neither touches the working tree, the index, or the current branch, so both are safe to run in the middle of a conflicted merge or a rebase.

6. Run git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check on a repository immediately after git init and after your first git commit of a single one-line file. Predict the exact object count before running the second one.

Solution

After git init: zero objects. git init writes no objects at all — it creates directories, HEAD, and a config file.

After committing one file at the top level: three objects — one blob for the content, one tree for the root directory, one commit. If the file were at src/main.py it would be four, because src needs a tree of its own; the count is 1 blob + 1 commit + (depth of the path) trees.

SECTION 4

References, HEAD and the Reflog

Section 3 built an object database: an immutable graph of blobs, trees, commits and tags, every one named by the hash of its own bytes. It is complete, and it is unusable on its own, because nobody can remember c02e471f8618617bd24a14d5ab57abc2f6f2b17e.

References are the layer that makes it usable. A ref is a human-readable name for an object id — and that is genuinely all it is. Every branch, every tag, every remote-tracking bookmark, HEAD itself, and the entire history of where those names have been are the same one mechanism seen from six angles.

This is a short mechanism with an outsized payoff. Once you know what a branch physically is, "how do I move a branch without checking it out", "why did my commits vanish", "what is a detached HEAD", "why does git checkout sometimes mean two different things" and "how do I get my work back after a bad reset" all stop being separate pieces of knowledge.

4.1 A Ref Is a File Containing an Object Id

cat .git/HEAD
cat .git/refs/heads/main
git rev-parse main

ref: refs/heads/main
f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d
f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d

A file under .git/refs/ containing forty hex characters and a newline. That is a branch. git rev-parse reads it, and you can too.

Because it is only a file, you can write one with a shell redirect:

git rev-parse HEAD~1 > .git/refs/heads/handmade
git branch -v

  handmade 21d1d85 one
* main     f98e5c9 two

A real branch, indistinguishable from one made by git branch, created by cat. This is worth doing once to demolish the intuition that a branch is a heavyweight object. It is not a copy of the code, it is not a directory, it is not a container of commits. It is one line of text.

Do not actually work this way. git update-ref does the same thing safely:

git update-ref refs/heads/safer HEAD~1
git for-each-ref

21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/heads/handmade
f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d commit	refs/heads/main
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/heads/safer

What update-ref adds over a redirect is not cosmetic:

Locking
Writes through .lock and renames atomically, so a concurrent reader never sees half a hash.
Validation
Refuses an id that is not in the object database, and refuses a malformed refname.
Reflog
Records the change (§4.10). A ref written by hand leaves no trace, which is why the reflog entries from the hand-edits earlier in this section have empty messages.
Backend independence
Works whether refs live in files or in a reftable (§4.9). The redirect only works on one of them.

git show-ref is the older, narrower form of the same query — it lists refs and their ids, and git show-ref --verify refs/heads/main is a compact existence test. git for-each-ref is the right way to enumerate refs in a script: it reads whichever backend is in use, it sorts, and its --format accepts the same placeholders as git log. Never walk .git/refs with find.

git for-each-ref repays a little study because it is the query interface for the whole ref namespace. git for-each-ref --format='%(refname:short) %(objectname:short) %(committerdate:relative)' --sort=-committerdate refs/heads is a better git branch -v than git branch -v, and the same formatting language works in git log --format (§9.6).

4.2 Branches Are Refs, and Nothing Else

Everything Git says about branches follows from §4.1.

Creating one is free
Forty-one bytes. This is why Git's branching is cheap and why every workflow in §12 assumes you make branches constantly.
Deleting one deletes nothing
git branch -d removes a file. The commits are untouched — they merely become unreachable, and stay recoverable for at least two weeks (§15.5).
A commit does not know its branch
Parent ids point backwards (§3.6). "Which branch is this commit on" is a search — git branch --contains — not a lookup, and the answer can be "several".
Committing moves the ref
git commit writes a commit whose parent is the current tip, then updates the ref to the new commit. The branch is a bookmark that follows you.
Merging can be a ref write
A fast-forward (§6.2) writes a new id into the file. Nothing is copied or applied.

That last pair explains the shape of most Git operations. git reset --hard <commit> (§7.3), git rebase (§8), and git merge --ff all end by writing one ref. The differences between them are entirely about what happens to the index and the working tree on the way.

4.3 HEAD, and Symbolic Refs

HEAD is a ref whose content is not an object id but a pointer to another ref:

git symbolic-ref HEAD

refs/heads/main

A ref whose value is another ref's name rather than an object id is a symbolic reference, and that indirection is what "being on a branch" means. Two consequences:

  • git commit moves the branch HEAD names, not HEAD itself. HEAD keeps saying ref: refs/heads/main while main advances.
  • Switching branches rewrites HEAD, then makes the index and working tree match the new commit.

You can write it directly, which is exactly what git switch does after its safety checks:

git symbolic-ref HEAD refs/heads/safer
git status -sb

## safer

The branch changed with no checkout — the working tree still holds main's content, so git status now reports every difference between the two branches as uncommitted changes. That is not a bug, it is what symbolic-ref is: a plumbing command that does one thing. It is also occasionally the right tool, most often on a bare repository where there is no working tree to update (§11.8):

git --git-dir=/srv/git/project.git symbolic-ref HEAD refs/heads/main

That one command is how you fix a server handing out master to every clone.

A symbolic reference is stored as the literal text ref: followed by another refname, and HEAD is very nearly the only one you will meet — refs/remotes/origin/HEAD, which records the remote's default branch, is the other. git symbolic-ref --short HEAD prints just the branch name, and it fails rather than printing an object id when HEAD is detached, which makes it a cleaner test than parsing git branch.

4.4 Detached HEAD

HEAD does not have to be symbolic. Check out a commit rather than a branch:

git checkout --detach HEAD~1
cat .git/HEAD
git status -sb

21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9
## HEAD (no branch)

.git/HEAD now holds an object id directly. This is a detached HEAD, and Git's warning about it is famously alarming for something so ordinary.

Everything works normally in this state. You can edit, commit, and commit again; each commit's parent is the previous one and HEAD advances to hold each new id. The one thing missing is a name. Nothing points at your work except HEAD, so the moment you check out a branch, those commits become unreachable — and Git says so, at length, on the way out.

Legitimate uses
Inspecting an old commit. Building a specific tag in CI — every CI checkout of a tag is detached. Bisecting (§9.10), which detaches for every step. Testing an idea you may throw away.
How you get there by accident
git checkout <tag>, git checkout origin/main (a remote-tracking ref is not a branch you can commit to — §4.6), or an interrupted rebase.
Getting out safely
git switch -c <name> gives the work a ref before you leave. If you already left, the reflog has the id (§4.10) and git branch <name> <id> rescues it.

git switch was introduced partly to make this harder to hit by accident: it refuses to detach unless you pass --detach, whereas git checkout <commit> does it silently. That is the clearest single argument for preferring switch and restore over checkout (§7.2).

4.5 The Ref Namespace, and the Six Disambiguation Rules

Refs live in a hierarchy that is a directory tree in the files backend and a sorted key space in reftable. The four namespaces you will use:

refs/heads/<name>
Local branches.
refs/tags/<name>
Tags — pointing at a commit (lightweight) or at a tag object (annotated), §3.8.
refs/remotes/<r>/<b>
Remote-tracking refs. Read-only bookmarks of what a remote's branches held at last fetch.
refs/stash
A single ref holding the stash stack (§7.7).

Others exist and are worth recognising when you see them: refs/notes/ (§9.12), refs/prefetch/ (written by the maintenance prefetch task, §15.9), refs/replace/, and on a forge, refs/pull/<n>/head, which is why cloning a busy repository fetches far more refs than it has branches.

When you type a short name, Git tries six locations in order and takes the first match:

1
$GIT_DIR/<name> — the pseudo-refs of §4.7, such as HEAD and ORIG_HEAD.
2
refs/<name>
3
refs/tags/<name>
4
refs/heads/<name>
5
refs/remotes/<name>
6
refs/remotes/<name>/HEAD

Tags beat branches. Make both with the same name and watch:

git branch dup HEAD~1
git tag dup HEAD
git rev-parse dup
git rev-parse refs/heads/dup refs/tags/dup

warning: refname 'dup' is ambiguous.
f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9
f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d

dup resolved to the tag. Git warns, but the ambiguity resolves rather than failing — so a script that assumes it got a branch quietly gets a tag. Two habits follow: name tags and branches differently (v1.2.0 versus release/1.2), and write the full refname in anything automated. The ref-prefix lines in §11.5's protocol trace are these six rules being asked over the network, which is why a fetch of main asks about six namespaces.

One name for the traditional storage: it is the files backend, so called because each ref is a file. git rev-parse --show-ref-format prints files for it and reftable for the alternative in §4.9, and the files backend remains the default.

4.6 Remote-Tracking Refs

refs/remotes/origin/main is an ordinary ref with one convention attached: you never write it, git fetch does. It records what origin's main pointed at the last time you spoke to the server.

That single fact explains a set of behaviours that otherwise look arbitrary:

  • git log origin/main shows a history that may be hours out of date, and no network traffic occurs. It is a local cache, not a query.
  • git checkout origin/main detaches (§4.4), because it is not a local branch — there is nothing to commit onto.
  • git status saying "your branch is ahead of 'origin/main' by 3 commits" is a purely local comparison of two refs. It is accurate about your last fetch and says nothing about the server now.
  • git fetch --prune deletes remote-tracking refs whose branches are gone from the server. Without it they accumulate forever, and git branch -r becomes a museum.

The mapping from server refs to local ones is the refspec (§10.4), which is a rule about renaming refs — nothing deeper.

4.7 Pseudo-Refs

A handful of names live directly in $GIT_DIR rather than under refs/, and rule 1 above is what makes them resolve. They are written by commands as they work, and they are how you address "the thing I was just doing".

HEAD
Where you are.
ORIG_HEAD
Where HEAD was before the last operation that moved it far — reset, merge, rebase, pull. The one-step undo (§7.8).
MERGE_HEAD
The commit being merged, during a conflicted merge (§6.8).
REBASE_HEAD
The commit being applied, during a stopped rebase.
CHERRY_PICK_HEAD
The commit being cherry-picked.
REVERT_HEAD
The commit being reverted.
FETCH_HEAD
What the last fetch brought — one line per ref, not a single id.
AUTO_MERGE
The tree the ort strategy produced, including conflict markers, during a conflicted merge.
git reset --hard HEAD~1
git rev-parse HEAD ORIG_HEAD

21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9
f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d

ORIG_HEAD still holds the pre-reset tip, so git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD puts everything back. Note that it holds only the most recent such move: a second reset overwrites it. For anything older, the reflog.

The presence of these files is also how git status knows what state you are in. A stopped rebase is a directory (.git/rebase-merge/) plus REBASE_HEAD; a conflicted merge is MERGE_HEAD plus unmerged index entries (§6.4). "Git is stuck in a weird state" almost always means one of these files exists, and git status will name it.

4.8 packed-refs

A repository with twenty thousand tags would have twenty thousand tiny files, and listing them would mean twenty thousand open() calls. git pack-refs collapses them into one:

git pack-refs --all
ls .git/refs/heads
cat .git/packed-refs

# pack-refs with: peeled fully-peeled sorted 
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 refs/heads/handmade
f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d refs/heads/main
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 refs/heads/safer

.git/refs/heads is now empty, and everything still resolves:

git rev-parse main

f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d

The header records what the file guarantees. sorted allows a binary search; peeled/fully-peeled means annotated tags carry a ^<commit-id> line giving the commit they ultimately point at, so peeling (§3.8) needs no object reads.

Now the part that catches people out. A ref update after packing writes a loose file again, and the loose file wins:

git commit --allow-empty -m three
ls .git/refs/heads
grep main .git/packed-refs
git rev-parse main

main
f98e5c9c1f84455d9400ba43ee32b0b49634008d refs/heads/main
0fb430085e4b522bc632df62aeed15510b52d501

packed-refs still holds the old value and the loose file holds the new one. This is correct and by design — packed-refs is a read-optimised snapshot, and the loose file is the authority — but it means reading packed-refs to find a ref's value is a bug. Use git rev-parse or git for-each-ref, which consult both.

Deletion is where the design creaks: removing a ref that exists only in packed-refs requires rewriting the whole file under a lock, so on a busy server every ref deletion serialises against every other. That, plus the fact that a filesystem cannot atomically update two refs at once, is what motivated a replacement.

4.9 The reftable Backend

reftable stores refs and their reflogs in a set of sorted binary tables instead of files. It is chosen at git init time:

git init --ref-format=reftable rt
cd rt
git branch b1
git branch b2
git tag v1
ls .git/reftable/

0x000000000001-0x000000000004-eb4a5f80.ref
0x000000000005-0x000000000005-ddbb6c21.ref
tables.list

tables.list names the stack of tables in order; each table is a sorted, block-compressed set of ref updates, and lookups read the stack newest-first. New updates append a small table, and tables are periodically compacted — the same shape as §15.7's multi-pack index, applied to refs.

The compatibility handling is worth seeing, because it is a nice piece of defensive design:

cat .git/HEAD
cat .git/config

ref: refs/heads/.invalid
[extensions]
	refstorage = reftable
[core]
	repositoryformatversion = 1

.git/HEAD still exists, and it deliberately points at an invalid refname. An old Git that does not understand the refstorage extension would otherwise read .git/HEAD, believe the repository is on main, find no refs/heads/main, and conclude the repository is empty — then happily commit over it. Pointing at .invalid makes that old Git fail loudly instead. repositoryformatversion = 1 is the same mechanism §3.11 used for SHA-256: version 1 means "refuse unless you understand every listed extension".

Everything above the backend is unchanged:

git for-each-ref
git rev-parse main

21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/heads/b1
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/heads/b2
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/heads/main
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/tags/v1
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9

An existing repository can be converted in place, reflog included:

git rev-parse --show-ref-format
git refs migrate --ref-format=reftable
git rev-parse --show-ref-format
git reflog

files
reftable
0fb4300 HEAD@{0}: commit: three
f98e5c9 HEAD@{1}: checkout: moving from 21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 to main
What it fixes
Atomic multi-ref updates. Ref deletion that does not rewrite everything. No case-folding or filesystem-encoding surprises from refnames being paths. Reflogs stored with the refs rather than in a parallel directory tree. Much better behaviour at hundreds of thousands of refs.
What it costs
Tooling that reads .git/refs or .git/packed-refs directly stops working — which is a good reason such tooling should never have existed. Git older than 2.45 cannot read the repository at all.

The files backend remains the default and will for some time. The reason to know reftable now is that you will meet it — hosting providers have begun migrating large repositories to it — and a repository whose .git/refs is empty is not broken.

git refs migrate is the conversion tool and it works in both directions — git refs migrate --ref-format=files converts a reftable repository back. It is a local operation that changes nothing anyone else can see, because the ref storage format is not part of the wire protocol (§11.5); two repositories with different backends interoperate normally.

4.10 The Reflog

Every time a ref moves, Git appends a line recording it. That log is the reflog, and it is the single most valuable safety feature in the tool.

git reflog

0fb4300 HEAD@{0}: commit: three
f98e5c9 HEAD@{1}: checkout: moving from 21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 to main
21d1d85 HEAD@{2}: checkout: moving from main to HEAD~1
f98e5c9 HEAD@{3}: 
21d1d85 HEAD@{4}: 
f98e5c9 HEAD@{5}: commit: two
21d1d85 HEAD@{6}: commit (initial): one

The raw file is as simple as everything else here:

cat .git/logs/HEAD

0000000000000000000000000000000000000000 21d1d851… Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000	commit (initial): one
21d1d851… f98e5c9c… Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000	commit: two
f98e5c9c… 21d1d851… Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000	
21d1d851… f98e5c9c… Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000

Each line is old id, new id, who, when, why. The all-zeros old id on the first line means the ref did not exist before.

The two blank reasons are instructive: those entries came from the hand-written symbolic-ref calls in §4.3. Plumbing that bypasses the porcelain leaves a reflog entry with no message, which is a small argument for git update-ref over a redirect and a large argument for reading reflog messages rather than assuming.

HEAD@{2}
The value HEAD held two moves ago. Positional, and it shifts every time HEAD moves.
main@{2}
The value that branch held two of its moves ago — a different log, in .git/logs/refs/heads/main.
main@{yesterday}
Time-based. Also @{2.hours.ago}, @{2026-08-01}.
git reflog show <ref>
That ref's log rather than HEAD's.
git log -g
The reflog formatted as a log, so --patch, --grep and friends all work.

The distinction between HEAD@{n} and <branch>@{n} matters and is missed constantly. HEAD's log records every move including checkouts between branches; a branch's log records only moves of that branch. When recovering from a bad rebase you usually want <branch>@{1}, because HEAD moved many times during the rebase itself.

Three properties define what the reflog is for:

It is strictly local. It is never fetched, never pushed, and never cloned. A fresh clone has a reflog with one entry. Your colleague's reflog cannot help you and yours cannot help them.

It records moves, not content. Every id in it is a real object in the database, which is why the recovery in §7.9 works — but if git gc --prune=now has run, the objects can be gone even though the log still names them.

It is why "I lost my work" is nearly always false. Any operation that moved a ref left a line saying where it was.

4.11 Expiry, and What It Actually Guarantees

The reflog is pruned, and by two different rules:

gc.reflogExpire
90 days
Entries older than this whose commits are still reachable from the ref's current tip.
gc.reflogExpireUnreachable
30 days
Entries whose commits are no longer reachable — the ones you would actually want after a bad rebase. This is the number that matters, and it is the shorter one.

Both accept never, and gc.<pattern>.reflogExpire scopes the setting to a namespace — gc.refs/stash.reflogExpire = never is the standard way to stop old stashes being swept up (§7.7).

Two combinations to keep straight, because one of them is a deliberate demolition:

git reflog expire --expire-unreachable=now --all

Drops the unreachable entries. The objects survive until gc prunes them (§15.5), so this alone is recoverable with git fsck --lost-found.

git reflog expire --expire=now --all
git gc --prune=now

Together these are the irreversible pair, and they are the correct final step after purging a secret (§8.10). Nothing here has a grace period and nothing can be recovered afterwards. Typing them because a repository "feels untidy" is how work is genuinely lost.

4.12 Atomic Ref Updates

git update-ref --stdin reads a small transaction language, and it is how any script that touches several refs should do it:

printf 'start\nupdate refs/heads/a %s\nupdate refs/heads/b %s\ncommit\n' \
  "$(git rev-parse HEAD)" "$(git rev-parse HEAD~1)" | git update-ref --stdin
git for-each-ref --format='%(refname) %(objectname:short)'

start: ok
commit: ok
refs/heads/a f98e5c9
refs/heads/b 21d1d85
refs/heads/dup 21d1d85
refs/heads/main f98e5c9
refs/tags/dup f98e5c9

One related plumbing command is worth knowing beside these: git var prints values Git computes rather than reads, such as git var GIT_COMMITTER_IDENT, which is how a script finds the identity a commit would actually be recorded with.

The commands are start, update, create, delete, verify, option, commit and abort. update <ref> <new> [<old>] takes an optional expected old value — and when it does not match, the whole transaction is refused:

printf 'start\nupdate refs/heads/e %s\nupdate refs/heads/main %s %s\ncommit\n' \
  "$(git rev-parse HEAD)" "$(git rev-parse HEAD)" "$(git rev-parse HEAD~1)" | git update-ref --stdin
git for-each-ref --format='%(refname)'

start: ok
fatal: commit: cannot lock ref 'refs/heads/main': is at f98e5c9c… but expected 21d1d851…
refs/heads/a
refs/heads/b
refs/heads/dup
refs/heads/main
refs/tags/dup

refs/heads/e was not created. The second update failed its old-value check, and the transaction rolled back — nothing was left half-done.

That expected-old-value check is the same mechanism as git push's <old-id> <new-id> <ref> request line (§11.7) and as --force-with-lease (§8.9). It is the one concurrency primitive Git has, and every safe ref operation in the system is built on it: update this ref, but only if it still says what I think it says.

Exercises

1. Without using git branch, git checkout or git switch, create a branch called release pointing at the commit three back from the current tip, and show it exists. Then explain why the obvious shell one-liner is a worse answer.

Solution 
git update-ref refs/heads/release HEAD~3
git for-each-ref refs/heads/release

The shell one-liner — git rev-parse HEAD~3 > .git/refs/heads/release — produces the same file and is worse for four reasons (§4.1): it does not lock, so a concurrent reader can see a partial write; it does not validate that the id exists or that the name is legal; it writes no reflog entry, so the branch has no history and release@{1} will never work; and it silently does nothing useful in a repository using the reftable backend (§4.9), where .git/refs is not the authority.

git update-ref is plumbing and does not touch the index or working tree, which is exactly why it is the right tool for creating a ref without leaving the branch you are on.

2. A colleague reports that git log v2.0 and git log refs/heads/v2.0 show different histories. Explain, and give the command that shows both.

Solution

There is both a tag and a branch called v2.0. The six disambiguation rules (§4.5) check refs/tags/ before refs/heads/, so the bare name resolves to the tag while the fully-qualified name resolves to the branch.

git rev-parse refs/tags/v2.0 refs/heads/v2.0
git for-each-ref --format='%(refname) %(objectname:short) %(objecttype)' | grep v2.0

Git does print warning: refname 'v2.0' is ambiguous. — but it warns and then proceeds, so a script that reads stdout and ignores stderr will use the tag while its author meant the branch.

The fixes, in order: rename one of them (a naming convention where tags are v2.0 and branches are release/2.0 prevents the whole class), and always use full refnames in scripts.

3. After git pack-refs --all and one new commit, .git/packed-refs shows an old id for main while git rev-parse main shows a new one. Which is right, and what general rule does this establish?

Solution

git rev-parse is right. packed-refs is a read-optimised snapshot, and any ref update after packing writes a fresh loose file which takes precedence (§4.8). Both files exist; the loose one is the authority.

The general rule: never read Git's internal storage directly to answer a question Git has a command for. git rev-parse for one ref, git for-each-ref for many, git symbolic-ref for HEAD. Each of them consults every layer — loose refs, packed refs, and the reftable backend where that is in use — and each keeps working when the storage format changes underneath.

The same rule retired a generation of scripts when reftable arrived: anything parsing .git/refs or .git/packed-refs reads an empty directory and a missing file, and reports that the repository has no branches (§4.9).

4. Explain, mechanically, why git checkout origin/main detaches HEAD while git checkout main does not.

Solution

main resolves to refs/heads/main, a local branch. Checking it out writes ref: refs/heads/main into .git/HEAD — a symbolic ref (§4.3) — so subsequent commits move that branch.

origin/main resolves to refs/remotes/origin/main, which is not a local branch. Nothing may write to it except git fetch (§4.6), so HEAD cannot symbolically point at it — Git would then have to update it on every commit, and the next fetch would overwrite your work. Instead Git writes the object id into .git/HEAD, which is a detached HEAD (§4.4).

Commits made there are reachable only from HEAD and become unreferenced the moment you switch away. The right move when you meant to work on it:

git switch -c my-feature origin/main

which creates a local branch at that commit and sets it to track the remote one (§10.5).

5. You rebased feature an hour ago and now believe the pre-rebase version was better. Give the commands, and say why HEAD@{1} is the wrong one to reach for.

Solution 
git reflog show feature
git branch feature-old 'feature@{1}'
git range-diff feature-old...feature

feature@{1} is the value the branch held one move ago — that is, immediately before the rebase updated it (§4.10).

HEAD@{1} is wrong because HEAD moved many times during the rebase: once to detach onto the new base, once per commit replayed, and once more to return to the branch. HEAD@{1} is somewhere in the middle of that, and on a five-commit rebase you would need HEAD@{7} or so, found by reading rather than by counting.

Creating feature-old rather than resetting straight away is the safer order: it gives the old tip a ref so nothing can prune it, and it makes git range-diff (§8.8) possible so you can see exactly what the rebase changed before deciding. Then either git reset --hard feature-old or keep both.

The whole recovery depends on the reflog entry surviving. Unreachable entries expire after 30 days by default (§4.11) and are destroyed immediately by git reflog expire --expire=now --all.

6. A deployment script updates three refs and sometimes leaves the repository in a state where one has moved and two have not. Fix it.

Solution

Three separate git update-ref calls are three separate transactions; a failure or a crash between them leaves exactly that partial state. Use one transaction (§4.12):

git update-ref --stdin < deploy.txn

where deploy.txn holds the transaction — its own small language, not shell:

start
update refs/heads/production <new> <old-production>
update refs/heads/staging <new> <old-staging>
create refs/tags/deployed-20260827 <new>
commit

Two properties make this correct. All updates apply or none do — a failed update aborts the whole transaction, as demonstrated above where a mismatched old value prevented an unrelated ref from being created. And passing the expected old value makes each update a compare-and-swap, so a concurrent deployment is refused rather than silently overwriting.

Note that full atomicity across refs is a property of the reftable backend (§4.9); the files backend implements it with a lock over the whole ref store, which works but serialises. Either way, --stdin is the correct interface — do not write the refs individually and hope.

SECTION 5

Branching and Merging

Branching is the feature Git is famous for, and the reason it is famous is not that Git can branch — every version control system can — but that branching in Git costs forty-one bytes and no time at all. That changes what branching is for. In a system where making a branch means copying a directory tree and merging one means an afternoon, you branch when a release demands it. In Git you branch to try an idea for ten minutes.

Section 4 established the mechanism: a branch is a file containing an object id, and HEAD is a symbolic ref naming the branch you are on. This section is what follows from that — how branches diverge, what a merge actually computes, and the vocabulary you need before §6 gets into what happens when a merge cannot finish.

5.1 A Branch Is a Pointer That Follows You

git switch -c feature
ls .git/refs/heads
cat .git/refs/heads/feature .git/refs/heads/main
cat .git/HEAD

Switched to a new branch 'feature'
feature
main
d5f18073a03862f16eb2b4d7fa76abb61c72fcf6
d5f18073a03862f16eb2b4d7fa76abb61c72fcf6
ref: refs/heads/feature

Two files with the same content, and HEAD naming one of them. Creating a branch copied one object id; nothing else happened. No files were touched, no history was duplicated, and the working tree is identical because both branches point at the same commit.

Now commit on it:

echo 'feature work' > feature.txt
git add -A
git commit -m 'add feature.txt'
git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate

* 18c8652 (HEAD -> feature) add feature.txt
* d5f1807 (main) second commit
* a1291d9 initial commit

feature moved forward and main did not. That is the whole of it: git commit writes a commit whose parent is the current tip, then updates the ref HEAD names (§4.3). Being "on a branch" means "commits I make will move this pointer".

The (HEAD -> feature) decoration is worth reading carefully — it is --decorate showing you the two-step indirection from §4.3, HEAD pointing at a branch pointing at a commit.

5.2 Creating and Switching

git switch <branch>              # move to an existing branch
git switch -c <branch>           # create it at HEAD and move to it
git switch -c <branch> <start>   # create it at some other commit
git switch -                     # back to the previous branch
git branch <branch>              # create it, and stay where you are

git branch <name> creating a branch without switching to it is the one people forget, and it is the right command when you want to bookmark the current commit before doing something risky — git branch backup-before-rebase (§8.12) costs nothing and has saved a great deal of work.

git switch was introduced in Git 2.23 alongside git restore to split git checkout's two unrelated jobs (§7.10). The equivalents still work — git checkout <branch>, git checkout -b <branch> — and you will see them everywhere, but switch is worth adopting for one concrete reason: it refuses to detach HEAD unless you pass --detach, so the accidental detached HEAD of §4.4 stops happening.

Switching is safe. Git refuses to move if it would overwrite uncommitted changes, and if your changes do not conflict with the difference between the two branches it carries them across. When it does refuse, the options are git stash (§7.7), a commit, or a worktree (§14.7).

git checkout -b <branch> is the older spelling of git switch -c <branch> and does exactly the same thing, so you will see git checkout -b in most documentation written before 2019 and in a great deal written since. Both create the branch and move to it; the difference is only in what else the surrounding command can be talked into doing.

5.3 The Merge Base

Let both branches move:

git switch main
echo 'main work' > main.txt
git add -A
git commit -m 'add main.txt'
git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate

* 18c8652 (feature) add feature.txt
| * acff908 (HEAD -> main) add main.txt
|/
* d5f1807 second commit
* a1291d9 initial commit

The history has forked. Neither branch is an ancestor of the other, and the commit where they last agreed is d5f1807:

git merge-base --all main feature

d5f18073a03862f16eb2b4d7fa76abb61c72fcf6

That is the merge base: the best common ancestor of the two tips. Everything about merging depends on it, and it is worth being precise about why.

Without a merge base, comparing two branches gives you only "these files differ" — and for each difference you would have to ask a human which side is right. With it, Git can tell who changed what: a file that differs from the base on one side only was changed by that side, so its version wins with no question asked. Only files changed on both sides need any thought at all.

git merge-base --all rather than plain git merge-base is the habit worth having, because there can be more than one (§6.11) and the plain form silently picks one.

5.4 Fast-Forward

The simplest merge is not a merge. When the merge base is your branch's tip — meaning the other branch is strictly ahead — there is nothing to combine:

Updating dae90f9..2ff6b4f
Fast-forward
 f | 1 +
 1 file changed, 1 insertion(+)

Git moves your branch's pointer forward and updates the working tree. No new commit is created, no merge commit exists, and afterwards there is no evidence a branch was ever involved.

That last point is the decision. --no-ff forces a merge commit even when a fast-forward is possible (§6.2), which records the branch's boundary and makes git log --first-parent a list of integrations rather than of commits (§9.7). --ff-only refuses anything but a fast-forward, which is the right setting for git pull (§10.7) because it turns a surprise merge into a question.

5.5 The Three-Way Merge

When both branches have moved, Git computes a genuine merge:

git merge --no-edit feature
git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate

Merge made by the 'ort' strategy.
 feature.txt | 1 +
 1 file changed, 1 insertion(+)
 create mode 100644 feature.txt
*   e634ff5 (HEAD -> main) Merge branch 'feature'
|\
| * 18c8652 (feature) add feature.txt
* | acff908 add main.txt
|/
* d5f1807 second commit
* a1291d9 initial commit

Three things happened, in this order:

1
Git found the merge base and compared all three trees, path by path (§6.1).
2
It built a new tree combining them, and wrote it to the object database.
3
It wrote a commit with two parents — first the branch you were on, second the one you merged — and moved main to it (§3.6).

"Merge made by the ort strategy" names the algorithm; ort has been Git's default since 2.34, and recursive is now a synonym for it rather than an alternative (§6.11).

Only main moved. feature still points where it did, which is why merging does not "consume" a branch and why you can keep working on it and merge again later.

The merge commit is an ordinary commit whose tree is the combined result. git show on it prints almost nothing, which surprises people until §6.8 explains combined diffs.

5.6 Managing Branches

git branch -v

  feature 18c8652 add feature.txt
* main    e634ff5 Merge branch 'feature'

The * marks the current branch. Beyond that, four flags do the work:

git branch -vv
Adds each branch's upstream and its ahead/behind counts (§10.6). The command to run when tracking is confusing.
git branch --merged
Branches fully contained in the current one — safe to delete.
git branch --no-merged
Branches with commits not yet here. What you actually want to look at.
git branch --contains <c>
Which branches contain a given commit. Note this is a search, not a lookup, because parent links point backwards (§3.6).
git branch --sort=-committerdate
Most recently touched first — far more useful than alphabetical once you have thirty branches.

Deleting refuses when it would lose commits:

git branch -d stale

error: the branch 'stale' is not fully merged
hint: If you are sure you want to delete it, run 'git branch -D stale'

git branch -D stale

Deleted branch stale (was 2ae5a70).

-d is safe, -D is not — and note that even -D is recoverable: the message prints the commit id, the reflog has it (§4.10), and the commits survive at least two weeks (§15.5). git branch -m <old> <new> renames, carrying the upstream configuration and the reflog with it.

Two important limits on --merged. It reports branches merged into the branch you are currently on, so run it on main — git branch --merged main if you are elsewhere. And it is defeated by squash merging (§6.2), which records no ancestry, so a squash-merged branch is never listed as merged and cleanup has to be manual.

Two of these earn their keep daily. git branch -vv is the one to run whenever a push or pull goes somewhere unexpected, because it prints each branch's upstream alongside its ahead/behind counts. And git branch -m <old> <new> renames — including renaming the branch you are on, if you give it a single argument — carrying the upstream configuration and the reflog across, which is why it is better than deleting and recreating.

5.7 Naming Branches

Refnames are paths, so slashes create a hierarchy that Git and every tool understands:

feature/user-avatars
fix/1423-null-parser
release/2.4
ada/experiment
Prefixes group
git branch --list 'feature/*', git push origin 'refs/heads/release/*:…' (§10.4), and forge branch-protection rules all take globs.
A name cannot be a prefix of another
Refs are files (§4.1), so feature and feature/login cannot both exist — one would have to be a file and a directory. This is a real and confusing error, and it is the reason to pick a convention and keep it.
Include the ticket
Six months later, the branch name is the only link between the code and the discussion. A prepare-commit-msg hook can lift it into the message automatically (§13.7).
Avoid .., ~, ^, :, ?, *, [ and spaces
Every one is revision syntax (§9). git check-ref-format --branch <name> validates a name if you are generating them.

The default branch is main in Git 2.28 and later via init.defaultBranch, and master before it. Both are conventions; nothing in Git treats either specially, and a repository can call its trunk anything. The one place it matters is HEAD in a bare repository, which is what tells clones which branch to check out (§11.8).

5.8 Topic Branches and Long-Lived Branches

Two kinds of branch, doing different jobs.

Topic branch
One change. Created from the trunk, lives hours to days, merged and deleted. Should be small enough to review in one sitting. This is what almost every branch you make should be.
Long-running branch
A permanent line: main, and in some projects an integration branch such as develop, or release/2.4. Each long-running branch represents a state — what is deployed, what is next, what is supported — rather than a piece of work.

The failure mode is a topic branch that becomes long-lived by accident. Every day it stays open, the trunk moves and the eventual merge gets harder; a branch open for three months is a branch whose merge is a project. The cost is not linear, because conflicts compound: two changes to the same function are one conflict, and twenty changes to the same file are twenty.

Three mitigations, in order of how much they help:

  1. Make the change smaller. Almost always possible, and it fixes the problem rather than managing it. Feature flags let unfinished work merge safely.
  2. Integrate the trunk into the branch regularly — git merge main or git rebase main every day or two — so conflicts arrive a few at a time.
  3. Split the branch into a stack, each part reviewed and merged separately. git rebase --update-refs (§8.3) makes rebasing the whole stack one operation.

A project with an integration branch — conventionally develop — has two long-running branches rather than one: work merges into the integration branch continuously, and the integration branch merges into main at release time. It is the arrangement git flow formalises (§12.2), and it is worth adopting only when releases are discrete events rather than continuous.

5.9 Merge or Rebase

Both integrate one branch into another; they differ in what history they leave behind.

git merge feature
A new commit with two parents. Every original commit keeps its object id. The history records that two lines of work existed and when they came together.
git rebase main
Your commits are replayed onto the other branch, each becoming a new commit with a new id (§8.1). The result is linear and the fork is erased.
Merge preserves
What actually happened, including the fact that work was parallel. Nothing is rewritten, so anyone who already has your commits is unaffected.
Merge costs
A busier graph, and merge commits with generated messages that carry no information.
Rebase preserves
Readability. git log is a straight line; each commit's diff is against the current trunk rather than against a months-old base, which makes git bisect (§9.10) and review more reliable.
Rebase costs
New object ids, and therefore §8.11's rule: never rebase commits other people have. It also collapses several small conflicts into one large one, replayed commit by commit.

The consensus that has emerged, and the one to follow absent a project convention: rebase your own unpublished work to tidy it, merge to integrate finished work. In practice that means rebasing your feature branch onto the trunk while it is in review, and merging it into the trunk when it lands (§12.6).

§8 covers rebasing in full, including the interactive form that is really a history-editing tool rather than an integration one.

5.10 Reading the Graph

git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate

*   e634ff5 (HEAD -> main) Merge branch 'feature'
|\
| * 18c8652 (feature) add feature.txt
* | acff908 add main.txt
|/
* d5f1807 second commit
* a1291d9 initial commit

Four flags, each answering a different question, and they are worth understanding separately rather than as a magic incantation:

--oneline
One line per commit: abbreviated id and subject.
--graph
The ASCII columns on the left. |\ is a fork point read downwards — a merge commit with two parents; |/ is where two lines rejoin.
--all
Start from every ref, not just HEAD. Without it you see only your branch's ancestry and the fork is invisible.
--decorate
Show which refs point at which commits. On by default in recent Git.

Reading the example: e634ff5 is a merge (|\ below it) whose two parents are acff908 on the left column and 18c8652 on the right. The left column is the first parent — the branch that was checked out when the merge ran — which is the ordering §9.7's --first-parent and §7.5's git revert -m 1 both depend on.

Two additions worth knowing. --topo-order guarantees that no commit appears before its descendants, which the default date ordering does not — a rebased or imported commit can carry a date from years ago and appear in the wrong place. And %d in a --format string gives the decorations, so you can build a compact view of your own:

git log --graph --format='%C(auto)%h%d %s %C(dim)(%ar, %an)'

This is the one command worth turning into an alias (§13.4), because you will run it constantly and nobody remembers the flags.

Exercises

1. You are three commits into work on main that should have been on a branch, and you have not pushed. Move it, and explain why nothing is copied.

Solution 
git branch feature
git reset --hard HEAD~3
git switch feature

git branch feature creates a ref pointing at the current commit — 41 bytes (§5.1), no copying. git reset --hard HEAD~3 moves main back three commits (§7.3); the three commits are untouched in the object database and are now reachable from feature. git switch feature puts you back on them.

Nothing is copied because a branch is an object id in a file. The commits belonged to no branch in the first place — they were merely reachable from one — so "moving work to a branch" is just arranging which refs point where.

Check before the reset with git log --oneline --graph --all, and note that git reset --hard is the one command here that can lose uncommitted work: commit or stash first if the tree is dirty.

Since nothing was pushed, this is entirely safe. If main had been pushed, moving it back would be a rewrite of published history (§8.11) and the answer would instead be to branch, then git revert on main.

2. git merge feature printed "Fast-forward" and no merge commit exists. Was this a merge? When would you want it to have been?

Solution

Nothing was merged. The merge base was main's tip — feature contained every commit main had, plus more — so there was nothing to combine and Git simply moved the pointer forward (§5.4). No commit was created and no three-way merge ran.

You would want --no-ff when the branch's existence is information worth keeping:

  • git log --first-parent then lists one entry per feature rather than every commit (§9.7) — the measured difference on one Git release was 758 commits against 161 integrations.
  • Reverting the whole feature becomes one git revert -m 1 <merge> (§7.5) instead of n reverts.
  • The graph shows what was developed in parallel.

You would want --ff-only — refuse anything else — for git pull, where an unexpected merge commit is almost never what you meant (§10.7).

Many teams set merge.ff = false for the trunk and rely on the forge's merge button, which does the same thing.

3. Two branches have diverged. Explain what git merge-base returns and why a merge would be impossible without it.

Solution

It returns the best common ancestor of the two tips — the last commit both branches agree on (§5.3).

Without it, Git could only compare the two tips, which tells it that files differ but not why. Every difference would need a human decision, because there is no way to distinguish "you changed this and they did not" from "you both changed it differently".

With the base, each path falls into a case that mostly answers itself (§6.1): changed on neither side, take either; changed on one side only, take that side; changed identically, take either; changed differently, and only this case needs attention. That is what makes a merge mostly automatic.

Use git merge-base --all, not the plain form. There can be several common ancestors when the branches have merged each other before — a criss-cross (§6.11) — and the plain form silently picks one, while the default ort strategy actually merges them into a virtual base. When a merge conflicts on a line neither branch touched, more than one line of --all output is the diagnosis.

4. git branch --merged on main does not list a branch you merged last week. Give two possible causes.

Solution

It was squash-merged. Squashing creates a single new commit with no parent link to the branch (§6.2), so the branch's commits are not ancestors of main and the reachability test that --merged performs correctly says no. This is the usual cause on teams that use a forge's "Squash and merge" button, and it means branch cleanup after squashing has to be manual — which is exactly why the forge offers to delete the branch for you.

You are not on main. git branch --merged with no argument tests against the current branch. Run git branch --merged main to be explicit.

A third possibility worth checking: the branch was rebased after merging, so its ref now points at commits that were never merged even though equivalent ones were. git cherry -v main <branch> (§12.7) answers this, because it compares by patch-id rather than by object id and so recognises equivalent content under a different commit id.

5. A colleague asks whether to git merge main or git rebase main into their two-week-old branch, which has been reviewed. Advise them.

Solution

Merge, in this case.

The deciding fact is that it has been reviewed. Rebasing rewrites every commit on the branch with new object ids (§8.1) and requires a force push (§8.9). Reviewers' comments become anchored to commits that no longer exist, and anyone who pulled the branch has to reset. Merging changes nothing that already exists, so review continues undisturbed.

If it had not been reviewed yet, rebase would be better: a linear branch produces a cleaner series, each commit's diff is against current main rather than a fortnight-old base, and the eventual merge fast-forwards.

Two more things worth saying to them:

  • Whichever they choose, do it now and regularly, not once at the end. Two weeks of divergence merged in one go is one large conflict; integrated daily it is several small ones (§5.8).
  • If the branch really needs to stay open for two weeks, the change is probably too big. Splitting it into a stack, or hiding the incomplete part behind a feature flag, removes the problem instead of managing it.

6. Explain what |\ and |/ mean in git log --graph, and why the left-hand column of a merge is not arbitrary.

Solution

|\ appears immediately below a merge commit: the history forks into two lines as you read downwards. |/ is where two lines rejoin at a common ancestor. Each * is a commit and each | is a parent link, drawn from child to parent.

The left column is the first parent — the branch that was checked out when the merge ran — and the ordering is recorded in the commit object itself, as two parent lines in a defined order (§3.6). It is not a drawing convention.

Three things depend on it:

  • git log --first-parent follows only that column, collapsing each merged branch to the merge that brought it in — the trunk's-eye view (§9.7).
  • HEAD^1 and HEAD~n follow first parents; HEAD^2 is the other side (§9.4).
  • git revert -m 1 <merge> means "keep the first parent's side", which is what makes reverting a merge meaningful at all (§7.5).

Which branch ends up as first parent is decided by where you were standing: merging feature into main gives main as first parent, and doing it the other way round gives the opposite. That is why "always merge into the trunk, never the trunk into the branch, for the final integration" is a convention worth keeping.

SECTION 6

Conflicts and Advanced Merging

Merging is where most people's confidence in Git ends. The commands are short, the output is terse, and the failure mode — a file full of angle brackets — looks like damage rather than information. It is not damage. A conflict is Git declining to guess, and every piece of information you need to make the decision it refused to make is available, in the index, in a form §3 already explained.

This section takes the position that a conflict is a data structure, not an accident. The working-tree file with markers in it is a rendering; the actual state is three versions of the file sitting in the index at three different stage numbers, and once you can read that, the whole family of resolution tools stops being folklore.

Section 5 covered ordinary merging: branch pointers, the merge base, the fast-forward case. This section covers what happens when the automatic merge cannot finish, plus the strategy machinery underneath it.

6.1 What a Merge Is: Three Trees, Not Two

A three-way merge takes three commits: ours (where HEAD is), theirs (what you are merging), and the merge base — their best common ancestor, found by git merge-base. For each path, Git compares all three.

Base vs ours
Base vs theirs
Result
unchanged
unchanged
Take either. Nothing happened.
changed
unchanged
Take ours.
unchanged
changed
Take theirs.
changed
changed, identically
Take either — they agree.
changed
changed, differently
Try to combine line by line. If the changes overlap, conflict.

Two things follow immediately, and both are frequently misunderstood.

"Both sides changed the file" is not a conflict. Two people editing different functions in the same file merge silently and correctly. A conflict requires the changes to touch overlapping regions — by default, the same lines or adjacent ones.

The base is doing all the work. Without it, Git could only see that two files differ and would have to ask about every difference. With it, Git can tell who changed what, which is why a two-way diff tool is a bad merge tool and why §6.5's conflict styles that hide the base make resolution harder than it needs to be.

6.2 Fast-Forward, and When to Refuse It

When the merge base is the other branch's tip — meaning your branch has no commits the other lacks — there is nothing to merge. Git moves the ref forward:

git merge topic
git log --oneline --graph

Updating dae90f9..2ff6b4f
Fast-forward
 f | 1 +
 1 file changed, 1 insertion(+)
* 2ff6b4f b
* dae90f9 a

No merge commit; the history stays linear and there is no record that a branch ever existed. Whether that is good or bad is a team decision, and Git gives you three ways to state it:

git merge --no-ff --no-edit topic
git log --oneline --graph

*   436227c Merge branch 'topic'
|\
| * 2ff6b4f b
|/
* dae90f9 a
--no-ff
Always make a merge commit. The branch's existence, its scope and the moment it landed all stay in the history — git log --first-parent then reads as one line per feature (§9.7).
--ff-only
Refuse unless it is a fast-forward. The right setting for git pull (pull.ff = only), because it turns "you have diverged" into an error you handle deliberately rather than a merge commit you did not intend.
--squash
Apply the combined change and stop, leaving it staged. Not a merge at all — see below.

--squash is the one that surprises people:

git merge --squash topic
git status -s
git log --oneline -1

M  f
dae90f9 a

HEAD did not move and nothing was committed. The changes are staged and waiting; when you commit them you get an ordinary single-parent commit. Git records no relationship to topic whatsoever, so a later git merge topic will try to merge the same work again, and git branch --merged will not list topic as merged. That is exactly what a forge's "Squash and merge" button does, and it is why deleting the branch afterwards matters: the branch is the only remaining evidence.

The two to configure once are git merge --no-ff and git merge --squash. git merge --no-ff is worth making the default for the trunk (merge.ff = false), because it is what preserves the branch boundary; git merge --squash is worth understanding precisely because it looks like a merge and is not one.

6.3 Reading a Conflict

Two branches change the same line:

git merge feature

Auto-merging hello.py
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in hello.py
Automatic merge failed; fix conflicts and then commit the result.

git status -s
cat hello.py

UU hello.py
def greet(name):
<<<<<<< HEAD
    return "Hi there, " + name
=======
    return f"Hello, {name}!"
>>>>>>> feature


def main():
    print(greet("world"))

UU means "unmerged, unmerged" — both sides modified it. And the merge stopped: git merge exits 1, HEAD has not moved, and no commit was made. You are in a paused state that git status will describe in full if you run it without -s.

The markers are a rendering of a decision you have to make. <<<<<<< to ======= is what HEAD has; ======= to >>>>>>> is what the incoming branch has. Resolving means editing the file until it is what you want — which may be either side, both, or neither — and then git adding it. git add on a conflicted path is the act of declaring it resolved; there is no separate "resolve" command.

Note what the default marker style does not tell you: which side changed the line. Both alternatives are shown as equals, so if HEAD's version is identical to the original and only feature changed anything, you cannot tell. That omission is fixable, and §6.5 fixes it.

6.4 The Index During a Conflict: Three Stages

The markers are cosmetic. The real state is in the index, and §3.4's git ls-files -s output had a column we skipped:

git ls-files -u

100644 dde3fd16ace0552f66a79fae35ae8aa44138c14d 1	hello.py
100644 d8199a584d455853a489a0af4f7ff13d7a11f00a 2	hello.py
100644 0a84d7cff972dff221cdc29fc6e8cc0d221a0126 3	hello.py

Three rows for one path, differing only in the stage number, which is 0 for every ordinary entry and 1, 2 or 3 during a conflict.

1
base
The common ancestor's version.
2
ours
HEAD's version.
3
theirs
The incoming branch's version.

Each is a real blob in the object database, addressable with the :<stage>:<path> syntax (§9.2):

git show :1:hello.py
git show :2:hello.py
git show :3:hello.py

def greet(name):
    return "Hello, " + name


def main():
    print(greet("world"))
def greet(name):
    return "Hi there, " + name


def main():
    print(greet("world"))
def greet(name):
    return f"Hello, {name}!"


def main():
    print(greet("world"))

Three complete files. This is the whole reason a conflict is recoverable no matter how badly you mangle the working-tree copy: nothing has been lost, the file on disk is a suggestion, and git checkout --merge <path> regenerates it from the stages at any point.

git add collapses the three rows into one stage-0 row holding whatever is on disk. That is what "resolved" means mechanically, and it is why git add on a file that still contains <<<<<<< succeeds cheerfully — Git does not read your resolution, it records it. Guarding against that is pre-commit's job (§13.8).

git ls-files -u is the command to run first on any conflict you do not immediately understand, because the set of stages present tells you what kind of conflict it is before you read a single line of content (§6.6). Its -u is short for --unmerged, and it prints nothing at all when the index is clean.

6.5 Conflict Styles: merge, diff3, zdiff3

The default marker style hides the base, which throws away the most useful piece of information in the merge. Compare all three on one conflict, where both sides also added an identical line:

cat cfg.txt

header
<<<<<<< HEAD
value = 2
=======
value = 3
>>>>>>> theirs
logging = on
footer

git checkout --conflict=diff3 cfg.txt
cat cfg.txt

header
<<<<<<< ours
value = 2
logging = on
||||||| base
value = 1
=======
value = 3
logging = on
>>>>>>> theirs
footer

git checkout --conflict=zdiff3 cfg.txt
cat cfg.txt

header
<<<<<<< ours
value = 2
||||||| base
value = 1
=======
value = 3
>>>>>>> theirs
logging = on
footer
merge
The default. Two alternatives, no base. You cannot tell whether one side is unchanged, and a whitespace-only change on one side looks exactly as significant as a rewrite.
diff3
Adds the base between ||||||| and =======. Now you can see that value went from 1 to 2 on one side and 1 to 3 on the other. But logging = on appears twice inside the conflict, even though both sides agree on it.
zdiff3
Same as diff3, but common lines at the edges are hoisted out of the conflict region. The conflict is now exactly the one line that actually disagrees. This is the one to use.
git config --global merge.conflictstyle zdiff3

That single setting is probably the highest-value line in this whole section. It costs nothing, it changes no behaviour, and it turns "two blocks of text, pick one" into "here is precisely what each side did".

Note the marker labels change too. During the merge itself they name the refs (HEAD and theirs); after git checkout --conflict=… regenerates them they read ours and theirs. merge.conflictStyle sets the default so you rarely need the flag.

6.6 The Kinds of Conflict Git Distinguishes

Content conflicts are the common case but not the only one. This merge produces three different kinds at once:

git merge side

Auto-merging added.txt
CONFLICT (add/add): Merge conflict in added.txt
CONFLICT (modify/delete): doc.txt deleted in side and modified in HEAD.  Version HEAD of doc.txt left in tree.
Automatic merge failed; fix conflicts and then commit the result.

git status -s
git ls-files -u

AA added.txt
UD doc.txt
R  both.txt -> renamed.txt
100644 <sha> 2	added.txt
100644 <sha> 3	added.txt
100644 <sha> 1	doc.txt
100644 <sha> 2	doc.txt

Read the stage numbers — they say exactly what kind of conflict each is.

AA
add/add
Stages 2 and 3, no stage 1. Both sides created a file that did not exist in the base, so there is no common ancestor to merge against — Git falls back to a two-way merge of the two new files.
UD
modify/delete
Stages 1 and 2, no stage 3. We changed it; they deleted it. There is no content to merge, only a decision: keep the modified file (git add doc.txt) or honour the deletion (git rm doc.txt). Git deliberately refuses to pick, because "someone deleted the file I was fixing" is a question for a human.
R
rename
Not a conflict. They renamed both.txt to renamed.txt while we edited it, and the merge carried our edit into the renamed file automatically.

That last row is worth dwelling on, because §3.9 established that Git does not record renames. It detected one here — a deleted path and an added path whose contents are similar enough — and then applied our content change to the new name:

cat renamed.txt

shared
plus

Our added line, in their new filename. Rename detection is why merges across a big refactor usually work, and the reason it sometimes does not is the similarity threshold: if the rename also rewrote most of the file, Git sees an unrelated delete and add, and you get a modify/delete conflict plus a mysterious new file. -X rename-threshold=<n> lowers the threshold when you know a rename happened, and -X no-renames turns detection off entirely.

Two more kinds you will meet: directory/file (one side made logs a file, the other a directory) and submodule (§14.4), where the two sides record different commits of the same submodule.

6.7 Resolving: the Tools

Editing the markers by hand is the normal path. These are the alternatives, each for a case where hand-editing is wrong.

git checkout --ours <path>
Take our whole version of the file, discarding theirs. Note it is the whole file, not per-hunk.
git checkout --theirs <path>
The mirror image.
git checkout --merge <path>
Regenerate the marked-up file from the stages. The undo button for "I destroyed the conflict markers".
git restore --staged --worktree <path>
The modern spelling of the same reset for one path.
git mergetool
Launch a three-pane visual tool per conflicted file, configured by merge.tool. It writes the result and stages it for you.
git merge-file
Merge three files that are not in a repository at all. Useful for re-running a merge with different whitespace options after extracting the stages.

The --ours/--theirs pair has a trap that has caused real data loss: during a rebase, "ours" and "theirs" are swapped relative to what you expect. A rebase replays your commits onto their branch, so the branch being built on — theirs, from your point of view — is checked out as HEAD, making it --ours. The commit being applied, which is yours, is --theirs. If you take one thing from this subsection, take the habit of checking git status, which names the branches explicitly, rather than trusting the words.

Extracting the stages to files is the escape hatch for a merge that a tool is handling badly:

git show :1:hello.py > hello.base.py
git show :2:hello.py > hello.ours.py
git show :3:hello.py > hello.theirs.py
git merge-file -p hello.ours.py hello.base.py hello.theirs.py > hello.py

git merge-tree does the same job one level up: it merges two commits' trees and prints the result — including conflict markers — without touching HEAD, the index or the working tree at all, which makes it the right tool for a server or a script that wants to know whether a merge would conflict. git merge-file takes its three files in the order ours, base, theirs and writes a merged result with markers. It accepts --ours, --theirs and --union to resolve automatically, and it is the only way to re-run a merge with different options on one file without redoing the whole merge.

Three of these are one-line answers to "just take that side": git checkout --ours <path>, git checkout --theirs <path>, and git mergetool for the cases where a three-pane view is genuinely easier. git mergetool picks its program from merge.tool, and git mergetool --tool-help lists what your machine has — setting merge.tool once is what stops it asking every time. git merge-tree sits outside this group: it is the non-interactive one, for a program that needs to know the answer without a working tree.

6.8 Investigating: log --merge and diff --cc

Two commands answer "why is this conflicting" without leaving the paused merge.

git log --merge --oneline

1fc2b2e ours
5535400 theirs

--merge restricts the log to commits that touched a currently-conflicted file and are on exactly one side of the merge. On a real conflict in a busy repository this is the difference between reading two commits and reading two hundred.

git log --oneline --left-right HEAD...MERGE_HEAD

< 1fc2b2e ours
> 5535400 theirs

MERGE_HEAD is a real ref written by git merge when it pauses, so all of §9's revision syntax works on it. The ... is the symmetric difference (§9.5) and --left-right marks which side each commit came from.

And git diff behaves differently during a conflict:

git diff

diff --cc hello.py
index d8199a5,0a84d7c..0000000
--- a/hello.py
+++ b/hello.py
@@@ -1,5 -1,5 +1,11 @@@
  def greet(name):
++<<<<<<< ours
 +    return "Hi there, " + name
++||||||| base
++    return "Hello, " + name
++=======
+     return f"Hello, {name}!"
++>>>>>>> theirs


  def main():

That is a combined diff, which git diff --cc produces explicitly. There are two columns of +/- instead of one, one per parent. A line marked ++ differs from both parents; a line marked + (space in the first column) differs only from the second. Combined diffs are also what git show prints for a merge commit, which is why a merge commit's git show looks nearly empty: it only lists files whose result differs from both parents, so a merge that took one side wholesale shows nothing at all. git show --first-parent or git show -m is what you want when reviewing what a merge actually brought in.

git diff --cc names the combined format explicitly, and it is worth knowing because git log --merge and git diff --cc are the two halves of a conflict investigation: git log --merge narrows the commits to look at, and git diff --cc shows both sides of the result at once.

6.9 Strategy Options: -X

-X passes an option to the merge strategy. The two most used resolve content conflicts automatically:

git merge -X ours theirs
ls
cat cfg.txt

NEW.md
cfg.txt
value = 2

The conflicting line took our version — and NEW.md, which only the other branch added, is still there. -X ours means "when a hunk conflicts, prefer ours"; everything that does not conflict merges normally.

-X ours / -X theirs
Auto-resolve conflicting hunks in favour of one side. Non-conflicting changes from both sides are kept.
-X ignore-space-change
Treat runs of whitespace as equivalent while merging. The tool for the merge where someone reformatted a file.
-X ignore-all-space
Ignore whitespace entirely. Stronger, and dangerous in whitespace-significant languages.
-X rename-threshold=<n>
Set the rename-detection similarity threshold; -X no-renames disables it.
-X subtree[=<path>]
Shift one side's tree by a path prefix before merging — the mechanism behind subtree merges (§14.10).
-X diff-algorithm=histogram
Use a different diff algorithm for the three-way merge. Sometimes resolves a conflict the default produces on heavily reindented code.

-X ours deserves a warning. It is silent: you get no conflict, no list of what it decided, and no record in the merge commit of which hunks it discarded. Reaching for it because a merge is annoying is how a colleague's fix quietly disappears. It is defensible for generated files and lock files, and .gitattributes (§13.9) is the better home for that policy because it applies per path rather than to the whole merge.

-Xours and -Xtheirs are the two you will actually type; note that -Xtheirs is not the mirror of a strategy but of an option, so -Xtheirs keeps their side of each conflicting hunk and still merges everything of yours that did not conflict. -Xignore-space-change is the third worth remembering, because a merge against a reformatted file is otherwise unresolvable by hand.

6.10 The ours Strategy, and Why It Is Not -X ours

-s ours looks like a longer spelling of -X ours. It is a completely different operation.

git merge -s ours theirs
ls
cat cfg.txt

cfg.txt
value = 2

NEW.md is gone. -s ours discards the other branch's content entirely and records a merge commit whose tree is exactly ours:

git rev-parse 'HEAD^{tree}' 'HEAD^1^{tree}'

f5685219a78124b6b9148051ffd0a5298cf934d5
f5685219a78124b6b9148051ffd0a5298cf934d5

The merge commit's tree is byte-identical to the first parent's. Nothing changed, and yet the commit has two parents, so theirs is now recorded as merged. git branch --merged lists it, git log theirs..HEAD is empty, and a future merge of that branch will do nothing.

That is the entire purpose: it is a lie recorded deliberately. The legitimate uses are narrow and real — marking an abandoned branch as dealt with so it stops appearing in "unmerged branches", or recording that a release branch's changes were incorporated by other means so future merges do not re-raise them. Using it to "resolve" a merge you did not want to think about silently deletes the other branch's work while leaving a history that claims it was integrated, and that is very hard to spot later.

-X ours
A tie-breaker for conflicting hunks. Their non-conflicting work is kept.
-s ours
