A recorded no-op. All of their work is discarded, and the history says otherwise.

6.11 More Than One Merge Base

Everything so far assumed a single merge base. Branches that have merged each other before can have several. Build one:

git log --graph --oneline --all

*   2d926db M1: bx merges by, keep X
|\
| | * a806e71 M2: by merges x1, keep Y
| |/|
| |/
|/|
* | 749a9b7 x1: b -> X
| * e96d85f y1: b -> Y
|/
* 943284a init

Each branch merged the other's early commit, so neither x1 nor y1 is an ancestor of the other, and both are ancestors of both tips:

git merge-base --all bx by

749a9b72d4609fc9d6c0a9a833ea8dd4be3a1d93
e96d85fce8c7812dbdd489aef79db52ab744915a

Two merge bases. This shape is called a criss-cross merge, and it is common in any repository where people merge main into their branch and also merge their branch into main. Plain git merge-base picks one arbitrarily, and merging against an arbitrary base gives wrong answers.

The default strategy handles it by merging the merge bases together to produce a virtual merge base, then doing an ordinary three-way merge against that virtual merge base. Recursively, if the bases themselves have multiple bases — which is where the old name came from.

That strategy is called ort, and the Git manual is candid about the name: it is an acronym for "Ostensibly Recursive's Twin", because it was written as a replacement for the previous default, recursive. Two version facts matter here and are the kind that go stale in blog posts:

ort merge strategy
The default for merging a single head since Git 2.34. Faster, handles renames better, and — unlike its predecessor — never touches the working tree until it knows the merge succeeds.
recursive merge strategy
Now a synonym for ort. It was a separate implementation until 2.49; in 2.50 it was redirected. Advice that tells you to pass -s recursive for different behaviour is describing a program that no longer exists.
resolve strategy
Three-way merge using a single base. No virtual base, no rename detection. Occasionally useful precisely because it is dumber.
octopus merge
The default when merging more than two heads. An octopus merge refuses to run if any of them conflicts, so it is only for combining several already-agreeing branches at once.
ours merge strategy
§6.10.

The practical consequence of the virtual base is worth stating plainly: a criss-cross merge can conflict on a line neither of the two commits you are merging ever touched, because the bases disagreed about it and the virtual base inherited a conflict. When a merge produces a conflict that makes no sense from the two branches involved, git merge-base --all is the first thing to run.

The names are worth keeping straight because three of them look interchangeable and are not. The ort merge strategy is the default and does everything; the recursive merge strategy is now the same code; the ours merge strategy discards the other side entirely; and the resolve strategy is a simpler three-way merge that ignores extra merge bases. Only the ours merge strategy changes what ends up in the tree.

6.12 rerere: Reuse Recorded Resolution

If you merge or rebase a long-lived branch repeatedly, you resolve the same conflict repeatedly. rerere records how you resolved it and replays that automatically.

git config rerere.enabled true
git merge topic

CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in cfg.txt
Recorded preimage for 'cfg.txt'
Automatic merge failed; fix conflicts and then commit the result.

"Recorded preimage" means rerere hashed the conflict — the base, ours, theirs — and filed it under that hash in .git/rr-cache/. Resolve and stage as normal:

printf 'value = 23\n' > cfg.txt
git add cfg.txt
git commit -q --no-edit

Recorded resolution for 'cfg.txt'.

Now throw the merge away and do it again:

git reset -q --hard HEAD~1
git merge topic
cat cfg.txt
git status -s

Auto-merging cfg.txt
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in cfg.txt
Resolved 'cfg.txt' using previous resolution.
value = 23
UU cfg.txt

The file already contains your resolution — and the status is still UU. That is deliberate and it is the detail people miss: rerere fills in the content but does not stage it, because it will not commit a resolution on your behalf. You are expected to look at what it did and git add it. git rerere diff shows what it changed, and git rerere status lists the paths it is handling.

Where it pays
A long-lived branch rebased repeatedly; a release branch merged forward every week; any workflow where you abandon and redo a merge while getting it right.
What it keys on
The exact conflict text. Change one of the three sides and the hash differs and rerere correctly says nothing.
The risk
It replays a resolution that was right last time and might be wrong now. This is why it does not stage, and why git rerere forget <path> exists.
Housekeeping
The cache is local, is never pushed, and is pruned by the rerere-gc maintenance task (§15.9).

Note that rerere.enabled is per repository unless you set it globally, and rerere.enabled = true is one of the settings worth turning on everywhere (§13.5) — it costs nothing on a repository where you never hit the same conflict twice.

6.13 Aborting, and Undoing a Finished Merge

While a merge is paused, one command puts everything back:

git merge --abort
git status -s
ls

added.txt
both.txt
doc.txt

Clean tree, original filenames, no markers. --abort restores the pre-merge state including uncommitted changes you had before starting, which git reset --hard would have destroyed — so prefer it. Its one limitation is the one it warns about: if you had uncommitted changes and the merge overwrote them, it cannot always reconstruct them. git merge --quit leaves the working tree as it is and only clears the merge state, for when you want to keep a partial resolution.

Undoing a merge that has already been committed has two answers, and picking the wrong one causes a well-known long-running bug.

If it is unpublished, move the branch back:

git reset --hard HEAD~

HEAD~ is the first parent (§9.4), which for a merge commit is the branch you were on. The merge is gone as if it never happened.

If it is published, you cannot rewrite it (§8.11), so revert it:

git revert -m 1 <merge-commit>

-m 1 names the mainline parent — the parent whose side you want to keep. §3.6 established that a merge commit's parents are ordered, first parent being the branch you were on, so -m 1 means "undo what came in from the other side". Without -m, git revert refuses: it cannot know which side of the merge you consider the trunk.

Now the trap. Reverting a merge produces a commit that undoes the content, but the merge itself is still in the history — so the branch is still an ancestor, and Git still believes its commits are merged. When that branch is eventually fixed and merged again, Git computes the diff from the merge base and correctly concludes that those changes are already present, so it brings back nothing, and the revert's deletion stands. The feature silently fails to appear.

The fix is to revert the revert before merging again:

git revert <the-revert-commit>
git merge feature

That restores the content, after which the new work merges normally. Linus Torvalds wrote a well-known explanation of this in the Git documentation itself (git help revert links it as the revert-a-faulty-merge How-To), and it is worth reading once, because the failure is invisible until someone asks where their feature went.

git merge --abort is available at any point while the merge is paused and is always the safe exit; after the merge has been committed it is gone, and the choice is between git reset and reverting the mainline parent with -m 1. Git's own documentation on that second case is worth reading once — it lives on git-scm.com as the revert-a-faulty-merge How-To and ships with the manual.

Exercises

1. A merge reports CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in app.py. Without opening the file, print the three versions Git is choosing between and say which command declares the conflict resolved.

Solution 
git show :1:app.py    # the merge base
git show :2:app.py    # ours (HEAD)
git show :3:app.py    # theirs (the incoming branch)

git add app.py declares it resolved — it collapses the three stage rows into a single stage-0 entry holding whatever is in the working tree. There is no separate resolve command, and git add does not check that the markers are gone.

git ls-files -u first is a good habit: which stages are present tells you what kind of conflict it is (§6.6). Stages 1, 2, 3 is a content conflict; 2 and 3 only is add/add; 1 and 2 only is modify/delete.

2. Both -X ours and -s ours were used on the same merge. One produced a tree containing the other branch's new file; the other did not. Explain, and say which one leaves a misleading history.

Solution

-X ours is a strategy option: a real three-way merge runs, and only hunks that actually conflict are decided in our favour. The other branch's new file did not conflict with anything, so it merged in normally.

-s ours is a strategy: it does no merging at all. The result tree is set equal to the first parent's tree, and the other branch's content — conflicting or not — is discarded.

-s ours leaves the misleading history. The commit has two parents, so git branch --merged reports the branch as merged, git log theirs..HEAD is empty, and any future merge of that branch is a no-op — while none of its changes are present. That is sometimes exactly what you want (retiring an abandoned branch), and it is a quiet disaster when used to make a difficult merge go away.

3. You merge release into main and get a conflict in a file that neither branch has modified since they diverged. Give the mechanism and the command that confirms it.

Solution

Multiple merge bases (§6.11). The branches have merged each other before, so there is more than one common ancestor, and the two ancestors disagree about that file. The ort strategy merges the bases together into a virtual base; that base merge itself conflicts, and the conflict is inherited into the real merge — even though neither tip touched the file.

Confirm it with:

git merge-base --all main release

More than one line of output is the diagnosis. git log --graph --oneline main release will then show the criss-cross shape.

Resolution is ordinary: pick the right content and stage it. If it recurs on every merge of these two branches, turn on rerere (§6.12) so you only decide it once.

4. Your team squash-merges every pull request. A colleague reports that merging main back into their still-open branch produces conflicts in code they already had merged. Explain.

Solution

--squash records no relationship between the squashed commit and the branch it came from (§6.2). Their branch's original commits are not ancestors of anything on main, even though the same content is.

So when they merge main into their branch, the merge base is wherever the branch actually diverged — before any of their work. Git therefore believes their commits are new changes that main does not have, while main contains the same changes arriving via an unrelated commit. Both sides "changed" the same lines relative to that old base, and every one of them conflicts.

The workable options:

  • After the squash lands, do not merge main in — rebase onto it and drop the commits that are already there, or simply delete the branch and start a fresh one from main. This is why forges offer to delete the branch after a squash merge, and why taking that offer matters.
  • If a branch is going to be long-lived and merged with main repeatedly, do not squash it. Use --no-ff merges, which record the relationship and make every subsequent merge base correct.

5. During a rebase you run git checkout --ours conflicted.py expecting your own version and get someone else's. Why?

Solution

During a rebase the roles are reversed relative to intuition (§6.7). A rebase checks out the upstream branch and replays your commits onto it one at a time, so at the moment of a conflict HEAD is the upstream — which makes it --ours — and the commit being applied is yours, which is --theirs.

The reliable habit is not to memorise the reversal but to read git status, which names them:

You are currently rebasing branch 'feature' on '9a4f2c1'.
  (fix conflicts and then run "git rebase --continue")

and to use zdiff3 markers (§6.5), which label the sides and show the base, so the content itself tells you which is which. git log --oneline --left-right HEAD...REBASE_HEAD also disambiguates.

6. A merge commit's git show output is empty, yet the merge clearly changed files. Is something wrong?

Solution

No. git show on a merge prints a combined diff (§6.8), which by design lists only the hunks that differ from every parent — the parts the merge resolution invented. A merge that took each conflicting hunk wholesale from one side or the other differs from at least one parent everywhere, so there is nothing left to report and the output is empty. This is a feature: it isolates the human decisions in the merge from the mechanical combination.

To see what the merge brought in:

  • git show --first-parent <merge> — the diff against the branch you were on, i.e. everything the merge added to it. Usually what you want.
  • git show -m <merge> — one full diff per parent, shown separately.
  • git log --first-parent on the branch — one entry per merge, which reads as a changelog of features rather than of commits (§9.7).
SECTION 7

Undoing: restore, reset, revert, clean and stash

Git has five commands for undoing things, and they are the part of the tool people are most afraid of. The fear is reasonable — one of them can genuinely destroy work — and it is also almost entirely avoidable, because which command you want is determined by two questions with short answers.

This section is organised around those two questions. Once you can place a situation on both axes, the command is decided rather than guessed, and the one genuinely dangerous case is easy to recognise before you type it.

Everything here operates on the three trees of §3.7: HEAD (the commit you are on), the index (what the next commit will contain), and the working tree (the files on disk). Every undo command is a statement about copying state between those three, plus possibly moving a branch ref (§4.2).

7.1 The Question to Ask First

Has it been published?
If the commits are only on your machine, you may rewrite them (reset, rebase). If anyone else has them, you must add a new commit that undoes them (revert) — §8.11 has the mechanics of why.
What are you undoing?
A commit (move HEAD), a staged change (index ← HEAD), an edit on disk (working tree ← index), or an untracked file (delete it). Different targets, different commands.

Crossing the two gives the whole section:

Target
Private
Published
A commit
git reset
git revert
A staged change
git restore --staged
—
An edit on disk
git restore
—
An untracked file
git clean
—
Everything, for now
git stash
—

Three of the five never touch history at all, which is why only reset --hard and clean can lose work permanently. Learn to recognise those two and the rest is safe to experiment with.

7.2 restore: the Working Tree and the Index

git restore was introduced to take over half of git checkout's job (§7.10), and its two flags map exactly onto the two copies you can make.

Set up a file that differs in all three places:

git status --short

MM f.txt

Two Ms: the first column is index against HEAD, the second is working tree against index. So the file has one version committed, a second staged, and a third on disk:

HEAD:  one two three
index: one two three four
disk:  one two three four five

Unstage — that is, copy HEAD's version over the index, leaving disk alone:

git restore --staged f.txt
git status --short

 M f.txt

index: one two three
disk:  one two three four five

The staged version is gone; your edit is untouched. Now discard the edit — copy the index over disk:

git restore f.txt
git status --short
git diff HEAD --stat

(no output — the tree is clean)

Clean: git status --short prints nothing, and neither does git diff. The edit is gone and there is no way back. That is the one dangerous thing restore does, and Git warns about it in git status's long form for exactly this reason: a change that was never staged and never committed exists in no object in the database, so no recovery technique in §7.9 can reach it.

git restore <path>
Working tree ← index. Discards unstaged edits. Unrecoverable.
git restore --staged <path>
Index ← HEAD. Unstages. The edit survives on disk.
git restore --staged --worktree <p>
Both. Equivalent to --source=HEAD on everything: throw the path back to its committed state.
git restore --source=<rev> <p>
Take the path's content from any commit. git restore --source=HEAD~3 src/ is "put that directory back the way it was three commits ago", staged nowhere and committed nowhere.
git restore -p <path>
Choose hunk by hunk. The safe way to discard part of a change.

--source is the underused one. It turns restore into a general "get me that version of that path" tool, and combined with git log -- <path> (§9.6) it is how you recover a file you deleted or broke ten commits ago without touching anything else.

7.3 reset: Moving the Branch

git reset moves the current branch ref, and then optionally propagates that move to the index and the working tree. It does three things in order, and each mode stops at a different point:

Step 1 — always
Move the branch ref to the target commit. --soft stops here.
Step 2 — default
Make the index match the new HEAD. --mixed stops here.
Step 3 — on request
Make the working tree match too. --hard. Overwrites your files.

The same starting state through all three modes, showing the last line of each tree:

=== start ===
HEAD=c3   index=three disk=three  status=[]
=== reset --soft HEAD~1 ===
HEAD=c2   index=three disk=three  status=[M  f.txt]
=== reset --mixed HEAD~1 (the default) ===
HEAD=c2   index=two  disk=three  status=[ M f.txt]
=== reset --hard HEAD~1 ===
HEAD=c2   index=two  disk=two   status=[]

Read the status codes across. --soft leaves everything staged, so the commit's content is sitting ready to be recommitted. --mixed leaves it as unstaged edits. --hard leaves nothing at all — which is exactly what makes it the dangerous one and also what makes it useful.

--soft
"Undo the commit, keep everything staged." git reset --soft HEAD~3 then git commit is the simplest way to squash the last three commits into one without an interactive rebase.
git reset --mixed
The default. "Undo the commit and unstage." This is what makes splitting a commit work during a rebase (§8.5): git reset HEAD^ puts the changes back as unstaged edits so git add -p can redistribute them.
--hard
"Undo the commit and throw the changes away." The only Git command in everyday use that destroys uncommitted work with no warning. Committed work it discards is recoverable (§7.9); uncommitted work is not.

git reset -p is the per-hunk form: it unstages part of a change interactively, the mirror image of git add -p (§2.6). Two safer relatives are worth knowing. git reset --keep <commit> moves HEAD but refuses if that would overwrite local changes, which is --hard's behaviour with a seatbelt. And git reset --merge is the same idea for aborting a merge, though git merge --abort (§6.13) is the command to reach for there.

Of the three, git reset --soft and git reset --mixed are the everyday ones and --hard is the one to type carefully. git reset --soft is the squashing tool; git reset --mixed — which is what you get with no flag at all — is the unstaging tool and the first step of the commit-splitting recipe in §8.5.

7.4 reset With Paths

Give reset a path and it behaves completely differently: step 1 is skipped. The branch does not move — how could it, when only some files are named? — and only the index is updated.

git reset HEAD~2 -- src/config.py

That sets the index entry for src/config.py to its content two commits ago, leaving HEAD and the working tree alone. With no commit named, git reset -- <path> means git reset HEAD -- <path>, which is the classic unstage.

This is why git status used to suggest git reset HEAD <file> to unstage, and why it now suggests git restore --staged <file>: the new command says what it does, while the old one is the same verb doing an entirely different operation depending on whether a path is present. git reset --hard <path> is not even valid — Git rejects it, because "move the branch and overwrite the tree, but only for one file" is not a coherent request.

git reset -p completes the interactive family: git add -p stages hunk by hunk, git restore -p discards hunk by hunk, and git reset -p unstages hunk by hunk. All three use the same interface described in §2.6.

7.5 revert: Undoing in Public

git revert <commit> computes the inverse of a commit's diff and applies it as a new commit. History grows; nothing is rewritten; anyone who already has the original commit is unaffected.

git revert <commit>

Because it is an ordinary commit, it can conflict — if later work touched the same lines, reverting is a three-way merge like any other, and §6's tools apply. git revert --continue, --skip and --abort work as you would expect. git revert -n (--no-commit) applies the inverse to the index without committing, which is how you revert several commits into one:

git revert -n A B C
git commit -m 'revert the ill-advised feature'

Reverting a merge needs -m to say which side to keep:

git revert -m 1 <merge-commit>

-m 1 names the mainline — the first parent, which §3.6 established is the branch you were on. So -m 1 means "undo everything that came in from the other side". Without -m, git revert refuses, because there is no way for it to guess which parent you consider the trunk.

The trap that follows is important enough that §6.13 covers it in full and it is worth the one-line summary here: reverting a merge undoes the content but leaves the merge in the graph, so Git still considers that branch merged and a later re-merge brings in nothing. Revert the revert before merging again.

7.6 clean: Untracked Files

git clean deletes files Git is not tracking. Nothing it removes was ever in the object database, so nothing it removes can be recovered.

Because of that, it refuses to run without -f, and the first thing to type is always -n:

git clean -n -d

Would remove junk.txt
Would remove tmpdir/

git clean -n -d -x

Would remove build/
Would remove junk.txt
Would remove tmpdir/

git clean -f -d

Removing junk.txt
Removing tmpdir/
-n
Dry run. Always first.
-f
Actually do it. Required unless clean.requireForce is false, which you should not set.
-d
Include untracked directories. Without it, an untracked directory is left alone entirely.
-x
Also remove ignored files. This is what turns a working checkout into a pristine one — and it deletes .env, node_modules, virtualenvs and build caches.
-X
Remove only ignored files. Clears build output while keeping new source files you have not staged yet.
-i
Interactive. Worth using the first few times.

git clean -xfd is the "make it exactly like a fresh clone" command, and it is the right one in CI and the wrong one on a laptop where your untracked local configuration is about to evaporate. The dry run costs one keystroke.

7.7 stash

A stash sets your uncommitted work aside and gives you a clean tree, without making a commit on your branch. It is the answer to "I need to look at something else right now" — though for a job lasting more than a few minutes a worktree (§14.7) is better, because nothing has to be set aside at all.

git status --short
git stash push -m 'wip on f'
git status --short
git stash list

MM f.txt
?? newfile.txt
Saved working directory and index state On main: wip on f
?? newfile.txt
stash@{0}: On main: wip on f

Tracked changes are gone; the untracked file is still there, because by default a stash only takes tracked content.

A stash is not a special data structure. It is commits:

git log --oneline --graph refs/stash
git cat-file -p refs/stash

*   7220a3b On main: wip on f
|\
| * 0336565 index on main: 6826083 ignore build
|/
* 6826083 ignore build
tree 12f17c1595a4a98eb727539ac5ef3e86fa5dca1f
parent 6826083dd4d8b0d185a6cc4aa695441f0ce7a57e
parent 0336565fc9503428eef8b7c3802e634471fd687c

A merge commit with two parents: the commit you were on, and a commit holding the index state. Its own tree holds the working tree state. That is how one stash entry can remember the staged/unstaged split, and it is also why every stashed change is a real object in the database and therefore recoverable (§7.9) even after git stash drop.

The stack lives in refs/stash, with older entries reachable through that ref's reflog — which is why stash@{1} is reflog syntax (§4.10) and why gc.refs/stash.reflogExpire = never is the setting that stops old stashes being swept away.

That split is preserved only if you ask:

before:               MM f.txt
after plain pop:       M f.txt
after pop --index:    MM f.txt

Plain pop merges everything into unstaged changes; --index restores the staging as it was. Two more flags matter:

git stash push --keep-index -m 'keep'

git stash --keep-index stashes everything but leaves the staged version on disk — so you can run the test suite against exactly what you are about to commit, which is what a rigorous pre-commit hook does (§13.8).

git stash push -u -m 'with untracked'

git stash -u includes untracked files (-a includes ignored ones too). Worth making a habit, because "I stashed and my new file is still dirtying the tree" is the most common stash complaint.

git stash list
The stack. Give every entry a message with -m; WIP on main ×7 is useless.
git stash show -p
The diff of an entry, without applying it.
git stash apply
Restore and keep the entry. Safer than pop: if the apply conflicts, you still have it.
git stash pop
Restore and drop. Note it does not drop if the apply conflicts.
git stash branch <name>
Create a branch from the commit the stash was made on, apply the stash, and drop it. The correct escape when a stash no longer applies because the branch has moved.
git stash push -- <path>
Stash only some paths.

Two habits prevent the usual pain. Prefer apply to pop until you have seen it succeed. And treat the stash as a scratch buffer, not storage — a stash is invisible to git log, is not pushed, is not backed up, and is easy to forget across a machine rebuild. Work you care about belongs on a branch, which costs one command (§4.2).

Four of these are worth having in muscle memory. git stash apply restores without consuming the entry and git stash pop restores and drops it, so git stash apply is the safer default; git stash drop removes an entry you have finished with; and git stash branch <name> is the escape hatch for an entry that will no longer apply. git stash -u should probably be your habit rather than plain git stash, since an untracked file left behind is the commonest stash complaint, and git stash --keep-index is what lets you test exactly what you are about to commit.

git clean -fd is the form you will type most: -f because Git refuses without it, -d because otherwise an untracked directory is left entirely alone. Run git clean -n -d first, every time — git clean -fd cannot be undone.

7.8 ORIG_HEAD and the One-Step Undo

Any command that moves HEAD a long way — reset, merge, rebase, pull — first records where it was in ORIG_HEAD (§4.7).

git reset --hard HEAD~2
git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD

That is the one-step undo, and it is worth having in muscle memory because it covers the most common panic: a reset --hard to the wrong place, or a pull that merged something unwanted.

Its limit is that it holds only the most recent such move. A second reset overwrites it, and then you need the reflog.

7.9 Recovering With the Reflog

Almost nothing committed is ever really lost, and this subsection is the reason to believe that.

git log --oneline | head -2
git reset --hard HEAD~2
git log --oneline | head -1

6826083 ignore build
c81a615 c3
HEAD is now at 652dbc7 c2
652dbc7 c2

Two commits are gone from the branch. They are not gone from the repository:

git reflog

652dbc7 HEAD@{0}: reset: moving to HEAD~2
6826083 HEAD@{1}: reset: moving to HEAD
6826083 HEAD@{2}: reset: moving to HEAD
6826083 HEAD@{3}: reset: moving to HEAD

git reset --hard 'HEAD@{1}'
git log --oneline | head -1

6826083 ignore build

The recovery procedure in general:

1
Stop. Run nothing else, and in particular do not run git gc --prune=now. Every additional command adds reflog noise and can only make it harder.
2
git reflog for HEAD's moves, or git reflog show <branch> for that branch's own log. Prefer the branch's when recovering from a rebase — HEAD moved once per replayed commit, the branch moved once (§4.10).
3
Give the good commit a name first: git branch rescue <id>. Now it cannot be pruned and you can inspect it at leisure.
4
Compare before committing to it — git log rescue, or git range-diff (§8.8) if a rebase was involved.
5
git reset --hard rescue, or cherry-pick the parts you want.

When the reflog itself is gone — a fresh clone, an expired entry, work made on a detached HEAD long ago — there is one more layer:

git fsck --lost-found

This walks the object database and writes every unreachable commit and blob into .git/lost-found/. It is blunt: you get object ids with no context, and git show on each is the only way to tell them apart. It works because objects survive at least two weeks after becoming unreachable (§15.5).

The two things that defeat all of this are worth naming so you recognise them: git gc --prune=now after git reflog expire --expire=now --all (§4.11), and changes that were never staged or committed, which exist in no object and can be recovered by nothing.

The general shape of a lost commit recovery is worth stating as a rule, because it applies to every case in this section: a lost commit is almost never deleted, only unreferenced, so the job is to find its object id and give it a name. The reflog holds the id for at least thirty days, git fsck --lost-found finds it after that, and only a change that was never staged is genuinely beyond recovery.

7.10 checkout and Why It Was Split

git checkout does two unrelated jobs, and that overloading is the source of a lot of confusion — including a genuinely dangerous case.

git checkout <branch>          # switch branches       -> git switch
git checkout -- <path>         # discard changes       -> git restore

The first is safe: Git refuses to switch if it would lose your changes. The second is destructive and silent — and until Git 2.23, a single missing -- was all that separated them. git checkout main on a repository that also has a file called main did something you did not want, and git checkout . threw away your entire working tree with no confirmation.

Git 2.23 split the two:

git switch
Change branch. -c creates, -C force-creates, --detach is required to detach — so §4.4's accidental detached HEAD stops happening.
git restore
Change file content. --staged, --worktree, --source, -p as in §7.2.

git checkout is not deprecated and will not be removed — far too much documentation, tooling and muscle memory depends on it. But new work should use the two specific commands, and git status now suggests them. The one thing checkout still does that neither replacement covers is git checkout --conflict=zdiff3 <path> for regenerating conflict markers (§6.5).

7.11 A Decision Table

I staged the wrong file
git restore --staged <path>
I want to throw away my edits
git restore <path>
…but only some of them
git restore -p <path>
I want that file as it was 5 commits ago
git restore --source=HEAD~5 <path>
My last commit message is wrong
git commit --amend
I forgot a file in the last commit
git commit --amend --no-edit
Undo the last commit, keep the changes staged
git reset --soft HEAD~1
Undo the last commit, keep the changes unstaged
git reset HEAD~1
Undo the last commit and the changes
git reset --hard HEAD~1
Undo a pushed commit
git revert <commit>
Undo a pushed merge
git revert -m 1 <merge>
Undo the reset I just did
git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD
Undo something further back
git reflog
Set work aside briefly
git stash push -u -m '…'
Set work aside for hours
git switch -c wip && git commit
Delete untracked files
git clean -n -d, then -f -d
Make it exactly like a fresh clone
git reset --hard && git clean -xfd

The two rows to be careful with are reset --hard and clean. Everything else either preserves your work or leaves it recoverable.

Exercises

1. git status --short shows MM app.py. Explain the two letters, and give the command that keeps your latest edit but throws away what you staged.

Solution

The two columns compare different pairs of trees (§7.2). The first is index against HEAD and the second is working tree against index, so MM means the file is modified in the index relative to the commit, and modified on disk relative to the index. Three different versions exist.

To discard the staged version while keeping what is on disk:

git restore --staged app.py

This copies HEAD's version into the index and touches nothing else — the status becomes M app.py and your latest edit is intact.

Getting it the wrong way round is git restore app.py, which copies the index over the working tree and destroys the newest edit permanently. That change exists in no object in the database, so nothing in §7.9 can recover it. When in doubt, git diff (working tree against index) and git diff --staged (index against HEAD) show you exactly what each command would discard.

2. You have made three commits that should have been one. Give two ways to combine them and say when each is right.

Solution 
git reset --soft HEAD~3
git commit -m 'the one commit'

--soft moves the branch back three commits and stops (§7.3) — the index and working tree are untouched, so all three commits' content is sitting staged and ready. Simple, and correct when the three commits are the most recent ones and you want exactly one result.

git rebase -i HEAD~3

marking the second and third as squash or fixup. Slower, and necessary when the commits are not at the tip, when you want two commits rather than one, or when you want to reorder as well (§8.4).

Both rewrite history, so both are only for commits you have not published (§8.11). If they are already pushed to a shared branch, do neither — the confusing history is cheaper than the disruption.

A third answer worth knowing: if you have not made the mess yet, git commit --fixup as you go and one git rebase -i --autosquash at the end is less work than any of this.

3. Explain why git reset --hard is recoverable when it discards commits but not when it discards uncommitted edits.

Solution

The difference is whether an object exists.

A commit discarded by reset --hard is still in the object database, immutable and intact (§3.1). It has merely stopped being reachable from any ref. The reflog recorded the branch's previous value (§4.10), so git reflog names it and git reset --hard 'main@{1}' restores it. Even without the reflog, git fsck --lost-found finds it, because unreachable objects survive at least two weeks (§15.5).

An uncommitted edit was never written to the object database at all. git add would have created a blob; git stash would have created a commit. Without either, the only copy was the bytes on disk, and --hard overwrote them. There is nothing for Git to find, and no Git command can recover it — an editor's local history or a filesystem snapshot is the only hope.

The practical rule: git add is a backup. Staging something, even work you are unsure about, puts a blob in the database that git fsck --lost-found can retrieve. It costs nothing.

4. A stash will not apply because the branch has moved on since you made it. What is the cleanest recovery, and what makes it work?

Solution 
git stash branch recovered

This creates a new branch at the commit the stash was originally made on, checks it out, applies the stash there — where it applies cleanly, because the tree is exactly the one it was taken from — and drops the entry. You then merge or rebase that branch normally, with §6's conflict tools rather than a half-applied stash.

What makes it possible is the structure from §7.7: a stash entry is a merge commit whose first parent is the commit you were on when you stashed. Git knows the original base, so it can reconstruct the situation precisely.

The general habit that avoids the problem: prefer git stash apply to pop so a failed application does not consume the entry, and prefer a real branch (git switch -c wip && git commit -m wip) for anything you will not resume within the hour.

5. Your team reverted a broken feature branch's merge on main. Six weeks later the branch is fixed and re-merged, and none of the feature's code appears. Explain and fix.

Solution

git revert -m 1 <merge> created a commit that undoes the content of the merge, but the merge commit itself is still in the graph (§7.5, §6.13). So the feature branch's commits remain ancestors of main, and Git correctly computes that a second merge has nothing new to bring in — the merge base already contains them. The merge succeeds, changes nothing, and the revert's deletion stands.

The fix is to revert the revert before merging again:

git revert <the-revert-commit>
git merge feature

The first command restores the original content; the second brings in whatever was fixed since.

If the branch was substantially rewritten in the meantime, the cleaner alternative is to abandon the old branch entirely: create a fresh branch from current main and cherry-pick or re-apply the work, so the new commits have no ancestry relationship to the reverted merge.

Git's own documentation covers this at length — git help revert links the revert-a-faulty-merge How-To — because the failure is invisible until someone asks where their feature went.

6. Give the command that returns a working tree to exactly the state of a fresh clone, and say what you might lose.

Solution 
git reset --hard
git clean -xfd

The first discards tracked modifications, staged and unstaged. The second removes untracked files (-f), untracked directories (-d) and ignored files (-x).

-x is the one to think about, because ignored files are exactly the ones that are not reproducible from the repository: .env files with local credentials, node_modules and virtualenvs that take minutes to rebuild, IDE settings, local database files, build caches. None of it is recoverable by any Git command, because none of it was ever in the object database.

So:

git clean -n -xd

first, every time. The dry run costs one keystroke and lists precisely what is about to disappear.

git clean -xfd is right in CI, where the checkout is disposable and stale build output causes real bugs. On a working machine, git clean -fd without -x — or -X, which removes only ignored files and keeps new source you have not staged — is usually what you actually meant.

SECTION 8

Rewriting History

Git's history is immutable, and Git's history is routinely rewritten. Both statements are true, and holding them together is the whole of this section.

§3 established that every object is named by the hash of its own bytes, and that a commit's parent ids are part of the bytes that get hashed. Nothing can be edited. What "rewriting history" actually means is: build new commits that resemble the old ones, then move a branch pointer to the new chain. The old commits do not change and do not immediately vanish — they simply stop being reachable from any branch, which is why §7.9's recovery works and why §15.5's grace period matters.

Once that is clear, everything in this section is one idea applied at different scales: --amend rewrites one commit, interactive rebase rewrites a handful, filter-repo rewrites all of them. The commands differ; the mechanism does not.

The reason to do any of it is that a commit history is a document, and first drafts are bad. The commits you make while working record how you found the answer — three false starts, a debugging print left in, a typo fixed two commits later. The commits you publish should record what the answer is. Rewriting is editing, and §8.11 says exactly where editing has to stop.

8.1 Rewriting Means Writing New Objects

The smallest possible demonstration:

git log --oneline -1
git rev-parse HEAD
git commit --amend -m 'add documentation'
git log --oneline -1
git rev-parse HEAD

1750af5 add docs
1750af5ea4ae385e8d12813bd144f14e8bc981f8
0933a46 add documentation
0933a46021623b27342b9935ef520ac366dbf62e

A different object id. Nothing was edited: git commit --amend read the old commit, built a new commit object with the same tree and the same parent but a new message, and pointed main at it (§3.7). Commit 1750af5 still exists in the object database — git cat-file -p 1750af5 prints it happily — it is simply unreferenced.

Now the consequence that governs everything else. Because a commit's content includes its parent ids, rewriting a commit necessarily rewrites every commit after it. Watch it happen across a rebase that only meant to change one thing:

before                       after
62811e8 initial commit  ->   62811e8 initial commit      unchanged
2c44fe7 add parser      ->   2c44fe7 add parser          unchanged
2cbcdd1 add tests       ->   95a96d6 add tests           NEW
47af6bb fix typo        ->   b378287 fix typo            NEW
0933a46 add docs        ->   4328140 add docs            NEW

The two commits before the change keep their ids. Everything from the changed commit onward is new. This is not Git being wasteful — it is the only possible behaviour for a hash-linked chain, and it is the mechanical reason why rewriting published history is a problem rather than a preference (§8.11).

8.2 --amend

git commit --amend replaces the tip commit. It is the right tool for exactly three situations, and it is worth naming them because it gets used for a fourth where --fixup is better.

git commit --amend
Edit the message of the commit you just made.
git commit --amend --no-edit
Fold the currently staged changes into the tip, keeping its message. The "I forgot a file" fix.
git commit --amend --author=…
Correct a wrong author — after committing with the wrong user.email, which happens on every freshly set-up machine.
git commit --amend --reset-author
Set the author to you and reset the author date. Use after cherry-picking or applying a patch you have substantially rewritten.

Two things --amend does that surprise people. It resets the committer date to now while leaving the author date alone (§3.6), so an amended commit can appear out of order in tools that sort by committer date. And it only touches the tip — if the commit you want to fix is three back, --amend is the wrong tool, and reaching for it means resetting first, which loses the intervening commits. §8.4 has the right answer.

8.3 The Interactive Rebase Todo List

git rebase -i <upstream> opens an editor containing a script: one line per commit, oldest first. Git then executes the script.

The editor is GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR if set, otherwise core.editor. Setting it to cat prints the todo list without doing anything, which is the fastest way to see what a rebase is about to do:

GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR='cat' git rebase -i HEAD~4

pick 2c44fe7 # add parser
pick 2cbcdd1 # add tests
pick 47af6bb # fix typo in parser
pick 1750af5 # add docs

# Rebase 62811e8..1750af5 onto 62811e8 (4 commands)
#
# Commands:
# p, pick <commit> = use commit
# r, reword <commit> = use commit, but edit the commit message
# e, edit <commit> = use commit, but stop for amending
# s, squash <commit> = use commit, but meld into previous commit
# f, fixup [-C | -c] <commit> = like "squash" but keep only the previous
#                    commit's log message, unless -C is used, in which case
#                    keep only this commit's message; -c is same as -C but
#                    opens the editor
# x, exec <command> = run command (the rest of the line) using shell
# b, break = stop here (continue rebase later with 'git rebase --continue')
# d, drop <commit> = remove commit
# l, label <label> = label current HEAD with a name
# t, reset <label> = reset HEAD to a label
# m, merge [-C <commit> | -c <commit>] <label> [# <oneline>]
#         create a merge commit using the original merge commit's
#         message (or the oneline, if no original merge commit was
#         specified); use -c <commit> to reword the commit message
# u, update-ref <ref> = track a placeholder for the <ref> to be updated
#                       to this position in the new commits. The <ref> is
#                       updated at the end of the rebase
#
# These lines can be re-ordered; they are executed from top to bottom.
#
# If you remove a line here THAT COMMIT WILL BE LOST.

Four things about this list matter more than the individual verbs.

The order is oldest-first, the reverse of git log. Every operation reads top to bottom, so "squash this into the one above" means "into the older one".

The subject after the id is a comment. In current Git the format is pick <id> # <subject>, with a # making the subject unambiguously inert — a commit message containing shell metacharacters or a leading verb cannot confuse the parser. Older documentation shows pick <id> <subject> without it; both are just display text and neither is read back.

Deleting a line deletes the commit. The warning at the bottom is literal. drop and deleting the line do the same thing; drop is better because it survives review.

update-ref is the modern piece. A update-ref refs/heads/other line in the todo makes the rebase move that branch too when it reaches that point. With --update-refs Git inserts those lines automatically, which is what makes rebasing a stack of dependent branches a single operation instead of five.

pick
Keep the commit as it is.
reword
Keep the change, open an editor for the message.
edit
Stop after applying it, with the commit as HEAD, so you can --amend or split it (§8.5).
squash
Combine into the previous commit; both messages are offered for editing.
fixup
Combine into the previous commit; discard this one's message.
drop
Discard the commit entirely.
exec
Run a shell command at this point (§8.7).
break
Stop here for no reason but your own inspection.

git rebase -i --root rebases from the very first commit, which is how you fix the initial commit — otherwise unreachable, because it has no parent to rebase onto.

8.4 squash, fixup and --autosquash

To squash commits — combine several into one — the mechanism is the squash and fixup verbs, and the common real workflow is not "reorganise ten commits". It is "I found a bug in the commit I made an hour ago, and I want the fix to be part of that commit rather than a separate 'fix typo' commit forever".

Doing it by hand means finding the commit, running an interactive rebase, moving your fix's line up next to it and changing pick to fixup. Git automates the whole thing:

git commit --fixup HEAD~2
git log --oneline -3

b1ca9c0 fixup! add tests
0933a46 add documentation
47af6bb fix typo in parser

--fixup <commit> makes an ordinary commit whose message is fixup! followed by the target's subject. That prefix is a machine-readable marker. Now:

GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR='cat' git rebase -i --autosquash HEAD~4

pick 2cbcdd1 # add tests
fixup b1ca9c0 # fixup! add tests # empty
pick 47af6bb # fix typo in parser
pick 0933a46 # add documentation

Git reordered the todo list itself. The fixup! commit was moved from the top of the list to sit directly after its target and its verb changed from pick to fixup. Running it produces a history in which the fix never existed as a separate commit:

git rebase -i --autosquash HEAD~4
git log --oneline

4328140 add documentation
b378287 fix typo in parser
95a96d6 add tests
2c44fe7 add parser
62811e8 initial commit

Four commits, no fixup!. Note again that add tests and everything after it got new ids, while add parser and initial commit did not.

--fixup <c>
Fold the change in, keep <c>'s message. The usual case.
--fixup=amend:<c>
Fold in the change and replace the message — an amend of an older commit, done properly.
--fixup=reword:<c>
Change only <c>'s message, staging nothing.
git commit --squash <c>
Fold in and combine both messages, offering them for editing.

Set rebase.autoSquash = true and every interactive rebase honours the markers without the flag. Combined with rebase.autoStash (§8.7) this makes the whole loop — notice a problem, fix it, fold it in — a two-command habit rather than an operation.

Finding the right target is the last friction, and git commit --fixup accepts any revision expression, so git log -1 --format=%H -- path/to/file piped in, or simply :/some phrase (§9.2), usually beats scrolling.

git commit --squash <commit> is the sibling of --fixup: it marks the new commit squash! instead of fixup!, so the rebase keeps both messages and opens an editor to combine them. Use --fixup when the change needs no explanation and git commit --squash when it does.

To squash commits with squash rather than fixup is to keep both messages; either way, the point of choosing to squash commits at all is that the reader of the history should see the change, not the process of arriving at it.

8.5 Reordering, Dropping and Splitting

All three of these are edits to the todo list, and the reason they belong together is that each is one line moved, deleted or changed. To reorder commits, move a line; to drop a commit, delete one; to split a commit, change its verb to edit and do the work when the rebase stops.

Reordering commits is moving lines in the todo list. It works when the commits are independent and produces a conflict when they are not, which is Git correctly telling you the order was load-bearing.

Dropping a commit is the drop verb, or deleting the line. The same caveat applies: dropping a commit that a later one depends on gives you a conflict at that later commit, not a silent bad result.

Splitting a commit into two is the operation with a real recipe, and it is worth memorising because there is no single command for it. Mark the commit edit, and when the rebase stops:

git reset HEAD^
git add -p
git commit -m 'first half'
git commit -am 'second half'
git rebase --continue

That recipe is worth reading closely, because it is the one that is not obvious. git reset HEAD^ is a mixed reset (§7.3): it moves the branch back one commit and updates the index to match, but leaves the working tree exactly as it was. So all of the commit's changes are now present and unstaged, and git add -p lets you stage them a hunk at a time into as many commits as you like. git rebase --continue then replays the rest.

The same recipe with git add -p replaced by git add <path> splits by file, which is the more common need — one commit that touched the parser and the tests becomes two.

In summary: to reorder commits, move todo lines; to drop a commit, delete or drop one; and to split a commit, mark it edit and use the reset recipe. Reorder commits and drop a commit are one keystroke each; split a commit is the only one with a procedure.

8.6 --onto: Moving a Range

git rebase <upstream> replays "commits on my branch that are not on <upstream>". That default is right most of the time and wrong in one important case: a branch built on another branch.

git log --graph --oneline --all

* f065644 client: retry logic
* f74be24 client: fetch helper
* cc21eb1 server: connection pool
* 8517b65 server: db layer
| * 733f833 main: unrelated work
|/
* 26b74aa main: base

client was branched from server. If the client work is ready and the server work is not, a plain git rebase main client would drag both server commits along, because they too are absent from main.

git rebase --onto main server client
git log --graph --oneline --all

* 1da9945 client: retry logic
* c6bf92f client: fetch helper
* 733f833 main: unrelated work
| * cc21eb1 server: connection pool
| * 8517b65 server: db layer
|/
* 26b74aa main: base

Exactly the two client commits moved. Read the three arguments as a sentence:

--onto main
Put them here.
server
Take everything after this.
client
…up to this, and move the branch.

The same shape solves two other problems. git rebase --onto HEAD~3 HEAD~2 drops the third-from-last commit without an editor. And git rebase --onto main main~5 topic is the rescue for a branch accidentally started from an old commit.

--onto also works with an explicit commit id as the destination, which is how you move a branch onto a commit that no ref points at any more — for example one you recovered from the reflog (§7.9).

8.7 exec, break and --autostash

The exec command runs a shell command at a point in the todo list, and --exec <cmd> inserts an exec line after every commit:

git rebase -i --exec 'git log --oneline -1' HEAD~2

Executing: git log --oneline -1
b378287 fix typo in parser
Executing: git log --oneline -1
4328140 add documentation
Successfully rebased and updated refs/heads/main.

Replace the echo with your test command and this becomes the single most valuable thing in the section: a check that every commit in the series builds and passes, not just the tip. A non-zero exit stops the rebase at that commit with the tree in place, so you fix it, git commit --amend, and git rebase --continue. Bisecting (§9.10) depends on every commit being buildable, and this is how you make that true before publishing rather than discovering it six months later.

break is exec's manual cousin: it stops the rebase at that point with no command, so you can look around.

git rebase --autostash handles the most annoying refusal in Git:

error: cannot rebase: You have unstaged changes.

With rebase.autoStash = true — or the flag — Git stashes the working tree, rebases, and pops the stash afterwards. It is safe: if the pop conflicts, the stash is kept rather than discarded, and Git tells you. There is no good reason not to set it.

Three related settings that belong in the same breath:

git config --global rebase.autoStash true
git config --global rebase.autoSquash true
git config --global rebase.updateRefs true

The exec command is also available by hand in the todo list, one line at a time, which is how you run a check after a particular commit rather than after all of them. And git rebase --autostash is worth setting permanently with rebase.autoStash = true — it is the difference between a rebase that just works and one that refuses until you have tidied up.

8.8 range-diff: Reviewing a Rewrite

After a rebase you have two versions of a series and no obvious way to check that you changed only what you meant to. git diff between the old tip and the new one is useless — it shows the difference between two trees, which will be empty for a pure reordering and misleading for anything else.

git range-diff compares two series of commits, matching them up by similarity:

git range-diff "$BASE..topic-v1" "$BASE..topic"

1:  1cdb165 = 1:  1cdb165 add a
2:  dff19bc ! 2:  d0af515 add b
    @@ Metadata
     Author: Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com>

      ## Commit message ##
    -    add b
    +    add b (renamed)

      ## b.txt (new) ##
     @@
3:  ad7d538 < -:  ------- add c
-:  ------- > 3:  6030feb tweak a

Four markers, and between them they answer every question you have about a rewrite:

=
Identical in both series. Nothing to look at.
!
Matched, but changed — and the diff below shows the diff of the diffs, including message changes. Here only the subject moved.
<
Present in the old series, gone from the new. A dropped commit.
>
New in the second series.

The < line is the one that earns the command. A commit silently lost during a conflicted rebase — resolved by taking "ours" at the wrong moment — leaves a history that looks entirely plausible and is missing a feature. range-diff is the only tool that shows it, and running it after any non-trivial rebase should be a habit. It is also how a reviewer compares version 2 of a patch series against version 1, which is why forges show "compare with previous version" and why git format-patch --range-diff exists for the email workflow (§12.8).

Tag or branch the pre-rewrite tip before you start — git branch topic-v1 costs nothing and is what makes range-diff possible afterwards. Failing that, the reflog (§7.9) has the old tip.

git format-patch produces the same commit-by-commit view as a file per commit (§12.8), and git format-patch --range-diff=<old> embeds exactly the comparison above into the cover letter — which is how a patch series sent by email tells its reviewers what changed since the last round. A patch series reviewed twice needs that comparison as much as a force-pushed branch does.

8.9 Force Pushing Safely

A rewritten branch cannot fast-forward, so publishing it requires force. There are three levels, and the difference between them is measured here rather than asserted.

Alice and Bob both clone. Alice amends and force-pushes:

git push --force-with-lease origin main

 + dae90f9...f449c1e main -> main (forced update)

Bob, who has not fetched, tries the same:

git push --force-with-lease origin main

 ! [rejected]        main -> main (stale info)

This is --force-with-lease working. It sends the value Bob's origin/main holds as the expected old value; the server's ref no longer matches, so it refuses. Plain --force sends "whatever is there" and would have destroyed Alice's push without comment.

Now the part that is routinely got wrong. Bob runs git fetch — updating origin/main without looking at it — and tries again:

git fetch origin
git push --force-with-lease origin main

 + f449c1e...da80897 main -> main (forced update)

It succeeded, and Alice's work is gone. The lease is a promise about what origin/main said, and Bob's blind fetch made that promise true again. Any command that fetches in the background — an IDE, a prompt, a git pull in another window — defeats --force-with-lease in exactly this way.

The fix is a second condition:

git push --force-with-lease --force-if-includes origin main

 ! [rejected]        main -> main (remote ref updated since checkout)
error: failed to push some refs to '…'
hint: Updates were rejected because the tip of the remote-tracking branch has
hint: been updated since the last checkout. If you want to integrate the
hint: remote changes, use 'git pull' before pushing again.

--force-if-includes asks a stronger question: is the remote's current tip reachable from the reflog of the branch I am pushing? In other words, did I ever actually have that commit locally, at some point, on this branch? A background fetch updates origin/main but does not put that commit into Bob's branch reflog, so the check fails and the push is refused.

--force
Overwrite unconditionally. Never type this on a shared branch.
--force-with-lease
Overwrite only if the remote is where my remote-tracking ref says. Defeated by a background fetch.
…--force-if-includes
Additionally require that I have actually had the remote's tip on this branch. This is the one to use.
git config --global push.useForceIfIncludes true

That makes the third form the behaviour of --force-with-lease everywhere, which is the setting to adopt. Note that --force-if-includes is meaningless without --force-with-lease and Git will tell you so.

The mechanism is worth naming precisely: --force-with-lease compares against your remote-tracking branch, which is a local cache (§10.2). Any command that updates that remote-tracking branch — including a fetch you did not think about — satisfies the lease.

8.10 filter-repo: Rewriting Everything

Interactive rebase rewrites a handful of commits you can see. Sometimes you have to rewrite all of them: a secret was committed, a 400 MB binary is bloating every clone (§15.2), a repository must be split into two, or an author's email must be corrected across a decade.

git filter-branch was the historical tool. It is now actively discouraged by Git's own documentation — it is dangerously slow (a shell process per commit per file), and its defaults produce subtly wrong results often enough that the manual page opens with a warning and a recommendation to use something else. That something else is git-filter-repo, a separate single-file Python program.

A secret in history looks like this. It was committed, and then removed — which does nothing:

ls -a
git log --oneline --all -- .env
git show 'HEAD~2:.env'

.  ..  .git  app.py
a424f3f remove .env (oops)
49f162a add config
AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG

The file is absent from the working tree and fully present in the object database, in every clone anyone has made. Remove it from every commit:

git filter-repo --path .env --invert-paths --force

Parsed 4 commits
HEAD is now at f959ee2 feature work

New history written in 0.29 seconds; now repacking/cleaning...
Repacking your repo and cleaning out old unneeded objects
Completely finished after 0.62 seconds.

git log --oneline
git show 'HEAD~1:.env'

f959ee2 feature work
cf58480 initial
fatal: path '.env' does not exist in 'HEAD~1'

Two things to notice. The history went from four commits to two: with .env gone, both add config and remove .env (oops) became empty, and filter-repo pruned them. And every remaining commit has a new id, because the very first affected commit changed.

filter-repo leaves a record of what it did:

head -3 .git/filter-repo/commit-map

old                                      new
49f162ab0ec136311867e62a56145de2e0dc4ad2 0000000000000000000000000000000000000000
75cd6f9bf04669be64823c169bbe41d1967175ea f959ee2c123f00c6a51af8dd85e3b36e1796167e

All-zeros in the new column means the commit was dropped. That map is how you translate a commit id someone quoted in an issue tracker into its replacement.

It also refuses to run on a repository that is not a fresh clone:

Aborting: Refusing to destructively overwrite repo history since
this does not look like a fresh clone.
  (refs/heads/main exists, but refs/remotes/origin/main not found)
Please operate on a fresh clone instead.  If you want to proceed
anyway, use --force.

That refusal is doing you a favour. On a fresh clone, the original is still safe on the server if the rewrite goes wrong.

--path <p> --invert-paths
Remove a path from all history. Add --path repeatedly for several.
--path <p>
Keep only that path — the way to extract a subdirectory into its own repository.
--path-rename old:new
Rename a path throughout history.
--subdirectory-filter <d>
Make a subdirectory the new repository root.
--replace-text <file>
Replace matching strings in every blob — for a secret embedded in a file you must keep.
--mailmap <file>
Rewrite author and committer identities from a mailmap.
--strip-blobs-bigger-than 10M
Remove every large blob. The blunt fix for a bloated repository.

git replay, introduced in Git 2.44 and still marked experimental, is a related but different tool: it replays commits onto a new base entirely inside the object database, touching no working tree and no index, and printing the ref updates it would make rather than performing them. It is built for server-side and automated rebasing — a forge applying a pull request, or a script rebasing a thousand branches — where checking anything out would be absurd.

The operation of removing a secret or a large file from every commit is usually described as purging history, and the phrase is worth being careful with: you can purge history in your repository, and every other copy of it stays exactly as it was until someone acts.

Rewriting is only half the job. Afterwards, on the server and on every clone:

  1. Force-push every branch and tag (git push --force --all and --tags), with the whole team warned in advance and no open work outstanding.
  2. Expire and prune locally — the old objects are still on disk under the grace period (§15.5), so run git reflog expire --expire=now --all followed by git gc --prune=now --aggressive.
  3. Have everyone re-clone. A colleague who merges their old branch into the rewritten history reintroduces every removed object.
  4. Rotate the secret. This is the step people skip, and it is the only one that actually matters. The credential was in a clone, in a CI cache, in a forge's object store and quite possibly in a fork you cannot reach. Treat the rewrite as tidying up after a leak, never as containing one.

One more distinction inside this family. --path-rename old:new renames a path throughout history, which is the tool for a repository whose layout was wrong from the first commit. And a leaked secret is the case where none of this is sufficient on its own: git filter-branch, git filter-repo and git replay all rewrite your copy, and the credential is already elsewhere.

Two more things --path-rename and friends share with everything else here: author and committer are both preserved by filter-repo unless you ask otherwise, so a rewrite does not silently reassign authorship, and the author and committer dates are preserved too. To purge history is to change what objects exist, not who is recorded as having made them.

8.11 The Rule About Published History

The rule is usually stated as "never rebase public commits". The rule is right; the reason is worth having in mechanical terms, because then you can tell which situations it actually covers.

Suppose you rewrite main and force-push. A colleague's repository still has the old chain, and their main still points at it. When they fetch, Git brings the new chain in alongside. Now their branch and origin/main have diverged, with no common ancestor after the rewrite point. If they run git pull, Git merges the two — producing a history containing both the old and the new versions of every rewritten commit, with every change duplicated. Push that back and the rewrite is undone and doubled.

Nothing here is Git behaving badly. Every step is correct; the premise — that the two histories describe the same work — is the thing that stopped being true.

Always safe
Commits that exist only on your machine. Amend, squash and reorder as much as you like — this is the whole point of local commits.
Normally safe
Your own pushed feature branch that nobody else has based work on. This is the standard "rebase before merge" workflow, and force-pushing your own PR branch is expected. Use §8.9's flags.
Needs coordination
A shared branch two or three people are on. Possible, with everyone told when it will happen and what to run afterwards (git fetch && git reset --hard origin/<branch> for anyone with no local work).
Do not
main in a repository with more than a handful of contributors, or anything that has been tagged for release. The cost is everyone's time, not yours.

The server-side enforcement is receive.denyNonFastForwards (§11.8) or a forge's protected-branch setting, and turning it on for main is the right default: it converts a mistake into an error message.

The shorthand for the rule is that you must not rebase published commits — a rebase published to a shared branch is a rebase everyone else has to recover from. "Published" here means precisely one thing: someone else's repository contains those object ids.

8.12 When the Rebase Goes Wrong

Every rebase is interruptible, and the three ways out are worth knowing before you need them.

git rebase --continue
Proceed after resolving a conflict. Requires the conflict staged (§6.4).
git rebase --skip
Discard the commit being applied and move on. Correct when the change is already present upstream — and a data loss bug when used to make a conflict go away.
git rebase --abort
Put everything back exactly as it was. Always available while the rebase is in progress.

git status during a rebase is unusually helpful, naming the branch, the commit being applied, and the exact commands available. Read it rather than guessing.

Once a rebase has finished badly, --abort is no longer offered, and the tool is the reflog:

git reflog
git reset --hard 'main@{1}'

Every step of the rebase wrote a reflog entry, and the entry immediately before the rebase started is the pre-rebase tip (§7.9). Nothing was lost: the old commits are unreachable but present, and will be for at least two weeks (§15.5).

The habit that makes all of this cheap is one command before you start:

git branch backup-before-rebase

It costs one ref, it makes git range-diff (§8.8) possible afterwards, and git branch -D deletes it when you are satisfied. Every experienced Git user has this reflex, and it comes from having once needed it.

git rebase --abort is the one to remember, because git rebase --abort is always safe and always available while the rebase is running. git rebase --skip is the one to be careful with: git rebase --skip discards the commit being applied, which is right when it is already upstream and is data loss otherwise.

Exercises

1. You made five commits. The third one has a bug. Write the sequence that folds a fix into it, without touching commits four and five.

Solution 
# fix the bug in the working tree, then:
git add -p
git commit --fixup HEAD~2
git rebase -i --autosquash HEAD~4

HEAD~2 is the third-from-last commit (§9.4). --fixup marks the new commit; --autosquash reorders it next to its target and sets its verb to fixup, so the editor opens with the job already done — you just save.

Commits four and five will get new object ids, because their ancestor changed (§8.1). Their content and messages are untouched, which is what "without touching" means in practice. If either has been used as a reference elsewhere, that is the cost.

With rebase.autoSquash = true set globally, the last line is git rebase -i HEAD~4.

2. Explain why git diff old-tip new-tip is the wrong tool for reviewing a rebase, and what to use instead.

Solution

git diff compares two trees — the final state of the project at two commits. A rebase that reorders commits, rewords messages, or splits one commit into two produces an identical final tree, so git diff shows nothing while a great deal changed. Conversely, a rebase onto a moved upstream produces a diff dominated by everything upstream did, in which your own changes are invisible.

git range-diff <base>..<old-tip> <newbase>..<new-tip> compares the two series commit by commit, matching them by content similarity and showing a diff of each pair's diffs plus their messages (§8.8). Its four markers are the whole answer: = unchanged, ! changed (with the difference shown), < present before and now gone, > newly added.

The < marker is the reason to run it. A commit silently dropped during a conflicted rebase leaves a plausible-looking history with a feature missing, and nothing else in Git will point at it.

3. A secret was committed six months ago and removed the next day. Someone says "it's fine, the file isn't in the repo any more." Give the two commands that disprove that, and the four steps to actually deal with it.

Solution

Disproof:

git log --oneline --all -- path/to/.env      # the commits that touched it
git show <that-commit>:path/to/.env          # its full contents, printed

The blob is still in the object database and in every clone, because the commit that added it is still in history (§3.1, §15.2).

Four steps, in this order of importance:

  1. Rotate the credential. First and non-negotiable. It has been in every clone, every CI cache, and possibly a fork you cannot reach. Nothing below reduces that exposure.
  2. Rewrite history on a fresh clone: git filter-repo --path path/to/.env --invert-paths. Use --replace-text instead if the secret is inside a file you need to keep.
  3. Force-push all refs, having warned everyone, then have every clone re-cloned. A colleague merging an old branch back in restores the object.
  4. Expire and prune everywhere: git reflog expire --expire=now --all && git gc --prune=now. Ask your host to run their own GC — the object may live in their storage until they do, and on some forges a commit remains reachable by URL indefinitely.

Prevention is .gitignore plus a pre-commit hook that greps for credential patterns (§13.8), because step 1 is expensive every time.

4. --force-with-lease is described in a team wiki as making force-push safe. Construct the case where it is not, and give the flag that closes it.

Solution

The lease is a claim about your remote-tracking ref, not about what you have seen.

  1. Alice force-pushes a rewritten main.
  2. Bob's editor, shell prompt, or a git fetch in another window updates his origin/main in the background. Bob never looks at it.
  3. Bob runs git push --force-with-lease. Git compares his origin/main — now Alice's commit — with the server's origin/main — also Alice's commit. They match. The push succeeds and Alice's work is gone.

Measured, this is exactly what happens: the same push is rejected with (stale info) before the fetch and accepted with (forced update) after it.

The fix is --force-if-includes, which additionally requires that the remote's current tip appears in the reflog of the branch being pushed — that you actually had that commit on this branch, not merely that a fetch wrote it into a tracking ref. With it, the push is rejected with (remote ref updated since checkout).

git config --global push.useForceIfIncludes true

That makes it the default behaviour of --force-with-lease everywhere.

5. Your branch feature was accidentally created from a commit five back on main instead of from main. Fix it without recreating the branch by hand.

Solution 
git rebase --onto main main~5 feature

Read it as the sentence from §8.6: put them onto main, take everything after main~5, up to feature.

The plain git rebase main feature would appear to work but is subtly different: its range is "commits on feature not on main", which is the same set only if none of the five intervening commits are also on your branch. When main has moved on since, the plain form can pick up commits you did not intend. --onto states the boundary explicitly, which is why it is the right tool whenever the branch point is not what you would guess.

Before running it: git branch feature-backup. Afterwards: git range-diff feature-backup...feature to confirm every commit came across and none changed unexpectedly.

6. Why does git commit --amend on a commit that is three back require a different tool, and what happens if you try to do it with reset instead?

Solution

--amend replaces the commit HEAD points at. It has no way to address an older one, because replacing a commit means rewriting every descendant (§8.1) and --amend does not rewrite descendants — it only moves the branch.

The reset approach — git reset --soft HEAD~3, amend, then recommit — does work, and it destroys the two commits in between: they become one lump combined with your fix, losing their messages and their separation. That is fine if that was the goal and a silent loss if it was not.

The right tools:

  • git commit --fixup=amend:<commit> then git rebase -i --autosquash, which folds the change in and lets you change that commit's message (§8.4).
  • Or git rebase -i with the target marked edit, then git commit --amend, then git rebase --continue.

Both rewrite the target and faithfully replay the descendants onto it, giving them new ids and unchanged content.

SECTION 9

Revision Selection and History Investigation

Every Git command that takes a commit takes an expression, and the expression language is large, orthogonal and almost never taught. That is a shame, because it is the difference between using Git as a set of memorised recipes and using it as a query tool over a graph you understand.

This section is in two halves. The first is the language: how to name one commit, a set of commits, a tree, a blob, or a path inside a commit. The second is the investigative commands built on it — log's filters, grep, bisect, blame — which are where the language pays off, because a question like "which commit introduced this string, and who wrote the line before it" is one command in each direction once you can address things precisely.

The whole language is documented in git help revisions, which is the single most useful manual page Git ships and the one to reread once a year.

9.1 Naming a Single Commit

Six ways to name one commit, in rough order of how often you will use them:

HEAD
Where you are (§4.3).
<branch> / <tag>
Resolved by the six rules of §4.5. Tags beat branches.
f78ce2f
Any unambiguous prefix of the object id, four characters or more.
HEAD~3, HEAD^2
An ancestry reference (§9.4).
main@{2}
Reflog position or time (§4.10).
:/fix the parser
The most recent commit whose message contains that text.

An abbreviated SHA — any unambiguous prefix — is what you will see and type most of the time, and the length is not fixed. In the Git repository:

git log --oneline -1
git rev-parse --short HEAD
git rev-parse --verify f78ce2f

f78ce2f7b6 The 19th batch
f78ce2f7b6
f78ce2f7b6df702f93d40b85d6bda92a3f65da79

Ten characters, not the seven everyone quotes. git log --abbrev-commit (which --oneline implies) and git rev-parse --short both use this length, and core.abbrev defaults to auto, which picks a length from the repository's object count with margin for growth — seven digits is still unambiguous here, as the successful --verify shows, but Git chose ten to stay safe as the repository grows. On a small repository you will see seven; on a very large one, twelve.

Two consequences. Never hard-code a hash length in a regex — [0-9a-f]{40} is wrong for SHA-256 repositories (§3.11) and [0-9a-f]{7} is wrong for abbreviations. And git rev-parse --verify is the command for scripts: plain git rev-parse foo echoes foo back unchanged when it cannot resolve it, on the theory that it might be a filename, which turns a typo into a confusing failure three commands later.

:/text is underused and excellent:

git log --oneline -1 ':/Sync with'

6673acef38 Sync with 'master'

It searches backwards from HEAD for the first commit whose message matches, which beats scrolling through git log to find a commit you half-remember. :/^fix anchors it; <rev>^{/text} searches back from a specific starting point.

Three settings and flags govern how long an abbreviated SHA is. core.abbrev sets the default length (or auto); --abbrev-commit asks a git log to use it; and --abbrev=<n> overrides it for one command. An ancestry reference such as HEAD~3 is resolved before any of this applies, so the two mechanisms compose freely.

9.2 rev-parse and the Expression Language

git rev-parse turns any expression into an object id, and it is the command to reach for when you want to know what an expression means rather than what it does.

The suffixes are where the language gets interesting, because they let you address objects other than commits:

<rev>^{tree}
The tree that commit points at. Also ^{commit}, ^{blob}, ^{tag} — the peel operation of §3.8, which follows tag objects until it reaches the requested type.
<rev>:<path>
The blob or tree at that path in that commit. git show HEAD~5:src/app.py prints a file as it was, without checking anything out.
:<path>
The index entry for a path.
:<n>:<path>
Merge stage n during a conflict (§6.4).
<rev>^{/text}
Search backwards from <rev> for a message match.
@{u} / @{upstream}
The upstream of the current branch (§10.5).
@{push}
Where git push would send it — different from @{u} in a triangular workflow.
@
A bare @ is shorthand for HEAD.
git rev-parse 'HEAD^{tree}' 'HEAD:README.md'
git rev-parse --abbrev-ref '@{u}'

d9737b932efa2e9cd623781aa371f52f5d90807e
46489b0971d04d02c1ba3eea5cd5c134e60c4f77
origin/master

Two practical notes. Quote expressions containing {, }, ^ or ~ — { and } are shell metacharacters and ^ is special in some shells; the failure modes are obscure and the habit is cheap.
