And --abbrev-ref prints a ref's name rather than its id, which is what you want when the answer is going into a message or a filename.

rev-parse also answers questions about the repository itself, which is how scripts orient themselves:

--show-toplevel
Absolute path of the working tree root.
--git-dir
Path to .git — which in a linked worktree is not what you expect (§14.8).
--is-inside-work-tree
true/false. The standard guard at the top of a Git-aware script.
--abbrev-ref HEAD
The current branch name, or HEAD when detached.
--show-object-format
sha1 or sha256 (§3.11).
--show-ref-format
files or reftable (§4.9).

One more index-related expression completes the set: :<n>:<path> addresses a merge stage during a conflict, and it is the only way to get at the base and the two sides as separate blobs (§6.4). A merge stage exists only while a merge is unresolved, so these expressions fail with "path ... is unmerged" at any other time.

9.3 When a Name Is Ambiguous

§4.5 gave the six-rule precedence order. The practical consequences are worth restating here because they bite in this section's commands specifically.

A bare name that is both a tag and a branch resolves to the tag, with a warning on stderr that a script will not see. A bare name that is also a filename produces the "ambiguous argument" error, which is why -- exists:

git log -- main
git log main --

The first is "the history of the file main"; the second is "the history of the ref main". Whenever a path and a ref could collide — and test, build, docs and main all can — put -- in explicitly. In scripts, always.

9.4 Ancestry: ^ and ~

An ancestry reference names a commit relative to another one. Two suffixes — caret notation and tilde notation — and the difference between them only matters at merge commits.

<rev>^
The first parent.
<rev>^2
The second parent — only a merge commit has one. This is the tip of the branch that was merged in (§3.6).
<rev>~
Identical to ^: the first parent.
<rev>~3
Follow the first parent three times. Equivalent to ^^^.

So caret notation chooses which parent and tilde notation chooses how far back along first parents. They compose: HEAD~3^2 is "go back three commits, then take the second parent of that one".

git rev-parse --verify 'HEAD^2'

fatal: Needed a single revision

on an ordinary commit, because there is no second parent. That error is a useful test: git rev-parse -q --verify "$c^2" >/dev/null succeeds exactly when $c is a merge.

The one that catches people: HEAD^^ is not HEAD^2. The first is two steps back; the second is the other parent of one commit. And in cmd.exe, ^ is an escape character, so HEAD^ must be written HEAD^^ — which is why so much Windows-facing documentation uses ~ exclusively. Using ~ for distance and ^n only for choosing a merge parent avoids the whole problem.

9.5 Ranges

A range — a revision range — names a set of commits. There is one core idea: the double dot form A..B means reachable from B, but not from A.

git rev-list --count v2.50.0..v2.51.0
git rev-list --count v2.51.0..v2.50.0

758
0

758 commits went into the release; the reverse is empty because v2.50.0 is an ancestor of v2.51.0, so there is nothing in it that the later tag lacks. A range is not a subtraction of two lists, it is a graph reachability query, and that asymmetry is the whole point.

A..B
Reachable from B, not from A. The workhorse.
A...B
The triple dot form: the symmetric difference, reachable from either but not both. What you want when the two have genuinely diverged.
^A B
Exactly the same as A..B, written as a set expression.
B --not A
The same again. This form takes many arguments, so git log B C --not A D is expressible and .. is not.
..B
HEAD is assumed on the missing side. git log origin/main.. is "what I have that the remote does not".
--all
Every ref as a starting point.

Three range idioms cover most daily use:

git log --oneline '@{u}..'         # what I am about to push
git log --oneline '..@{u}'         # what I am about to pull
git log --oneline --left-right main...feature   # how the two diverged

--left-right marks each commit with < for the left side and > for the right:

> c44beea485 Git 2.51
> e5ab6b3e5a Merge tag 'l10n-2.51.0-2' of https://github.com/git-l10n/git-po
> ba8bef458c cmake: accommodate for `UNIT_TEST_SOURCES`

and git rev-list --count --left-right A...B prints the two counts as a tab-separated pair, which is exactly the "ahead 3, behind 12" that git status reports (§10.6).

Note that A...B means different things in git log and git diff. In git log it is the symmetric difference of two commit sets. In git diff it means "diff B against the merge base of A and B" — the change your branch introduced, ignoring what happened on main meanwhile. Both are useful, both are documented, and they are genuinely different operations that share a spelling.

The mnemonic worth keeping: a revision range with the double dot form is a subtraction — everything in the right side that is not in the left — and the triple dot form is a symmetric difference. The double dot form is what you want nine times in ten; the triple dot form is for when both sides have moved.

9.6 Shaping git log

git log has three independent knobs: which commits, in what order, displayed how. Mixing them up is what makes its manual page intimidating.

Selection
A range (§9.5), -n, --since/--until, --author, --grep, --no-merges, --merges, a pathspec after --, and the content filters of §9.8.
Traversal
--first-parent (§9.7), --topo-order, --date-order, --reverse, --ancestry-path.
Display
--oneline, --graph, --stat, -p, --format=, --decorate, --name-status.

--format deserves attention because it turns git log into a report generator. Each format placeholder begins with %, and they are the same ones git for-each-ref uses:

git log --format='%h %ad %an %s' --date=short -5

%h abbreviated id, %H full, %an/%ae author name and email, %cn/%ce committer, %ad/%cd dates, %s subject, %b body, %d ref decorations, %G? signature status (§11.12). --date= takes short, iso, relative, format:%Y-%m and more.

Two traversal flags are worth knowing beyond the obvious. --topo-order guarantees that no commit is shown before its descendants, which matters because the default ordering is by commit date and commit dates lie — a rebased or imported commit can carry a date from years ago and appear in the wrong place. --ancestry-path A..B restricts to commits that are both descendants of A and ancestors of B, which is how you answer "how did this change reach that release" without seeing everything else merged in the meantime.

Every format placeholder is documented under "PRETTY FORMATS" in git help log, and a format placeholder written with %C(auto) turns on the same colouring Git uses by default, which is what makes a custom format still readable at a glance.

9.7 --first-parent

§3.6 established that a merge commit's parents are ordered: the first is the branch you were on, the second is what you merged in. --first-parent walks only the first, which collapses each merged branch to the single merge commit that brought it in.

git rev-list --count v2.50.0..v2.51.0
git rev-list --count v2.50.0..v2.51.0 --first-parent

758
161

The same release, two honest answers. 758 is every commit anyone wrote; 161 is the number of things that were integrated into the trunk. For a changelog, a release note, or "what changed", the second number is almost always the one you want, and the first is noise from other people's work-in-progress.

This is the strongest practical argument for merging with --no-ff (§6.2): it makes --first-parent meaningful. In a repository where everything is squashed or fast-forwarded, first-parent history and full history are the same thing and the distinction is lost.

git log --first-parent --oneline is worth setting up as an alias. git blame --first-parent and git bisect --first-parent exist too, the latter being useful when you want to identify the merge that broke something rather than the individual commit inside it.

9.8 Finding a Change: -S, -G, -L

"Where did this string come from" is answered by pickaxe search, and the two forms answer subtly different questions. Take a file whose TIMEOUT constant is introduced, changed twice, and finally inlined:

git log --oneline -S 'TIMEOUT'

3a439e8 inline the constant
2c7c827 initial: fetch with a timeout

git log --oneline -G 'TIMEOUT'

3a439e8 inline the constant
f1b0054 lower the timeout to 5
cf31144 raise the timeout to 60
2c7c827 initial: fetch with a timeout
-S
Commits where the number of occurrences of the string changed. Two here: the one that introduced TIMEOUT and the one that removed it. The two commits that changed its value touched lines containing it but added and removed one occurrence each, so the count was unchanged and they are correctly omitted.
-G
Commits whose diff contains an added or removed line matching the regex. All four.

-S is the one to reach for when asking "who added this, and who deleted it" — it cuts straight through a decade of noise. -G is for "every commit that ever touched this". -S takes a literal string unless you add --pickaxe-regex; -G is always a regex.

Two display commands belong beside these. git diff --word-diff colours changes within a line rather than marking whole lines, which is what makes a prose or a minified-file diff readable at all. And git difftool opens each change in a configured external viewer instead of printing it, with -y skipping the per-file prompt.

-L traces a range of lines or a function through history:

git log --oneline -L :fetch:app.py

3a439e8 inline the constant

diff --git a/app.py b/app.py
--- a/app.py
+++ b/app.py
@@ -4,4 +3,4 @@
 def fetch(url):
-    return get(url, timeout=TIMEOUT)
+    return get(url, timeout=5)

9ed231b add fetch_all
...

-L :<funcname>:<file> finds the function by name and follows it as it moves and changes; -L 40,60:<file> takes explicit line numbers. Git tracks the range across edits, so a function that drifted from line 40 to line 300 is still followed. The function-name form uses the same language detection as .gitattributes' diff=<driver> (§13.9), so teaching Git your language's function pattern improves both.

Finally, --follow continues a single file's history across renames — necessary because §3.9 established that renames are not recorded and must be detected:

git log --follow --oneline -- src/parser.py

It works on exactly one path, it is a heuristic, and a rename that also rewrote most of the file will break the chain. When it does, -S on a distinctive string from the file finds the other side.

Pickaxe search is the name for both -S and -G together, and the distinction to remember is that pickaxe search with -S counts occurrences while -G matches the diff text. Neither is a substitute for git difftool, which opens each change in an external viewer rather than filtering which changes you see — git difftool -y --tool=meld on a range is the right tool when the diff is too large to read in a terminal.

9.9 git grep

git grep searches tracked content. That sounds like a small distinction from grep -r and is not.

git grep -n 'trace2_region_enter' -- '*.c'

builtin/fetch.c:1445:		trace2_region_enter("fetch", "fetch_refs", the_repository);
builtin/fetch.c:1454:	trace2_region_enter("fetch", "consume_refs", the_repository);
builtin/fetch.c:1999:		trace2_region_enter("fetch", "remote_refs", the_repository);
builtin/fetch.c:2790:		trace2_region_enter("fetch", "negotiate-only", the_repository);

Four properties earn it its place:

Tracked files only
Build output, node_modules, virtualenvs and everything else in .gitignore are skipped automatically — no --exclude-dir incantation. An untracked file containing the term is simply not found, which is usually what you meant.
Any revision
git grep <pattern> <rev> searches a commit's tree without checking anything out: git grep -c 'trace2_region_enter' v2.30.0 -- trace2.c answers "how many were there in 2.30" in one command.
Pathspecs
The same -- '*.c' magic-pathspec language every other Git command takes, including ':(exclude)test/'.
Code-aware output
-p prints the enclosing function for each hit, git grep --count (-c) counts matches per file, -l lists filenames, -W prints the whole enclosing block.

It is not automatically faster than grep -r on a warm cache — on the Git repository the two are within a few tenths of a second of each other. The win is correctness of scope and the ability to address history, not raw speed.

git grep -n is worth making a reflex — line numbers turn a list of matches into a list of places you can jump to — and git grep --count collapses the same search to one line per file, which is the fastest way to see where a symbol is concentrated before reading anything.

9.10 git bisect

Bisect finds the commit that introduced a change by binary search. Given a known-good and known-bad commit it checks out the midpoint, you say which it is, and it halves the range — so a thousand commits take about ten steps.

The manual form is git bisect start, git bisect bad, git bisect good <rev>, then good/bad at each step. The form worth learning is the automated one, because it turns a twenty-minute chore into a coffee break.

Write a script that exits 0 for good and non-zero for bad:

git bisect start HEAD HEAD~12
git bisect run ./test.sh

Bisecting: 5 revisions left to test after this (roughly 3 steps)
[5c49020d72212c6ed18c195d466a49a57ec0ea09] commit 6
running './test.sh'
Bisecting: 2 revisions left to test after this (roughly 2 steps)
[c7903808a4174d60be1040453da5e49ee9ff97f5] commit 9
running './test.sh'
Bisecting: 0 revisions left to test after this (roughly 1 step)
[dfce5024dd43bee8e2ae25804537197afe370a29] commit 8
running './test.sh'
Bisecting: 0 revisions left to test after this (roughly 0 steps)
[b2dc83bdec5078f42b64416e58623dc1e47effd0] commit 7
running './test.sh'
b2dc83bdec5078f42b64416e58623dc1e47effd0 is the first bad commit
commit b2dc83bdec5078f42b64416e58623dc1e47effd0
Author: Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com>
Date:   Thu Aug 27 10:00:00 2026 +0000

    commit 7

bisect found first bad commit

Thirteen commits, four tests, the exact culprit. Note git bisect start <bad> <good> — bad first — which saves two commands.

git bisect skip
This commit cannot be tested — it does not build, or the feature did not exist yet. Bisect works around it. A run script should exit 125 to mean "skip".
git bisect reset
Return to where you started. Bisect leaves you on a detached HEAD (§4.4) until you do.
git bisect log
Replayable record of the session. git bisect replay takes it back, which is how you recover from one wrong answer without starting over.
git bisect terms
Rename good/bad to something honest like fast/slow, since bisect finds any transition, not only regressions.

Three things determine whether bisect works at all:

Every commit must build. A history where half the commits are broken forces skip constantly and the result is a range rather than a commit. This is precisely what git rebase --exec (§8.7) prevents before publishing.

The test must be deterministic. A flaky test makes bisect converge confidently on the wrong commit. Run it a few times at a known-bad commit first.

The script must exit 125 for untestable, not 1. Exiting 1 means "bad", so a commit that fails to compile gets blamed for a bug it does not contain.

The five subcommands in the manual form are git bisect good, git bisect bad, git bisect skip, git bisect reset and git bisect run. git bisect good and git bisect bad are what you type at each step by hand; git bisect skip is for a commit that cannot be tested; git bisect reset returns you to where you started; and git bisect run replaces the first two with a script.

9.11 git blame

git blame attributes each line to the commit that last touched it. Its reputation as a tool for assigning fault is unfortunate; its actual use is finding the commit whose message explains why a line is the way it is.

git blame -L 1,3 calc.py

b2dc83bd (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 1) def total(xs): return sum(xs) - 1
9696d569 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 2) # filler 1
c1dc5d04 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 3) # filler 2

The default is confounded by reformatting, and this is the problem worth solving. After a commit that reindented every line:

git blame calc.py

^487c75d (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 1) def total(xs):
105848e9 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 2)         return sum(xs)
6a1c1272 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 3) 
6a1c1272 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 4) 
6a1c1272 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 5) def mean(xs):
105848e9 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 6)         return total(xs) / len(xs)

105848e9 is the reformat, and it now owns every line whose indentation changed — which is every interesting line. Tell blame to look through it:

git blame --ignore-rev 105848e9 calc.py

^487c75d (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 1) def total(xs):
^487c75d (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 2)         return sum(xs)
6a1c1272 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 3) 
6a1c1272 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 4) 
6a1c1272 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 5) def mean(xs):
6a1c1272 (Ada Lovelace 2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000 6)         return total(xs) / len(xs)

Every line attributed to the commit that actually wrote it. Make it permanent by listing such commits in a file:

git config blame.ignoreRevsFile .git-blame-ignore-revs

.git-blame-ignore-revs is committed to the repository, one full object id per line with # comments, so everybody's blame is corrected and forges honour it too. Add the commit id to that file in the same pull request that does the reformat — it is the single most effective thing you can do to keep a codebase's history readable across a formatter rollout.

-w
Ignore whitespace-only changes. The quick version of the above.
-M
Detect lines moved within the file.
git blame -C
Detect lines copied from other files in the same commit. -C -C and -C -C -C widen the search, at increasing cost.
-L <a>,<b>
Only those lines. Also -L :<funcname>.
<rev>
Blame as of that commit, not the working tree. git blame HEAD~50 -- file.
--reverse <a>..<b>
Backwards: for each line, the commit where it disappeared. The tool for "when was this deleted".

A ^ before an id means the line comes from a boundary commit — the oldest commit blame examined — so the true origin may be older still.

Two flags to remember beyond --ignore-rev: git blame -L <a>,<b> limits the output to a line range, which matters because blaming a two-thousand-line file prints two thousand lines, and git blame -C follows lines copied in from another file in the same commit. git blame -L also accepts :<funcname> for a function rather than numbers.

git blame -w is the quick fix for a file mangled by a formatter and is worth trying before reaching for --ignore-rev: git blame -w ignores changes that are whitespace-only, which covers reindentation but not a reformat that also rewrapped lines.

9.12 describe, shortlog and notes

Three commands that turn history into something for humans.

git describe names a commit relative to the nearest tag:

git describe
git describe --abbrev=0

v2.55.0-731-gf78ce2f7b6
v2.55.0

Read it as tag, commits since that tag, g for git, abbreviated id. It is the canonical way to stamp a build with a version, it is a valid revision expression in its own right, and --dirty appends -dirty when the working tree has uncommitted changes — which is how a build artefact can honestly say it was not built from a clean checkout. --abbrev=0 gives just the tag name. --tags considers lightweight tags too, since by default only annotated ones count (§3.8), and --always falls back to a bare id rather than failing when there is no tag — which is what makes it safe in a shallow clone (§15.10).

git shortlog groups commits by author:

git shortlog -sn v2.50.0..v2.51.0

   200	Junio C Hamano
   111	Patrick Steinhardt
    71	Mark Levedahl
    58	Johannes Sixt
    29	Taylor Blau

-s summarises to counts, -n sorts by count. Without them you get each author's commit subjects grouped underneath their name, which is a release-notes draft. It takes the same range syntax as git log, and --group=trailer:Co-authored-by groups by trailer instead of author, which is how a team that pairs gets an honest count.

Two smaller commands round out the set. git name-rev is the inverse of git rev-parse: given an object id it prints a human-readable name relative to the nearest ref, which is what turns a bare hash in a log file into something meaningful. And git last-modified, added in Git 2.52, reports for every path in a tree the commit that last touched it — the whole-directory version of what git log -1 -- <path> answers for one file, and far faster than running that per file.

git notes attaches mutable text to an immutable commit:

git notes add -m 'Reviewed-by: Ada Lovelace' <commit>
git log --show-notes -1 <commit>

Notes live in refs/notes/commits (§4.5) as a separate object graph mapping commit ids to blobs, so adding one does not change the commit id — which is the entire point, and why notes can annotate published history that must not be rewritten. The cost is that they are not fetched or pushed by default; sharing them means an explicit refspec (§10.4):

git config --add remote.origin.fetch '+refs/notes/*:refs/notes/*'

Notes are the right answer for machine-generated metadata attached after the fact — build results, review status, benchmark numbers — and the wrong answer for anything a human needs to see reliably, because in practice most people never fetch them.

git notes add -m '<text>' <commit> is the form to remember, and git name-rev --name-only <id> is the one to reach for when a log or a stack trace hands you a bare object id and you want to know roughly where in history it sits. git last-modified answers the directory-wide version of the same question.

Exercises

1. Print the contents of src/config.py exactly as it was fifty commits ago, without changing your working tree, and give the two ways to name that blob.

Solution 
git show HEAD~50:src/config.py

The <rev>:<path> syntax (§9.2) resolves the path through that commit's tree and prints the blob. Nothing is checked out, so this is safe in the middle of a conflicted merge or a stopped rebase.

The two names for that blob:

git rev-parse 'HEAD~50:src/config.py'    # the blob's object id
git ls-tree HEAD~50 -- src/config.py     # mode, type, id and path

If the file did not exist then, both fail with path ... does not exist, which is itself the answer to "when was this added" — feed it to git log --diff-filter=A -- src/config.py.

2. A string LEGACY_MODE appears in the codebase and nobody knows why. git log -G LEGACY_MODE returns 340 commits. Give a better query and explain the difference.

Solution 
git log --oneline -S 'LEGACY_MODE'

-G matches every commit whose diff has an added or removed line containing the string — including every commit that merely edited a nearby part of a line, reindented it, or moved it. On a long-lived flag that is hundreds of commits.

-S matches only commits where the number of occurrences of the string changed (§9.8). That is a much smaller set and it contains exactly the commits you want: the one that introduced it, the ones that added or removed a use, and the one that eventually deletes it. In the worked example the same file gave 2 commits under -S and 4 under -G, and the two extra were changes to the constant's value, which touch the line without changing the count.

Then read the message on the first one: git show -s $(git log --format=%H -S 'LEGACY_MODE' | tail -1).

3. Your release notes should list what was integrated into main between two tags, not every individual commit. Give the command and say what makes it work.

Solution 
git log --first-parent --oneline v1.4.0..v1.5.0

--first-parent follows only the first parent of each merge (§9.7), which is by definition the trunk you were on when you merged (§3.6). Each merged branch collapses to the one merge commit that brought it in. On the measured example this took a release from 758 commits to 161 entries.

What makes it work is that merges exist at all. In a repository where every branch is squashed or fast-forwarded there is no second parent anywhere, first-parent history equals full history, and the flag does nothing. If you want this view, merge feature branches with --no-ff (§6.2) — that is the main reason to.

For a formatted draft, git shortlog --no-merges v1.4.0..v1.5.0 groups the same range by author with subjects.

4. You know a bug appeared somewhere in the last 400 commits and you have a script that reproduces it. Write the whole procedure, including what the script must do about commits that do not build.

Solution 
git bisect start HEAD HEAD~400
git bisect run ./repro.sh
git bisect reset

About nine tests instead of 400. git bisect start <bad> <good> takes bad first, which saves two commands.

repro.sh must use three exit statuses:

  • 0 — good, the bug is absent.
  • 1–124 (except 125) — bad, the bug is present.
  • 125 — cannot test this commit. This is the one people get wrong. A commit that fails to compile must exit 125 so bisect skips it; exiting 1 tells bisect the bug is present and it will happily blame a commit that merely had a syntax error.

A skeleton:

#!/bin/sh
make -s || exit 125
./run-the-failing-case

Two preconditions worth checking first (§9.10): the test must be deterministic — run it three times at the known-bad commit — and enough commits must build for bisect to converge on one rather than a range. git bisect log records the session so git bisect replay can undo a wrong answer.

5. Explain the difference between git log main...feature and git diff main...feature.

Solution

They share a spelling and are different operations (§9.5).

git log main...feature is the symmetric difference of two commit sets: every commit reachable from one but not both. It answers "how have these two diverged", and --left-right marks which side each commit came from.

git diff main...feature means "diff feature against the merge base of main and feature". It shows the change your branch introduced, with everything that happened on main in the meantime excluded. It is what a pull request displays, and it is almost always what you want when reviewing a branch — git diff main feature (two dots or none) would additionally show, in reverse, everything main gained since you branched.

A useful mnemonic: in log, three dots widen the selection to both sides; in diff, three dots narrow the comparison to one side's own work.

6. After rolling out an auto-formatter, git blame attributes every line in the project to one commit. Fix it for the whole team.

Solution 
git rev-parse <the-format-commit> >> .git-blame-ignore-revs
git config blame.ignoreRevsFile .git-blame-ignore-revs
git add .git-blame-ignore-revs && git commit -m 'ignore the formatting commit in blame'

--ignore-rev makes blame look through a commit: lines it touched are attributed to whoever last changed them meaningfully (§9.11). The measured example went from the reformat owning four of six lines to owning none.

Three details make this work for the team rather than just for you:

  • The file holds full 40-character object ids, one per line, with # comments. Abbreviations are rejected.
  • It is committed to the repository, and the blame.ignoreRevsFile setting is what activates it — so document that one git config line in the README, or ship it in a setup script. GitHub and GitLab honour a file with this conventional name automatically.
  • Add the id in the same pull request as the reformat. Retrofitting it means someone has to go and find the commit, which nobody does.

git blame -w ignores whitespace-only changes and is the quick fallback when no such file exists.

SECTION 10

Remotes, Refspecs and Tracking Branches

Git is distributed, which in practice means every repository is complete and independent and nothing is ever automatically synchronised. Two repositories exchange objects only when you tell them to, and what "synchronised" means is entirely a matter of which refs got copied where.

This section is about that copying. The centre of it is one small piece of syntax — the refspec — which is simply a rule for mapping refs on one side to refs on the other. Once you can read a refspec, fetch, pull, push, remote-tracking branches, upstream configuration and every "your branch is ahead by 3 commits" message become one mechanism rather than six.

Section 11 covers the wire protocol underneath. This section is the model above it: which refs move, where they land, and what the resulting local state means.

10.1 A Remote Is Three Config Lines

git config --get-regexp '^(remote|branch)\.'

remote.origin.url /…/rem/origin.git
remote.origin.fetch +refs/heads/*:refs/remotes/origin/*
branch.main.remote origin
branch.main.merge refs/heads/main

That is a fully configured clone. A URL, a fetch refspec, and two keys saying what main tracks. There is no connection, no session, no cached credentials, and no server-side record that you exist. git remote add writes the first two lines and contacts nothing.

git remote -v
List remotes with their fetch and push URLs.
git remote add <n> <url>
Add one. -f fetches immediately.
git remote show <n>
Contacts the server and reports which branches are tracked, which are stale, and what push would do. The best diagnostic in this section.
git remote rename
Rewrites the config keys, including the refspec and every branch's tracking configuration.
git remote remove <n>
Deletes the remote and every remote-tracking ref under it.
git remote set-url --push
Give a remote different fetch and push URLs — read over HTTPS, write over SSH.

The origin remote is a convention with no special meaning: origin is simply what git clone names the remote it just cloned from, and nothing else in Git treats that name specially. An upstream remote conventionally named upstream, pointing at the canonical repository in a fork workflow (§12.4), is the same kind of convention — a name everyone agreed on.

Four of these you will type; two are names you will read. git remote -v is the one to run first in an unfamiliar repository, git remote show <name> is the one that actually asks the server, and git remote rename and git remote remove do the obvious things while correctly rewriting every dependent config key. The origin remote and an upstream remote are the two names conventions have settled on, and §10.11 is where the second one earns its place.

10.2 Remote-Tracking Refs Are a Cache

git ls-remote origin
git for-each-ref refs/remotes

21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9	HEAD
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9	refs/heads/main
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/remotes/origin/main

git ls-remote asks the server what it has right now. git for-each-ref refs/remotes reports what your last fetch recorded. They are different questions, and only the first involves the network.

refs/remotes/origin/main is an ordinary ref (§4.6). The only special thing about it is a convention: nothing writes it except git fetch. From that one rule everything else follows — you cannot commit onto it, checking it out detaches HEAD (§4.4), git log origin/main shows possibly stale history with no network traffic, and every "ahead/behind" number is a statement about your last fetch rather than about the server.

git ls-remote is also the right tool for a script that needs to know a branch's current tip without cloning anything: git ls-remote --heads <url> main prints one line and downloads no objects at all.

10.3 Fetch, and Why It Is Always Safe

git fetch downloads objects and updates remote-tracking refs. It never touches your branches, your index, or your working tree. It cannot cause a conflict, cannot lose work, and cannot leave you in a broken state.

That makes it the right thing to run whenever you want to know what is going on:

git fetch --all --prune
git log --oneline --graph --all
git fetch
The current branch's remote, using its configured refspec.
git fetch --all
Every remote.
git fetch --prune
Also delete remote-tracking refs whose branches are gone from the server (§10.9).
git fetch <remote> <branch>
Just that one, which on a large repository is dramatically cheaper (§11.5).
git fetch --dry-run
Report what would change without changing it.

FETCH_HEAD (§4.7) records what the last fetch brought, one line per ref, and is what git pull merges when you give it a branch name rather than relying on configuration.

10.4 Refspecs

A refspec is [+]<src>:<dst> — a mapping from a ref on one side to a ref on the other, with an optional leading + meaning "allow a non-fast-forward update".

The default fetch refspec, read literally:

+refs/heads/*:refs/remotes/origin/*

Take every branch on the remote, and store it under refs/remotes/origin/ with the same name, forcibly. The + is why a fetch always succeeds even when the remote's branch was rewritten: your local copy is a cache and is allowed to jump. That is also why a rewritten upstream shows up as a diverged branch rather than an error.

You can give an explicit refspec on the command line and watch it land wherever you say:

git fetch origin 'refs/heads/main:refs/remotes/origin/mymain'
git for-each-ref refs/remotes

 * [new branch]      main       -> origin/mymain
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/remotes/origin/main
21d1d8517385e8b981389d3e97cd440c735714a9 commit	refs/remotes/origin/mymain

Nothing forces the destination into refs/remotes/ either:

git fetch origin 'refs/heads/*:refs/heads/from-origin/*'
git branch -a

 * [new branch]      main       -> from-origin/main
  from-origin/main
* main
  remotes/origin/main

Real local branches, created by a fetch. This is worth doing once because it demolishes the idea that remote-tracking branches are a separate species: they are ordinary refs in a namespace, and the refspec is the only thing that puts them there.

Push refspecs read in the same direction — source first, destination second — but source is now local:

git push origin main
Shorthand for main:main under the default push.default.
git push origin main:qa/main
Push my main to a differently-named branch on the server.
git push origin HEAD:refs/heads/x
Push whatever I am on to a branch called x. Fully qualifying the destination avoids creating a tag by accident.
git push origin :topic
Empty source — delete topic on the server. --delete topic is the readable spelling.
git push origin +main
Force, via the refspec rather than --force. Prefer the flags in §8.9.

Adding a refspec permanently is how you fetch things Git does not fetch by default — notes (§9.12), or a forge's pull-request refs:

git config --add remote.origin.fetch '+refs/pull/*/head:refs/remotes/origin/pr/*'

After that, git fetch brings every open pull request in as origin/pr/1234, checkoutable like anything else. remote.<name>.fetch is a multi-valued key, which is why --add matters (§13.2).

One default worth setting deliberately: push.default decides what a bare git push means, and its modern value simple refuses to push when the local and remote branch names differ. Leaving push.default alone is fine; knowing what it is set to is what stops a surprise.

10.5 Upstream Configuration

Two config keys say what a local branch tracks:

branch.main.remote origin
branch.main.merge refs/heads/main

Together they define @{upstream} (§9.2), and they are what let you type git pull and git push with no arguments.

git push -u origin <branch>
Push and set the upstream in one go. The usual way to get it.
git branch -u origin/<b>
Set it without pushing.
git switch -c <b> origin/<b>
Create a local branch from a remote-tracking one; tracking is configured automatically.
git switch <b>
With no local <b> but exactly one remote having it, this does the above automatically — the DWIM shortcut.
git branch -vv
Every branch with its upstream and ahead/behind counts. The command to run when tracking is confusing.

push.autoSetupRemote = true makes the first git push on a new branch set the upstream by itself, which removes the "fatal: The current branch has no upstream branch" step from every new branch. It is worth setting.

The upstream is advice, not a constraint: it says where pull and push go by default and what ahead/behind is measured against. git push other-remote HEAD works regardless.

git push -u is the form to use the first time you publish a branch, because it both pushes and records the upstream. git branch -u origin/<b> does the second half alone, for a branch that already exists on both sides.

10.6 Ahead and Behind

git status -sb
git rev-list --count --left-right 'main...@{u}'

## main...origin/main [ahead 2]
2	0

Two commits on main that origin/main lacks, none the other way. It is exactly the symmetric-difference count from §9.5, computed between two local refs.

No network was involved. The numbers describe your last fetch, so "up to date" means "up to date as of whenever you last fetched", which may be days ago. git fetch first, then read the numbers. This is the single most misread piece of output in Git, and the fix is a habit rather than a setting.

Four states, and what each calls for:

ahead only
You have new commits. git push.
behind only
The remote has new commits and you have none. git pull fast-forwards; nothing can conflict.
ahead and behind
Diverged. This is the case that needs a decision — §10.7.
neither
Identical.

The phrase for what those keys define is the branch's upstream branch, and a branch with no upstream branch is why git push sometimes asks you to name a remote. git branch -vv prints every branch's upstream branch in one column.

10.7 Pull: Three Different Commands

git pull is git fetch followed by an integration step, and which integration step is a configuration choice that changes what your history looks like. Run it on a diverged branch three ways:

git -c pull.ff=only pull origin main

fatal: Not possible to fast-forward, aborting.

The same three choices are available per invocation as git pull --ff-only, git pull --no-rebase and git pull --rebase, which is how you override a configured default once without changing it.

git -c pull.rebase=false pull --no-edit origin main
git log --oneline --graph

*   aeac286 Merge branch 'main' of /…/o
|\
| * 23b3397 a work
* | 74c191b b work
|/
* db25c3f base

git -c pull.rebase=true pull origin main
git log --oneline --graph

Rebasing (1/1)Successfully rebased and updated refs/heads/main.
* 58729fe b work
* 23b3397 a work
* db25c3f base
pull.ff=only
Refuse when the branches have diverged. Nothing happens and you decide deliberately. The right default — it converts a silent history change into a question.
pull.rebase=false
Merge. Truthful — it records that two lines of work existed — and produces "Merge branch 'main' of …" commits that carry no information anyone wants.
pull.rebase=true
Replay your commits on top. Linear history, at the cost of rewriting your local commits — note b work got a new id. Fine for unpublished work, and §8.11's rule applies if it was published.

The recommendation: set pull.ff = only globally, and when it refuses, choose git rebase origin/main for your own feature branch or git merge origin/main for a shared one. Two extra keystrokes buys you never being surprised by your own history.

pull.rebase = interactive and pull.rebase = merges exist; the latter preserves merge commits within your local work rather than flattening them.

Between them, git pull --ff-only, git pull --rebase and git pull --no-rebase cover every case, and the reason to prefer git pull --ff-only as the configured default is that it is the only one that never silently changes the shape of your history. Choosing git pull --rebase when it refuses is how you get linear history on purpose rather than by accident.

10.8 Push, and the Rejections

git push asks the server to move a ref, and the server checks that the ref still holds the value you observed (§11.7). Almost every push failure is that check.

git push origin main

error: failed to push some refs to '/…/origin.git'
hint: Updates were rejected because the remote contains work that you do not
hint: have locally. This is usually caused by another repository pushing to
hint: the same ref. If you want to integrate the remote changes, use
hint: 'git pull' before pushing again.
hint: See the 'Note about fast-forwards' in 'git push --help' for details.
non-fast-forward
Someone pushed first. Integrate (§10.7) and push again. Never reach for --force here — it deletes their commits.
stale info
--force-with-lease refusing because your remote-tracking ref is out of date (§8.9).
remote ref updated since checkout
--force-if-includes refusing because you never actually had the remote's tip (§8.9).
protected branch / hook
A server-side hook or a forge rule refused it (§13.7). The objects arrived; the ref did not move.
no upstream branch
Not a rejection — Git does not know where to send it. git push -u origin HEAD, or set push.autoSetupRemote.

push.default decides what a bare git push means. The modern default is simple: push the current branch to its upstream, and refuse if the names differ. The alternatives are current (push to a same-named branch, creating it if needed — convenient in a triangular workflow), upstream (allow differing names), matching (push every branch that exists on both sides — the old default, and a good way to publish something by accident) and nothing.

10.9 Deleting and Pruning

Deleting a branch on the server is a push with an empty source, and git push --delete is the readable spelling of it:

git push origin --delete doomed
git ls-remote --heads origin

 - [deleted]         doomed
ac2a7302e0e7b06fec857e6ec917c1d7cf42f520	refs/heads/main

Deleting it there does not delete your remote-tracking copy of it. That is pruning, and it is separate:

git branch -r
git fetch --prune origin
git branch -r

  origin/HEAD -> origin/main
  origin/main
  origin/mymain
 - [deleted]         (none)     -> origin/mymain
  origin/HEAD -> origin/main
  origin/main

Without pruning, refs/remotes/ accumulates every branch anyone has ever pushed, forever, and git branch -r becomes a museum of deleted feature branches. Set it once:

git config --global fetch.prune true

Three deletions to keep straight, because they are genuinely independent: git branch -d <b> removes a local branch, git push origin --delete <b> removes the server's branch, and git fetch --prune removes your cached copy of branches the server no longer has. Cleaning up after a merged pull request usually needs all three.

git branch --merged lists local branches fully contained in the current one, which makes the cleanup scriptable — with the caveat from §6.2 that a squash-merged branch is not "merged" by this test, because squashing records no relationship.

git push --delete <branch> is the readable form and git push origin :<branch> is the older one; both compile to the same empty-source refspec. Note that git push --delete needs the branch not to be protected on the server (§12.10), which is usually exactly the point.

10.10 Tags Across Remotes

Tags follow different rules from branches and the differences cause real surprises.

Fetched automatically
Tags pointing at objects you fetch arrive without being asked for. --no-tags or remote.<n>.tagOpt = --no-tags stops it, which matters on repositories with tens of thousands of tags.
Not pushed automatically
git push sends branches only. A tag exists only for you until git push origin <tag> or git push --tags.
Not namespaced
Branches land under refs/remotes/<remote>/; tags land in the shared refs/tags/. Two remotes with the same tag name at different commits conflict, and the first one fetched wins.
Meant to be immutable
Moving a published tag is worse than rewriting a branch: everyone who already has it keeps the old value silently, because a fetch will not clobber an existing tag without --force. Cut a new tag instead.

git push --follow-tags is the sensible middle ground: push the branch, and also any annotated tags reachable from it. That is almost always what you want at release time and it will not push your local scratch tags.

git push --tags pushes every tag you have, including the scratch ones, which is why --follow-tags is the better habit. If you do use git push --tags, look at git tag -l first.

10.11 Several Remotes and Triangular Workflows

Nothing limits you to one remote:

git remote add upstream ../upstream-sim
git remote -v

origin	/…/origin.git (fetch)
origin	/…/origin.git (push)
upstream	../upstream-sim (fetch)
upstream	../upstream-sim (push)

The standard fork arrangement (§12.4) is origin = your fork, upstream = the canonical repository: fetch from upstream, push to origin. That is a triangular workflow — the place you pull from is not the place you push to — and Git supports it explicitly:

git config remote.pushDefault origin
git branch -u upstream/main

Now git pull follows main's upstream to upstream/main, while git push goes to origin regardless. @{push} (§9.2) resolves to the push destination and @{upstream} to the pull source; in a non-triangular setup they are the same ref, which is why the distinction usually goes unnoticed.

Keeping a fork current is then:

git fetch upstream
git switch main
git merge --ff-only upstream/main
git push origin main

--ff-only is doing real work there: if it refuses, you have accidentally committed to your fork's main, and you want to know rather than to create a merge.

git merge --ff-only upstream/main is the load-bearing line in that sequence and is worth using anywhere you expect a branch to be strictly behind: it succeeds silently when it is, and fails loudly when it is not.

10.12 Clone Options

git clone is fetch plus configuration plus a checkout. Its options change what you get:

git clone --bare
No working tree; the repository's contents at the top level. What a server holds (§11.8).
--mirror
--bare plus a refspec mapping every ref, not just branches, and configured so git push updates them all. This is the backup/migration clone.
git clone --single-branch
Configure the refspec for one branch only. Reduces transfer and means later fetches stay narrow.
--depth=<n>
Shallow (§15.10). Implies --single-branch.
--filter=blob:none
Partial (§15.11).
--recurse-submodules
Initialise submodules too (§14.4).
--origin <name>
Call the remote something other than origin.
--reference <path>
Borrow objects from a local clone instead of transferring them. Fast, and fragile — the borrowed repository must not be deleted or pruned. --dissociate copies them in afterwards and removes the dependency.

The one to know for backups and migrations is --mirror, because it is the only form that captures everything:

git clone --mirror https://example.com/project.git
git push --mirror git@newhost:project.git

Every branch, every tag, every note, every remote-tracking ref. A plain clone plus git push --all misses tags, notes, and anything else outside refs/heads/.

The two to remember are git clone --bare, which is what you run on a server, and git clone --mirror, which is what you run before a migration. git clone --single-branch is the one to add to a CI checkout when a shallow clone would break something, since git clone --single-branch keeps full history for one branch and skips the rest.

--recurse-submodules on git clone initialises submodules as part of the clone (§14.4); the same --recurse-submodules flag exists on fetch, pull and push, where it means "do this for the submodules too".

Exercises

1. Read +refs/heads/*:refs/remotes/origin/* aloud and explain each of its four parts, including why the + matters.

Solution

"Forcibly, take every ref under refs/heads/ on the remote, and store each under refs/remotes/origin/ with the same trailing name."

  • + — allow a non-fast-forward update. Without it, a fetch would fail whenever the remote's branch was rewritten. Your remote-tracking refs are a cache of what the server says (§10.2), so they are allowed to move backwards or sideways; this is what turns a rewritten upstream into a "diverged" state rather than an error.
  • refs/heads/* — the source pattern, on the remote. Branches only: tags and other namespaces are not covered by this line, which is why §10.10's tag rules are different.
  • : — separates source from destination.
  • refs/remotes/origin/* — the destination, local. The * captures whatever the source * matched.

Nothing about refs/remotes/ is privileged. Fetching with refs/heads/*:refs/heads/from-origin/* creates real local branches instead, which is worth trying once to prove that remote-tracking branches are just refs in a namespace (§10.4).

2. git status says "Your branch is up to date with 'origin/main'", and a colleague insists they pushed an hour ago. Who is right?

Solution

Both. The message compares two local refs — refs/heads/main and refs/remotes/origin/main (§10.6) — and involves no network traffic at all. It says your branch matches what your last fetch recorded, which may be from yesterday.

git fetch
git status -sb

Or without changing anything:

git ls-remote origin refs/heads/main

which asks the server directly (§10.2).

This is the single most misread output in Git, and the fix is a habit: fetch before believing any ahead/behind number. git maintenance start (§15.9) helps by prefetching hourly in the background, which makes the cached answer much more likely to be current — but it still does not make git status a network command.

3. Explain, in terms of refs, why git push origin --delete feature and git branch -d feature and git fetch --prune are three different operations.

Solution

Three separate refs exist for one "branch", in three repositories or namespaces (§10.9):

  1. refs/heads/feature on the server — deleted by git push origin --delete feature, which is a push with an empty source refspec (§10.4).
  2. refs/heads/feature in your repository — deleted by git branch -d feature.
  3. refs/remotes/origin/feature in your repository — your cached copy of (1), deleted by git fetch --prune, which removes tracking refs for branches the server no longer advertises.

Deleting any one leaves the other two. That is why a merged pull request typically needs all three, and why git branch -r on a long-lived clone lists dozens of branches that no longer exist anywhere.

fetch.prune = true handles (3) permanently. For (2), git branch --merged lists locals safe to delete — with the caveat that a squash-merged branch is not detected as merged (§6.2), because squashing records no ancestry.

4. Your team wants linear history but keeps getting "Merge branch 'main' of github.com:..." commits. Diagnose and fix, and say what the fix costs.

Solution

Someone ran git pull on a diverged branch with pull.rebase unset, so Git merged (§10.7). The merge commit's message is Git's default, and it carries no information anyone wants.

git config --global pull.ff only

This makes git pull refuse rather than choose: when the branches have diverged it stops with fatal: Not possible to fast-forward, aborting. and you decide — git rebase origin/main for your own feature branch, git merge origin/main for a shared one.

pull.rebase = true also removes the merge commits and is more automatic, but it rebases silently on every pull, which rewrites your local commits (b work got a new id in the worked example) and does so even on a branch someone else has already pulled. That is a rule violation waiting to happen (§8.11).

The cost of ff only is two extra keystrokes when the branches diverge, in exchange for never being surprised by your own history. Cleaning up the ones already made is a git rebase — and per §8.11, only if that branch is not shared.

5. You forked a project. Set the repository up so git pull follows the original and git push goes to your fork, and give the command to bring your fork's main up to date.

Solution 
git remote add upstream https://github.com/original/project.git
git fetch upstream
git branch -u upstream/main main
git config remote.pushDefault origin

origin stays your fork; upstream is the canonical repository. branch.main.merge/branch.main.remote (§10.5) now point at upstream, so git pull follows the original, while remote.pushDefault sends every push to your fork. This is a triangular workflow, and @{upstream} and @{push} now resolve to different refs (§9.2).

Bringing the fork's main up to date:

git fetch upstream
git switch main
git merge --ff-only upstream/main
git push origin main

--ff-only is not decoration. If it refuses, you have committed to your fork's main — which is the mistake this workflow exists to prevent — and you want an error rather than a merge commit that will make every future update messier.

6. Migrate a repository to a new server, losing nothing. Explain why the obvious approach is incomplete.

Solution 
git clone --mirror https://old.example.com/project.git
cd project.git
git push --mirror git@new.example.com:project.git

--mirror on clone configures the refspec +refs/*:refs/* — every ref, not just refs/heads/* — and --mirror on push sends all of them, deleting on the destination anything not present locally, so the result is an exact copy.

The obvious approach — git clone then git push --all — is incomplete because the default fetch refspec only ever brought refs/heads/* (§10.4), and --all only pushes refs/heads/*. That silently loses:

  • Tags, unless you also run git push --tags.
  • Notes in refs/notes/* (§9.12), which nothing fetches by default.
  • Any other namespace the project uses — refs/replace/*, and forge-specific refs.

Two things --mirror still does not carry, because they are not refs: hooks and server-side configuration, and the pull requests, issues and permissions that live in the forge's database rather than in Git. Those need the forge's own migration tooling.

SECTION 11

Transfer Protocols, Hosting, Credentials and Signing

Everything so far has been local. A repository is a self-contained object database (§3) with a set of refs pointing into it (§4), and every command in sections 5 through 9 operates on one machine. This section is about the moment two of them talk to each other.

That conversation is worth understanding in detail, for three reasons. It is the only part of Git with a latency cost, so it is where slowness comes from. It is the only part with an authentication cost, so it is where credentials leak. And it is remarkably simple — a line-framed text protocol you can read with curl — which means "why is my push hanging" is a question with an observable answer rather than a guess.

Everything in this section was captured from real conversations: some against GitHub over HTTPS, some against a bare repository on the same machine. GIT_TRACE_PACKET=1 prints every frame Git sends and receives, and it is the single most useful diagnostic in this whole area.

11.1 What a Remote Actually Is

A remote is a name, a URL, and a refspec (§10.4). Nothing else — no state, no connection, no session. git remote add origin <url> writes three lines of config and contacts nothing:

git remote add origin https://github.com/git/git.git
git config --get-regexp '^remote\.origin\.'

remote.origin.url https://github.com/git/git.git
remote.origin.fetch +refs/heads/*:refs/remotes/origin/*

Every network operation is therefore a complete, stateless transaction: connect, agree on capabilities, exchange refs, transfer a packfile, disconnect. There is no login, no working session, and no server-side notion of "your checkout". This is why a Git server can be a plain file share, and why git fetch interrupted halfway leaves nothing to clean up.

The two verbs are fetch and push, and they are not symmetric. A fetch is a download negotiation: the client says what it wants and what it already has, and the server computes a pack. A push is a ref update request: the client sends objects and asks the server to move refs, and the server may refuse. §11.6 and §11.7 show both on the wire.

11.2 The Four Transports

HTTPS transport
https://host/path.git. Authenticated with a username and a token. Works through corporate proxies and firewalls, which is why it is the default nearly everywhere. Two flavours — see §11.4.
SSH transport
git@host:path.git or ssh://git@host/path.git. Authenticated with a key pair. Git runs git-upload-pack or git-receive-pack on the far side over the SSH channel — there is no Git-specific server software involved at all.
git://
The dedicated daemon on port 9418. Fast, and completely unauthenticated and unencrypted. Read-only in every sane deployment. Effectively obsolete; GitHub removed support in 2022.
Local
/path/to/repo.git or file:///path. A plain path may hardlink objects instead of copying them, which is instant and disk-free; file:// forces the full protocol, which is what you want when testing.

The git@host:path form is worth a note because it is not a URL — it is scp syntax, and Git recognises it by the colon before the first slash. That is why git clone myserver:repo.git works and git clone myserver:8080/repo.git does something surprising: 8080/repo.git is read as a path, not a port. Use the explicit ssh://host:8080/repo.git when a port is involved.

url.<base>.insteadOf rewrites URLs on the way out, which is the clean way to force SSH for a host everyone else configures over HTTPS:

git config --global url."git@github.com:".insteadOf "https://github.com/"

The SSH transport and the HTTPS transport are the only two you are likely to meet in a working week, and the choice between SSH transport and HTTPS transport is almost entirely about credentials (§11.10) rather than about speed. The git protocol on port 9418 — the git:// scheme — is the third, and it is read-only and unauthenticated wherever it still runs.

11.3 pkt-line: the Framing

Git's wire protocol has exactly one framing rule, and knowing it makes every trace in this section readable. A pkt-line is four hexadecimal digits giving the length of the frame including those four digits, followed by that many bytes minus four.

Fetch the handshake with curl and read the raw bytes:

curl -s 'https://github.com/git/git.git/info/refs?service=git-upload-pack' \
     -H 'Git-Protocol: version=2' | head -c 400

001e# service=git-upload-pack
0000000eversion 2
0028agent=git/github-9955570c15e5-Linux
0013ls-refs=unborn
0027fetch=shallow wait-for-done filter
0012server-option
0017object-format=sha1
0000

Check the arithmetic on the first frame. 001e is 30 decimal. The payload # service=git-upload-pack\n is 26 bytes, and 26 + 4 = 30.

001e
length
4 bytes
# service=git-upload-pack\n
payload — 26 bytes

Three lengths are special and carry no payload:

0000
flush-pkt
End of this group of lines. The most common frame on the wire.
0001
delim-pkt
Separates a section header from its body in protocol v2.
0002
response-end-pkt
End of a v2 response, used in stateless HTTP.

That is the entire framing layer. There is no length prefix larger than fff0 (65,516 bytes of payload), which is why the packfile itself is chunked into side-band frames rather than sent as one enormous line.

11.4 Dumb HTTP, Smart HTTP, and the Handshake

There were originally two HTTP protocols, and the distinction still shows up in error messages.

The dumb protocol requires no Git-specific software on the server: the client fetches info/refs, HEAD, objects/info/packs and then individual object files by URL, with plain GET requests. Any static web server can host it. It is also inefficient and slightly broken — the server must be told to regenerate info/refs and objects/info/packs with git update-server-info after every push, and forgetting to do so produces clones that are silently out of date. Use it only as an emergency read-only mirror.

The smart protocol runs a real process on the server — git-upload-pack for reads, git-receive-pack for writes — with a matching process on the client: fetch-pack talks to upload-pack, and send-pack talks to receive-pack. Both ends negotiate. This is what every hosting provider runs and what the rest of this section is about.

Omit the Git-Protocol header from that curl and you get the older v0 handshake:

curl -s 'https://github.com/git/git.git/info/refs?service=git-upload-pack' | head -c 320

001e# service=git-upload-pack
0000015bf78ce2f7b6df702f93d40b85d6bda92a3f65da79 HEAD\0multi_ack thin-pack side-band side-band-64k ofs-delta shallow deepen-since deepen-not deepen-relative no-progress include-tag multi_ack_detailed allow-tip-sha1-in-want allow-reachable-sha1-in-want no-done symref=HEAD:refs/heads/master f

Read what just happened. The first line of the response is the first ref, with the server's entire capability list glued on after a NUL byte — capabilities are smuggled into a ref advertisement because v0 had nowhere else to put them. And then it keeps going: the server sends every ref it has, unasked, before the client has said a single word about what it wants.

For the Git repository that is roughly twenty thousand refs on every fetch, including a git fetch origin main that will end up transferring nothing. On repositories with hundreds of thousands of tags the advertisement alone was measured in megabytes and seconds. That is the problem protocol v2 was built to fix.

Note the symref=HEAD:refs/heads/master capability: this is how a clone learns which branch to check out. Git has no other way to ask "what is your default branch", which is why a bare repository with a wrong HEAD produces clones checked out on the wrong branch (§11.8).

Four programs, in two pairs. On a fetch, fetch-pack runs on your machine and talks to upload-pack on the server; on a push, send-pack runs on your machine and talks to receive-pack. The names appear verbatim in error messages, so fatal: could not read from remote repository immediately after a line mentioning upload-pack means the server could not start it — usually a path or a permissions problem rather than anything to do with Git.

The dumb protocol has one operational footgun worth naming: update-server-info must be run after every push to regenerate the files it depends on, and forgetting it produces clones that are silently stale. A post-update hook that runs update-server-info is the standard fix, and it is one of the sample hooks Git ships.

Everything from here on is the smart protocol; the smart protocol is what every hosting provider runs, and the dumb one survives only as a curiosity and an emergency mirror.

11.5 Protocol v2 and ref-prefix

Protocol v2 inverts the conversation: the server begins with a capability advertisement rather than a list of refs, and the client then issues a command. Watch a real one:

GIT_TRACE_PACKET=1 git ls-remote https://github.com/git/git.git 2>&1 | head -25

packet:          git< # service=git-upload-pack
packet:          git< 0000
packet:          git< version 2
packet:          git< agent=git/github-9955570c15e5-Linux
packet:          git< ls-refs=unborn
packet:          git< fetch=shallow wait-for-done filter
packet:          git< server-option
packet:          git< object-format=sha1
packet:          git< 0000
packet:    ls-remote> command=ls-refs
packet:    ls-remote> agent=git/2.53.0-Linux
packet:    ls-remote> object-format=sha1
packet:    ls-remote> 0001
packet:    ls-remote> peel
packet:    ls-remote> symrefs
packet:    ls-remote> unborn

< is inbound and > is outbound. The server named three commands it supports — ls-refs, fetch, server-option — plus object-format=sha1 (§3.11) and its agent string. The client then issued command=ls-refs, a 0001 delimiter, and the arguments.

The decisive argument appears on a real fetch:

GIT_TRACE_PACKET=1 git fetch origin master 2>&1 | grep 'fetch> '

fetch> command=ls-refs
fetch> agent=git/2.53.0-Linux
fetch> object-format=sha1
fetch> 0001
fetch> peel
fetch> symrefs
fetch> unborn
fetch> ref-prefix master
fetch> ref-prefix refs/master
fetch> ref-prefix refs/tags/master
fetch> ref-prefix refs/heads/master
fetch> ref-prefix refs/remotes/master
fetch> ref-prefix refs/remotes/master/HEAD
fetch> ref-prefix refs/tags/
fetch> 0000

ref-prefix is protocol v2's entire reason for existing. The client tells the server which namespaces it cares about, and the server sends only matching refs instead of all of them. The six master prefixes are the disambiguation rules of §9.3 being sent over the wire — Git does not yet know whether master names a branch, a tag or a remote, so it asks about all the places it could be.

The reply carries the peeled targets of annotated tags inline, so the client never needs a second round trip to resolve them:

fetch< f78ce2f7b6df702f93d40b85d6bda92a3f65da79 refs/heads/master
fetch< d5aef6e4d58cfe1549adef5b436f3ace984e8c86 refs/tags/gitgui-0.10.0 peeled:3d654be48f65545c4d3e35f5d3bbed5489820930

Protocol v2 became the default in Git 2.26, was briefly reverted, and has been the default since 2.29. protocol.version controls it, and GIT_TRACE_PACKET=1 plus the presence or absence of ref-prefix lines is how you tell which one a given server actually negotiated — some older self-hosted installations still fall back to v0 silently.

The capability advertisement is also where protocol.version is settled: the client asks for a version and the server's capability advertisement either offers version 2 or does not. Setting protocol.version = 2 explicitly is harmless and occasionally useful against an old server that needs prompting.

11.6 Negotiation: want, have, ACK

The interesting half of a fetch is deciding what to send. Set up two repositories on one machine, put two new commits in one, and fetch:

git init -q --bare srv/repo.git
git init -q up
echo one > up/a.txt
git -C up add a.txt
git -C up commit -q -m one
git -C up remote add origin ../srv/repo.git
git -C up push -q origin main
git clone -q srv/repo.git down
echo two >> up/a.txt
git -C up commit -q -am two
echo three >> up/a.txt
git -C up commit -q -am three
git -C up push -q origin main
GIT_TRACE_PACKET=1 git -C down fetch origin 2>&1 | grep -E 'fetch[<>] '

git -C <dir> runs a command as if from that directory, which keeps a transcript like this one honest — every command is independent, and there is no hidden working directory to lose track of.

The second half of that trace is the negotiation:

fetch> command=fetch
fetch> agent=git/2.53.0-Linux
fetch> object-format=sha1
fetch> 0001
fetch> thin-pack
fetch> no-progress
fetch> include-tag
fetch> ofs-delta
fetch> want 55a65a7c416708dcd9ef8d088e06bd976bbb2ee7
fetch> have 544208e480d34d78b9ede9213825738a5ab51da4
fetch> 0000
fetch< acknowledgments
fetch< ACK 544208e480d34d78b9ede9213825738a5ab51da4
fetch< ready
fetch< 0001
fetch< packfile
want line
An object id the client needs. Always a ref tip the server advertised, unless the server allows arbitrary ids.
have
An object id the client already possesses. Sent in batches, walking backwards from the client's own tips.
ACK
The server recognises that id and can use it as a delta base and a traversal boundary.
ready
The server has enough common ground to build the pack. Negotiation stops.
packfile
Everything after this is pack data, chunked into side-band frames.

The negotiation is a distributed set-difference: send me everything reachable from want that is not reachable from anything you ACKed. On a deep divergence the client sends have lines in exponentially widening batches until the server acknowledges one, which is why fetching after a long absence has a brief pause before any data moves.

Two of those capabilities matter in practice. A thin pack — requested with the thin-pack capability — lets the server send objects deltified against a base it knows the client has but which is not in the pack; the client "fattens" the thin pack on receipt. ofs-delta lets a delta reference its base by relative offset rather than by object id, saving twenty bytes per delta across hundreds of thousands of objects.

The two line types are worth remembering by name because they appear in every trace: a want line names something the client needs, and a have line names something it already has. The client sends have lines in batches until the server acknowledges one, so a fetch after a long absence sends many more have lines than a fetch after an hour.

11.7 The Push Side

Push uses a different program on the server — git-receive-pack — and, notably, still speaks v0 in most deployments. The advertisement and the request are both a single line per ref:

GIT_TRACE_PACKET=1 git push origin main 2>&1 | grep -E 'push[<>] '

push< 55a65a7c416708dcd9ef8d088e06bd976bbb2ee7 refs/heads/main\0report-status report-status-v2 delete-refs side-band-64k quiet atomic ofs-delta object-format=sha1 agent=git/2.53.0-Linux
push< 0000
push> 55a65a7c416708dcd9ef8d088e06bd976bbb2ee7 064404514c22b17d611376d0eebf11faeef3fd6b refs/heads/main\0 report-status-v2 side-band-64k quiet object-format=sha1 agent=git/2.53.0-Linux
push> 0000
push< unpack ok
push< ok refs/heads/main
push< 0000

The client's request line is <old-id> <new-id> <ref> — and the <old-id> is the whole safety mechanism. The server compares it against what the ref currently holds and refuses if they differ, which is how a push is rejected when someone else pushed first. git push --force sends 0000000… as the old id, meaning "whatever it is, overwrite it". git push --force-with-lease sends the id you actually observed, which is the difference between "I don't care what's there" and "I checked, and it was this" (§8.9).

Setting the new id to all zeros is a deletion, which is what git push origin --delete branch and the older git push origin :branch both compile down to.

The response is two parts: unpack ok says the pack was received and stored, and then one line per ref saying whether that ref moved. They are separate because a push can succeed at transferring objects and still be rejected by a hook (§13.7) or by branch protection. The atomic capability turns the per-ref results into all-or-nothing.

One forge-side note on the same mechanism. Branch protection on a hosting provider is implemented as a pre-receive hook (§13.7) that inspects exactly the <old-id> <new-id> <ref> lines above, which is why branch protection can distinguish a force push from an ordinary one — the old id it was sent does not match.

11.8 Hosting a Repository

The minimum viable Git server is a bare repository on a machine you can reach over SSH. There is nothing else to install.

git init --bare /srv/git/project.git

A bare repository is a repository with no working tree: the contents of .git sit at the top level, and core.bare is true. Pushing to a non-bare repository is refused by default, because updating the current branch would leave that repository's working tree and index disagreeing with HEAD — and Git has no way to fix that safely from the far side.

Two details make a bare repository behave correctly:

git --git-dir=/srv/git/project.git symbolic-ref HEAD refs/heads/main
git --git-dir=/srv/git/project.git config receive.denyNonFastForwards true

The first sets the default branch clones will check out — the symref=HEAD capability from §11.4 is read straight off this file, and a bare repository created before init.defaultBranch was set will hand out master forever until you change it. The second rejects any push that would discard commits, which is the server-side half of §8.11's rule about published history.

For SSH access, the accounts do not need shells. git-shell is a restricted login shell that accepts only Git's own commands:

chsh -s "$(command -v git-shell)" git

Every user then shares the git account and is distinguished by their SSH key in ~git/.ssh/authorized_keys, which is exactly how the hosting providers do it.

Three other server-side setups, and when each is right:

Smart HTTP
Run git http-backend as CGI behind any web server. Gives you HTTPS, ordinary web authentication, and firewall traversal. This is what to deploy if SSH is unavailable.
git daemon
git daemon --export-all --base-path=/srv/git serves git:// read-only with no authentication. Fast, trivial, and appropriate only for public read-only mirrors on a trusted network.
A forge
GitLab, Gitea, Forgejo. Everything above plus issues, review, CI and access control. The right answer for a team, and none of it changes the protocol underneath — §12 is about the workflow layer these add.

The SSH key arrangement is worth stating plainly because it is the one piece of server setup that is not obvious: every developer's public SSH key goes into ~git/.ssh/authorized_keys on the server, and the SSH key is the entire authentication story. There are no Git accounts, no passwords and no user database.

11.9 Bundles: Git Without a Network

A bundle is a packfile plus a ref list in a single file. It is a complete, verifiable repository transfer that can travel by any means at all — email, USB stick, an artefact store, a machine with no route to your Git server.

git bundle create project.bundle --all
git bundle verify project.bundle
git clone project.bundle restored

git bundle verify checks the pack's integrity and reports which objects the bundle requires but does not contain — because a bundle can be incremental:

git bundle create update.bundle main~10..main

That contains only the last ten commits and records a prerequisite. Applying it to a repository that lacks main~10 fails cleanly rather than corrupting anything, which makes bundles a safe way to ship updates into an air-gapped environment. A bundle is also the correct way to hand someone a repository for debugging: it is one file, it is verifiable, and unlike a tar of .git it cannot carry your config, your credentials, or your other branches unless you ask it to.

11.10 Credentials, and Where They Actually Live

Git never asks the network layer for credentials directly. It runs a credential helper: a separate program that speaks a tiny key-value protocol on stdin and stdout. You can drive it by hand, which is the fastest way to understand it.

git config --global credential.helper 'store --file=./creds'
printf 'protocol=https\nhost=example.com\nusername=ada\npassword=hunter2\n\n' | git credential approve
cat creds

https://ada:hunter2@example.com

Look at that file. The store helper writes your password to disk in plain text, as a URL, with file permissions as its only protection. It is the default suggestion in a great deal of old documentation and it is the wrong choice on any machine with more than one user or any backup you do not control.

Retrieval is the fill verb:

printf 'protocol=https\nhost=example.com\n\n' | git credential fill

protocol=https
host=example.com
username=ada
password=hunter2

And reject deletes it. Those three verbs — fill, approve, reject — are the whole interface, which is why writing a helper for a corporate secret store is a twenty-line script.

store
Plain text on disk, forever. Avoid.
cache
A daemon holding it in memory for --timeout seconds (900 by default). Nothing is written to disk. Good on a shared build machine.
OS keychain
libsecret on Linux, osxkeychain on macOS, manager on Windows. Encrypted at rest and unlocked with your login. The right default on a workstation.
SSH keys
Sidesteps the whole mechanism. A passphrase-protected key held by ssh-agent is never transmitted at all, and revocation is removing one public key from one file.

Two rules that prevent most credential incidents. Never put a token in a remote URL — https://user:token@host/repo.git ends up in .git/config, in every git remote -v, and in any log that records the command line. And scope tokens narrowly and rotate them, because a personal access token with repo scope is a password to every repository you can see, and unlike a password it is often pasted into CI configuration by hand.

credential.<url>.helper scopes a helper to one host, which is how you use a corporate secret store for the company forge and the keychain for everything else.

A credential helper is chosen with credential.helper, and credential.<url>.helper scopes a credential helper to one host. Whichever you pick, a personal access token should be treated as a password with a scope and an expiry — and if you use SSH instead, ssh-agent holds the decrypted key for the session so the passphrase is typed once. Running ssh-agent is what makes a passphrase-protected key tolerable.

11.11 Signing Commits and Tags

Git records who wrote a commit as an unverified string. The author line is whatever user.email says, and setting it to someone else's address takes one command. Signing is what turns an assertion into evidence.

There are two mechanisms. A GPG key is the original arrangement, selected with gpg.format = openpgp (the default) and used by git commit --gpg-sign, whose short form is -S; it works well and requires the recipient to have your public key, historically from a key server. SSH signing, added in Git 2.34, uses the key you already have and needs no key server, so it is the one to reach for now.

ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -N '' -C 'ada@example.com' -f signkey
git config gpg.format ssh
git config user.signingkey "$PWD/signkey.pub"
git commit -q -S -m 'signed with ssh'
git cat-file -p HEAD

tree b4ed918248039b78f24383523fa4e51f80994fac
author Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000
committer Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> 1787824800 +0000
gpgsig -----BEGIN SSH SIGNATURE-----
 U1NIU0lHAAAAAQAAADMAAAALc3NoLWVkMjU1MTkAAAAg//0lWj/R/SB22Wn/KE49zE1iQa
 3ifViBu/TAk9laBQUAAAADZ2l0AAAAAAAAAAZzaGE1MTIAAABTAAAAC3NzaC1lZDI1NTE5
 AAAAQCO2PkxnDuEJdLsQSKl1W+odzoD5EfFpgGz5wbWqHwRDCdWcUsyeoYa8wK+6itkZOR
 x31qJ5LM+c6/u25ka7VgI=
 -----END SSH SIGNATURE-----

signed with ssh

This is §3.6's commit format with one more header. The header is called gpgsig even for an SSH signature, for backward compatibility, and its continuation lines are indented by a single space in the classic RFC-822 style. Two consequences follow directly from it being inside the object:

  • The signature is part of the hashed content, so it covers the tree, the parents, the author, the committer and the message. Change any of them and the commit id changes and the signature no longer applies to anything.
  • Signing changes the commit id. A commit cannot be signed after the fact without rewriting it, and rebasing a signed commit destroys the signature unless you re-sign (git rebase --exec 'git commit --amend --no-edit -S', or git rebase -S).

Turn it on permanently with commit.gpgsign = true. Tags sign the same way with git tag -s, producing a tag object (§3.8) whose message block carries the signature — which is why annotated tags exist at all and why release tags should always be annotated.

The commands, in the order you will need them: ssh-keygen to make the key, git config commit.gpgsign true to sign every commit without asking, git tag -s for a signed release tag, and git commit --gpg-sign (or -S) for a single commit when commit.gpgsign is off. A signed tag is the higher-value one of the two, because a release tag is what other people actually verify.

The choice between a GPG key and SSH signing is mostly about distribution: a GPG key has to reach the verifier somehow, and SSH signing reuses a key they very likely already have. SSH signing also needs no extra software, which is why it is the recommendation here.

11.12 Verifying — and What a Signature Does Not Prove

Verification needs a statement of which keys are trusted for which identities. For SSH that is an allowed_signers file:

printf 'ada@example.com %s\n' "$(cat signkey.pub)" > allowed_signers
git config gpg.ssh.allowedSignersFile "$PWD/allowed_signers"
git log --show-signature -1

commit 3702b91d31df42f7fc8add9780661018fc981da1
