Good "git" signature for ada@example.com with ED25519 key SHA256:lOwclNpL2vgMUB0oAD4XsPKEH9Cigdofekv6CJj0DD4
Author: Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com>
Date:   Thu Aug 27 10:00:00 2026 +0000

    signed with ssh

Without that file, the same command reports something you must read carefully:

error: gpg.ssh.allowedSignersFile needs to be configured and exist for ssh signature verification
commit 3702b91d31df42f7fc8add9780661018fc981da1
No signature

"No signature" here does not mean the commit is unsigned. It means verification could not be attempted. A script that greps for Good signature is fine; one that treats the absence of BAD as success will accept everything. Use the porcelain format instead, where the states are distinct single characters:

git log --format='%h %G? %s' -2

4e04a17 N unsigned
3702b91 G signed with ssh
G
Good signature.
B
Bad signature — the content does not match.
U
Good signature, unknown validity.
X
Good signature that has expired.
Y
Good signature made by an expired key.
R
Good signature made by a revoked key.
E
Signature could not be checked — missing key, or the misconfiguration above.
N
No signature.

git verify-commit and git verify-tag do the same check with an exit status, which is what a hook or a CI step should use:

git verify-commit HEAD~1
git verify-commit HEAD

The signed commit exits 0 and the unsigned one exits 1. git merge --verify-signatures refuses to merge a branch whose tip is not validly signed, and git pull --verify-signatures extends that to the pull.

Now the honest part, because signing is routinely oversold.

It does prove
That the holder of a particular private key vouched for this exact tree, message, parents and author line. Because the signature covers the parent ids, and each parent covers its parents, signing one commit transitively vouches for the entire history behind it.
It does not prove
That the code is good, that the author line is honest — you can sign a commit authored by anyone — or that the key belongs to who you think. That last one is entirely the allowed_signers file's job, and a file everyone edits without review is a trust root nobody is guarding.

The verified badge on a forge is exactly this check with the forge's own copy of your public key, which means it proves the commit was made by someone holding a key registered to that account. It is a strong statement about account, and a weaker one about person, and it says nothing whatever about the diff.

git verify-tag is the tag counterpart of git verify-commit and takes the same exit-status contract, so git verify-tag v1.4.0 in a release script is one line. For interactive use, git log --show-signature and git show --show-signature print the verification result alongside the commit.

Exercises

1. A colleague reports that git fetch on your company's self-hosted server takes eleven seconds even when there is nothing to fetch. What single command would you run to diagnose it, and what would you expect to see?

Solution 
GIT_TRACE_PACKET=1 git fetch origin main 2>&1 | head -40

Look for ref-prefix lines in the client's output. If they are present, the server negotiated protocol v2 and the slowness is elsewhere — object negotiation, a slow disk, a hook.

If instead the trace begins with thousands of inbound <sha> refs/… lines before the client says anything, the server is speaking v0 (§11.4) and is advertising every ref on every connection. On a repository with a large number of tags or refs/pull/* entries, that advertisement alone accounts for the eleven seconds.

The fix is server-side: upgrade the server, or set receive.advertiseObjectInfo/protocol.version=2 in its configuration. A useful stopgap on the client is git fetch origin main rather than a bare git fetch, and remote.origin.tagOpt = --no-tags if tags are the bulk of it.

2. Why can a push be rejected after the objects have already been transferred successfully? Name two mechanisms, and say what the wire looks like in each case.

Solution

The protocol has two separate results (§11.7): unpack ok reports on receiving and storing the pack, and a per-ref line reports on moving the ref. Objects can arrive perfectly and the ref update still be refused.

Two mechanisms:

  1. Non-fast-forward. The client sent <old-id> <new-id> <ref> and the server's ref no longer holds <old-id>, because someone else pushed in the meantime. The response is ng refs/heads/main non-fast-forward (or fetch first). receive.denyNonFastForwards produces the same shape for a deliberate force push.
  2. A server hook. pre-receive or update (§13.7) exited non-zero — a commit-message policy, a signature requirement, a branch-protection rule on a forge. The response is ng refs/heads/main <the hook's message>, and the hook's stderr is relayed to the user over the side-band, which is why forge error messages can be so descriptive.

In both cases the objects are on the server. They are simply unreachable, and its next gc prunes them (§15.5).

3. You have a repository on a machine with no network route to your Git server, and a USB stick. Get three weeks of commits across, and explain how the receiving side verifies it got everything.

Solution

On the isolated machine, bundle a range rather than the whole history — the receiving repository already has everything up to the point they diverged:

git bundle create /media/usb/update.bundle origin/main..main

On the receiving machine:

git bundle verify /media/usb/update.bundle
git fetch /media/usb/update.bundle main:refs/remotes/isolated/main

git bundle verify does two things: it validates the packfile's checksum, and it lists the bundle's prerequisites — the objects the bundle refers to but does not contain, which is exactly the boundary the .. range created. If the receiving repository is missing any prerequisite, verify says so and the fetch fails cleanly rather than importing a broken graph.

If you cannot be sure what the far side has, git bundle create full.bundle --all is self-contained: no prerequisites, and it can be cloned directly.

4. A CI job stores a personal access token by running git config --global credential.helper store. Name three distinct problems and give a better arrangement.

Solution
  1. Plain text at rest. The token lands in the helper's file as https://user:token@host (§11.10). Any process running as that user reads it, and it survives into any snapshot or backup of the build image.
  2. No expiry. store never forgets. A container image layer built with that file in it carries a live credential wherever the image goes.
  3. Scope. A token with repo scope reaches every repository the account can see, so a compromise of one build reaches all of them.

Better, in increasing order of effort:

  • Use credential.helper 'cache --timeout=…', which keeps it in a daemon's memory only.
  • Better: supply the credential through the environment for the life of the job and never persist it — most CI systems inject a short-lived, repository-scoped token automatically for exactly this reason.
  • Best: no long-lived secret at all. Use the CI provider's OIDC/workload identity to mint a token scoped to the one repository, valid for the length of the job.

And in all cases, never write the token into the remote URL, where git remote -v and every shell trace will print it.

5. All commits on main show G in %G?. What can you conclude about the code on main, and what can you not?

Solution

You can conclude that for each commit, someone holding a private key listed in your allowed_signers file signed that exact object — its tree, its parents, its author and committer lines, and its message. Because each signature covers the parent ids, and the parents' signatures cover theirs, this transitively attests the whole ancestry: no commit was inserted, removed or altered without a signature failing.

You cannot conclude:

  • That the author line is true. The signature binds the key to the object, not to the author string inside it. A key holder can sign a commit whose author is anyone.
  • That the key belongs to the person you have in mind. That comes entirely from allowed_signers, which is an ordinary file. If it is committed to the repository and anyone can add a line to it, it is not a trust root.
  • Anything about the code. A signature is not review. It is provenance.
  • That nothing was lost. Signatures verify what is present. A commit dropped during a rebase leaves a perfectly valid signed history that is simply missing it — which is what §8.8's git range-diff exists to catch.

6. Read this pkt-line frame and say exactly how many bytes are on the wire: 0032want 55a65a7c416708dcd9ef8d088e06bd976bbb2ee7.

Solution

0032 is hexadecimal for 50, and a pkt-line's length includes its own four length bytes (§11.3). So there are 50 bytes on the wire, of which 46 are payload.

Check the payload: want is 5 characters, the object id is 40, and a trailing newline makes 46. 46 + 4 = 50 = 0x32. ✓

This is the arithmetic that lets you read a raw curl of info/refs without any tooling, and it is worth doing once by hand — it makes the difference between the protocol being a black box and being a text format you can debug.

SECTION 12

Collaboration Workflows and the Forge

Nothing in Git tells you how to work with other people. The tool gives you commits, branches, remotes and merges; every "workflow" is a convention layered on top, and the conventions differ enough between projects that arriving with the wrong one is a reliable way to annoy a maintainer.

This section is about those conventions: what the main ones are, what each optimises for, and — more usefully — how to read an unfamiliar project and work out which one it uses before your first contribution.

The mechanics were all established earlier. This is about the decisions: when a branch should be merged rather than rebased, what a commit message is for, why a maintainer wants your patches one way rather than another, and which parts of a forge's interface are Git and which are the forge's own database.

12.1 Four Workflows

Pro Git names four arrangements, and they are still the complete list — every real project is one of them or a mixture.

Centralized workflow
One repository, everyone pushes to it. Simplest possible arrangement and correct for a team of two or three who trust each other. Its failure mode is the push race of §10.8, which is why pull.ff = only matters here more than anywhere.
Integration-manager workflow
Everyone has their own public repository; one person's is canonical. Contributors push to their own and ask the integrator to pull. This is what a fork plus a pull request is — the forge did not invent it, it gave it a button.
Dictator and lieutenants
The same, one level deeper: lieutenants integrate a subsystem each, and the dictator merges the lieutenants. Scales to thousands of contributors and is how the Linux kernel works. The cost is latency — a change can take weeks to reach the top.
Patches by email
No shared repository at all: contributions travel as email (§12.8). Git itself, the kernel, and much of the older free-software world work this way, and Git has first-class support for it because that is what it was built for.

The one thing they share is that integration is a person's job. In every arrangement someone decides what lands, and the workflow is mostly about making that decision cheap: small changes, clear messages, a branch that applies cleanly.

The names are worth being able to place. The centralized workflow and the integration-manager workflow differ only in who may write to the canonical repository; the dictator and lieutenants arrangement adds a layer of integrators between contributors and the top. All three are social arrangements built from the same push and fetch.

12.2 Branching Models

Independently of who pushes where, projects differ in what their branches mean.

Trunk-based development
One long-lived branch. Feature branches live hours to days and merge back constantly; anything unfinished hides behind a feature flag. Optimised for continuous deployment: main is always releasable, and merge conflicts stay small because nothing diverges for long.
GitHub flow
Trunk-based with the branch made visible: branch, push, open a pull request, review, merge, delete. The dominant model for web services.
Git flow
main, develop, feature/*, release/* and a hotfix branch per urgent production fix. Designed for versioned software with supported releases — the release branches are the point. Widely applied to web services, where the extra branches buy nothing and its own author has said as much.
Release branches
Trunk plus a long-lived branch per supported version, with fixes cherry-picked back (§12.7). What you need when customers run several versions at once, and the minimum that actually requires backporting.

The question that decides between them is not team size, it is how many versions you support at once. One (a deployed service) means trunk-based. Several (a library, a database, an operating system) means release branches, and the ceremony is unavoidable.

A related decision is whether to merge with --no-ff (§6.2). Doing so makes git log --first-parent (§9.7) a changelog of features rather than of commits — the measured difference on one Git release was 758 commits against 161 integrations. If you want that view, you must merge; squashing and fast-forwarding both destroy it.

In practice you will meet trunk-based development and GitHub flow most often — they are nearly the same thing — and git flow in older or release-oriented projects. What actually distinguishes them is whether a hotfix branch is a defined concept: in trunk-based development an urgent fix is an ordinary short branch, and in git flow a hotfix branch has its own rules about where it merges back.

12.3 What Makes a Good Commit

Two properties, and the second is the one people skip.

A commit should be one logical change. Not one file, not one day's work: one thing that can be described in a sentence, reviewed on its own, reverted on its own, and — crucially — bisected through (§9.10). A commit that changes a data structure and fixes an unrelated typo cannot be reverted without collateral damage. git add -p (§2.6) is the tool for splitting a messy working tree into such commits, and §8.5's recipe splits one after the fact.

A commit message should say why. The diff already says what. A message that reads fix bug or update parser is a message that costs its reader a git blame and a guess in two years' time.

The conventional shape, which is the format git log --oneline, git format-patch (§12.8) and every forge assume:

feat: add retry logic to the fetch helper

The upstream API returns 503 under load roughly once in a thousand
requests, which surfaced as intermittent test failures rather than as
anything users noticed.  Retrying twice with a 200ms backoff removes it
without meaningfully changing latency.

Chose a fixed backoff over exponential because the failure is transient
rather than load-shedding — see the discussion in #412.

Refs: #412
Subject ≤ 50 characters
Because git log --oneline, forge lists and git shortlog all truncate. Fifty is a target, seventy-two is a hard limit.
Imperative mood
"Add retry logic", not "Added" or "Adds". Git's own generated messages use it — "Merge branch", "Revert" — so the mood is consistent when read as a list of instructions.
Blank line, then the body
Git parses the blank line: everything before it is %s, everything after is %b. Without it, the whole message becomes one long subject.
Body wrapped at 72
Seventy-two characters, because Git does not wrap and git log indents by four. Longer lines run off the screen in every tool.
Trailers at the end
Signed-off-by:, Co-authored-by:, Refs:, Fixes:. Machine-readable — git interpret-trailers and git shortlog --group=trailer:Co-authored-by both read them.

Conventional Commits — the type(scope): summary prefix, with types like feat, fix, docs, refactor, test, chore — is a widely used convention on top of this, and the reason to adopt it is not tidiness: it lets tooling derive a changelog and a semantic version bump automatically. feat bumps the minor version, fix the patch, and a BREAKING CHANGE: trailer bumps the major. Enforce it with a commit-msg hook (§13.8) and check it in CI, since the hook is bypassable.

Signed-off-by: deserves a note because it is often misunderstood. git commit -s adds it, and in projects that use the Developer Certificate of Origin it is a legal assertion that you have the right to contribute the code. It is not a cryptographic signature — that is git commit -S (§11.11), which lowercases to the same thing in text and is a completely different mechanism.

Three numbers to hold: a subject under fifty characters, a hard limit of seventy-two characters on any line, and the imperative mood throughout. The imperative mood is the one that feels unnatural at first — "Fix the parser" rather than "Fixed the parser" — and the justification is that Git's own generated messages use it, so a log read as a list is consistent.

12.4 The Fork-and-Pull-Request Model

The mechanics, entirely in terms of §10:

1
Fork — the forge makes a server-side copy under your account. Nothing Git-specific happens; it is a clone on their machine.
2
Clone your fork, and add the original as a second remote: git remote add upstream … (§10.11). Your origin is the fork; upstream is canonical.
3
Branch from upstream/main, never from your fork's main, which drifts. git switch -c fix-parser upstream/main.
4
Commit and push to your fork: git push -u origin fix-parser.
5
Open a pull request — called a merge request on GitLab — a forge record saying "please merge this ref in my repository into that ref in yours". It is the git request-pull of §12.8 with a database row and a comment thread.
6
Iterate. More commits pushed to the same branch appear in the same pull request automatically, because the pull request references the branch rather than a fixed set of commits.

Keeping the branch current while it is under review is where people go wrong. Two options, and they are not equivalent:

git fetch upstream
git rebase upstream/main
git push --force-with-lease --force-if-includes

Rebasing keeps the branch a clean series that will fast-forward on merge, and it requires a force push (§8.9) which rewrites the commits reviewers may already have commented on. Most forges cope, showing "force-pushed" and letting reviewers diff versions — and git range-diff (§8.8) is how you check you changed only what you meant to.

git merge upstream/main

Merging never rewrites anything, so review comments stay anchored, at the cost of merge commits inside the branch that the eventual squash or rebase will throw away anyway.

The rule of thumb: rebase while nobody has reviewed it, merge once review is in progress, and follow whatever the project says if it says anything.

gh and equivalents drive all of this from the terminal — gh repo clone owner/name, gh pr create, gh pr checkout 412, gh pr status — and the one worth learning first is gh pr checkout, because reviewing a change in your editor with the tests running beats reading a diff in a browser.

gh repo clone owner/name is a small convenience worth knowing — it clones and, for a fork, configures the upstream remote for you — and gh pr create --fill opens the merge request or pull request from the branch you are on without leaving the terminal.

12.5 Reviewing a Branch

Code review is the point of all of this machinery, and three commands do most of the work. The first is the one people get wrong.

git fetch upstream
git diff upstream/main...feature

Three dots. In git diff that means "diff feature against the merge base of the two" (§9.5) — the change the branch actually introduces, with everything that landed on main meanwhile excluded. Two dots would additionally show, inverted, every commit main gained since the branch started, which on a week-old branch is most of the diff. This is exactly what a forge's "Files changed" tab shows, and knowing the syntax means you can reproduce it locally.

git log --oneline --reverse upstream/main..feature

The commits in order, oldest first — the order the author intends them to be read. Reviewing a series commit by commit catches things a combined diff hides: a debugging statement added and removed, a function introduced in one commit and rewritten in the next, a change that is not what its message claims.

git range-diff 'upstream/main...feature@{1}' upstream/main...feature

After the author force-pushes, this shows what changed between the two versions of the series (§8.8), which is the review question on round two. The < marker — a commit present before and gone now — is the one to look for.

Two more that pay off:

git diff --stat …
Shape first: which files, how big. A pull request touching forty files and one touching two need different reviews.
git log -p --reverse …
Each commit with its diff, in order. The full careful read.

git diff --stat is the right thing to run before any of these: a review of a change whose shape you have not seen is a review that misses the file nobody expected to be touched. Good code review starts by asking what the change is, and git diff --stat answers that in one screen.

12.6 Landing a Branch

Three buttons, three different histories, and the choice is not aesthetic — it changes what later Git commands can tell you.

Merge commit
--no-ff. Every commit is preserved and the branch's boundary is recorded, so git log --first-parent reads as one line per feature (§9.7) and reverting the whole feature is one git revert -m 1 (§7.5). Cost: a busy git log, and every messy work-in-progress commit is now permanent.
Squash
One commit on main per pull request. Clean, easy to revert, and the dominant choice for web services. Cost: it records no relationship to the branch (§6.2), so git branch --merged will not list it and a later merge of the same branch conflicts against work that is already present. Delete the branch afterwards — it is the only remaining evidence.
Rebase and merge
Every commit replayed onto main, linear, no merge commit. Keeps the commits and the linearity. Cost: no record that they were one unit, so --first-parent gains nothing and reverting a feature means reverting n commits.

Squashing is the right default for a team shipping a service continuously, because a pull request is the unit of change and nobody wants the eleven commits that produced it. Merging with --no-ff is right when the individual commits are genuinely worth keeping — a carefully structured series, a refactor split into reviewable steps — which is more often true in libraries and infrastructure than in application code.

Whatever the project chooses, be consistent: a history that mixes all three is one where no single git log invocation tells the truth.

12.7 Cherry-Picking and Backports

Release branches need fixes without the features that landed alongside them. git cherry-pick applies one commit's diff somewhere else, producing a new commit with the same author and message and a different object id (§8.1).

git switch release-1.0
git cherry-pick -x <the-fix>
git log -1 --format='%B'

fix: guard against a null input

(cherry picked from commit 54afc60c7e80674ce9dd323c550a681fe7e2136e)

Always use -x when backporting. The trailer it adds is the only record connecting the two commits, and it is what lets someone six months later find out whether a fix reached a release branch. Without it the two commits are unrelated as far as Git is concerned.

Git can also work it out from content:

git cherry -v main release-1.0

- 7814a3334d3630a2dd5f5ca49bfd4d7172d90a15 fix: guard against a null input

git cherry compares two branches by patch-id — a hash of the diff, ignoring commit metadata, whitespace and context line numbers — rather than by object id. The - marks a commit whose change is already present upstream, + one that is not. So the cherry-picked commit, with a completely different id, is correctly recognised as already applied. This is what makes "which of my patches have landed" answerable, and it is the same mechanism git rebase uses to silently drop commits that are already upstream.

git cherry-pick A..B
A range, in order.
git cherry-pick -n
Apply to the index without committing — for combining several into one.
git cherry-pick -m 1
Cherry-pick a merge commit, specifying which parent's side to take.
--continue / --skip / --abort
As with rebase; a cherry-pick is a three-way merge and can conflict (§6).

The signal that you are cherry-picking too much: if most fixes need backporting to three branches, the branches have diverged too far, and the cost is now paid on every fix rather than once on a merge.

git cherry-pick -x is the form to use for backports and git cherry-pick -n is the form to use when combining several commits into one — git cherry-pick -n A B C applies all three to the index and lets you write a single message.

12.8 The Email Workflow

No forge, no shared repository, no accounts: patches as email. Git's support for this is complete and is worth knowing even if you never use it, because it is how you contribute to Git, the Linux kernel, and a great deal of infrastructure.

git format-patch main --cover-letter -o ../patches
ls ../patches

0000-cover-letter.patch
0001-feat-add-feature-A.patch
0002-feat-add-feature-B.patch

Each file is a complete mbox-format email:

From 593ecebee135d220ebde7556f541052f9da37169 Mon Sep 17 00:00:00 2001
From: Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com>
Date: Thu, 27 Aug 2026 10:00:00 +0000
Subject: [PATCH 1/2] feat: add feature A

This is the body of the message, wrapped at 72 columns so that it reads
correctly in git log and in an email client.
---
 app.py | 1 +
 1 file changed, 1 insertion(+)

diff --git a/app.py b/app.py
index 626799f..c7aee86 100644
--- a/app.py
+++ b/app.py
@@ -1 +1,2 @@
 v1
+feature A
-- 
2.53.0

The commit message is the email body, which is why §12.3's rules about subject length and wrapping are not stylistic. [PATCH 1/2] numbers the series so a maintainer's tooling can order it.

The cover letter is a template to fill in:

Subject: [PATCH 0/2] *** SUBJECT HERE ***

*** BLURB HERE ***

Ada Lovelace (2):
  feat: add feature A
  feat: add feature B

 app.py | 2 ++
 1 file changed, 2 insertions(+)

It is where the rationale goes — what problem the series solves, what you considered and rejected, what you are unsure about. On a mailing list it is what people reply to.

git send-email sends them, threaded, with [PATCH v2] numbering via -v2. On the receiving end:

git am ../patches/000[12]-*.patch
git log --pretty='%h %an <%ae> | %cn <%ce> | %s' -3

Applying: feat: add feature A
Applying: feat: add feature B
32cbf1f Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> | Linus Maintainer <maint@example.com> | feat: add feature B
10edfc1 Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> | Linus Maintainer <maint@example.com> | feat: add feature A
459dc6e Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> | Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> | initial

There is §3.6's two-identity design doing exactly the job it was built for. The author stays the contributor; the committer becomes the maintainer. Attribution is preserved through a workflow with no shared repository and no accounts — which is precisely why Git records the two separately, and why git log showing only the author is the right default.

git am --3way (short form -3) falls back to a three-way merge when a patch does not apply cleanly, which needs the blobs the patch references — hence the index 626799f..c7aee86 line in every patch, which exists for exactly this.

Beneath git am is git apply, which applies a patch's diff without reading its message or making a commit — the right tool for a patch that arrived from anywhere other than format-patch. git apply --check reports whether it would apply and changes nothing, which is what a CI job should run before trying.

A cherry-pick can pause the same way a rebase can, and git cherry-pick --continue resumes it after you have staged the resolution.

And if a contributor does have a public repository, git request-pull generates the message asking you to fetch from it:

git request-pull main <url> feature

The following changes since commit 459dc6e0cc591e39db1bda5d398c9cc8b698b9ff:

  initial (2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000)

are available in the Git repository at:

  <url> feature

for you to fetch changes up to 8b312f129f931bcb2841d46ac01643a61853dc58:

  feat: add feature B (2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000)

That is a pull request, in the original sense of the phrase, from before it was a button.

git send-email is the piece with the most setup, because it needs SMTP credentials in [sendemail]; git format-patch --stdout | git am is how you test the round trip locally without any of that. The mbox format those files use is the same one mail clients have used for decades, which is why a patch pasted into an email body still applies with git am, and why a cover letter is simply message zero of the series. When a patch does not apply, git am --3way and then git apply --check are the two diagnostics, and git cherry-pick --continue is the equivalent step when the same thing happens during a cherry-pick.

12.9 Releases and Tags

A release is an annotated, ideally signed tag (§3.8, §11.11):

git tag -s v1.4.0 -m 'Release 1.4.0'
git push --follow-tags

-s signs it; -a at minimum, so the tag records who made it and when. --follow-tags pushes the branch and any annotated tags reachable from it, without pushing your local scratch tags (§10.10).

Semantic versioning
MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH: breaking, feature, fix. It is a promise to consumers about what an upgrade can do to them, which is why it is worth being strict about the major number.
Never move a tag
Anyone who fetched it keeps the old value silently — a fetch will not clobber an existing tag without --force (§10.10). Cut v1.4.1 instead.
Generate the notes
git shortlog --no-merges v1.3.0..v1.4.0 groups commits by author with subjects; git log --first-parent --oneline v1.3.0..v1.4.0 lists integrations (§9.7). Conventional Commits makes this fully automatic.
Stamp the build
git describe --tags --dirty (§9.12) inside the repository; export-subst (§13.12) for a source tarball, which has no .git.

git describe --tags is what a build script should call rather than plain git describe, since git describe --tags considers lightweight tags too and will not fail on a repository whose releases were tagged casually. Semantic versioning is the convention the tag names follow, and a signed tag is what makes the release verifiable.

A changelog generated from the history is the payoff for all of §12.3's discipline: git shortlog produces a changelog draft grouped by author, and Conventional Commits makes a changelog derivable without any human step at all.

12.10 Protection, Ownership and CI

The forge layer that is not Git. It matters because §13.7 established that client-side hooks are advisory — anything that must actually hold has to run where the contributor cannot bypass it.

Protected branches
Refuse direct pushes and force pushes to main. This is receive.denyNonFastForwards (§11.8) with a web interface, and it is the single most valuable setting on the list.
Required status checks
A pull request cannot merge until named continuous integration jobs pass. The enforcement layer for everything a pre-commit hook only suggests.
Required reviews
n approvals, optionally dismissed when new commits are pushed.
CODEOWNERS
A tracked file mapping path globs to people or teams, who are then requested as reviewers automatically. Version-controlled ownership.
Merge queue
Tests each pull request against the result of merging it, in order, rather than against main as it was when the branch was created. Fixes the "both pull requests passed CI and together they break main" failure, which no amount of per-branch testing can catch.
Signed commits required
Reject unsigned commits (§11.12). Strong provenance, and a real burden — everyone needs a key registered.

Two things worth knowing about the boundary. Pull requests are not Git objects. They live in the forge's database; the comments, approvals and review threads are not in the repository and are not in a git clone --mirror (§10.12). Migrating between forges needs their API. What is in Git is the branch — and, on GitHub, refs/pull/<n>/head, which you can fetch with a custom refspec (§10.4).

And CI is the only enforcement that survives contact with reality. A pre-commit hook is bypassed by --no-verify and is uninstalled by default; a required status check runs on the forge's machine and is not. Design accordingly: hooks for fast feedback, CI for the actual rule.

CODEOWNERS deserves emphasis because it is the only one of these that is versioned: it is a tracked file, reviewed like code, and it is how ownership stays accurate as a codebase grows. Everything else on the list lives in the forge's settings, where nobody reads it. And every one of them is enforced by continuous integration rather than by good intentions.

12.11 Reading a Project's Conventions

Before contributing to an unfamiliar project, five minutes of reading saves a rejected pull request.

CONTRIBUTING.md
Read it. It usually states the branching model, the message format, and whether they want pull requests or emails.
git log --oneline -40
The message convention, revealed by example: prefixes, capitalisation, imperative or not, whether merge commits appear.
git log --merges -10
Merge commits present means merge-based landing. Absent means squash or rebase.
git log --format='%h %p' -20
Two parents on many commits confirms merges; one everywhere confirms a linear policy.
git tag --sort=-v:refname | head
The version scheme, and whether releases are tagged at all.
ls .github/ .gitlab-ci.yml
Which CI runs, and therefore what your change must pass.
cat .gitattributes
Line endings and diff drivers you are expected to inherit (§13.9).

The general rule holds regardless of what any document says: match what the project already does. A pull request whose commits look like the surrounding history gets read; one that arrives with a different message style, a different merge assumption and a different line-ending convention gets a round of review about none of the things you wanted reviewed.

Two more files repay a look. A diff driver configured in .gitattributes (§13.9) tells you the project cares about readable diffs, and its absence in a polyglot repository is a small contribution you can make on your first day.

Exercises

1. You are reviewing a branch opened eight days ago. git diff upstream/main feature shows 900 changed lines across 40 files, most of which you recognise as other people's work. Give the correct command and explain the difference.

Solution 
git diff upstream/main...feature

Three dots. In git diff that means "diff feature against the merge base of upstream/main and feature" (§9.5) — the change this branch introduces, with everything that landed on main in the intervening eight days excluded.

Two dots (or no dots) compares the two tips, so it also shows, inverted, every commit main gained since the branch started. On a week-old branch in an active repository that is most of the output, and it makes the review impossible.

This is exactly what a forge's "Files changed" tab computes, which is why the numbers there and the numbers from a careless local git diff disagree.

The complementary command is git log --oneline --reverse upstream/main..feature — two dots here, because in git log the range means "commits reachable from feature but not upstream/main", which is the right set. The doubled meaning of ... between log and diff is worth memorising: in log it widens to both sides, in diff it narrows to one.

2. Your team squash-merges. A colleague's branch was merged last week; today they merge main into their still-open follow-up branch and get conflicts everywhere. Explain and prescribe a policy.

Solution

Squashing records no ancestry between the squashed commit on main and the branch it came from (§6.2, §12.6). The branch's original commits are not ancestors of anything on main, even though the same content is there.

So the merge base for the follow-up branch is wherever it originally diverged — before the first branch's work. Git sees their commits as new changes main lacks, and main as containing the same changes via an unrelated commit. Both sides "changed" the same lines relative to that old base, and every one conflicts.

The policy, in two parts:

  1. Delete the branch when the squash lands — the forge offers to, and taking that offer is what prevents this. Any follow-up work starts fresh from main.
  2. For a genuinely stacked branch, rebase it onto main after the parent lands rather than merging: git rebase --onto main <old-parent-tip> follow-up (§8.6). Git will drop the commits already present, recognising them by patch-id (§12.7).

If long-lived stacked branches are common, squashing is the wrong landing strategy for that team — --no-ff merges record the relationship and make every subsequent merge base correct.

3. A critical fix landed on main between two features. Backport it to release-2.3 and make the connection discoverable later.

Solution 
git switch release-2.3
git cherry-pick -x <the-fix>

-x appends (cherry picked from commit <full-id>) to the message. That trailer is the only record linking the two commits — cherry-picking produces a new commit with a new object id (§8.1), and without the trailer nothing in Git connects them.

To check afterwards which fixes have and have not been backported:

git cherry -v main release-2.3

git cherry compares by patch-id — a hash of the diff, ignoring metadata and line numbers — so it recognises the cherry-picked commit as already present despite its different id, marking it -. Commits marked + are still missing.

If the cherry-pick conflicts, the fix depends on something not on the release branch. Resolve it as an ordinary three-way merge (§6), and note in the commit message what you had to change, because the backported code now differs from the original and a future reader will need to know why.

4. Explain why git am sets a different committer from the author, and what would be lost if it did not.

Solution

git am applies a patch that arrived by email from someone with no access to the repository. The commit records two identities (§3.6): the author is preserved from the patch's From: header, and the committer becomes whoever ran git am.

32cbf1f Ada Lovelace <ada@example.com> | Linus Maintainer <maint@example.com> | feat: add feature B

If the two were collapsed into one field, one of two things would be lost. Keeping only the applier would erase attribution entirely — the contributor's name would appear nowhere, in a workflow with no accounts and no pull requests where the commit is the only record. Keeping only the contributor would erase provenance: nothing would say who chose to put this in the project's history, which is the accountable act.

The same split appears wherever a commit moves between contexts: git cherry-pick, git rebase, git commit --amend on someone else's commit. It is also why git log shows the author by default, --pretty=fuller shows both, and rebased branches look "all committed at once" in tools that sort by committer date.

5. A team enforces conventional commit messages with a commit-msg hook. Non-conforming messages keep reaching main. Diagnose and fix properly.

Solution

Client-side hooks are advisory (§13.7). Three ways past this one, all of them ordinary:

  • git commit --no-verify skips commit-msg entirely.
  • The hook lives in .git/hooks/, which is not cloned — anyone who did not run the setup step never had it.
  • Merge commits and commits made through the forge's web editor never run any local hook at all.

The fix has to run where the contributor has no control:

  1. A required status check on pull requests that validates every commit in the range, or the pull request title if you squash. This is the layer that actually holds, because it runs on the forge's machine and merging is blocked until it passes (§12.10).
  2. Keep the local hook for fast feedback — catching it at commit time is far cheaper than at review time — and distribute it via core.hooksPath and a tracked .githooks/ directory (§13.7) so it updates when people pull.
  3. If you squash-merge, the commit that lands is created from the pull request title, so that is what to validate; the branch's own messages are discarded.

The general principle: hooks are ergonomics, server-side checks are policy. A rule that exists only as a local hook is not enforced.

6. A project's history has no merge commits, tags like v3.2.1, a .github/workflows/ directory, and messages beginning fix: and feat:. What does each tell you about how to contribute?

Solution

No merge commits — the project squashes or rebases when landing (§12.6). Your branch will be flattened, so making it a beautifully structured series is wasted effort; keep it small and focused instead, and expect git log --first-parent to be meaningless here.

v3.2.1 tags — semantic versioning (§12.9), so the maintainers care whether a change is breaking, a feature, or a fix. Say which yours is; if it is breaking, say so prominently, because it forces a major bump.

Conventional Commits prefixes — almost certainly machine-read, to generate the changelog and derive the version bump. feat: bumps minor, fix: bumps patch, a BREAKING CHANGE: trailer bumps major. Getting the type wrong produces a wrong release. Check whether the pull request title is what gets validated, which it usually is when squashing.

.github/workflows/ — GitHub Actions runs on every pull request, and those checks are very likely required (§12.10). Run the same commands locally before pushing rather than discovering it in review.

The one thing none of that tells you is whether they want a rebase or a merge to keep your branch current. CONTRIBUTING.md, or the pattern in recently merged pull requests, will.

SECTION 13

Configuration, Attributes and Hooks

Three mechanisms let you change what Git does without changing Git, and they are cleanly separated by what they are keyed on.

Configuration
Keyed on where you are. Settings in files with a precedence order, scoped to a machine, a user, a repository, or a worktree. Not versioned.
Attributes
Keyed on which file. Per-path rules in .gitattributes that change how Git diffs, merges, stores and exports individual files. Versioned, so the whole team gets them.
Hooks
Keyed on which event. Executables Git runs at specific points, which can inspect and refuse. Not versioned by default, which is the central problem with them.

The versioned/not-versioned split is the thing to internalise. Anything that must be true for everyone belongs in .gitattributes, because it travels with the repository. Anything in config has to be set on each machine, and anything relying on a hook has to be installed on each machine — so a policy that only exists as a local hook is a policy that is not enforced.

13.1 Where Configuration Lives

Five files, read in order, each overriding the last:

system
/etc/gitconfig
All users on the machine. git config --system.
global
~/.gitconfig
You, everywhere. --global. Also read from ~/.config/git/config.
local
.git/config
This repository. --local, and the default when you pass no scope.
worktree
.git/worktrees/<n>/config.worktree
This worktree only, and only when extensions.worktreeConfig is on (§14.9).
command
git -c key=value …
One invocation. Beats everything.

The file format is INI-like: sections in brackets, keys indented, and — a detail worth knowing — section and key names are case-insensitive while subsection names are case-sensitive. So [User] Email = x is the same key as user.email, but [remote "Origin"] and [remote "origin"] are two different remotes. Every "my config is being ignored" report is worth checking against this.

Three environment variables override the file locations entirely: GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM, GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL and GIT_CONFIG_COUNT plus GIT_CONFIG_KEY_<n>/GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_<n>. Setting the first two to /dev/null gives a hermetic Git with no configuration at all, which is the correct way to run tests or produce reproducible output.

git config --system is the one you will almost never use, because a system-wide setting affects every user on the machine and is usually the package maintainer's business rather than yours. The per-worktree file, config.worktree, is the one you will not know exists until §14.9 needs it.

13.2 Precedence, and Finding Out Which File Won

Config precedence — "later wins" — is easy to state and hard to apply when four files are involved. Git will tell you which file supplied a value:

git config --local user.email 'local@example.com'
git config --list --show-origin --show-scope | grep user
git config user.email

global	file:/home/ada/.gitconfig	user.name=Ada Lovelace
global	file:/home/ada/.gitconfig	user.email=ada@example.com
local	file:.git/config	user.email=local@example.com
local@example.com

--show-origin gives the file, --show-scope gives the level, and git config <key> gives the winner. Together they answer every config precedence question in one command, and they are far more reliable than reasoning about which file you edited last.

Two more things worth knowing about how values combine. git config --add appends rather than replaces, and some keys are genuinely multi-valued — remote.<name>.fetch (§10.4) and include.path are the common ones. git config --get-all shows every value; plain --get shows the last, which is the one that usually wins. And --unset removes one, --unset-all removes them all, which matters when a key was added twice.

git config --local is the explicit form of the default, and writing git config --local rather than relying on the default is worth doing in scripts and in documentation, where "which file does this land in" should not be a question.

13.3 Conditional Includes

includeIf is the clean answer to having a work identity and a personal one on the same machine. Add to ~/.gitconfig:

[includeIf "gitdir:~/work/"]
	path = ~/.gitconfig-work

and in ~/.gitconfig-work:

[user]
	email = ada@work.example

git config user.email
git config --show-origin user.email

ada@work.example
file:/home/ada/.gitconfig-work	ada@work.example

Inside ~/work/anything, the work identity applies; outside it, the global one does. The trailing slash matters — gitdir:~/work/ matches the directory and everything below it, gitdir:~/work matches only that exact path.

The mechanism is git config include.path, which unconditionally pulls in another file, with includeIf adding a condition. Four condition forms exist and each solves a different problem:

gitdir:<pattern>
The repository's path. Case-sensitive glob.
gitdir/i:<pattern>
The same, case-insensitively — needed on macOS and Windows.
onbranch:<pattern>
The currently checked-out branch. Useful for making main behave differently from feature branches.
hasconfig:remote.*.url:<p>
Any remote URL matches. This is the strong one: it keys on which server the repository talks to rather than where it happens to sit on disk, so a repository cloned into the wrong directory still gets the right identity.

The single most valuable use of this is not aesthetics — it is not committing to an employer's repository with a personal address, or the reverse. A companion trick makes the failure loud rather than silent: remove user.email from the global config entirely and set it only in the included files, so a repository matching no condition refuses to commit at all with Please tell me who you are.

git config --show-origin on a single key is the fastest form of all this: it prints the value and the file it came from on one line, and is the answer to almost every "why is Git using that address" question. Note that git config include.path can appear in an included file too, so includes nest — which is convenient and occasionally confusing, and git config --show-origin untangles it.

13.4 Aliases

An alias is a config key under alias. — set with git config alias.<name> <expansion> — and there are two kinds.

git config --global alias.lg 'log --graph --oneline --decorate'
git config --global alias.unstage 'reset HEAD --'
git config --global alias.wip '!git add -A && git commit -m WIP'
git config --get-regexp '^alias\.'

alias.lg log --graph --oneline --decorate
alias.unstage reset HEAD --
alias.wip !git add -A && git commit -m WIP

Without a leading !, the value is prepended to a git command line, so git lg --all becomes git log --graph --oneline --decorate --all — extra arguments append naturally. With a leading !, the value is run by the shell from the repository root, which lets you chain commands and call other programs but means arguments no longer append usefully; a shell alias that needs arguments should be a function: alias.co = "!f() { git checkout \"$1\"; }; f".

Aliases that pay for themselves quickly:

lg
log --graph --oneline --decorate
trunk
log --first-parent --oneline — the integration view of §9.7.
unstage
reset HEAD --
last
log -1 HEAD --stat
amend
commit --amend --no-edit
please
push --force-with-lease --force-if-includes (§8.9)
wat
!git status && git log --oneline -5

Two limits. An alias cannot shadow a real Git command — defining alias.log does nothing, which is a deliberate safety measure. And aliases are not versioned, so an alias your teammates do not have is an alias you cannot put in the README's instructions.

One caution about git config alias: the expansion is stored verbatim, so a git config alias whose value contains a quote or a ! needs care in the shell. Writing aliases directly into ~/.gitconfig with an editor avoids the escaping entirely and is easier to review.

13.5 Settings Worth Turning On

Most of Git's defaults are conservative for backwards compatibility rather than because they are the best behaviour. These are the ones worth changing on a new machine, each already justified elsewhere in the course:

git config --global init.defaultBranch main
git config --global pull.ff only
git config --global merge.conflictstyle zdiff3
git config --global push.useForceIfIncludes true
git config --global rebase.autoStash true
git config --global rebase.autoSquash true
git config --global rebase.updateRefs true
git config --global fetch.prune true
git config --global diff.algorithm histogram
git config --global diff.submodule log
git config --global submodule.recurse true
git config --global column.ui auto
git config --global help.autocorrect prompt
pull.ff = only
Turns a surprise merge commit into an error you handle deliberately (§6.2).
merge.conflictstyle
zdiff3 shows the base and hoists common lines out of the conflict (§6.5). The best value-for-effort setting here.
fetch.prune
Delete remote-tracking refs for branches the server no longer has (§4.6).
diff.algorithm
histogram produces noticeably better diffs than the default myers on refactored code, at a small cost.
help.autocorrect
prompt offers the command you meant instead of running it after a delay, which the numeric settings do.
rerere.enabled
Worth adding if you rebase long-lived branches (§6.12).

git maintenance start (§15.9) belongs on the same list, per repository rather than globally.

submodule.recurse is the one on that list with the largest behaviour change: with it on, checkout, switch, pull and reset all recurse into submodules, which is what stops §14.4's "my build broke and git status is clean" problem. Turn submodule.recurse on only if you actually use submodules — on a repository without any it does nothing at all.

13.6 Ignoring Files

Four places tell Git to ignore a path, and they differ in scope rather than in syntax:

.gitignore
Tracked and shared. Applies to its own directory and below. This is where project build output belongs.
.git/info/exclude
This repository, not shared. For your own scratch files.
core.excludesFile
Global, usually ~/.gitignore. For editor and OS droppings — .DS_Store, *.swp, .idea/. These do not belong in a project's .gitignore; your choice of editor is not the project's business.
.gitattributes
Not for ignoring — but export-ignore (§13.12) excludes from archives.

The matching rules are worth stating precisely because two of them surprise people:

cat .gitignore

*.log
!important.log
build/

git check-ignore -v a.log important.log logs/keep.log build/out.o

.gitignore:1:*.log	a.log
.gitignore:2:!important.log	important.log
logs/.gitignore:1:!keep.log	logs/keep.log
.gitignore:3:build/	build/out.o

git check-ignore -v is the answer to "why is this file ignored" — it names the file, the line number and the rule. Nothing else in this area is worth guessing about when one command tells you.

Last match wins
Unlike most of Git, later patterns override earlier ones. That is what makes !important.log work.
A negation cannot resurrect
If a parent directory is excluded, a ! rule for a file inside it does nothing. Git does not descend into an excluded directory, so it never sees the file. Exclude the contents (build/*) rather than the directory when you need exceptions.
A trailing slash means directory
build/ matches only a directory; build matches a file or a directory of that name.
A leading or middle slash anchors
/build matches only at that .gitignore's level. doc/*.txt is anchored too, because it contains a slash. A pattern with no slash matches at any depth.
Deeper files can override
A .gitignore in a subdirectory takes precedence over one above it — which is how logs/.gitignore un-ignored keep.log above.

The most common real complaint is that ignoring does nothing: .gitignore only affects untracked files. A file already tracked stays tracked forever. Untrack it without deleting it:

git rm --cached path/to/file

And when a whole .gitignore has been added after the fact:

git rm -r --cached .
git add .
git commit -m 'apply .gitignore'

.git/info/exclude is the one of the four that people forget exists, and it is exactly right for a scratch file you keep in the repository but nobody else should have to know about — a notes.md, a local script. Unlike .gitignore, .git/info/exclude is not tracked, so it costs the project nothing.

13.7 Hooks: What Runs When

Hooks are executables in .git/hooks/, named for the event. Git ships samples with a .sample suffix so they do not run:

ls .git/hooks/

applypatch-msg.sample		post-update.sample	pre-rebase.sample
commit-msg.sample		pre-applypatch.sample	pre-receive.sample
fsmonitor-watchman.sample	pre-commit.sample	prepare-commit-msg.sample
push-to-checkout.sample		pre-merge-commit.sample	sendemail-validate.sample
update.sample

Each is a sample hook, and making one live is removing the .sample suffix and setting the executable bit. There is no registry and no configuration. The hook exit status is the whole interface: zero means proceed, non-zero aborts the operation for hooks that run before it.

Client-side, around committing:

pre-commit
Before the message is asked for. Sees the staged content. Non-zero aborts. The linter/formatter hook.
prepare-commit-msg
After the default message is built, before the editor opens. For inserting a ticket number from the branch name.
commit-msg
After the message is written. Gets the message file path as $1. Non-zero aborts. The message-policy hook.
post-commit
After. Cannot abort anything. Notifications only.

Client-side, around other operations: pre-rebase, post-checkout, post-merge, post-rewrite (after amend or rebase), pre-push (sees the refs about to be sent, and can refuse), pre-auto-gc, and the applypatch-* family for git am (§12.8).

Server-side, on the receiving repository:

pre-receive
Once per push, with every ref update on stdin as <old> <new> <ref>. Non-zero rejects the whole push.
update hook
Once per ref, with old and new as arguments. Non-zero rejects that ref only.
post-receive
After everything. For deployments and notifications. Cannot reject.

Server-side hooks are the only ones that are actually enforcement, because they run on a machine the contributor does not control. Everything client-side is advisory: git commit --no-verify skips pre-commit and commit-msg, and git push --no-verify skips pre-push. A client hook is a helpful early warning, and CI is the thing that actually holds the line.

The other structural problem is distribution. .git/hooks/ is not versioned and is not cloned, so a hook you write exists only for you. core.hooksPath fixes it:

git config core.hooksPath .githooks

Now hooks live in a tracked .githooks/ directory, are reviewed like code, and update when people pull — at the cost of one git config line that each person still has to run. Putting that line in a make setup target, or in a tool like pre-commit or husky, is how teams close the last gap.

The remaining client-side hooks are worth knowing by name even if you never write one. post-commit, post-checkout and post-merge all run after the thing has happened and cannot refuse it, so they are for notifications and for regenerating derived files — a post-merge hook that runs your dependency installer when the lock file changed is the classic useful one. post-rewrite runs after git commit --amend and after a rebase, which is how a tool learns that commit ids have moved. pre-auto-gc can veto the background garbage collection Git triggers on its own. applypatch-msg, pre-applypatch and post-applypatch are the git am equivalents of the committing trio, and matter only in the email workflow (§12.8).

On the server side, the update hook is the per-ref counterpart of pre-receive, and post-receive is what deployments hang off.

Two mechanical facts govern all of them. The hook exit status is the entire protocol: zero means proceed, non-zero aborts for any hook that runs before the action, and is ignored for any hook that runs after. And every one starts life as a sample hook with a .sample suffix, so ls .git/hooks shows you the complete list of what is possible on your version of Git — a better reference than any table, including this one.

13.8 Writing a Hook That Earns Its Keep

Two hooks are worth writing by hand before reaching for a framework, because they are short and they catch real things.

A commit-msg hook enforcing a message convention. $1 is the path to the file holding the message:

#!/bin/sh
if ! head -1 "$1" | grep -qE '^(feat|fix|docs|refactor|test|chore)(\(.+\))?: .{1,60}$'; then
	echo "commit-msg: subject must be 'type: summary' (conventional commits)" >&2
	echo "  got: $(head -1 "$1")" >&2
	exit 1
fi

git commit -m 'stuff'

commit-msg: subject must be 'type: summary' (conventional commits)
  got: stuff

git commit -m 'feat: add the f file'
git log --oneline

0238055 feat: add the f file

A pre-commit hook that refuses obviously broken content. The useful discovery here is that Git already has the check built in:

#!/bin/sh
if ! git diff --cached --check; then
	echo "pre-commit: whitespace errors above" >&2
	exit 1
fi

printf 'ok\n<<<<<<< HEAD\nbad\n' > g
git add g
git commit -m 'feat: add g'

g:2: leftover conflict marker
pre-commit: whitespace errors above

printf 'ok   \n' > h
git add h
git commit -m 'feat: add h'

h:1: trailing whitespace.
+ok   
pre-commit: whitespace errors above

git diff --cached --check catches trailing whitespace, space-before-tab, and leftover conflict markers — the last being the one that matters most, since committing a file with <<<<<<< in it is a mistake everyone makes eventually. Three lines of hook, no dependencies.

Four rules for hooks that stay useful:

Be fast
A pre-commit hook that takes eight seconds is a hook everyone runs with --no-verify. Check only the staged files, not the whole tree: git diff --cached --name-only --diff-filter=ACM.
Check what is staged
Not the working tree. They differ, and linting unstaged changes fails on code the commit does not contain. git stash push --keep-index before running the linter is the rigorous version.
Say why
Exit status alone tells the user nothing. Print the offending file and line to stderr.
Never be the only check
Client hooks are bypassable and uninstalled by default. The same check must exist in CI, or it is not a policy.

13.9 Attributes: .gitattributes

An attributes file assigns properties to paths, using the same pattern syntax as .gitignore:

*.txt text eol=lf
*.png binary
*.json diff=json
secrets.yml filter=redact
tests/ export-ignore

git check-attr reports what applies, which is the check-ignore of this area:

git check-attr -a notes.txt
git check-attr -a image.png
git check-attr -a data.json

notes.txt: text: set
notes.txt: eol: lf
image.png: binary: set
image.png: diff: unset
image.png: merge: unset
image.png: text: unset
data.json: diff: json

Notice what happened to image.png. The binary attribute is a macro that expands to -diff -merge -text — do not diff it, do not try to merge it, do not touch its line endings. Attributes have four states: set (attr), unset (-attr), set to a value (attr=value), and unspecified.

The most immediately useful attribute is diff=<driver>, because Git ships language definitions for hunk headers. With *.py diff=python:

git diff | grep '^@@'

@@ -7,4 +7,4 @@ def total(xs):

and without it:

@@ -7,4 +7,4 @@

The function name after the @@ is the difference between a diff you can read at a glance and one you have to scroll to place. Git has built-in patterns for ada, bash, bibtex, cpp, csharp, css, dts, elixir, fortran, fountain, golang, html, java, kotlin, markdown, matlab, objc, pascal, perl, php, python, ruby, rust, scheme and tex — and adding the right one-line .gitattributes entry for your language improves git diff, git log -p, git grep -p and git log -L :func: (§9.8) all at once.

Two related attributes:

diff=<name>
with diff.<name>.textconv set to a program: convert the file to text before diffing. This makes git diff meaningful for PDFs, spreadsheets, and images (via exiftool). The conversion is one-way and display-only — the stored blob is untouched.
merge=<name>
with merge.<name>.driver: a custom merge driver for that path. merge=ours plus merge.ours.driver = true is the standard way to say "on conflict in this generated file, keep mine, never ask" — a per-path version of §6.9's -X ours, which is a better place for that policy because it applies only where you meant it.

Four attributes make up most real .gitattributes files. The binary attribute is the macro that turns off diffing, merging and line-ending conversion at once. A diff driver named with diff=<lang> gives readable hunk headers, and pairing it with a textconv program makes even a binary format diffable — textconv runs on the file and Git diffs the text it prints. A merge driver named with merge=<name> replaces the merge algorithm for one path. And line log — git log -L :func:file — depends on the same function-pattern definition the diff driver supplies, as does git grep -p.

13.10 Line Endings

The text attribute controls newline normalisation, and getting it right removes a whole category of cross-platform noise.

The model is: in the repository, line endings are always LF. The text attribute says whether a file participates in conversion on the way in and out.

text
Normalise CRLF to LF when staging; convert on checkout according to eol or core.autocrlf.
-text
Never touch it. What binary implies.
text=auto
Normalise if Git decides the file is text. The safe default for a whole repository.
eol=lf
Always check out with LF, regardless of platform. Correct for shell scripts, which break with CRLF.
eol=crlf
Always check out with CRLF. For Windows batch files and some Visual Studio project files. The core.eol setting supplies the default for files marked text without an explicit eol.

The minimum viable .gitattributes for a cross-platform project:

* text=auto
*.sh text eol=lf
*.bat text eol=crlf
*.png binary

Prefer this to core.autocrlf. core.autocrlf is per-machine configuration that everyone has to set correctly and nobody does; .gitattributes is committed, so the repository decides once and every clone behaves the same. If you inherit a repository with CRLF already committed, git add --renormalize . restages everything through the current rules and produces one large, reviewable normalisation commit — a good candidate for .git-blame-ignore-revs (§9.11).

core.eol is the setting behind all of this and is worth understanding once: it supplies the default line ending for files marked text without an explicit eol attribute, and its default value native is what makes the same repository check out with CRLF on Windows and LF elsewhere. Setting core.eol in config is the per-machine approach; setting eol in .gitattributes is the per-project one, and the per-project one is right.

13.11 Clean and Smudge Filters, and Git LFS

A filter driver transforms file content as it crosses between the working tree and the object database. It is named by a filter=<name> attribute and defined by two config keys.

clean — on the way in
Runs on git add. Working tree → object database.
smudge — on the way out
Runs on git checkout. Object database → working tree.
git config filter.redact.clean "sed -e 's/^\(password:\).*/\1 REDACTED/'"
git config filter.redact.smudge cat
printf 'user: ada\npassword: hunter2\n' > secrets.yml
git add secrets.yml
cat secrets.yml
git cat-file -p ':secrets.yml'

user: ada
password: hunter2
user: ada
password: REDACTED

The working tree keeps the real password; the blob in the object database has it redacted. That is the mechanism working exactly as designed — and here is the part that must be said plainly:

rm secrets.yml
git checkout -- secrets.yml
cat secrets.yml

user: ada
password: REDACTED

The original is gone. The clean filter is one-way unless the smudge filter can reconstruct the value from somewhere else, and cat cannot. A filter like this is a format transformation, not a vault: it is right for stripping notebook output or normalising generated code, and it is wrong as a secrets mechanism unless the smudge side genuinely fetches the real value from a secret store.

Git LFS is this mechanism and nothing more. Watch:

git lfs install --local
git lfs track '*.bin'
cat .gitattributes

Git LFS initialized.
Tracking "*.bin"
*.bin filter=lfs diff=lfs merge=lfs -text

git config --local --get-regexp 'filter.lfs'

filter.lfs.clean git-lfs clean -- %f
filter.lfs.smudge git-lfs smudge -- %f
filter.lfs.process git-lfs filter-process
filter.lfs.required true

An attribute and a pair of filters. Commit a 200 KB binary and look at what Git stored:

git add blob.bin
git commit -m 'add a binary'
git cat-file -p HEAD:blob.bin
git cat-file -s HEAD:blob.bin

version https://git-lfs.github.com/spec/v1
oid sha256:f2b4fb1be1e435ea89f8187364ab1cfc4ac46e827fe9ec3d7dd273d74191e103
size 200000
131

A 131-byte pointer file. The 200,000 real bytes went somewhere else:

find .git/lfs -type f

.git/lfs/objects/f2/b4/f2b4fb1be1e435ea89f8187364ab1cfc4ac46e827fe9ec3d7dd273d74191e103

A separate store, uploaded to and downloaded from an LFS server alongside the normal Git transfer. Your repository's history contains only pointers, so clones stay small and git gc has nothing large to delta (§15.3).

Use it for
Large binaries that change: game assets, design files, model weights, test fixtures. Anything where §15.3's delta compression cannot help because the content is already compressed.
The costs
Every clone needs git-lfs installed, or people get pointer files instead of their assets, which is a confusing failure. It needs server support and usually storage quota. And filter.lfs.required = true means a clone without the tool fails rather than silently misbehaving — which is the right choice and still a support burden.

Adopting LFS does not shrink an existing repository: the old blobs are still in history and need git filter-repo (§8.10) — git lfs migrate import wraps exactly that.

So the pieces are: an attribute naming a filter driver, and two config keys defining it. The clean filter runs on the way in and the smudge filter on the way out, and a filter driver with only a clean filter is one-way by construction. git lfs track '<glob>' writes the attribute for you and git lfs install writes the config keys; running git lfs track with no arguments lists what is currently tracked.

13.12 Archives: export-ignore and export-subst

git archive produces a tarball or zip of a tree, and two attributes shape it.

tests/ export-ignore
.gitattributes export-ignore
VERSION export-subst

git archive --format=tar HEAD | tar -t

calc.py
notes.txt
secrets.yml

tests/ and .gitattributes are tracked and present in the working tree, and absent from the archive. This is how a release tarball omits CI configuration, test fixtures and development scaffolding without those files being untracked.

export-subst performs git log --format substitution on the file's content as it is written into the archive. With VERSION containing commit: $Format:%H$:

git archive --format=tar HEAD VERSION | tar -xO

commit: 11af6e887a280a4bb72be979cb103278922e7201
date:   2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000

The file in the repository holds the placeholders; the file in the archive holds the values. That is how a source tarball can know which commit it was built from without a build step — which matters because the archive has no .git directory and git describe (§9.12) is therefore unavailable inside it.

The related attribute is the ident attribute, which expands $Id$ to the blob's object id on checkout. It is a survival from RCS and is worth knowing only so you recognise it: it identifies the blob, not the commit, so it answers a question almost nobody has.

The ident attribute is the one to recognise and not use. ident expands $Id$ to the blob's object id on checkout, which identifies the file's content rather than the commit — so the ident attribute cannot tell you which version of the project a file came from, which is the question anyone asking has.

Exercises

1. A colleague's commits keep showing their personal address on work repositories. Fix it so that it cannot happen again, including for repositories they clone tomorrow.

Solution

Remove the identity from the global config so there is no fallback, and add conditional includes (§13.3):

# ~/.gitconfig
[includeIf "hasconfig:remote.*.url:git@github.com:acme/**"]
	path = ~/.gitconfig-work
[includeIf "gitdir:~/personal/"]
	path = ~/.gitconfig-personal

with user.email set only in the two included files, and no user.email in ~/.gitconfig itself.

Two details make this robust. Using hasconfig:remote.*.url: rather than gitdir: keys the decision on which server the repository talks to, so a work repository cloned into the wrong directory still gets the right identity. And leaving the global user.email unset means a repository matching no condition refuses to commit with Please tell me who you are — a loud failure instead of a wrong address.

Verify with git config --show-origin user.email, which names the file that supplied the value.

2. .gitignore contains build/ and !build/keep.txt, and build/keep.txt is still ignored. Explain and fix.

Solution

A negation cannot re-include a file inside an excluded directory (§13.6). Once build/ matches, Git does not descend into it, so it never evaluates any pattern against build/keep.txt.

Exclude the contents rather than the directory:

build/*
!build/keep.txt

Confirm with the command that removes all guesswork:

git check-ignore -v build/keep.txt

which prints the file and line of the rule that matched, or nothing at all when the file is not ignored.

Also worth checking: if build/keep.txt was already tracked, none of this matters — .gitignore affects untracked files only, and a tracked file stays tracked until git rm --cached removes it from the index.

3. Your team wants every commit message to reference a ticket. Design the enforcement and say what each layer can and cannot guarantee.

Solution

Three layers, and only one of them is enforcement.

A commit-msg hook (§13.8) checking the message against a pattern, distributed via core.hooksPath and a tracked .githooks/ directory. This gives fast local feedback — the commit is refused at the moment it is made, before any of the work of pushing. It guarantees nothing: git commit --no-verify skips it, and anyone who has not run the one-time git config core.hooksPath .githooks never had it.

A prepare-commit-msg hook that inserts the ticket automatically from the branch name. This is worth more than the check, because the best way to enforce a convention is to make following it require no effort.

A CI check, or a server-side pre-receive hook (§13.7) that inspects every commit in the pushed range. This is the layer that actually holds, because it runs where the contributor has no control and is not bypassable. On a forge it is a required status check on protected branches (§12.10).

The rule of thumb: client-side hooks are ergonomics, server-side checks are policy. Building only the first and calling it a policy is the common mistake.

4. Explain why Git LFS needs no support inside Git itself, and what happens when someone clones an LFS repository without git-lfs installed.

Solution

LFS is entirely built on two existing mechanisms (§13.11). git lfs track writes one line to .gitattributes:

*.bin filter=lfs diff=lfs merge=lfs -text

and git lfs install sets filter.lfs.clean and filter.lfs.smudge. On git add, the clean filter replaces the file with a small pointer — 131 bytes for a 200 KB file in the worked example — and stores the real bytes in .git/lfs/objects/, keyed by their SHA-256. On checkout, the smudge filter swaps the pointer back for the content. Git only ever sees the pointer, so no Git code knows LFS exists.

Without git-lfs installed, the filters cannot run. Because git lfs install also sets filter.lfs.required = true, Git fails loudly rather than checking out pointer files that look like corrupt assets. That is the right design — the alternative, silently getting a three-line text file where a texture should be, produces much more confusing bug reports — and it does mean every contributor and every CI image needs the tool.

Note also that adopting LFS does not shrink existing history; the old blobs remain, and removing them needs git filter-repo (§8.10), which git lfs migrate import wraps.

5. git diff on a rebased branch shows hunks headed @@ -120,7 +120,7 @@ with no context about where you are. Fix it for everyone on the team.

Solution

Add a diff driver for the language in .gitattributes (§13.9):

*.py diff=python
*.go diff=golang
*.rs diff=rust

Git ships built-in hunk-header patterns for ada, bash, bibtex, cpp, csharp, css, dts, elixir, fortran, fountain, golang, html, java, kotlin, markdown, matlab, objc, pascal, perl, php, python, ruby, rust, scheme and tex, so no configuration beyond that line is needed. The measured difference on one hunk:

@@ -7,4 +7,4 @@ def total(xs):     # with diff=python
@@ -7,4 +7,4 @@                    # without

It works for everyone because .gitattributes is tracked — unlike a config setting, which each person would have to apply. And it improves four commands at once: git diff, git log -p, git grep -p, and git log -L :funcname:file (§9.8), all of which use the same pattern to find function boundaries.

For a language Git does not know, define diff.<name>.xfuncname as a regex in config and reference it the same way — but that half is per-machine, so document it.

6. A release tarball built with git archive contains the CI configuration and test fixtures, and has no way to report which commit it came from. Fix both.

Solution

Both are .gitattributes entries (§13.12):

tests/          export-ignore
.github/        export-ignore
.gitattributes  export-ignore
.gitignore      export-ignore
VERSION         export-subst

export-ignore omits those paths from git archive output while leaving them tracked and present in every clone. Verify with git archive --format=tar HEAD | tar -t.

For provenance, put placeholders in a tracked file:

commit: $Format:%H$
date:   $Format:%ci$

export-subst runs git log --format substitution on that file as it is written into the archive, so the tarball contains the values:

commit: 11af6e887a280a4bb72be979cb103278922e7201
date:   2026-08-27 10:00:00 +0000

This matters because an archive has no .git directory, so git describe (§9.12) cannot run inside it — export-subst is the only way the extracted source can know where it came from. Adding $Format:%(describe)$ gets the human-readable version string too.

SECTION 14

Submodules, Subtrees and Worktrees

Three features share this section because they are constantly confused with one another, and the confusion is expensive. Two of them — submodules and subtrees — answer "how do I put one repository inside another". The third, worktrees, answers a completely different question: "how do I have two branches checked out at once". They are grouped here so the distinction is unmissable, because reaching for a submodule when you wanted a worktree is a week of pain.

All three are built out of machinery §3 and §4 already established. A submodule is a tree entry with a mode you have already seen. A worktree is a second index and HEAD pointing into one object database. Neither is magic, and both behave predictably once you know which files exist on disk.

14.1 Two Problems, Three Answers

Submodule
A pointer to a commit in another repository. The other repository stays separate: its history is its own, its objects are its own, and your repository records only "at this path, that project, at that commit".
Subtree
A copy of another repository's files, merged into a subdirectory of yours. Your repository contains the actual files; the other project's history is either squashed into one commit or grafted in wholesale.
Worktree
A second checkout of the same repository, on a different branch, in a different directory. One object database, one set of branches, two working trees.

The tell is what ends up in your repository's tree object. A submodule puts a 160000 gitlink there; a subtree puts real blobs there; a worktree changes nothing at all, because it is not a content feature.

14.2 Submodules: the Gitlink

Add one and look at what changed:

git submodule add ../lib vendor/lib
git status -s
cat .gitmodules

A  .gitmodules
A  vendor/lib
[submodule "vendor/lib"]
	path = vendor/lib
	url = ../lib

Two things were staged: a new file .gitmodules, and — despite vendor/lib being a directory full of files — a single entry for the path itself. Commit and look at the tree:

git commit -m 'add lib as a submodule'
git ls-tree HEAD vendor/lib
git -C vendor/lib rev-parse HEAD

160000 commit 8b3a538111100d57b0c836b5a30b2cb154d32793	vendor/lib
8b3a538111100d57b0c836b5a30b2cb154d32793

Mode 160000, type commit — the gitlink from §3.10, and the only tree entry whose target is not in this object database. Your repository records a commit id belonging to a repository it does not contain. The submodule's blobs and trees are not in your object database and never will be.

That single fact generates every submodule behaviour, good and bad:

Your repository stays small
A 2 GB dependency costs you forty bytes. This is the good reason to use submodules.
A clone is incomplete
git clone gets the gitlink and not the content. Someone must fetch the other repository too — §14.4.
The pin is exact
Not a version range, not a branch: one commit. Reproducible builds come free.
Updating is a commit
Moving to a newer version of the dependency changes the gitlink, which is a change to your tree, which is a commit to review like any other.
The URL is advice
.gitmodules is an ordinary tracked file. It tells Git where to look; the gitlink is the truth. A moved or deleted upstream leaves you with a commit id nobody can resolve.
