That last row is the most common way a submodule setup dies, and it is worth planning for: mirror any submodule you do not control.

git submodule add <url> <path> is the only command that creates all three pieces at once — the gitlink, the .gitmodules stanza, and the clone under .git/modules/ — which is why removing a submodule takes three steps and adding one takes a single command.

14.3 Where a Submodule's Repository Lives

cat vendor/lib/.git
ls .git/modules/

gitdir: ../../.git/modules/vendor/lib
vendor

vendor/lib/.git is a file, not a directory, containing a gitdir: line. The submodule's actual repository lives under .git/modules/<name>/ in the superproject. That indirection is the same mechanism worktrees use (§14.8), and it has a practical consequence people find alarming: rm -rf vendor/lib does not delete the submodule's repository, and git submodule add of the same path afterwards finds the old one still there and reuses it — including any local commits and any stale remote URL.

Removing a submodule properly takes three steps, because three places record it:

git submodule deinit -f vendor/lib
git rm vendor/lib
rm -rf .git/modules/vendor/lib

deinit empties the working tree and removes the submodule.<name>.url entry from .git/config; git rm removes the gitlink and the .gitmodules stanza; the third deletes the stored repository. Skipping the last is what leaves a "submodule already exists" error six months later.

14.4 Cloning, Updating, and Submodule Conflicts

A plain clone gives you the gitlink and an empty directory:

git clone app app-clone
ls app-clone/vendor/lib
git -C app-clone submodule status

-8b3a538111100d57b0c836b5a30b2cb154d32793 vendor/lib

Zero files. The leading - in submodule status means "not initialised". Fix it:

git -C app-clone submodule update --init
git -C app-clone submodule status
git -C app-clone/vendor/lib status -sb

Submodule path 'vendor/lib': checked out '8b3a538111100d57b0c836b5a30b2cb154d32793'
 8b3a538111100d57b0c836b5a30b2cb154d32793 vendor/lib (8b3a538)
## HEAD (no branch)

The submodule is on a detached HEAD (§4.4) — a submodule detached like this is the normal state, correct rather than broken: the superproject recorded a commit, not a branch, so that is what gets checked out. It is also the single biggest source of lost work with submodules — commits made inside a submodule without first checking out a branch are unreferenced, and switching branches in the superproject discards them silently. Before editing inside a submodule, git switch main in it.

Or do the whole thing in one command:

git clone --recurse-submodules app app-clone2

The status prefixes are the vocabulary for everything that follows:

(none)
Checked out at exactly the recorded commit. Everything agrees.
-
Not initialised — the directory is empty.
+
Checked out at a different commit from the recorded one. Either you moved it deliberately, or you forgot to commit the new gitlink.
U
Merge conflict inside the submodule.

There are two update commands and they do different things:

git submodule update

Check out the recorded commit. This is the normal one, and it is what you run after git pull brings a new gitlink. It moves the submodule to what the superproject says.

git submodule update --remote
git status -s
git submodule status
git diff --submodule=log

Submodule path 'vendor/lib': checked out '3ede5d9278d96736a45c3cb8e438ffe91d5f3dbf'
 M vendor/lib
+3ede5d9278d96736a45c3cb8e438ffe91d5f3dbf vendor/lib (remotes/origin/HEAD)
Submodule vendor/lib 8b3a538..3ede5d9:
  > lib v2

--remote fetches the submodule's branch tip (submodule.<name>.branch, defaulting to the remote's HEAD) and moves to it. Note what happened to the superproject: M vendor/lib — the gitlink is now modified, and the + prefix says the checkout differs from the record. You must commit that or the upgrade exists only on your machine.

git diff --submodule=log is the right way to review such a change: instead of two opaque hashes it lists the commits the submodule moved across. git config --global diff.submodule log makes it the default and is worth setting on day one.

Submodule merge conflicts happen when two branches record different commits for the same submodule. The ort strategy handles the easy case itself: if one side's commit is a descendant of the other's, it fast-forwards to the descendant. Otherwise you get a conflict whose resolution is to pick a commit — usually one that contains both, which may mean going into the submodule, merging there, and recording the result:

git -C vendor/lib merge <the-other-commit>
git add vendor/lib

Git will suggest a merged commit if one already exists.

git submodule status is the command to run whenever a build fails for reasons that make no sense, because its one-character prefix answers the question immediately. A submodule detached at the recorded commit is normal; a submodule detached and modified means someone committed inside it without recording the new gitlink.

git submodule update --init is the command to memorise, and git submodule update --init --recursive is the one that also handles submodules inside submodules.

14.5 Making Submodules Bearable

Most submodule pain is not intrinsic; it is the default of not recursing. Four settings remove nearly all of it:

submodule.recurse = true
Makes checkout, switch, pull, reset and others recurse automatically. The single highest-value setting here — it is what stops the submodule silently staying at the wrong commit after a branch switch.
fetch.recurseSubmodules = on-demand
Fetch submodule objects when a superproject commit refers to a commit you do not have.
diff.submodule = log
Show submodule changes as a list of commits instead of two hashes.
status.submoduleSummary = true
git status summarises submodule movement instead of saying "modified".

Two more commands worth knowing. git submodule foreach '<cmd>' runs a command in each submodule — git submodule foreach 'git switch main' fixes the detached-HEAD problem repository-wide. And git submodule absorbgitdirs moves an old-style submodule whose repository is still inside its own directory into .git/modules/, which is what modern Git expects.

One security note that surprises people. Cloning a repository whose submodule URL is a local path is refused by default:

fatal: transport 'file' not allowed

That is deliberate — it closed a vulnerability where a hostile repository could make a clone read from arbitrary local paths. -c protocol.file.allow=always re-enables it, and you should only do that for repositories you trust, which in practice means your own tests.

14.6 Subtrees

git subtree takes the opposite approach: copy the other project's files into a subdirectory of yours and merge future changes in.

git remote add libremote ../lib
git subtree add --prefix vendor/lib libremote main --squash
git ls-tree HEAD vendor
git log --oneline

040000 tree 059842f7176adfebff717705d639c6e65375499f	vendor
b2686cc Merge commit '5e92a03ac644337b105ad7136f68e1f8d06c519d' as 'vendor/lib'
dae4621 app initial
5e92a03 Squashed 'vendor/lib/' content from commit 3ede5d9

040000 tree, not 160000 commit. The files are genuinely in your repository. A clone gets them; a checkout writes them; a build works with no extra step and no extra command for anyone who has never heard of subtrees. That is the whole appeal.

Pulling an update:

git subtree pull --prefix vendor/lib libremote main --squash
git log --oneline

Merge made by the 'ort' strategy.
 vendor/lib/lib.py | 1 +
 1 file changed, 1 insertion(+)
acac757 Merge commit '67f77a09a62f988270e1e66648638714474cca1a'
b2686cc Merge commit '5e92a03ac644337b105ad7136f68e1f8d06c519d' as 'vendor/lib'
67f77a0 Squashed 'vendor/lib/' changes from 3ede5d9..35ed26d
dae4621 app initial

--squash compresses the upstream history into one commit per update. Without it, the entire upstream history is grafted into yours — which preserves git log and git blame through the vendored code and makes your history considerably noisier. Pick one and be consistent, because mixing them produces a mess that is hard to unpick.

git subtree push --prefix vendor/lib libremote main sends changes you made in the subdirectory back upstream, and git subtree split --prefix vendor/lib extracts the subdirectory's history into a standalone branch — which is also a perfectly good way to break a monorepo apart.

In favour
Consumers need to know nothing. No extra clone flag, no extra command, no broken CI when someone forgets --init. Local edits are ordinary edits.
Against
Your repository carries the other project's bytes forever (§15.2). The commands are long and easy to get wrong, and git subtree is a shell script in contrib rather than a first-class builtin — its errors are terse and its behaviour is less polished.

git subtree split --prefix=<dir> -b <branch> is the reverse operation and is genuinely useful outside subtrees: it extracts a subdirectory's history into a standalone branch, which is one good way to break a monorepo into separate repositories. Every commit that touched that directory is rewritten to have it as the root.

14.7 Worktrees: One Repository, Several Checkouts

A worktree solves a different problem entirely. You are mid-change on a feature branch; an urgent fix is needed on main. The classic answers are git stash (§7.7) or a second clone. A worktree is better than both:

git worktree add ../hotfix -b hotfix
git worktree list

Preparing worktree (new branch 'hotfix')
HEAD is now at 21d1d85 one
/…/wt/main-repo  21d1d85 [main]
/…/wt/hotfix     21d1d85 [hotfix]

A second directory, on its own branch, with its own working tree and its own index — sharing one object database and one set of branches. Commit in either and both see the commit:

git -C ../hotfix commit --allow-empty -m 'from the hotfix worktree'
git log --oneline hotfix

a906c3b from the hotfix worktree
21d1d85 one

No fetch, no push, no copying. It is one repository.

Compared with the alternatives: unlike git stash, nothing is set aside and nothing can be popped into the wrong place — your feature branch's working tree is simply still there, with its build artefacts and its editor state intact. Unlike a second clone, there is no duplicated object database (which for the Git repository would be another 318 MB, §15.11), no second remote to keep in sync, and no risk of committing to the wrong copy of a branch.

The one rule that trips everyone:

git worktree add ../second main

Preparing worktree (checking out 'main')
fatal: 'main' is already used by worktree at '/…/wt/main-repo'

A branch can be checked out in only one worktree at a time. This is not an arbitrary restriction — two worktrees on one branch would mean two indexes and two working trees claiming to represent the same ref, and a commit in either would leave the other's working tree describing a commit that is no longer the tip. Git refuses rather than letting you find out.

Detached is allowed, because a detached HEAD makes no such claim:

git worktree add --detach ../second main
git worktree list

/…/wt/main-repo  21d1d85 [main]
/…/wt/hotfix     a906c3b [hotfix]
/…/wt/second     21d1d85 (detached HEAD)

14.8 The Linked-Checkout Metadata

Three working trees, three branches, one repository — and none of the second directory's existence is visible from the first except through git worktree list. Look at what a linked worktree actually is:

ls -a ../hotfix
cat ../hotfix/.git

.  ..  .git
gitdir: /…/wt/main-repo/.git/worktrees/hotfix

.git is a file, exactly like a submodule's (§14.3), pointing at a private directory inside the main repository:

ls .git/worktrees/hotfix/

HEAD
ORIG_HEAD
commondir
gitdir
index
logs
refs
HEAD
This worktree's own HEAD. Independent of every other worktree's.
index
Its own staging area, so a conflicted merge here does not disturb anything there.
gitdir
The path back to the worktree's .git file. This is the link Git follows to find the working tree, and a stale one is what git worktree repair fixes.
commondir
../.. — the relative path to the shared repository. This is what makes objects and branches shared.
logs, refs
Per-worktree reflogs and per-worktree refs (§14.9).

Git therefore has two directory variables inside a linked worktree, and the difference is visible:

git -C ../hotfix rev-parse --git-dir --git-common-dir --show-toplevel

/…/wt/main-repo/.git/worktrees/hotfix
/…/wt/main-repo/.git
/…/wt/hotfix
$GIT_DIR
The per-worktree directory. HEAD, index, and per-worktree refs live here.
$GIT_COMMON_DIR
The shared directory. Objects, branches, tags, config and hooks live here.

This is the detail that breaks tooling, and it is worth knowing because the failure is confusing. A script that computes "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/.git/hooks" finds nothing in a linked worktree, because .git there is a file. A script that uses $(git rev-parse --git-dir) to find hooks or config also fails, because those are shared and live in the common dir. The correct forms are git rev-parse --git-common-dir for anything shared, git rev-parse --git-path hooks to let Git resolve a path for you, and git config core.hooksPath when you just want the hooks.

The management commands follow from the file layout:

git worktree list
All worktrees, their HEADs and branches. --porcelain for scripts, --verbose to show lock reasons.
git worktree remove <p>
Delete the directory and its metadata. Refuses if the worktree is dirty; --force overrides.
git worktree prune
Drop metadata for worktrees whose directory has vanished — which is what happens if you rm -rf one instead of removing it.
git worktree move <p> <q>
Relocate, updating both link files. Moving with mv breaks them.
git worktree repair
Rebuild the links after a move that did break them, or after the main repository itself moved.
git worktree lock <p>
Protect from pruning — for a worktree on removable media, whose absence is not abandonment.

Deleting a worktree directory by hand and then listing shows the intermediate state:

git worktree list

/…/wt/main-repo  21d1d85 [main]
/…/wt/hotfix     a906c3b [hotfix]
/…/wt/second     21d1d85 (detached HEAD) prunable

A prunable worktree — the metadata still exists and git worktree prune will remove it, as will the worktree-prune maintenance task (§15.9). Until then the branch it held is still considered checked out, which is why "that branch is already used by a worktree" sometimes names a directory that no longer exists.

Three of these you will use. git worktree remove <path> is the correct way to finish with one — it deletes the directory and the metadata, and refuses if the worktree is dirty. git worktree move relocates one safely, which plain mv does not. And git worktree lock marks one as deliberately absent so pruning leaves it alone.

A prunable worktree is harmless but not free: while the metadata exists, the branch it held is still considered checked out, so a prunable worktree is the usual explanation for "that branch is already used by a worktree" naming a directory that is not there.

14.9 Per-Worktree Configuration and Refs

Some things are shared between worktrees and some are not, and the split is deliberate.

Shared
refs/heads/*, refs/tags/*, refs/remotes/*, the object database, .git/config, hooks, and the stash.
Per-worktree
HEAD, ORIG_HEAD, the index, the reflog for HEAD, refs/bisect/*, refs/worktree/*, and refs/rewritten/*.

That refs/bisect/* entry is why two worktrees can bisect independently, and refs/worktree/* is a namespace you can use for your own worktree-local bookmarks. Addressing another worktree's per-worktree refs is possible with a prefix — main-worktree/HEAD, or worktrees/hotfix/HEAD — which is how a script inspects what a sibling worktree is doing.

Config is shared by default, and sometimes that is wrong: a release worktree may need a different signing key or a different sparse-checkout (§15.12). Opt in per repository:

git config extensions.worktreeConfig true

then, from inside the hotfix worktree:

git config --worktree user.email 'release@example.com'
cat ../main-repo/.git/worktrees/hotfix/config.worktree

[user]
	email = release@example.com

git -C ../hotfix config user.email
git config user.email

release@example.com
ada@example.com

Two worktrees, two identities, one repository. extensions.worktreeConfig is the same repository-format-version mechanism as SHA-256 (§3.11) and reftable (§4.9): setting it bumps repositoryformatversion to 1, so an older Git refuses the repository rather than silently ignoring per-worktree settings.

One caveat worth stating, because it produces a confusing bug: when you enable extensions.worktreeConfig, Git moves core.bare and core.worktree out of the shared config into the main worktree's config, since those are inherently per-worktree. If you enable it by editing .git/config by hand instead of using git config, you can end up with a shared core.bare that every worktree wrongly inherits.

The main worktree is the one containing .git as a directory, and it is special in exactly one way: it cannot be removed, because removing it would leave the repository with nowhere to live. git config --worktree <key> <value> writes to whichever worktree you run it in, so git config --worktree in the main worktree writes to .git/config.worktree and in a linked one writes under .git/worktrees/<name>/.

14.10 The Subtree Merge Strategy

Underneath git subtree is an ordinary merge with a path shift, and it is worth seeing directly because you occasionally need it without the wrapper.

git read-tree --prefix=vendor/lib/ -u libremote/main
git commit -m 'vendor lib at its current tip'

git read-tree --prefix reads another tree into the index under a prefix, and -u updates the working tree to match. Later updates use the merge strategy option from §6.9:

git merge --squash -s ort -X subtree=vendor/lib libremote/main

-X subtree=<path> tells the merge machinery that the other side's root corresponds to that path on your side, so the three-way merge lines the trees up correctly. Without the hint, Git would see every file as deleted from one place and added in another.

Knowing this is useful for two reasons. It demystifies git subtree, which is a shell script wrapping exactly these commands. And when a subtree merge goes wrong — usually because the prefix is not quite right — you can drive the underlying machinery yourself rather than fighting the wrapper.

14.11 Choosing

Worktree
You want two branches of the same project checked out at once. There is no competitor: not stash, not a second clone. Use it freely and often, and add the worktree directories to .gitignore if you keep them inside the repository.
Submodule
A large dependency you do not want in your history, pinned to an exact commit, whose consumers are willing to learn one extra command. Set submodule.recurse = true and diff.submodule = log the day you add the first one.
Subtree
A vendored dependency that must Just Work for everyone cloning, where the size cost is acceptable and upstream changes are rare.
None of them
Most of the time. A package manager and a version constraint solve the dependency problem better than either, with a lock file doing the pinning job the gitlink does. Reach for submodules or subtrees when the dependency is not a package — a shared configuration repository, a documentation theme, a firmware blob, a set of protocol definitions.

The honest summary on submodules: they work, they are precise, and their failure modes are all variations on "someone did not run the extra command". If your team is disciplined and your CI does --recurse-submodules, they are fine. If the same person keeps arriving with an empty vendor/ directory, the tool is not the problem you can fix, and a subtree or a package will cost less.

Exercises

1. A colleague pulls, builds, and gets a compile error about missing headers in third_party/. git status says the tree is clean. Diagnose it in one command and fix it in one more.

Solution 
git submodule status

A leading - means the submodule was never initialised — the directory is empty (§14.4). A leading + means it is checked out at a different commit from the one the superproject records, which happens after a git pull that changed the gitlink without recursing.

git submodule update --init --recursive

--init handles the first case, plain update the second, and --recursive handles submodules inside submodules.

The permanent fix is git config --global submodule.recurse true, which makes pull, checkout and switch recurse automatically. It is the setting that removes most submodule complaints, and it is off by default.

Note that git status being clean is not evidence of anything here: an uninitialised submodule is an empty directory, and Git does not track empty directories (§3.5), so there is nothing to report.

2. Explain, from the tree object, why a git clone of a repository with a submodule produces an empty directory while one with a subtree does not.

Solution

The tree entries differ (§14.1):

160000 commit 8b3a538111100d57b0c836b5a30b2cb154d32793	vendor/lib     # submodule
040000 tree   059842f7176adfebff717705d639c6e65375499f	vendor         # subtree

A submodule's entry is a gitlink (mode 160000, §3.10) — a commit id belonging to a repository that is not this one. Cloning transfers this repository's reachable objects, and that commit is not among them, so there is nothing to check out. Git creates the directory as a mount point and leaves it empty.

A subtree's entry is an ordinary 040000 tree pointing at real trees and blobs in this object database. They are transferred by the clone like any other content and written out by the checkout. Nobody needs to know a subtree exists.

That is the entire trade: the gitlink keeps your repository small and makes the clone incomplete; the subtree makes the clone complete and puts the other project's bytes in your history forever (§15.2).

3. You need to build main while keeping a half-finished refactor on feature. Compare a worktree against git stash and against a second clone.

Solution 
git worktree add ../proj-main main

Against stash (§7.7): a stash puts your work in a hidden commit and reverts the working tree, so build artefacts are rebuilt twice, editor state is lost, and a test process that was running is now looking at different files. Popping into the wrong branch is a real and common error. With a worktree, the feature branch's directory is untouched — the files stay exactly where they were.

Against a second clone: a clone duplicates the entire object database (318 MB for the Git repository, §15.11), needs its own fetch to stay current, and gives you two independent copies of every branch, so a commit can land in the wrong one and need pushing between them. A worktree shares one object database and one set of branches: commit in either and both see it immediately.

The two constraints to know (§14.7): a branch may be checked out in only one worktree at a time, so git worktree add ../proj-main main fails if you already have main checked out — use --detach, or make the other worktree the one on main. And a fresh worktree is a fresh checkout, so gitignored files like .env and installed dependencies are not there.

4. A CI script does HOOKS="$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/.git/hooks" and works everywhere except inside a worktree, where it reports that the directory does not exist. Explain and fix.

Solution

In a linked worktree, .git is a file containing gitdir: /path/to/main/.git/worktrees/<name>, not a directory (§14.8). So <toplevel>/.git/hooks is a path under a regular file and cannot exist.

Worse, the obvious fix is also wrong. git rev-parse --git-dir in a worktree returns /…/.git/worktrees/<name> — the per-worktree directory — and hooks are not there either, because hooks are shared.

Two correct forms:

HOOKS="$(git rev-parse --git-path hooks)"
echo "$HOOKS"

HOOKS="$(git rev-parse --git-common-dir)/hooks"
echo "$HOOKS"

--git-path asks Git to resolve a path within the repository, which handles worktrees, core.hooksPath, submodules and $GIT_DIR overrides in one go. It is the right answer for any .git-relative path in a script.

The general rule (§14.8): --git-dir for anything per-worktree (HEAD, index), --git-common-dir for anything shared (objects, config, hooks, refs), and --git-path when you would rather not decide.

5. git worktree add ../release v2.0 fails with "already used by worktree", but that directory was deleted weeks ago. Explain and give two fixes.

Solution

Deleting the directory with rm -rf removes the checkout but not the metadata in .git/worktrees/<name>/ (§14.8). Git still believes the branch is checked out there, and a branch can be checked out in only one worktree at a time (§14.7).

git worktree list

shows the stale entry marked prunable — the metadata exists and the working tree does not.

Two fixes:

git worktree prune          # drop metadata for vanished worktrees

or, if the directory was moved rather than deleted and you want it back:

cd /new/location && git worktree repair

The habit that avoids it: git worktree remove <path> rather than rm -rf, which deletes both halves and refuses if the worktree has uncommitted changes. Scheduled maintenance also handles it — the worktree-prune task runs on its own once git maintenance start is enabled (§15.9).

6. Your team needs the same shared configuration repository inside four separate services. Argue for one of submodule, subtree, or neither.

Solution

The deciding questions are how often it changes, whether the four services must agree on a version, and who clones the repositories.

Submodule is right when the four services must be able to pin different versions and roll forward independently, and the shared repository is large or changes often. The gitlink is an exact pin, upgrading is a reviewable one-line diff, and git diff --submodule=log shows exactly which commits an upgrade brings (§14.4). The cost is that every clone and every CI checkout needs --recurse-submodules, and the failure is silent — an empty directory and a confusing build error.

Subtree is right when the shared configuration is small, changes rarely, and is consumed by people and pipelines that should not have to know it is shared. Everything works from a plain clone with no extra flags (§14.6). The cost is bytes in four histories and long, error-prone update commands.

Neither is the strongest answer if the configuration can be published as a versioned artefact — a package, a container image, an OCI artefact, a file fetched at build time and checksummed. That gives the exact pinning of a submodule with none of the clone-time coupling, and it works for consumers who are not using Git at all.

For four services sharing configuration that changes weekly, publishing a versioned package is the answer that will still be pleasant in a year.

SECTION 15

Scale: Packfiles, Maintenance and Large Repositories

Git was written to handle the Linux kernel, and for most repositories it needs no help at all. But "most" is doing real work in that sentence. A repository accumulates history monotonically — it is a log, and logs only grow — so every repository is eventually a large repository, and the tools that make a large one usable are the least-taught part of Git. They are also the part where the wrong choice is expensive: a partial clone applied to the wrong workflow makes a half-second command take two and a half minutes, and this section measures exactly that.

Everything here rests on §3. The object database stores immutable snapshots; the storage format underneath it is free to represent those objects however it likes, provided the object ids come out the same. That separation is what makes all of the following possible without changing a single hash.

All numbers below were measured on one machine against one real repository — the Git source repository itself, 82,079 commits and 419,976 objects at the time of writing. Your absolute numbers will differ. The ratios and the shapes will not.

15.1 The Four Things That Get Slow

"Git is slow on this repo" is four different complaints, and they have four different fixes. Diagnosing which one you have is most of the work.

Object count
Too many objects to transfer or to enumerate. Felt as a slow clone and a slow fetch. Fixed by partial clone (§15.11) or shallow clone (§15.10).
Graph size
Too many commits to walk. Felt as a slow log, merge-base, blame, branch --contains. Fixed by the commit-graph (§15.6).
Working tree size
Too many files to lstat(). Felt as a slow status and a slow checkout. Fixed by sparse-checkout and the sparse index (§15.12).
Pack layout
Objects spread across many packs, or badly deltified. Felt as everything being slightly slow and the repo being much larger than it should be. Fixed by repacking (§15.4) and the multi-pack index (§15.7).

The order matters, because the fixes are not interchangeable and two of them cost you something. A shallow clone loses history. A partial clone trades disk for network round trips, and §15.11 shows what that costs when it goes wrong. The commit-graph and repacking are pure wins — they are caches derived from data you already have, and deleting them costs only the time to rebuild.

15.2 Measuring a Repository

Three commands answer "what is in here and how big is it". Repository size is the number people react to, and running these before changing anything is the difference between a fix and a guess.

git count-objects -vH

count: 0
size: 0 bytes
in-pack: 419976
packs: 1
size-pack: 314.86 MiB
prune-packable: 0
garbage: 0
size-garbage: 0 bytes
count / size
Loose objects and the disk they occupy. A large count means a repack is overdue.
in-pack / packs
Objects inside packfiles, and how many packfiles there are. Many packs is its own problem (§15.7).
prune-packable
Loose objects that already exist inside a pack — pure waste, removed by git prune-packed.
garbage
Files in the object directory Git does not recognise. Almost always the debris of an interrupted operation.

The -H makes sizes human-readable; without it everything is in KiB, which is what scripts want.

Git 2.52 added git repo info, a companion that answers structured questions about the repository itself rather than about its size:

git repo info layout.bare object.format references.format

layout.bare=false
object.format=sha1
references.format=files

It takes explicit keys or --all, and --format=nul makes it safe to parse — the forward-looking way for a script to ask which object format (§3.11) and which ref backend (§4.9) it is dealing with. git repo structure is its companion and reports the object and ref layout.

The second question is which objects are big. git cat-file --batch-check over every object, sorted, answers it in one line:

git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check='%(objecttype) %(objectsize:disk) %(objectname)' \
  | awk '$1 == "blob"' | sort -k2 -n -r | head -5

%(objectsize:disk) rather than %(objectsize) is the important detail: it reports the bytes the object actually occupies after delta compression, which is the number that explains a large .git. A 200 MB log file committed once and deleted the next day still costs 200 MB of disk in every clone forever — the object is unreachable from any branch tip, but it is reachable from the commit that added it, and that commit is still in history. Removing it means rewriting history (§8.10), not deleting a file.

The third command is git verify-pack, and §15.3 is the reason it exists.

git prune is the standalone form of the deletion gc performs, and it is worth knowing separately because git prune --dry-run --expire=now lists exactly what would go without removing anything — the safest way to find out how much of your repository size is unreachable history before deciding whether to act.

15.3 Packfiles and Delta Compression

Build a repository with a pathological shape: one 400-line file, edited two hundred times.

One helper script, gen.py, to make the file:

import random
random.seed(7)
words = ['alpha', 'beta', 'gamma', 'delta', 'epsilon', 'zeta', 'eta', 'theta']
rows = (' '.join(random.choice(words) for _ in range(12)) for _ in range(400))
print('\n'.join(rows))

git init -q packs
cd packs
python3 gen.py > data.txt
git add data.txt
git commit -q -m 'initial 400 lines'
for i in $(seq 1 199); do
  sed -i "$(( i % 400 + 1 ))s/.*/edited at revision $i/" data.txt
  git commit -q -am "revision $i"
done
git count-objects -v
du -sh .git/objects

count: 600
size: 2984
in-pack: 0
packs: 0
size-pack: 0
prune-packable: 0
garbage: 0
size-garbage: 0
3.0M	.git/objects

Six hundred loose objects — 200 blobs, 200 trees, 200 commits — occupying 2,984 KiB. (gen.py is never added, so it contributes nothing.) The working tree is a single 18,306-byte file. Two hundred versions of an 18 KB file cost three megabytes, because each version is stored whole, individually zlib-compressed. That is the loose object format doing exactly what §3.2 described, and it is obviously untenable.

Now pack them.

git gc -q
git count-objects -v
du -sh .git/objects

count: 0
size: 0
in-pack: 600
packs: 1
size-pack: 71
prune-packable: 0
garbage: 0
size-garbage: 0
100K	.git/objects

2,984 KiB became 71 KiB — a factor of forty-two. Two hundred revisions of the file now cost about four times the size of one copy of it. The mechanism is delta compression, and git verify-pack shows it directly:

git verify-pack -v .git/objects/pack/*.idx | grep ' blob ' | head -3

631b0f626a4618e5513bf64a8906af2086822aaf blob   22203 3120 30266
89bf386e38e96e1570ef55cb494c9a3989bf9a59 blob   21 34 33386 1 631b0f626a4618e5513bf64a8906af2086822aaf
22628e7a1f156adcc2efa088554ac48b05332f58 blob   25 37 33420 2 89bf386e38e96e1570ef55cb494c9a3989bf9a59

Run this yourself and the blob ids will match, because they are functions of the content — but which blob appears first will not, because the packer's ordering is a heuristic that varies between runs. Read the shape of the rows, not the particular ids.

The columns are object id, type, size, size-in-packfile, offset, and for a deltified object two more: depth and base object id.

Row 1 — a base
No depth column. 22,203 bytes of content stored as 3,120 bytes of zlib.
Row 2 — depth 1
A 21-byte delta against row 1, stored in 34 bytes.
Row 3 — depth 2
A 25-byte delta against row 2, which is itself a delta. Chains nest.

Two details in there routinely mislead people.

For a deltified object, the size column is the size of the delta, not the size of the object. Check it:

git cat-file -s 89bf386e

22158

verify-pack said 21; the blob is 22,158 bytes. Both are true — twenty-one bytes of delta instructions reconstruct 22,158 bytes of content. Reading that column as the object size makes a packfile look impossibly small, and that misreading is the source of most "Git compresses 800:1" folklore.

A tiny delta can be larger on disk than in memory. Row 2 is 21 bytes of delta stored in 34 bytes, because a zlib stream has a header and a checksum of its own. Below roughly fifty bytes, compression costs more than it saves. Git does not care, because the alternative was 22,158 bytes.

Counting the whole pack:

git verify-pack -v .git/objects/pack/*.idx \
  | awk '$2 == "blob" { if (NF >= 7) d++; else b++ } END { print "delta blobs:", d+0, " base blobs:", b+0 }'

delta blobs: 198  base blobs: 2

Two full copies and a hundred and ninety-eight deltas. Note what Git is not doing: it is not storing a delta against "the previous version" in history order. Delta bases are chosen by content similarity, not by ancestry. Git sorts candidate objects by type, then path, then size, then walks a sliding window looking for the smallest delta — so a blob can be deltified against a blob from a completely unrelated commit, or against a newer one. Git in fact prefers deltas that point from old to new, so that recent history — the part you actually read — is at the shallow end of the chain.

This is why §3.9's answer to "isn't storing whole snapshots wasteful" is honest rather than evasive. The object model is snapshots. The storage is deltas. Neither knows about the other, and repacking can rearrange every delta in the repository without any object id changing by a single bit.

The vocabulary for a chain: the object at the bottom, stored in full, is the base object, and every delta above it reconstructs its content by applying instructions to the object below. Reading an object at depth 50 means reading the base object and applying fifty deltas in order, which is why pack.depth is a trade rather than a free win.

15.4 Depth, Window, and the Repack gc Will Not Redo

Two settings control the delta search, and both are visible in that pack:

git verify-pack -v .git/objects/pack/*.idx | tail -5

chain length = 47: 5 objects
chain length = 48: 6 objects
chain length = 49: 9 objects
chain length = 50: 1 object
pack.depth
Maximum delta chain length. Default 50, which is exactly where the histogram above stops. A deeper chain packs smaller but costs more work to read one object, because every link must be applied in order.
pack.window
How many candidate objects to consider as a delta base. Default 10. A wider window finds better bases and costs CPU and memory during packing only.

The trade-off is real and measurable. Force depth 1 — every object either a base or a single-step delta:

git -c pack.depth=1 repack -a -d -q -f
git count-objects -v | grep size-pack

size-pack: 99

Seventy-one KiB became 99 KiB, a 39% increase, from one setting. Now the finding that costs people real disk:

git gc -q
git count-objects -v | grep size-pack

size-pack: 99

git gc did not fix it. This is not a bug, and it is the single most useful piece of packfile operational knowledge: repacking reuses existing deltas by default. Recomputing every delta in a large repository is expensive, so when Git repacks it copies the delta encoding it already has for any object it can. If a previous repack produced a bad layout — done with a small window on a memory-constrained CI machine, or by a hosting provider's fast path — every subsequent gc faithfully preserves it.

The flag that says "throw the existing deltas away and search again" is -f:

git repack -a -d -q -f
git count-objects -v | grep size-pack

size-pack: 71

And --aggressive, which is -f plus gc.aggressiveWindow=250 and gc.aggressiveDepth=50 — a window twenty-five times the default:

git gc -q --aggressive
git count-objects -v | grep size-pack

size-pack: 68
Loose objects
2984 KiB
The starting point.
git gc
71 KiB
Default window 10, depth 50.
repack, pack.depth=1 -f
99 KiB
Deliberately bad.
git gc, after that
99 KiB
No change — the existing deltas were reused.
git repack -a -d -f
71 KiB
Deltas recomputed; back where we started.
git gc --aggressive
68 KiB
Window 250. Much slower, and a further 4% here.

Run the sequence yourself and the last two figures may move by a KiB or two: the delta search is a heuristic over a sliding window, and object ordering differs slightly between runs. Do not read significance into a few percent — read it into the 71-to-99 step, which is a setting, and into the step that did nothing.

--aggressive deserves a warning it rarely gets. It is not "gc, but better"; it is "spend a great deal of CPU and memory re-deltifying everything". On a repository the size of the kernel it can take hours and consume many gigabytes of RAM, and because §15.9's scheduled maintenance is designed never to do anything that expensive, running --aggressive habitually is usually a mistake. Run it once after a history rewrite (§8.10) or after importing from another system, and otherwise leave it alone. Git's own manual page is unusually blunt about this: "Not using this at all is the right trade-off for most users and their repositories."

One flag worth naming separately: git gc --aggressive is not "gc, but more" — it is -f plus a window twenty-five times the default, and running git gc --aggressive on a schedule is a well-known way to waste a great deal of CPU for a few percent.

The length of a delta chain is bounded by pack.depth, and a long delta chain trades disk for read time: reading one object means walking its delta chain to the base and applying every step.

15.5 What gc Deletes, and When

git gc is not only a repacker. It also deletes, and knowing exactly what it deletes is what makes §7.9's recovery techniques trustworthy rather than hopeful.

Packs loose objects
Everything reachable goes into a packfile.
Packs refs
Loose ref files are collected into packed-refs (§4.8).
Expires reflog entries
Reachable entries older than gc.reflogExpire (90 days) and unreachable ones older than gc.reflogExpireUnreachable (30 days) are dropped.
Prunes unreachable objects
Objects no ref and no reflog entry can reach, and older than gc.pruneExpire (2 weeks), are deleted.
Writes the commit-graph
gc.writeCommitGraph defaults to true (§15.6).

The two-week grace period is the safety net that matters. A commit orphaned by a bad reset --hard is unreachable immediately, but it is not deleted for a fortnight, and while the reflog entry survives it is not even unreachable. That is why "I destroyed my work" is almost always false and §7.9 almost always recovers it.

The mechanism that holds those objects during the grace period is worth seeing, because it changed in Git 2.41 and most advice predates it. Orphan a commit, destroy the reflog that would have saved it, and collect garbage:

git commit -q -am two
ORPHAN=$(git rev-parse HEAD)
echo "about to orphan $ORPHAN"
git reset -q --hard HEAD~1
git reflog expire --expire=now --all
git gc -q
ls .git/objects/pack/

about to orphan 26b13b5afbda11adb3bb97e1f081e977ce72e916
pack-16d84efb717c244848fda78fd20f7444d325539c.idx
pack-16d84efb717c244848fda78fd20f7444d325539c.pack
pack-16d84efb717c244848fda78fd20f7444d325539c.rev
pack-9b4bcfd5caa219b8492e388028b2b93b74572e7f.idx
pack-9b4bcfd5caa219b8492e388028b2b93b74572e7f.mtimes
pack-9b4bcfd5caa219b8492e388028b2b93b74572e7f.pack
pack-9b4bcfd5caa219b8492e388028b2b93b74572e7f.rev

Two packs, and the second has a .mtimes file the first does not. That is a cruft pack: unreachable objects, packed together, with a sidecar recording each object's own modification time so the grace period can be applied per object. gc.cruftPacks has defaulted to true since Git 2.41; before that, gc exploded unreachable objects out of packs into loose form so the filesystem mtime could serve the same purpose, which meant a repository with a lot of unreachable history could grow enormously during a gc. Advice that says "gc can temporarily balloon your repository" is describing the old behaviour.

The orphan is still there, and fsck says exactly what it is:

git cat-file -t "$ORPHAN"
git fsck --unreachable

commit
unreachable blob 422c2b7ab3b3c668038da977e4e93a5fc623169c
unreachable commit 26b13b5afbda11adb3bb97e1f081e977ce72e916
unreachable tree 196228cfdfdd814964f14972b3cdac82c2628dc7

The corollary is the dangerous flag. git gc --prune=now is the combination that makes deletion immediate:

git gc -q --prune=now
ls .git/objects/pack/
git cat-file -t "$ORPHAN"

pack-16d84efb717c244848fda78fd20f7444d325539c.idx
pack-16d84efb717c244848fda78fd20f7444d325539c.pack
pack-16d84efb717c244848fda78fd20f7444d325539c.rev
fatal: git cat-file: could not get object info

The cruft pack is gone and so is the commit. This is the correct final step after purging a secret from history (§8.10), and it is a catastrophe run casually.

Two more knobs decide when gc runs at all. gc.auto (default 6700 loose objects) and gc.autoPackLimit (default 50 packs) trigger the automatic git gc --auto that many commands run in the background after they finish. Setting gc.auto=0 disables that entirely, which is right on a server where you schedule maintenance yourself and wrong on a laptop where nothing else will ever do it.

15.6 The Commit-Graph

Walking history means reading commit objects: for each one, decompress it, parse the header, follow the parent ids. On the Git repository that is 82,079 objects to touch for a full traversal, and it is measurable.

git rev-list --count HEAD

82079

Timed three times with no commit-graph present, that command takes 1.28, 1.42 and 1.44 seconds. A git merge-base HEAD v2.20.0 takes 0.86 s. Now build the cache:

git commit-graph write --reachable
ls -l .git/objects/info/commit-graph

-r--r--r-- 1 user user 5128508 Aug 27 14:41 .git/objects/info/commit-graph

Writing it took 1.69 s and produced 5,128,508 bytes — about 62 bytes per commit. Re-timed:

Command
Before
After
 
git rev-list --count HEAD
1.28–1.44 s
0.10–0.12 s
Roughly twelve times faster.
git merge-base HEAD v2.20.0
0.86 s
0.09 s
Roughly nine times faster.
git log --graph --oneline --all
3.35 s
3.55 s
No improvement.

That last row is the one worth internalising, and it is why this measurement was worth doing rather than quoting. The commit-graph accelerates graph traversal, not output. git log --graph --oneline --all spends its time formatting and decorating 82,079 lines and writing them out; the walk was never the bottleneck. A cache helps the operation it caches. Claiming "the commit-graph makes git log faster" is the kind of half-true advice that leads someone to enable it, see no change in the command they actually run, and conclude the whole feature is snake oil.

What the file stores is why the speedup exists at all:

Flat, indexed records
Object id, root tree id, parent positions, commit date — in a fixed-width binary table, so reading one commit is a seek rather than a decompress-and-parse.
Generation numbers
Each commit's distance from a root. Two commits' generation numbers often prove one cannot be an ancestor of the other without walking anything at all. This is what makes merge-base, --contains and tag --merged collapse from seconds to milliseconds.
Changed-path Bloom filters
Optional, via --changed-paths: a probabilistic per-commit summary of which paths changed, so git log -- some/file can skip commits that certainly did not touch it.

It is a pure cache. Deleting .git/objects/info/commit-graph loses nothing but the speed, and Git verifies it against the object database rather than trusting it:

git commit-graph verify

Silence and exit status 0 means the cache agrees with reality. gc.writeCommitGraph is true by default, so any git gc refreshes it; fetch.writeCommitGraph extends that to every fetch and is worth enabling on a repository you fetch into constantly.

15.7 Multi-Pack Index and Incremental Repack

One packfile is ideal and unachievable. Every fetch that brings new objects writes a new pack, and looking up an object means searching each pack's index in turn — so lookup cost grows with the number of packs. The naive fix is to repack everything into one file on every fetch, which on a 315 MiB repository is unthinkable several times an hour.

The multi-pack index breaks that trade-off. It is a single .midx file mapping every object id to (which pack, what offset) across all packs, so lookup is one binary search regardless of how many packfiles exist.

git repack -q -d --write-midx
ls -l .git/objects/pack/

11760496 multi-pack-index
11760400 pack-bba935faff206d7c385d469d704daad2ee097414.idx
318399178 pack-bba935faff206d7c385d469d704daad2ee097414.pack
1679956 pack-bba935faff206d7c385d469d704daad2ee097414.rev

Note the sizes: with a single pack, the MIDX is 11,760,496 bytes against the .idx's 11,760,400 — it duplicates the index and buys nothing. A multi-pack index only pays when there are several packs, which is precisely the state that scheduled maintenance deliberately maintains.

A related artefact is the bitmap index (.bitmap), which records for selected commits exactly which objects are reachable from them, so a server answering a clone can compute the object set without walking the graph. git repack -b writes one, and hosting providers keep them on every large repository.

The .rev file is the pack reverse index, mapping offsets back to object ids. Git used to compute that in memory on every operation that needed it; storing it on disk is a quiet, significant win for anything that reads objects by position, and it appears automatically.

git multi-pack-index write builds or updates the file directly, and git multi-pack-index verify checks it against the packs. With a MIDX in place, incremental-repack becomes safe: repack a few small packs into one slightly larger pack, update the MIDX, and never touch the enormous base pack.

git maintenance run --task=incremental-repack

That completed in 0.19 s on the 315 MiB repository, because it correctly decided there was nothing worth doing. Compare that with the ~20 s a full git gc took on the same repository, and the design goal of §15.8 becomes obvious.

git multi-pack-index write and git multi-pack-index verify are the direct commands, and git multi-pack-index expire drops entries for packs that have been superseded. A bitmap index complements it rather than replacing it: the multi-pack index answers "which pack is this object in", and a bitmap index answers "which objects are reachable from this commit", which is the expensive half of serving a clone.

15.8 Integrity: fsck and What Verification Costs

git fsck walks the object database and checks two different things: that every object's content still hashes to its own name, and that every reference between objects resolves.

git fsck

On the Git repository this took 116.90 seconds — and it found something:

warning in tag d6602ec5194c87b0fc87103ca4d67251c76f233a: missingTaggerEntry: invalid format - expected 'tagger' line

That is not a corrupted clone. It is the v0.99 tag, made in 2005 before Git recorded a tagger line, and it is in every clone of Git's own history:

git cat-file -p d6602ec5194c87b0fc87103ca4d67251c76f233a

object a3eb250f996bf5e12376ec88622c4ccaabf20ea8
type commit
tag v0.99

Test-release for wider distribution.

I'll make the first public RPM's etc, thus the tag.
-----BEGIN PGP SIGNATURE-----

Object ids are immutable, so a malformed object made twenty years ago is permanent — the only way to remove it would rewrite every descendant. fsck therefore distinguishes warning from error, and fsck.<msg-id> config keys let a repository downgrade a specific historical wart to ignore without weakening the check for anything else.

Real corruption looks different. Damage one byte of a loose object and try again:

git fsck

error: corrupt loose object 'aa1d11903b7f1f06c41af54a473309d30057a812'
error: unable to unpack contents of .git/objects/aa/1d11903b7f1f06c41af54a473309d30057a812
error: aa1d11903b7f1f06c41af54a473309d30057a812: object corrupt or missing: .git/objects/aa/1d11903b7f1f06c41af54a473309d30057a812

This is content-addressing earning its keep. Nothing recorded a checksum of that file separately; the file's name is its checksum, so a single flipped bit is detected with certainty. Contrast a version-control system that stores a mutable working copy: silent corruption there is silent forever.

The 117 seconds is why fsck is not run automatically. There is a much cheaper mode:

git fsck --connectivity-only

That took 14.70 s — about eight times faster — because it skips re-hashing object contents and only verifies that every referenced object exists. It catches a missing object and misses a corrupted one, which is the right trade for a routine check.

git fsck
Full check. Slow. Run it when you suspect hardware trouble, after a filesystem-level recovery, or before trusting a repository you were handed.
git fsck --connectivity-only
Structural check. Fast enough to be routine.
git fsck --lost-found
Writes every dangling commit and blob into .git/lost-found/. The blunt recovery tool for when the reflog has already been expired (§7.9).
git fsck --unreachable
Lists what gc would eventually prune. Useful before --prune=now, to see what you are about to make unrecoverable.

Note that a dangling object in fsck output is informational, not an error. A dangling blob is the normal residue of git add followed by a change of mind, and a repository with a healthy amount of activity always has some.

The word for an object with no incoming reference is a dangling object, and a repository in daily use always has some. git fsck --dangling lists them; a dangling object becomes an unreachable one for garbage-collection purposes, and §15.5's grace period is what stands between it and deletion.

15.9 git maintenance: Scheduled and Non-Destructive

git gc was designed to be run occasionally by a human on a repository that had grown untidy. git maintenance was designed for the opposite: small, frequent, interruptible tasks that never block you and never delete anything surprising.

git maintenance register

That writes the repository's path into your global config's maintenance.repo list, and sets two keys in the repository itself:

maintenance.auto=false
maintenance.strategy=incremental

maintenance.auto=false is the important half. It disables the automatic git gc --auto that ordinary commands trigger, because scheduled maintenance is now responsible instead — you do not want both.

git maintenance start additionally installs the schedule. On a systemd machine it creates user timers:

git maintenance start

Created symlink '/home/user/.config/systemd/user/timers.target.wants/git-maintenance@hourly.timer' → '/home/user/.config/systemd/user/git-maintenance@hourly.timer'.
Created symlink '/home/user/.config/systemd/user/timers.target.wants/git-maintenance@daily.timer' → '/home/user/.config/systemd/user/git-maintenance@daily.timer'.
Created symlink '/home/user/.config/systemd/user/timers.target.wants/git-maintenance@weekly.timer' → '/home/user/.config/systemd/user/git-maintenance@weekly.timer'.

systemctl --user list-timers 'git-maintenance*' --no-pager

NEXT                          LEFT LAST PASSED UNIT                         ACTIVATES
Thu 2026-08-27 15:21:00 UTC  46min -         - git-maintenance@hourly.timer git-maintenance@hourly.service
Fri 2026-08-28 00:21:00 UTC     9h -         - git-maintenance@daily.timer  git-maintenance@daily.service
Mon 2026-08-31 00:21:00 UTC 3 days -         - git-maintenance@weekly.timer git-maintenance@weekly.service

3 timers listed.

Three timers, one per schedule bucket. --scheduler= picks the mechanism explicitly — systemd-timer, crontab, launchctl on macOS, schtasks on Windows — and auto chooses. git maintenance stop removes the timers and git maintenance unregister removes the config entry; running both leaves no trace.

The tasks, and which schedule each defaults to under the incremental strategy:

commit-graph
hourly
Extends the commit-graph with new commits (§15.6).
prefetch
hourly
Fetches from every remote into refs/prefetch/ — not into your remote-tracking branches, so nothing you see changes. Your next real git fetch is then almost free because the objects are already local. This is the task that makes the biggest difference to how Git feels.
loose-objects
daily
Sweeps a batch of loose objects into a pack. Batched, so it never runs long.
incremental-repack
daily
Consolidates small packs behind the multi-pack index (§15.7).
pack-refs
weekly
Collects loose refs into packed-refs (§4.8).
gc
—
Available but not scheduled by the incremental strategy, because it is the one task that both takes a long time and deletes things.

Two further tasks — reflog-expire and worktree-prune — plus rerere-gc are available by name, and geometric repacking is the default strategy for a manual git maintenance run on a large repository: it repacks so that pack sizes form a geometric sequence, which bounds the number of packs without ever rewriting the largest one.

git maintenance run --auto runs only what the thresholds say is needed, and git maintenance run --task=<name> runs one thing on demand. Every task is safe to interrupt.

maintenance.strategy is the key that selects between these sets, and its two useful values are incremental — the schedule above — and geometric. Geometric repacking is the one to choose for a very large repository maintained by hand: it keeps pack sizes in a geometric sequence so the number of packs stays bounded without the largest pack ever being rewritten.

15.10 Shallow Clones

A shallow clone truncates history at a given depth.

git clone --depth=1 https://github.com/git/git.git shallow

That took 5.97 s against 99.11 s for the full clone, and produced a 14 MB .git against 318 MB. The repository holds 4,955 objects instead of 419,976, and:

git rev-list --count HEAD
cat .git/shallow

1
f78ce2f7b6df702f93d40b85d6bda92a3f65da79

One commit. The .git/shallow file lists the commits whose parents are deliberately absent — Git treats them as roots, and refuses to walk past them.

Shallow clones are excellent for exactly one use and poor for everything else.

Good for
CI. A build job needs the tree, not the history. --depth=1 is often the single largest saving available in a pipeline, and it is why every CI provider's checkout step defaults to it.
Bad for
Anything historical. git blame attributes every line to the truncation commit. git log shows one entry. git describe cannot find a tag. A merge base with anything older than the cut does not exist, so git merge and git rebase misbehave in confusing ways rather than failing cleanly.

Deepening later is possible — git fetch --deepen=50, or git fetch --unshallow to fetch the rest — but it is a fresh transfer, and it is often slower in total than having cloned fully in the first place. Shallow fetches are also harder on the server than full ones, because the server must compute a custom pack rather than reusing a prepared one; several large hosts have asked users to stop using them in loops for this reason.

git fetch --unshallow is the escape hatch and is worth knowing before you need it: it converts a shallow clone into a complete one by fetching everything that was truncated. Because --unshallow is a full transfer, a CI job that shallow-clones and then discovers it needs history is usually slower overall than one that cloned fully.

15.11 Partial Clone, and the Trap

Partial clone is the modern answer, and it is a better one: it omits object contents rather than history. The commit graph arrives complete, so nothing about history is broken — the missing objects are fetched on demand, transparently, when something actually needs them.

Two filters matter. Measured against the same repository:

Clone
Time
.git
Objects
Pack
git clone
99.1 s
318 MB
419,976
322,421 KiB
--filter=blob:none
49.5 s
130 MB
259,178
130,676 KiB
--filter=tree:0
24.8 s
51 MB
91,411
49,884 KiB
--depth=1
6.0 s
14 MB
4,955
12,835 KiB

blob:none omits every file's content but keeps every tree, so all path-based operations work locally. tree:0 omits the directory structure too, which is dramatically smaller and means almost any operation touching a path must go to the network.

The clone records what it did, so Git knows it may fetch more later:

git config --get remote.origin.promisor
git config --get remote.origin.partialclonefilter

true
blob:none

A promisor remote is one that has promised to supply objects on demand. When Git needs a missing object it fetches it from there, without asking.

Now the trap. On a blob:none clone, history browsing is instant, because commits are all local:

git log --oneline -n 20000 > /dev/null

0.60 s

But ask for the patches:

git log -p -n 200 > /dev/null

153.02 s

The same command on the full clone takes 0.40–0.48 s. That is roughly three hundred and fifty times slower, and it is not a pathological case — git log -p on a couple of hundred commits is an ordinary thing to do while reviewing. Each commit's diff needs the blobs on both sides, none of them are local, and every one is a network round trip. The repository state afterwards tells the same story:

git count-objects -v

in-pack: 259598
size-pack: 132243

Object count rose by 420, and the packfile count went from 3 to 41: each lazy fetch wrote its own small packfile, several of them under 2 KB. Left alone, a partial clone used this way degrades into hundreds of tiny packs, which is exactly the condition §15.7's multi-pack index exists to survive — and the reason git maintenance should be enabled on any partial clone.

If you decide partial clone was the wrong choice, git backfill (added in Git 2.49) fills in the missing objects in batches instead of one at a time:

git backfill --min-batch-size=50000
git count-objects -v

in-pack: 415961
size-pack: 323537

That took 165 s and brought the repository back to essentially full size. The point of backfill is not the total — it is that it fetches in a few large, well-deltified batches rather than 400 individual round trips, and it can be told --sparse to fetch only what the current sparse-checkout needs.

Use blob:none
When you work on a small part of a large repository, stay online, and mostly read recent history. Pair it with git maintenance start.
Do not use it
For archaeology (log -p, blame over old history, bisect across thousands of commits), for anything that must work offline, or on a slow or metered link. The full clone's one-time cost is cheaper than the tenth git blame.

--filter=tree:0 is the aggressive one and is worth a warning: with --filter=tree:0 even git log -- <path> needs the network, because path filtering requires trees. The remote that supplies the missing objects is recorded as a promisor remote, and if that promisor remote becomes unreachable the repository is not merely slow — operations touching missing objects fail outright.

15.12 Sparse-Checkout and the Sparse Index

The last axis is the working tree. Partial clone reduces what is in .git; sparse-checkout reduces what is on disk.

git ls-files | wc -l
find . -path ./.git -prune -o -type f -print | wc -l
du -sh --exclude=.git .

4850
4846
59M

git sparse-checkout set Documentation/technical t/helper
find . -path ./.git -prune -o -type f -print | wc -l
du -sh --exclude=.git .

2060
24M

Files on disk fell from 4,846 to 2,060 and the working tree from 59 MB to 24 MB, by naming two directories. Note what did not change:

git ls-files | wc -l

4850

The index still has all 4,850 entries. Sparse-checkout works by setting the skip-worktree bit on the entries you excluded, which tells Git "this file is legitimately absent; do not report it as deleted":

git ls-files -t | awk '{print $1}' | sort | uniq -c

   2062 H
   2788 S

H is a normal cached entry, S is skip-worktree. git status is clean throughout, which is the whole point — without that bit, 2,788 files would show as deleted.

Cone mode is what makes this fast, and the generated pattern file shows why:

cat .git/info/sparse-checkout

/*
!/*/
/Documentation/
!/Documentation/*/
/t/
!/t/*/
/Documentation/technical/
/t/helper/

Two shapes only: a parent pattern (/Documentation/ then !/Documentation/*/ — the files directly inside, but not the subdirectories) and a recursive pattern (/t/helper/ — everything below). Because every rule is a directory prefix, matching is a hash lookup per directory rather than a glob test per path. Non-cone mode accepts arbitrary .gitignore-style patterns and costs patterns × paths comparisons; it still works, and the documentation now discourages it for exactly that reason.

The configuration lands in the worktree config rather than the repository config, so different worktrees (§14.7) can have different sparse definitions:

cat .git/config.worktree

[core]
	sparseCheckout = true
	sparseCheckoutCone = true
[index]
	sparse = true

core.sparseCheckout is the master switch, core.sparseCheckoutCone selects cone mode, and index.sparse is the second half of the feature. With it on, the index itself stores a single entry for a whole excluded directory — a tree id — instead of one entry per file underneath it:

ls -l .git/index

461720 bytes   (full index)
197375 bytes   (sparse index)

The index shrank by 57%, and on repositories with hundreds of thousands of files the effect is the difference between git status being instant and taking several seconds.

The sparse index has a rough edge you will meet, and it announces itself:

hint: The sparse index is expanding to a full index, a slow operation.

Commands that are not yet sparse-aware expand the index back to full form to do their work. git sparse-checkout reapply re-applies the patterns after such a command — or after a merge or rebase has materialised files outside the cone — and is the command the hint is really asking for. git status, git add, git commit, git checkout and git merge are sparse-aware; several plumbing commands are not, and git ls-files expands unless given --sparse. The hint is telling the truth rather than complaining — it is worth reading, not silencing.

Scalar — a separate command shipped alongside Git, not a subcommand — bundles all of this into one step: scalar clone <url> performs a blobless partial clone, turns on cone-mode sparse-checkout and the sparse index, registers the repository for background maintenance, and enables the file-system monitor. scalar register applies the same settings to a repository you already have. It is worth knowing as the assembled recipe rather than as a new mechanism — every part of it is something in this section.

Finally, on very large working trees, the remaining cost is the filesystem scan itself. core.fsmonitor hands that job to a background daemon that watches for changes, so git status asks the daemon what moved instead of calling lstat() on every path:

git config core.fsmonitor true

feature.manyFiles is a convenience that turns on index.version=4, core.untrackedCache and index.skipHash together, and is a reasonable first thing to try on any repository where git status has become annoying.

Four names complete the picture. The skip-worktree bit is the per-entry flag that makes a file's absence legitimate; index.sparse is what turns the flat index into a sparse one; non-cone mode is the older pattern-matching behaviour, still supported and discouraged; and git sparse-checkout reapply is what you run after a command has materialised files outside the cone. feature.manyFiles is the unrelated but adjacent setting that helps any large working tree, sparse or not.

15.13 Choosing

The four levers, and what each one costs you:

Lever
Buys
Costs
commit-graph
Fast graph queries.
Nothing. A rebuildable cache. Enable it everywhere.
git maintenance
Packs stay tidy; fetches feel instant.
Nothing you will notice. Enable it on any repository you use daily.
sparse-checkout
Smaller working tree, faster status and checkout.
Files you excluded are not there. Occasional index expansion.
--filter=blob:none
Half the clone size, half the clone time.
Network round trips for old content. Catastrophic for archaeology offline.
--depth=1
The smallest, fastest clone by a wide margin.
No history at all. Blame, describe, bisect and merge-base all break.

The defensible default for a developer's machine is: commit-graph and maintenance on, everything else off, and reach for sparse-checkout before partial clone, because sparse-checkout costs you nothing you did not choose. Reach for --depth=1 only in CI, where the job is stateless and throws the clone away.

Exercises

1. A repository's .git is 800 MB. git count-objects -vH reports size-pack: 780.00 MiB and count: 12. Someone suggests running git gc --aggressive. Why is that unlikely to help, and what should you run instead?

Solution

count: 12 means there are twelve loose objects — nothing to pack. The 780 MiB is already packed, so the size is not a packing problem; it is a contents problem. gc --aggressive will spend a great deal of CPU re-deltifying and, if the size is caused by large binary blobs, save almost nothing, because a binary file does not delta well against anything.

Find out what is actually big:

git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check='%(objecttype) %(objectsize:disk) %(objectname)' \
  | awk '$1 == "blob"' | sort -k2 -n -r | head -20

Then map the top ids back to paths with git rev-list --objects --all | grep <id>. If the answer is a handful of large files committed years ago, the fix is history rewriting with git filter-repo (§8.10) followed by git reflog expire --expire=now --all and git gc --prune=now, not repacking. If the answer is many large files that are still needed, the fix is Git LFS (§13.11).

2. Your CI pipeline runs git clone --depth=1 and a build step calls git describe --tags to stamp the version. It prints fatal: No names found, cannot describe anything. Explain, and give two fixes.

Solution

--depth=1 fetches one commit and, by default, only the tags that point at fetched commits. git describe needs to walk backwards to the nearest reachable tag, and there is no history to walk.

Two fixes:

  1. git fetch --depth=<n> deep enough to include a tag, or git fetch --unshallow if you need the lot. Fragile — you are guessing how far back the last tag is.
  2. git clone --filter=blob:none instead of --depth=1. The full commit graph and all tags arrive, blobs do not, and git describe touches no blobs at all so it never triggers a lazy fetch. The clone is slower than shallow but the build works, and this is generally the right modern answer for a CI job that needs any history.

A third answer worth knowing: pass the version in from the CI system's own tag variable and stop asking Git. It is the cheapest fix when it is available.

3. After a git gc, a colleague's repository is larger than before. Give a plausible mechanism.

Solution

The most common mechanism: before gc, many objects existed only as deltas inside a pack that was fetched from the server. gc repacked, and something forced those objects to be stored as bases — for example a repack run with a very small pack.window on a memory-limited machine, or pack.windowMemory clamping the search.

The second mechanism is unreachable history being retained. git gc moves unreachable objects into a cruft pack rather than deleting them, so the two-week grace period (§15.5) can apply per object. If a large branch was just deleted or a big history rewrite just landed, all of that history is still on disk — in a second packfile — until it ages out. On Git before 2.41 this was much worse, because those objects were exploded into loose form instead, and a repository could double in size during a gc. git gc --prune=now collapses it immediately, at the cost of making those objects unrecoverable.

The demonstration in §15.4 is a third: a repack with pack.depth=1 almost tripled the pack, and a subsequent plain git gc kept the bad layout because repacking reuses existing deltas. git repack -a -d -f is the fix in all three cases.

4. Explain why the commit-graph made git merge-base about nine times faster but made git log --graph --oneline --all no faster at all.

Solution

git merge-base is almost pure graph traversal: it walks backwards from two commits looking for a common ancestor, and it does that by reading commit objects. The commit-graph replaces "decompress and parse an object" with "read a fixed-width record", and its generation numbers let whole subgraphs be excluded without being walked. Both effects attack exactly what that command spends its time on.

git log --graph --oneline --all also walks the graph, but the walk is a small fraction of its cost. It must format 82,079 lines, compute the ASCII graph column layout, resolve decorations for every ref, and write it all to a pipe. Making the walk twelve times faster leaves the other 95% untouched, and the measured difference — 3.35 s to 3.55 s — is inside the noise.

The general rule: a cache helps in proportion to how much of the total time it addresses. Measuring the command you actually run is the only way to know.

5. You enable --filter=blob:none across your team's 4 GB monorepo. Clones drop from twelve minutes to three. Two weeks later people complain Git is slower than before. What is the most likely cause, and what two settings would you change?

Solution

Two effects compounding, both shown in §15.11.

First, every lazy fetch writes its own packfile. Two weeks of ordinary work — diffs, blames, checkouts of older branches — produces hundreds of small packs, and object lookup degrades as the pack count rises. The measured demonstration went from 3 packs to 41 from a single git log -p -n 200.

Second, any operation that touches historical content is now network-bound. git log -p over 200 commits went from 0.44 s to 153 s in the measurement above.

The two changes:

  1. git maintenance start in the repository. incremental-repack consolidates the small packs behind a multi-pack index, and prefetch warms objects hourly in the background so interactive commands find them already local.
  2. Add sparse-checkout, and reconsider the filter. If people work on one area, git sparse-checkout set plus a full clone often beats a partial clone outright — the working tree shrinks, status gets faster, and nothing becomes network-dependent. If the filter must stay, git backfill --sparse fills in the objects that area needs, in batches, once.

6. git fsck on a repository you were handed prints several dangling commit lines and one warning ... missingTaggerEntry. Is this repository healthy?

Solution

Almost certainly yes, on both counts.

dangling commit is informational. It means a commit no ref can reach — the normal residue of an amended commit, an abandoned branch, a rebase, or a reset. A repository in active use always has some, and they are exactly what §7.9 recovers from. They are not corruption, and gc will clean them up after the grace period.

missingTaggerEntry is a warning, not an error: a tag object that is well-formed enough to use but lacks a field modern Git would write. Historic tags from the mid-2000s routinely trip it — the Git repository's own v0.99 tag does. Since object ids are immutable, such an object can never be fixed in place without rewriting all of history, which is why fsck grades it as a warning and why fsck.<msg-id> exists to downgrade a known one to ignore.

What would be alarming: any line beginning error:, particularly corrupt loose object, object corrupt or missing, or broken link from. Those mean the object database cannot reconstruct something it references, and the fix is a fresh clone from a good copy rather than any local repair.

SECTION 16

Capstone: Build It, Break It, Recover It

This capstone runs one repository through its whole life: created with nothing but plumbing, damaged in three realistic ways at once, diagnosed, recovered, purged, and verified. Every command is one you have already met, and the point is the composition — that a repository which looks unsalvageable is a sequence of small, checkable operations away from being correct.

The three failures are deliberately the ones that actually happen:

A committed secret
Added, then deleted the next day — and therefore still in every clone (§8.10).
A bloated repository
A large binary committed and removed, still costing every clone its full size (§15.2).
A silently dropped commit
A rebase that lost a bug fix, leaving a history that looks entirely plausible (§8.8).

Work through it in a scratch directory. Every transcript below was produced by running exactly these commands.

16.1 Part One: A Repository With No Porcelain

Build a repository using only the five plumbing commands from §3.7 — no git add, no git commit.

git init -q forge
cd forge
B1=$(printf '# ledger\n\nA tiny double-entry ledger.\n' | git hash-object -w --stdin)
B2=$(printf 'def post(entries):\n    return sum(e["amount"] for e in entries)\n' | git hash-object -w --stdin)
echo "blobs: $B1 $B2"

blobs: a80878f8acd1625a5f848b17442bba501c0507f6 e9bee0f0c1ccce1f8cb0f4c358809c1c6cc72afb

Two blobs in the object database, and no files on disk. Give them names by putting them in the index (§3.4):

git update-index --add --cacheinfo "100644,$B1,README.md"
git update-index --add --cacheinfo "100644,$B2,ledger/post.py"
git ls-files -s

100644 a80878f8acd1625a5f848b17442bba501c0507f6 0	README.md
100644 e9bee0f0c1ccce1f8cb0f4c358809c1c6cc72afb 0	ledger/post.py

Two flat rows. Write them out as a tree and watch the nesting appear (§3.5):

T=$(git write-tree)
git ls-tree -r -t "$T"

100644 blob a80878f8acd1625a5f848b17442bba501c0507f6	README.md
040000 tree ce47b90990bba56f8cc69c25386ef38a39418c50	ledger
100644 blob e9bee0f0c1ccce1f8cb0f4c358809c1c6cc72afb	ledger/post.py

Wrap the tree in a commit and point a branch at it (§3.6, §4.1):

C1=$(echo 'ledger: initial import' | git commit-tree "$T")
git update-ref refs/heads/main "$C1" -m 'capstone: create main'
git log --oneline
git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check | sort -k2

58abb8a ledger: initial import
a80878f8acd1625a5f848b17442bba501c0507f6 blob 38
e9bee0f0c1ccce1f8cb0f4c358809c1c6cc72afb blob 64
58abb8a7716d93bf81822a5cd31c422f9b3eb2cf commit 183
ce47b90990bba56f8cc69c25386ef38a39418c50 tree 35
466fc0b7ccafe6bc528aa052b97438a0d365a918 tree 70

Five objects: two blobs, two trees, one commit. A real repository with real history — and no files:

ls -A
git status -s

.git
 D README.md
 D ledger/post.py

The three trees are genuinely independent (§1.5). HEAD and the index both describe two files; the working tree has none, so git status reports them as deleted. Materialise them:

git checkout -- .
find . -path ./.git -prune -o -type f -print | sort
git status -s

./ledger/post.py
./README.md

Clean. Nothing you will do for the rest of your career is more than a composition of those five commands.

16.2 Part Two: Make the Mess

Now use the porcelain, and make the three mistakes.

printf 'DB_PASSWORD=hunter2\nAPI_TOKEN=sk-live-9f3a2b\n' > .env
git add .env
git commit -q -m 'chore: add local config'

Then some real work, then the "fix":

git commit -q -am 'feat: add balance()'
git rm -q .env
git commit -q -m 'chore: remove .env'
git add -A
git commit -q -m 'feat: add reports'

Add and remove a large asset the same way:

mkdir -p assets
head -c 400000 /dev/urandom > assets/blob.bin
git add -A
git commit -q -m 'feat: add a large asset'
git rm -q assets/blob.bin
git commit -q -m 'chore: remove the asset'

Finally, a feature branch and a rebase that goes wrong. Three commits, the middle one a bug fix:

git switch -q -c feature
git commit -q -m 'feat: add audit()'
git commit -q -am 'fix: audit() handles None'
git commit -q -am 'feat: add audit_all()'
git branch feature-v1
git log --oneline -3

af1e850 feat: add audit_all()
8ff90b6 fix: audit() handles None
3782d42 feat: add audit()

git branch feature-v1 is the safety net of §8.12, and this capstone is about to show exactly why it is worth one command. Now the mistake — an interactive rebase where a line gets deleted:

git rebase -i HEAD~3
git log --oneline -3

658886c feat: add audit_all()
3782d42 feat: add audit()
3a445d0 chore: remove the asset

Two commits where there were three, and no error, no conflict, no warning.

16.3 Part Three: Diagnose

Three separate investigations. Take them one at a time.

The secret. The file is absent from the working tree, so git status is clean and everything looks fine:

git log --oneline --all -- .env
git show 'HEAD~2:.env'

16efc3b chore: remove .env
229c84e chore: add local config
DB_PASSWORD=hunter2
API_TOKEN=sk-live-9f3a2b

Two commits touched it, and the second one — the removal — did nothing to the first. The blob is in the object database, in every clone, and git show prints it in full (§8.10).

The size. Ask what is actually big (§15.2):

git count-objects -vH | grep -E 'in-pack|size-pack'
git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check='%(objecttype) %(objectsize:disk) %(objectname)' \
  | awk '$1 == "blob"' | sort -k2 -n -r | head -3

in-pack: 22
size-pack: 394.09 KiB
blob 400135 35f0d05de80f7fd8ffc6ec1d7f6294308593c996
blob 84 065917a811347c9d1d84f993f97b2294aa88a1f1
blob 55 d86a5882d8e1bf9c5e0077c98001128d57de47c3
