394 KiB for a project whose source is under a kilobyte, and one blob accounts for essentially all of it. Note %(objectsize:disk) rather than %(objectsize) — the number that explains disk usage is the post-compression one, and a random binary does not compress. Map the id back to a path:

git rev-list --objects --all | grep 35f0d05

35f0d05de80f7fd8ffc6ec1d7f6294308593c996 assets/blob.bin

The dropped commit. This is the one nothing obvious catches. git log looks fine — two sensible commits with sensible messages. The code is wrong:

cat ledger/audit.py

def audit(entries):
    return len(entries)


def audit_all(books):
    return sum(audit(b) for b in books)

The None guard is gone. Because feature-v1 exists, git range-diff (§8.8) says exactly what happened:

git range-diff 'feature-v1~3..feature-v1' 'HEAD~2..HEAD'

1:  3782d42 = 1:  3782d42 feat: add audit()
2:  8ff90b6 < -:  ------- fix: audit() handles None
3:  af1e850 < -:  ------- feat: add audit_all()
-:  ------- > 2:  658886c feat: add audit_all()

Read the markers. = unchanged. < present before and gone now — the bug fix, dropped. And af1e850 did not match 658886c closely enough to pair, so it appears as a < and a >: the replayed audit_all() commit has a different diff, because it was applied to a file that no longer had the guard.

This is the reason to run range-diff after every non-trivial rebase. Nothing else in Git would have told you.

16.4 Part Four: Recover

Nothing was lost. The reflog recorded every step (§4.10):

git reflog -6

658886c HEAD@{0}: rebase (finish): returning to refs/heads/feature
658886c HEAD@{1}: rebase (pick): feat: add audit_all()
3782d42 HEAD@{2}: rebase (start): checkout HEAD~3
af1e850 HEAD@{3}: commit: feat: add audit_all()
8ff90b6 HEAD@{4}: commit: fix: audit() handles None
3782d42 HEAD@{5}: commit: feat: add audit()

The rebase's own steps are visible, and HEAD@{3} is the pre-rebase tip. Note how many entries the rebase alone produced — which is why §4.10 recommends <branch>@{1} rather than counting HEAD entries:

git rev-parse --short 'feature@{1}'

af1e850

Name it before doing anything else (§7.9):

git branch rescue 'feature@{1}'
git log --oneline rescue -3

af1e850 feat: add audit_all()
8ff90b6 fix: audit() handles None
3782d42 feat: add audit()

All three commits, intact. Restore and verify:

git reset --hard rescue
cat ledger/audit.py
git range-diff 'feature-v1~3..feature-v1' 'HEAD~3..HEAD'

def audit(entries):
    if entries is None:
        return 0
    return len(entries)


def audit_all(books):
    return sum(audit(b) for b in books)

1:  3782d42 = 1:  3782d42 feat: add audit()
2:  8ff90b6 = 2:  8ff90b6 fix: audit() handles None
3:  af1e850 = 3:  af1e850 feat: add audit_all()

Three = markers: the series is identical to what it was before the rebase. That is a proof, not an impression, and it is the difference between "I think I fixed it" and knowing.

Merge the feature and clean up the scaffolding:

git switch -q main
git merge --no-ff --no-edit feature -m 'Merge branch feature'
git branch -D rescue feature-v1
git log --oneline --graph | head -8

*   ce9c75f Merge branch feature
|\
| * af1e850 feat: add audit_all()
| * 8ff90b6 fix: audit() handles None
| * 3782d42 feat: add audit()
|/
* 3a445d0 chore: remove the asset
* 29082e6 feat: add a large asset

--no-ff keeps the branch's boundary, so git log --first-parent will show this as one integration (§9.7).

16.5 Part Five: Purge and Shrink

Both remaining problems — the secret and the size — are the same operation: remove a path from all of history (§8.10).

git filter-repo refuses to run on a repository that is not a fresh clone, which is the safety feature that keeps the original recoverable. So clone first — and note the flag:

git clone --no-local forge clean
cd clean
git count-objects -vH | grep -E 'in-pack|size-pack'

in-pack: 35
size-pack: 395.67 KiB

--no-local matters. A local clone hardlinks the object database instead of copying it, and filter-repo refuses that outright — rewriting history through hardlinks would damage the original. It says so:

Note: when cloning local repositories, you need to pass
      --no-local to git clone to avoid this issue.

Now the rewrite:

git filter-repo --path .env --path assets/blob.bin --invert-paths

New history written in 0.12 seconds; now repacking/cleaning...
Repacking your repo and cleaning out old unneeded objects
Completely finished after 0.22 seconds.

git log --oneline --graph

*   6b35ef6 Merge branch feature
|\
| * 4613438 feat: add audit_all()
| * a828e77 fix: audit() handles None
| * 8fa38b3 feat: add audit()
|/
* 7d69cee feat: add reports
* 4780f93 feat: add balance()
* 58abb8a ledger: initial import

Seven commits where there were eleven. The four commits that only added or removed the two purged paths — chore: add local config, chore: remove .env, feat: add a large asset and chore: remove the asset — became empty and were pruned, and every commit after the first change has a new object id (§8.1). 58abb8a ledger: initial import kept its id, because nothing before the first change was affected — the same pattern §8.1 demonstrated.

git count-objects -vH | grep -E 'in-pack|size-pack'

in-pack: 25
size-pack: 3.87 KiB

395.67 KiB to 3.87 KiB — a hundredfold reduction, because the 400 KB blob is genuinely gone rather than merely unreferenced.

16.6 Part Six: Verify

Never take a rewrite on trust. Four checks, each of which would have caught a different failure:

git log --all --oneline -- .env assets/blob.bin
git show 'HEAD~1:.env'

fatal: path '.env' does not exist in 'HEAD~1'

No commit anywhere touches either path, and the blob cannot be retrieved.

git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check='%(objecttype) %(objectsize:disk) %(objectname)' \
  | awk '$1 == "blob"' | sort -k2 -n -r | head -2

blob 101 39098cc9cc5e935d0fd9840c9b5f28e6f3d3f91d
blob 84 065917a811347c9d1d84f993f97b2294aa88a1f1

The largest blob is 101 bytes. The binary is not hiding anywhere.

git fsck --connectivity-only

Silence and exit 0: every object referenced by the rewritten history exists (§15.8).

And the substance — that the code is still right:

git log --first-parent --oneline
cat ledger/audit.py

The None guard is present, and the first-parent log lists one entry per integration.

The step this does not cover, and the only one that actually matters: hunter2 and sk-live-9f3a2b were in every clone, in the CI cache, and in the forge's object store from the moment they were pushed. Rotate them. The rewrite is tidying up after a leak, never containment (§8.10). Then force-push every ref, have everyone re-clone, and run git reflog expire --expire=now --all && git gc --prune=now on any repository that keeps the old objects (§4.11).

One more check worth adding to the list: git log --all --oneline -- <path> searches every ref rather than just the current branch, which matters here because a purge that missed a branch would leave the secret reachable from that branch alone. Running git log --all rather than plain git log is the difference between checking one line of history and checking all of them.

16.7 What Each Step Used

Part 1
§3.2–§3.7 the object model · §3.4 the index · §4.1 update-ref · §1.5 the three trees
Part 2
§2.5 committing · §2.4 git rm · §5.2 branching · §8.3 the interactive rebase todo
Part 3
§9.2 <rev>:<path> · §15.2 %(objectsize:disk) · rev-list --objects · §8.8 range-diff
Part 4
§4.10 the reflog · §7.9 the recovery procedure · §7.3 reset --hard · §6.2 --no-ff
Part 5
§8.10 filter-repo · §8.1 why every descendant's id changes · §15.3 packfiles
Part 6
§15.8 fsck · §9.7 --first-parent · §11.10 credential rotation

Three habits did most of the work, and none of them is a command:

git branch feature-v1 before the rebase. One ref, no cost, and it is what made the diagnosis a range-diff instead of an archaeology exercise.

Reading range-diff's markers rather than git log. The broken history was entirely plausible. Only a comparison of the two series showed the missing commit.

Verifying after, not just before. Four checks after the rewrite, each catching a different way it could have gone wrong.

16.8 Extensions

Take the same repository further, using the rest of the course:

Prevent the leak
Add .env to .gitignore (§13.6) and a pre-commit hook that greps staged content for credential patterns (§13.8). Then distribute it with core.hooksPath and back it with a CI check, since hooks are bypassable (§13.7).
Prevent the bloat
Track assets/* with Git LFS (§13.11) and watch the committed object become a 131-byte pointer.
Prevent the drop
git rebase -i --exec 'pytest -q' so every replayed commit is tested (§8.7), and make git range-diff a habit after every rebase.
Find a regression
Plant a bug ten commits back and find it with git bisect run (§9.10). Note what it takes for this to work: every commit must build.
Publish it
git init --bare a server-side repository, set its HEAD (§11.8), push, and read the negotiation with GIT_TRACE_PACKET=1 (§11.6).
Sign the release
An SSH signing key, git tag -s v1.0, an allowed_signers file, and git log --format='%h %G? %s' to confirm (§11.11).
Make it fast
git maintenance start, then git commit-graph write --reachable and time git rev-list --count HEAD before and after (§15.6).

Exercises

1. In Part 1 the repository had a commit, a branch and a full history while ls showed only .git. Explain what each of the three trees held at that moment.

Solution
  • HEAD — refs/heads/main pointed at a commit whose tree listed README.md and ledger/post.py. A complete snapshot, in the object database.
  • The index — two entries, put there by git update-index --add --cacheinfo, naming the same two blobs. --cacheinfo takes an object that is already in the database and gives it a path, so no file on disk was ever involved.
  • The working tree — empty.

git status therefore reported both paths as deleted: HEAD and the index agree that they exist, and the disk disagrees.

This is the clearest possible demonstration that the three are independent (§1.5). git checkout -- . copies the index out to disk and makes them agree, and it is the only step in Part 1 that touched a file.

The practical use of --cacheinfo outside a demonstration is scripted repository construction — importing from another system, or building a commit whose content is generated rather than checked out.

2. After the bad rebase, git log looked entirely reasonable. Name three things that would not have found the dropped commit and say why.

Solution

git log — the remaining commits have sensible messages in a sensible order. Nothing marks the gap, because a rebase produces a genuinely valid history.

git diff between the old and new tips — comparing two trees would show only the net content difference. The None guard would appear as a missing three lines, indistinguishable from a deliberate removal, and on a larger series it would be lost among everything else that legitimately changed.

git status and git fsck — nothing is inconsistent. The repository is perfectly healthy; it merely contains the wrong history.

What did find it is git range-diff (§8.8), which matches the two series commit by commit and prints < for a commit present in the first and absent from the second. It works only because feature-v1 was created before the rebase — without it, the reflog's feature@{1} gives the old tip and the same comparison is still possible, so the recovery would have worked either way; the branch just makes it obvious.

Automated tests would also have caught it, which is why git rebase --exec (§8.7) is worth wiring in.

3. filter-repo reduced the pack from 395.67 KiB to 3.87 KiB, and git gc on the original would not have. Explain.

Solution

git gc removes objects that are unreachable (§15.5). The 400 KB blob was perfectly reachable: the commit feat: add a large asset is in the history of main, its tree names the blob, and the later removal commit did not change that. Reachable objects are never pruned, no matter how large or how long ago.

filter-repo attacks the reachability itself: it rewrites every commit so that no tree ever names that path. The blob becomes unreachable, and the repack that follows drops it. Two commits also became empty in the process and were pruned, which is why the history went from nine commits to seven.

The cost is the one §8.1 established: every commit from the first change onward gets a new object id. That is why this is a coordinated operation — force-push, everyone re-clones, and any branch based on the old ids has to be rebuilt.

The same logic explains why "just delete the file and commit" never shrinks a repository, and why git count-objects -vH plus a sort by %(objectsize:disk) (§15.2) is the right first step: it tells you whether you have a reachability problem or a packing problem, and only the first needs a rewrite.

4. The capstone recovered a rebase with feature@{1}. Under what circumstances would that have failed, and what is the next line of defence?

Solution

Three circumstances (§4.11):

  • The reflog entry expired. Unreachable entries default to 30 days (gc.reflogExpireUnreachable), reachable ones to 90.
  • git reflog expire --expire=now --all was run, which is part of the purge recipe in §16.6 — so doing the purge before the recovery would have destroyed it.
  • A fresh clone. Reflogs are strictly local and are never fetched, pushed or cloned. A colleague's repository cannot help.

The next line of defence is git fsck --lost-found, which walks the object database and writes every unreachable commit and blob into .git/lost-found/. It works because objects survive at least two weeks after becoming unreachable (§15.5). It is blunt — you get object ids with no context, and git show on each is the only way to tell them apart — but the commits are there.

The line of defence after that is a colleague's clone or the server: if the branch was ever pushed, the commits exist elsewhere.

And the one thing nothing can recover is a change that was never staged or committed. git add writes a blob into the database and is, in that sense, a backup (§7.9).

5. Design the prevention. Give three measures that would have stopped all three failures, and say which of them actually enforce anything.

Solution

Secret: .env in .gitignore (§13.6), plus a pre-commit hook that scans staged content for credential patterns (§13.8), plus a secret-scanning check in CI.

Bloat: assets/* filter=lfs in .gitattributes (§13.11), so a large binary is committed as a ~130-byte pointer. A pre-receive hook or CI check rejecting any blob over a threshold catches what LFS misses.

Dropped commit: git rebase -i --exec '<test command>' (§8.7) so every replayed commit is tested, plus git range-diff as a habit, plus CI running the full suite on the branch.

Which of these actually enforce anything:

  • .gitignore and .gitattributes are versioned — everyone gets them from the clone, so they work by default. These are the strongest of the local measures.
  • Hooks are advisory. git commit --no-verify skips them, and .git/hooks/ is not cloned, so anyone who has not run git config core.hooksPath .githooks never had them (§13.7).
  • Only the CI checks and server-side hooks enforce, because they run where the contributor has no control (§12.10).

The design rule: hooks for fast feedback, versioned config for defaults, and a required status check for anything that must actually hold. A rule that exists only as a local hook is not a rule.

6. You are handed a repository and asked whether it is healthy. Give the commands you would run, in order, and what each rules out.

Solution 
git fsck --connectivity-only        # every referenced object exists
git count-objects -vH               # size, loose-object count, pack count
git cat-file --batch-all-objects --batch-check='%(objecttype) %(objectsize:disk) %(objectname)' \
  | awk '$1 == "blob"' | sort -k2 -n -r | head -20
git log --oneline --graph --all | head -40
git log --all --oneline -- '*.env' '*.pem' 'id_rsa' | head

What each rules out:

  1. fsck --connectivity-only (§15.8) — a broken graph: a missing object, a dangling link. Fast enough to be routine; full fsck additionally re-hashes contents and took 117 s on the Git repository, so save it for a suspected hardware problem. dangling lines are informational, and a missingTaggerEntry warning on a very old tag is normal.
  2. count-objects -vH (§15.2) — tells you whether size is a packing problem (many loose objects, many packs → run git gc) or a contents problem (one large pack → step 3).
  3. The blob sort — finds what is actually big and, with git rev-list --objects --all | grep <id>, what path it came from. Anything large and binary is a candidate for LFS or a rewrite (§8.10).
  4. The graph — the branching model: merge commits or a linear history, long-lived branches, whether the history looks rewritten.
  5. The secret sweep — the one check with a deadline attached, because a hit means rotating a credential today.

Then two things no command answers: whether every commit builds (bisectability), and whether the messages are worth reading. Both are found by sampling git log -p for twenty commits.

You started this course able to use Git. You can now open it up: name the object behind each behaviour, predict what a command will do to each of the three trees before you run it, and tell the difference between a thing that is gone, a thing that is unreachable, and a thing that was never recorded. That is a different skill from knowing commands, and it is the one that holds when the command you know does not work.

What you can now do

  1. Reason from the object model. Four object types, each named by the hash of its own bytes, and a commit that contains a whole tree rather than a diff. Blobs holding no filename, trees holding the only filenames there are, and the reason Git has no concept of a rename.
  2. Read the reference layer. A branch as forty-one bytes in a file, HEAD as a symbolic ref, and detached HEAD as the absence of a name rather than a fault. Plus packed-refs, the reftable backend, and the six rules that decide what a bare name means.
  3. Predict add, commit, reset and restore from the three trees, rather than recalling which is which. Three reset modes as three stopping points along one operation.
  4. Merge deliberately. The merge base doing all the work, conflicts as three blobs in the index rather than as markers in a file, zdiff3 as the setting that makes them readable, and the difference between -X ours and -s ours that quietly deletes a colleague's work.
  5. Rewrite history safely. Amend, interactive rebase, --fixup and --autosquash, --onto for moving a range, and range-diff as the only thing that catches a commit silently dropped by a bad rebase.
  6. Recover. The reflog, ORIG_HEAD, fsck --lost-found, and the two-week grace period that makes "I destroyed my work" almost always false — plus the one case where it is true.
  7. Query the graph. ^ against ~, A..B against A...B, -S against -G, --first-parent as the trunk's-eye view, and bisect run turning a twenty-minute chore into four automated tests.
  8. Explain a fetch on the wire. pkt-line framing you can check with arithmetic, ref-prefix as protocol v2's entire reason for existing, and the want/have/ACK negotiation as a distributed set difference.
  9. Handle credentials and signatures. The three-verb helper protocol, why store is the wrong default, SSH signing living in a header called gpgsig, and what a green verified badge does and does not prove.
  10. Compose repositories. A submodule as a 160000 gitlink whose target is not in your object database, a subtree as real blobs, and a worktree as a second index and HEAD — three answers to two entirely different questions.
  11. Configure Git for a team. .gitattributes because it is versioned and config because it is not; includeIf for identities; hooks as ergonomics and CI as policy.
  12. Operate a large repository. Delta chains and why git gc will not undo a bad repack, the commit-graph and exactly which commands it speeds up, and the measured cost of choosing partial clone for the wrong workflow.

Three things worth carrying

Objects are immutable; only pointers move. Every "destructive" operation in Git — amend, rebase, reset, filter-repo, branch deletion — writes new objects and moves a ref. The old objects sit in the database for at least two weeks. That single fact is why the reflog works, why git branch backup before anything risky costs nothing and saves everything, and why the only genuinely unrecoverable loss in this course is a change that was never staged. git add is a backup.

A cache helps in proportion to what it caches. The commit-graph made git merge-base nine times faster and git log --graph --oneline --all no faster at all, because the second command spends its time formatting rather than walking. The same shape recurs everywhere: --force-with-lease protects against a stale ref and not against a background fetch; -s ours records a merge that merged nothing; a pre-commit hook enforces a policy for everyone who installed it. Measure the thing you actually do, and read what a mechanism claims rather than what it is reputed to do.

Verify the result, not the operation. A rebase that silently drops a commit leaves a history that looks completely reasonable — sensible messages, sensible order, clean git status, clean git fsck. Only git range-diff comparing the two series shows the gap. The habit generalises past Git: after any operation that rewrites something, the question is never "did the command succeed", it is "is the result what I meant", and there is usually a command that answers it directly.

Where to go next

The obvious continuations, and what each adds:

  • Linux & the Command Line is the machine all of this runs on — the file abstraction that makes .git a directory you can read, and the processes and permissions behind every hook and every server-side check.
  • Advanced Shell Scripting takes §13's hooks and §12's automation seriously: arrays, traps, getopts, strict mode, and ShellCheck as a gate rather than a suggestion.
  • DevOps & Deployment builds on §12 and §15: pipelines that consume this history, artefacts stamped from git describe, and the CI that turns a convention into a rule.
  • Web Security takes §11's credential and signing material up to the application, and takes §8.10's "rotate the secret first" from advice to practice.
  • Testing is what makes §9.10's bisect and §8.7's --exec work at all, because both depend on every commit building.

And the habit that matters more than any of them: when Git surprises you, ask what is on disk. There is an object, a ref, or an index entry behind every behaviour in this course, and git cat-file, git rev-parse, git ls-files and git for-each-ref will show you all four. Nothing in Git is hidden — it is merely one layer down, and you now know where to look.
